## I / 一
===Original===

Earliest Impressions

On the theory that our genuine impulses may be connected with our childish experiences, that one’s bent may be tracked back to that “No-Man’s Land” where character is formless but nevertheless settling into definite lines of future development, I begin this record with some impressions of my childhood.

All of these are directly connected with my father, although of course I recall many experiences apart from him. I was one of the younger members of a large family and an eager participant in the village life, but because my father was so distinctly the dominant influence and because it is quite impossible to set forth all of one’s early impressions, it has seemed simpler to string these first memories on that single cord. Moreover, it was this cord which not only held fast my supreme affections, but also first drew me into the moral concerns of life, and later afforded a clue there to which I somewhat wistfully clung in the intricacy of its mazes.

===Chinese===

最早的印象

基于这样一个想法——我们真正的冲动或许同童年的经历相连，一个人的志向或许可以追溯到那片“无人之境”，在那里性格尚未成形、却已在朝未来发展的明确路线沉淀——我便从童年的几点印象写起这份记录。

这些印象全都同我的父亲直接相关，虽然我自然也记得许多与他无关的经历。我是一个大家庭里年纪较小的孩子，是乡村生活的热心参与者；可是因为父亲毫无疑问是家中压倒一切的影响，也因为要把一个人全部的早期印象尽数写出是绝无可能的，把这些最初的记忆串在同一根绳上，似乎更简单些。何况，正是这根绳，不仅系着我至高的情感，而且头一个把我引进了人生的道德事务，后来在那座迷宫的重重曲折里，又给了我一条我不无怅惘地紧抓不放的线索。

===Original===

It must have been from a very early period that I recall “horrid nights” when I tossed about in my bed because I had told a lie. I was held in the grip of a miserable dread of death, a double fear, first, that I myself should die in my sins and go straight to that fiery Hell which was never mentioned at home, but which I had heard all about from other children, and, second, that my father﻿—representing the entire adult world which I had basely deceived﻿—should himself die before I had time to tell him. My only method of obtaining relief was to go downstairs to my father’s room and make full confession. The high resolve to do this would push me out of bed and carry me down the stairs without a touch of fear. But at the foot of the stairs I would be faced by the awful necessity of passing the front door﻿—which my father, because of his Quaker tendencies, did not lock﻿—and of crossing the wide and black expanse of the living room in order to reach his door. I would invariably cling to the newel post while I contemplated the perils of the situation, complicated by the fact that the literal first step meant putting my bare foot upon a piece of oilcloth in front of the door, only a few inches wide, but lying straight in my path. I would finally reach my father’s bedside perfectly breathless and having panted out the history of my sin, invariable received the same assurance that if he “had a little girl who told lies,” he was very glad that she “felt too bad to go to sleep afterward.” No absolution was asked for or received, but apparently the sense that the knowledge of my wickedness was shared, or an obscure understanding of the affection which underlay the grave statement, was sufficient, for I always went back to bed as bold as a lion, and slept, if not the sleep of the just, at least that of the comforted.

===Chinese===

想是从很早的年纪起，我就记得那些“可怕的夜晚”——因为说了谎，我在床上翻来覆去。我被一种对死亡的悲惨恐惧攥着：是双重的怕——一怕我自己带着罪孽死去，直坠那家里从不提起、我却从别的孩子嘴里听全了的烈火地狱；二怕我的父亲——他代表着我卑鄙地欺骗了的整个成人世界——会在我来得及向他坦白之前就死去。我唯一的解救之法，就是下楼到父亲的房间里彻底忏悔。这样做的宏愿会把我推出被窝，领我走下楼梯，一点不害怕。可一到楼梯脚下，我就得面对可怕的必经之途：先过前门——父亲因为贵格会的脾气，从不锁门——再横穿那间又宽又黑的堂屋，才够得着他的房门。我总要抱住楼梯柱盘算这一路的凶险，而事情更麻烦的是，字面意义上的第一步，意味着把赤脚踩上门前一条只有几英寸宽的油布，而它正横在我的去路上。等我终于气喘吁吁地摸到父亲床边，喘着气倒出我的罪状，得到的保证永远是一个：倘若他“有一个说谎的小姑娘”，他很高兴她“事后难过得睡不着觉”。赦免没有讨，也没有给；可是显然，知道我的坏有人分担了，或者对那严肃话语底下藏着的爱有一种朦昽的领会，也就够了，因为我总是雄赳赳气昂昂地回到床上，睡上一觉——纵然算不得义人的睡眠，至少是被安慰者的睡眠。

===Original===

I recall an incident which must have occurred before I was seven years old, for the mill in which my father transacted his business that day was closed in 1867. The mill stood in the neighboring town adjacent to its poorest quarter. Before then I had always seen the little city of ten thousand people with the admiring eyes of a country child, and it had never occurred to me that all its streets were not as bewilderingly attractive as the one which contained the glittering toyshop and the confectioner. On that day I had my first sight of the poverty which implies squalor, and felt the curious distinction between the ruddy poverty of the country and that which even a small city presents in its shabbiest streets. I remember launching at my father the pertinent inquiry why people lived in such horrid little houses so close together, and that after receiving his explanation I declared with much firmness when I grew up I should, of course, have a large house, but it would not be built among the other large houses, but right in the midst of horrid little houses like those.

Homestead at Cedarville.

===Chinese===

我记得一件事，必定发生在我七岁之前，因为父亲那天办事的那座磨坊1867年就关了。磨坊坐落在邻镇，紧挨着镇上最穷的街区。在那以前，我一直用一个乡下孩子仰慕的眼睛看这座万人小城，从没想过它并非条条街道都同开着闪亮的玩具店和糖果店的那条一样迷人。那一天，我头一回见到了意味着肮脏穷困的那种贫困，觉出了乡下红润的贫穷同一座哪怕很小的城市在最破败的街上呈现的贫穷之间那种古怪的分别。记得我朝父亲发出直截了当的疑问：人们干吗住在这样挤作一堆的可怕小房子里？听完他的解释，我斩钉截铁地宣布：等我长大，我自然也要有一所大房子，可它不盖在别的大房子中间，就盖在这类可怕小房子正当中。

塞达维尔的家宅。

===Original===

That curious sense of responsibility for carrying on the world’s affairs which little children often exhibit because “the old man clogs our earliest years,” I remember in myself in a very absurd manifestation. I dreamed night after night that everyone in the world was dead excepting myself, and that upon me rested the responsibility of making a wagon wheel. The village street remained as usual, the village blacksmith shop was “all there,” even a glowing fire upon the forge and the anvil in its customary place near the door, but no human being was within sight. They had all gone around the edge of the hill to the village cemetery, and I alone remained alive in the deserted world. I always stood in the same spot in the blacksmith shop, darkly pondering as to how to begin, and never once did I know how, although I fully realized that the affairs of the world could not be resumed until at least one wheel should be made and something started. Every victim of nightmare is, I imagine, overwhelmed by an excessive sense of responsibility and the consciousness of a fearful handicap in the effort to perform what is required; but perhaps never were the odds more heavily against “a warder of the world” than in these reiterated dreams of mine, doubtless compounded in equal parts of a childish version of Robinson Crusoe and of the end-of-the-world predictions of the Second Adventists, a few of whom were found in the village. The next morning would often find me, a delicate little girl of six, with the further disability of a curved spine, standing in the doorway of the village blacksmith shop, anxiously watching the burly, red-shirted figure at work. I would store my mind with such details of the process of making wheels as I could observe, and sometimes I plucked up courage to ask for more. “Do you always have to sizzle the iron in water?” I would ask, thinking how horrid it would be to do. “Sure!” the good-natured blacksmith would reply, “that makes the iron hard.” I would sigh heavily and walk away, bearing my responsibility as best I could, and this of course I confided to no one, for there is something too mysterious in the burden of “the winds that come from the fields of sleep” to be communicated, although it is at the same time too heavy a burden to be borne alone.

===Chinese===

小孩子常常显出一种替世界的运转操心负责的古怪心肠——因为“老人堵住了我们最早的岁月”——我记得在我自己身上，这心肠有过一个非常荒唐的表现。我一夜接一夜地梦见世上人人都死了，只剩我一个，造一只马车轮的责任全压在我身上。村街照旧，村里的铁匠铺样样俱全，炉子上火苗正旺，铁砧照惯例摆在门边，可一个人影也看不见。他们全都绕过山坡到村墓地里去了，只留我独自活在被遗弃的世界里。我总是站在铁匠铺里同一个位置上，阴沉沉地盘算该从哪儿下手；我从来不知道该从哪儿下手，虽然我完全明白，世界的事务不重新运转则已，要运转，至少得先造出一只轮子，先有个开动。我想，每一个梦魇的受害者都被过度的责任感和“自知力不从心却必须完成使命”的意识压垮；可是，同“世界的守卫者”作对的赔率，恐怕再没有比在我这些反复重演的梦里更悬殊的了——这些梦，无疑是一半孩童版的《鲁滨逊漂流记》、一半村里那几个基督复临派的末日预言调成的。第二天早晨，常常可以看见我——一个六岁的纤弱小姑娘，外加脊柱弯曲的毛病——站在村铁匠铺门口，焦灼地望着那个穿红衬衫的壮汉做工。我把做轮子过程中能观察到的细节一一存进脑子里，有时还鼓起勇气多问几句。“铁是不是总得在水里滋一下？”我会问，心里想着这活儿干起来多可怕。“那可不！”好脾气的铁匠答道，“那样铁才硬。”我长叹一声，尽力担着我的责任走开。这心事我当然谁也没告诉，因为“从睡眠的田野吹来的风”这样的担子，自有它神秘得说不出口的地方，可它同时又是重得没法独个儿挑的担子。

===Original===

My great veneration and pride in my father manifested itself in curious ways. On several Sundays, doubtless occurring in two or three different years, the Union Sunday School of the village was visited by strangers, some of those “strange people” who live outside a child’s realm, yet constantly thrill it by their close approach. My father taught the large Bible class in the lefthand corner of the church next to the pulpit, and to my eyes at least, was a most imposing figure in his Sunday frock coat, his fine head rising high above all the others. I imagined that the strangers were filled with admiration for this dignified person, and I prayed with all my heart that the ugly, pigeon-toed little girl, whose crooked back obliged her to walk with her head held very much upon one side, would never be pointed out to these visitors as the daughter of this fine man. In order to lessen the possibility of a connection being made, on these particular Sundays I did not walk beside my father, although this walk was the great event of the week, but attached myself firmly to the side of my Uncle James Addams, in the hope that I should be mistaken for his child, or at least that I should not remain so conspicuously unattached that troublesome questions might identify an Ugly Duckling with her imposing parent. My uncle, who had many children of his own, must have been mildly surprised at this unwonted attention, but he would look down kindly at me, and say, “So you are going to walk with me today?” “Yes, please, Uncle James,” would be my meek reply. He fortunately never explored my motives, nor do I remember that my father ever did, so that in all probability my machinations have been safe from public knowledge until this hour.

===Chinese===

我对父亲的崇敬与骄傲，用古怪的方式表达出来。有几个星期日——想来分属两三个不同的年头——村里的联合主日学来了生客，就是那种住在孩子的疆界之外、却又频频贴近得叫孩子心惊的“陌生人”。父亲在教堂里讲坛旁左手角落教那个大型读经班，在我眼里，他穿上星期日的长礼服，那颗漂亮的头颅高出众人一头，实在是顶威严的人物。我想象那些生客对这位气派的人物满怀倾慕，便全心全意地祈祷，千万别有人把这丑陋的、内八字的小姑娘——她弯曲的脊背害她走路时脑袋歪向一边——指给这些来客看，说是这位体面人物的闺女。为了尽量减少人家把这两者连在一起的可能，每逢这种星期日，我不走在父亲身边——虽说同他并肩走是每星期的大事——而是一头贴牢在詹姆斯·亚当斯伯伯身边，指望人家把我错当成他的孩子，或者至少不至于那么显眼地没有归属，招来费事的盘问，把一只丑小鸭同她威严的家长对上号。伯伯自己儿女成群，对这份反常的亲近想必有点纳闷，可他总是和气地低头看看我，说：“今天要跟伯伯走啦？”“是，麻烦您了，詹姆斯伯伯。”我乖乖地答。幸而他从没追究过我的动机，父亲——就我记得——也从来没有；所以我的这些机关，多半直到此刻才算公之于众。

===Original===

It is hard to account for the manifestations of a child’s adoring affection, so emotional, so irrational, so tangled with the affairs of the imagination. I simply could not endure the thought that “strange people” should know that my handsome father owned this homely little girl. But even in my chivalric desire to protect him from his fate, I was not quite easy in the sacrifice of my uncle, although I quieted my scruples with the reflection that the contrast was less marked and that, anyway, his own little girl “was not so very pretty.” I do not know that I commonly dwelt much upon my personal appearance, save as it thrust itself as an incongruity into my father’s life, and in spite of unending evidence to the contrary, there were even black moments when I allowed myself to speculate as to whether he might not share the feeling. Happily, however, this specter was laid before it had time to grow into a morbid familiar by a very trifling incident. One day I met my father coming out of his bank on the main street of the neighboring city which seemed to me a veritable whirlpool of society and commerce. With a playful touch of exaggeration, he lifted his high and shining silk hat and made me an imposing bow. This distinguished public recognition, this totally unnecessary identification among a mass of “strange people” who couldn’t possibly know unless he himself made the sign, suddenly filled me with a sense of the absurdity of the entire feeling. It may not even then have seemed as absurd as it really was, but at least it seemed enough so to collapse or to pass into the limbo of forgotten specters.

===Chinese===

孩子的孺慕之情，那么感情用事，那么不讲道理，同想象的事物缠夹不清，实在难以解释。我一想到“陌生人”会知道我那位英俊的父亲拥有这么个不好看的小姑娘，就简直受不了。可即便在这份护着他不受命运作弄的骑士心肠里，把伯伯搭进去，我也不大心安；我安抚自己的良心说，反正在伯伯那里反差不那么扎眼，何况他自家的小闺女“也不是顶好看”。我不觉得我平日常常惦记自己的模样，除非它作为一件不般配的东西闯进父亲的生活；而且，尽管有源源不断的反面证据，也真有过晦暗的时刻，容我疑心他兴许也怀着同样的感觉。所幸这个鬼魂还来得及长成一个缠身的恶念，就被一件顶小的事送进了坟墓。有一天，我在邻城的大街上遇见父亲从他的银行里出来——那座城在我看来简直是社会与商业的大漩涡。他带着一点玩笑式的夸张，举高那顶锃亮的大礼帽，朝我郑重其事地一躬。这一下有身份的公开相认，这一下全无必要的相认——在一大群“陌生人”中间，他们绝无从知晓，除非他亲手打这个招呼——忽然叫我觉出这整个念头的荒唐。它当时也许还没有实际上那么荒唐，可至少荒唐得够了，就此坍塌，被打发进被遗忘的鬼魂的阴曹地府。

===Original===

I made still other almost equally grotesque attempts to express this doglike affection. The house at the end of the village in which I was born, and which was my home until I moved to Hull House, in my earliest childhood had opposite to it﻿—only across the road and then across a little stretch of greensward﻿—two mills belonging to my father; one flour mill, to which the various grains were brought by the neighboring farmers, and one sawmill, in which the logs of the native timber were sawed into lumber. The latter offered the great excitement of sitting on a log while it slowly approached the buzzing saw which was cutting it into slabs, and of getting off just in time to escape a sudden and gory death. But the flouring mill was much more beloved. It was full of dusky, floury places which we adored, of empty bins in which we might play house; it had a basement, with piles of bran and shorts which were almost as good as sand to play in, whenever the miller let us wet the edges of the pile with water brought in his sprinkling pot from the millrace.

Mill at Cedarville.

In addition to these fascinations was the association of the mill with my father’s activities, for doubtless at that time I centered upon him all that careful imitation which a little girl ordinarily gives to her mother’s ways and habits. My mother had died when I was a baby and my father’s second marriage did not occur until my eighth year.

===Chinese===

为表达这份狗一般的深情，我还做过别的几乎同样古怪的努力。我出生的那座房子在村尾，一直是我的家，直到我搬去赫尔馆（Hull House）为止；我最早的时候，它对面——只隔着一条马路，再隔一小片草皮——立着父亲的两座磨坊：一座磨面坊，邻近的农夫们把各样谷物送到那里；一座锯木坊，本地林木的圆木在那里锯成板材。后者的最大刺激，是坐在一根原木上，随它慢慢逼近那把它锯成板的嗡嗡作响的锯子，然后恰好在猝死和血光之前跳下来。可磨面坊更得宠。它满是幽暗的、扑着面粉的角落，我们爱极了；满是空粮柜，可以在里面过家家；还有一间地下室，麸皮和次粉堆得几乎同沙子一样好玩——只要磨坊主肯让我们用他从磨坊水渠里提来的喷壶水，把堆的边上打湿。

塞达维尔的磨坊。

除了这些迷人之处，磨坊还同父亲的活动连在一起；因为毫无疑问，那时我把小姑娘认给母亲的那份细心的模仿，整个儿给了父亲。我母亲在我襁褓中就去世了，父亲续弦是在我八岁那年。

===Original===

I had a consuming ambition to posses a miller’s thumb, and would sit contentedly for a long time rubbing between my thumb and fingers the ground wheat as it fell from between the millstones, before it was taken up on an endless chain of mysterious little buckets to be bolted into flour. I believe I have never since wanted anything more desperately than I wanted my right thumb to be flattened, as my father’s had become, during his earlier years of a miller’s life. Somewhat discouraged by the slow process of structural modification, I also took measures to secure on the backs of my hands the tiny purple and red spots which are always found on the hands of the miller who dresses millstones. The marks on my father’s hands had grown faint, but were quite visible when looked for, and seemed to me so desirable that they must be procured at all costs. Even when playing in our house or yard, I could always tell when the millstones were being dressed, because the rumbling of the mill then stopped, and there were few pleasures I would not instantly forego, rushing at once to the mill, that I might spread out my hands near the millstones in the hope that the little hard flints flying form the miller’s chisel would light upon their backs and make the longed-for marks. I used hotly to accuse the German miller, my dear friend Ferdinand, “of trying not to hit my hands,” but he scornfully replied that he could not hit them if he did try, and that they were too little to be of use in a mill anyway. Although I hated his teasing, I never had the courage to confess my real purpose.

===Chinese===

我有一份火烧火燎的心愿：要长出一根磨坊主的大拇指。磨盘间磨出的麦粒，在被打上传送链那一串神秘小桶拿去筛成面粉之前，我会把它夹在拇指和指头间捻上半天，坐得心满意足。我相信，此后我再没有像盼望我的右手拇指变扁那样热切地盼望过什么——父亲早年做磨坊主，拇指就磨扁了。眼看这番身体改造进展缓慢，我有点泄气，便另想办法，要在我手背上弄到那种紫红小点——给磨盘凿齿的磨坊主手上总有。我父亲手上的印记淡了，可留心看还看得见，在我眼里那样可羡，必须不惜代价弄到手。哪怕在家里院子里玩，我总能知道什么时候在修磨盘，因为那时磨坊的轰隆声停了。几乎没有什么我不肯立刻撇下的乐趣，一溜烟奔到磨坊，把手背摊在磨盘近旁，指望磨匠凿子上迸出的小硬燧石能落在手背上，砸出我朝思暮想的印记。我常常气呼呼地控告那位德国磨匠、我的好朋友费迪南德，说他“存心不砸我的手”，可他不屑地回敬：他就是想砸也砸不着，再说这两只手也太小，在磨坊里压根使不上。我恨他的打趣，却始终没勇气说出我的真实目的。

===Original===

This sincere tribute of imitation, which affection offers to its adored object, had later, I hope, subtler manifestations, but certainly these first ones were altogether genuine. In this case, too, I doubtless contributed my share to that stream of admiration which our generation so generously poured forth for the self-made man. I was consumed by a wistful desire to apprehend the hardships of my father’s earlier life in that faraway time when he had been a miller’s apprentice. I knew that he still woke up punctually at three o’clock because for so many years he had taken his turn at the mill in the early morning, and if by chance I awoke at the same hour, as curiously enough I often did, I imagined him in the early dawn in my uncle’s old mill reading through the entire village library, book after book, beginning with the lives of the signers of the Declaration of Independence. Copies of the same books, mostly bound in calfskin, were to be found in the library below, and I courageously resolved that I too would read them all and try to understand life as he did. I did in fact later begin a course of reading in the early morning hours, but I was caught by some fantastic notion of chronological order and early legendary form. Pope’s translation of the *Iliad*, even followed by Dryden’s Virgil, did not leave behind the residuum of wisdom for which I longed, and I finally gave them up for a thick book entitled *The History of the World* as affording a shorter and an easier path.

===Chinese===

这份爱慕献给它崇拜的对象的诚挚的模仿之礼，但愿后来有过更精微的表现，可最初这些，确实无一不真。在这件事上，我也无疑为那股敬佩之流添了我的一份——我们那一代人对白手起家的人，倾泻得何等慷慨。我热切地巴望领会父亲早年的艰辛，那他还当磨坊学徒的遥远岁月。我知道他至今仍旧在凌晨三点准时醒来，因为那么多年里，清晨的磨坊轮着他照管；倘若碰巧我也在同一时刻醒来——说来也怪，我常常如此——我就想象他趁着黎明，在伯伯的老磨坊里，把整个村图书馆的书一本接一本读完，从《独立宣言》签署人的传记读起。同样这批书，多半是小牛皮装帧的，就摆在楼下的图书馆里；我勇敢地下定决心，也要把它们全读完，也像他那样去努力理解人生。后来我确实在清晨开始了一门阅读功课，可惜被某种按年代顺序、从上古传说读起的怪念头缠住了。蒲柏译的《伊利亚特》，甚至接着德莱顿译的维吉尔，都没能留下我渴求的那点智慧残渣，我最终撇下它们，改攻一部厚厚的《世界史》，图的是一条更短更容易的路。

===Original===

Although I constantly confided my sins and perplexities to my father, there are only a few occasions on which I remember having received direct advice or admonition; it may easily be true, however, that I have forgotten the latter, in the manner of many seekers after advice who enjoyably set forth their situation but do not really listen to the advice itself. I can remember an admonition on one occasion, however, when, as a little girl of eight years, arrayed in a new cloak, gorgeous beyond anything I had ever worn before, I stood before my father for his approval. I was much chagrined by his remark that it was a very pretty cloak﻿—in fact so much prettier than any cloak the other little girls in the Sunday School had, that he would advise me to wear my old cloak, which would keep me quite as warm, with the added advantage of not making the other little girls feel badly. I complied with the request but I fear without inner consent, and I certainly was quite without the joy of self-sacrifice as I walked soberly through the village street by the side of my counselor. My mind was busy, however, with the old question eternally suggested by the inequalities of the human lot. Only as we neared the church door did I venture to ask what could be done about it, receiving the reply that it might never be righted so far as clothes went, but that people might be equal in things that mattered much more than clothes, the affairs of education and religion, for instance, which we attended to when we went to school and church, and that it was very stupid to wear the sort of clothes that made it harder to have equality even there.

===Chinese===

虽然我不断向父亲倾诉我的过失和困惑，可我记得只有过寥寥几回直接的忠告或训诫；不过也很可能是我把后者忘了——许多讨主意的人都这样，津津有味地摆完自己的处境，对主意本身却并不真听。可有一回训诫我记得：那年我八岁，穿一件新斗篷，阔气得超过我从前穿过的任何衣服，站在父亲面前等他夸奖。他的一句话叫我大为沮丧——他说这斗篷很好看，其实比主日学里别的姑娘的哪一件都好看得多，所以他劝我还是穿旧的那件，旧的一样暖和，还有个好处，不至于叫别的姑娘心里难受。我照办了，只怕心里并不情愿；我陪着这位顾问先生，规规矩矩走在村街上，身上肯定没有半点自我牺牲的喜悦。可我的脑子没闲着，转着人间际遇不平等所永远挑起的那个老问题。直到走近教堂门口，我才敢问：这件事可有什么办法。得到的回答是：就衣服而论，也许永远摆不平；可人可以在比衣服要紧得多的事情上平等——比如教育和宗教，上学和上教堂时我们操心的就是这些；再说，穿那种叫人在这些地方也难平等的衣服，是很愚蠢的。

===Original===

It must have been a little later when I held a conversation with my father upon the doctrine of foreordination, which at one time very much perplexed my childish mind. After setting the difficulty before him and complaining that I could not make it out, although my best friend “understood it perfectly,” I settled down to hear his argument, having no doubt that he could make it quite clear. To my delighted surprise, for any intimation that our minds were on an equality lifted me high indeed, he said that he feared that he and I did not have the kind of mind that would ever understand foreordination very well and advised me not to give too much time to it; but he then proceeded to say other things of which the final impression left upon my mind was, that it did not matter much whether one understood foreordination or not, but that it was very important not to pretend to understand what you didn’t understand and that you must always be honest with yourself inside, whatever happened. Perhaps on the whole as valuable a lesson as the shorter catechism itself contains.

My memory merges this early conversation on religious doctrine into one which took place years later when I put before my father the situation in which I found myself at boarding school when under great evangelical pressure, and once again I heard his testimony in favor of “mental integrity above everything else.”

At the time we were driving through a piece of timber in which the wood choppers had been at work during the winter, and so earnestly were we talking that he suddenly drew up the horses to find that he did not know where he was. We were both entertained by the incident, I that my father had been “lost in his own timber” so that various cords of wood must have escaped his practiced eye, and he on his side that he should have become so absorbed in this maze of youthful speculation. We were in high spirits as we emerged from the tender green of the spring woods into the clear light of day, and as we came back into the main road I categorically asked him:﻿—

“What are you? What do you say when people ask you?”

His eyes twinkled a little as he soberly replied:

“I am a Quaker.”

“But that isn’t enough to say,” I urged.

===Chinese===

稍晚一些时候，我同父亲谈过一次宿命论的教义，它一度叫我孩子气的头脑大惑不解。我把难处摆在他面前，抱怨说我想不明白，虽然我最好的朋友“完全懂了”，然后安安稳稳坐好听他的论证，毫不怀疑他能讲得一清二楚。他叫我喜出望外——凡有一句半句暗示我们俩头脑平起平坐的话，都能把我高高抬起——他说，他恐怕同我一样，都不是那种能把宿命论弄得很明白的头脑，劝我别在这上头花太多工夫；随后他又讲了些别的，留在我心里的最后印象是：懂不懂宿命论，关系不大；要紧的是，不懂千万不要装懂，而且无论出了什么事，对自己内心要永远诚实。总的说来，这或许同短篇要理问答本身所包含的一样，是宝贵的一课。

我的记忆把这场关于宗教教义的早期谈话，同多年后的一场合而为一：那时我向父亲摆出自己在寄宿学校的处境——正处在福音派的重压之下。我再一次听见他的证词：“心灵的正直高于一切。”

那回我们正驾车穿过一片树林，伐木工人们冬天在里面干过活；我们谈得太专心，他猛地勒住马，才发现自己迷了路。这件事我们俩都觉得好玩：我玩味的是父亲竟“在自家的林子里迷了路”，成堆的木柴竟逃过了他那双行家的眼睛；他那边呢，是懊恼自己竟会沉溺在这座少年玄想的迷宫里。我们从春天树林柔嫩的新绿里钻出来，回到光天化日之下，两人兴致都很高；等上了大路，我直截了当地问他：——

“您算什么？人家问起您，您怎么说？”

他的眼睛微微一亮，一本正经地答道：

“我是贵格会教徒。”

“可这么说不顶事啊。”我催促道。

===Original===

“Very well,” he added, “to people who insist upon details, as someone is doing now, I add that I am a Hicksite Quaker”; and not another word on the weighty subject could I induce him to utter.

These early recollections are set in a scene of rural beauty, unusual at least for Illinois. The prairie around the village was broken into hills, one of them crowned by pine woods, grown up from a bag full of Norway pine seeds sown by my father in 1844, the very year he came to Illinois, a testimony perhaps that the most vigorous pioneers gave at least an occasional thought to beauty. The banks of the mill stream rose into high bluffs too perpendicular to be climbed without skill, and containing caves of which one at least was so black that it could not be explored without the aid of a candle; and there was a deserted limekiln which became associated in my mind with the unpardonable sin of Hawthorne’s “Lime-Burner.” My stepbrother and I carried on games and crusades which lasted week after week, and even summer after summer, as only free-ranging country children can do. It may be in contrast to this that one of the most piteous aspects in the life of city children, as I have seen it in the neighborhood of Hull House, is the constant interruption to their play which is inevitable on the streets, so that it can never have any continuity﻿—the most elaborate “plan or chart” or “fragment from their dream of human life” is sure to be rudely destroyed by the passing traffic. Although they start over and over again, even the most vivacious become worn out at last and take to that passive “standing ’round” varied by rude horseplay, which in time becomes so characteristic of city children.

===Chinese===

“好吧，”他补充道，“对那些非要打破砂锅问到底的人——好比此刻的某一位——我就再添一句：我是希克斯派贵格会教徒。”这个重大题目，我再没能引他说出半个字。

这些早期的回忆，都镶嵌在一种乡野的美景里，至少对伊利诺伊州来说是难得的。村子四周的大草原起伏成丘，其中一座顶着一片松林，是父亲1844年——正是他来伊利诺伊的那一年——播下一袋挪威云杉种子长起来的；这或许是个见证，说明最有活力的拓荒者，对美也并非从不下偶尔的思量。磨坊溪的两岸升成高崖，陡得没点本事爬不上去，崖里藏着山洞，其中至少有一个黑得没蜡烛不敢进；还有一座废弃的石灰窑，在我心里同霍桑那“烧石灰人”的不可赦之罪连在一起。我和继兄进行的游戏和远征，一星期连一星期，甚至一个夏天连一个夏天——只有撒开脚野跑的乡下孩子才办得到。也许正因为对照，城市儿童生活里最令我痛心的景象之一——我在赫尔馆附近见过——就是他们的游戏在街上不可避免地不断被打断，永远无法连贯：再精心的“规划或蓝图”，再美的“从人生之梦里摘下的片段”，也保准被过往的车辆粗暴地毁掉。他们一遍又一遍从头开始，可连最有生气的，到头来也磨得没了劲，转为那种被动的“扎堆站着”，中间掺和些粗野的打闹——日子一久，这便成了城市儿童的标记。

===Original===

We had of course our favorite places and trees and birds and flowers. It is hard to reproduce the companionship which children establish with nature, but certainly it is much too unconscious and intimate to come under the head of aesthetic appreciation or anything of the sort. When we said that the purple windflowers﻿—the anemone patens﻿—“looked as if the winds had made them,” we thought much more of the fact that they were wind-born than that they were beautiful: we clapped our hands in sudden joy over the soft radiance of the rainbow, but its enchantment lay in our half belief that a pot of gold was to be found at its farther end; we yielded to a soft melancholy when we heard the whippoorwill in the early twilight, but while he aroused in us vague longings of which we spoke solemnly, we felt no beauty in his call.

We erected an altar beside the stream, to which for several years we brought all the snakes we killed during our excursions, no matter how long the toil﻿—some journey which we had to make with a limp snake dangling between two sticks. I remember rather vaguely the ceremonial performed upon this altar one autumn day, when we brought as further tribute one out of every hundred of the black walnuts which we had gathered, and then poured over the whole a pitcher full of cider, fresh from the cider mill on the barn floor. I think we had also burned a favorite book or two upon this pyre of stones. The entire affair carried on with such solemnity was probably the result of one of those imperative impulses under whose compulsion children seek a ceremonial which shall express their sense of identification with man’s primitive life and their familiar kinship with the remotest past.

Long before we had begun the study of Latin at the village school, my brother and I had learned the Lord’s Prayer in Latin out of an old copy of the Vulgate, and gravely repeated it every night in an execrable pronunciation because it seemed to us more religious than “plain English.”

===Chinese===

我们自然有我们心爱的去处、心爱的树、鸟和花。孩子们同自然建立起来的这份交情，很难复述；但可以肯定，它太不自觉、太亲昵，算不得审美鉴赏之类。当我们说紫色的银莲花（anemone patens）“看着就像风做出来的”，我们想的更多的是它们由风而生，而不是它们美：彩虹柔光乍现，我们欢喜得直拍手，可它的迷人处在于我们半信半疑，以为它的尽头能找到一罐金子；薄暮里听见三声夜鹰叫，我们也会生出一种软软的忧郁，可它唤起我们郑重谈论的种种朦昽渴望时，我们对它的叫声并不觉得美。

我们在溪边立了一座祭坛，一连好几年，远足中打死的蛇，无论多远都送到那里去——有时得两个人用棍子抬着一条耷拉的蛇走上一路。我记得不大真切了，有一年秋天，我们在这座祭坛上举行的仪式：我们把采来的黑胡桃，每一百颗里献上一颗，作为追加的贡物，再把满满一罐苹果酒浇上去——酒是刚从谷仓地上的榨酒坊里出来的。我想我们还在这石头的柴堆上烧过一两本心爱的书。这桩办得如此郑重其事的整个儿的事，多半是那种不由分说的冲动使然——孩子们受它驱使，要寻求一种仪式，来表达他们同人类原始生活的认同，表达他们同最遥远的过去那份熟稔的亲缘。

早在村里的学校开始教拉丁文之前很久，哥哥和我已经从一本旧版的《武加大译本》里用拉丁文学会了主祷文，每天晚上用糟得不能再糟的发音庄重念诵，因为在我们看来，它比“平平常常的英文”更像宗教。

===Original===

When, however, I really prayed, what I saw before my eyes was a most outrageous picture which adorned a songbook used in Sunday School, portraying the Lord upon his throne, surrounded by tiers and tiers of saints and angels all in a blur of yellow. I am ashamed to tell how old I was when that picture ceased to appear before my eyes, especially when moments of terror compelled me to ask protection from the heavenly powers.

I recall with great distinctness my first direct contact with death when I was fifteen years old: Polly was an old nurse who had taken care of my mother and had followed her to frontier Illinois to help rear a second generation of children. She had always lived in our house, but made annual visits to her cousins on a farm a few miles north of the village. During one of those visits, word came to us one Sunday evening that Polly was dying, and for a number of reasons I was the only person able to go to her. I left the lamp-lit, warm house to be driven four miles through a blinding storm which every minute added more snow to the already high drifts, with a sense of starting upon a fateful errand. An hour after my arrival all of the cousin’s family went downstairs to supper, and I was left alone to watch with Polly. The square, old-fashioned chamber in the lonely farmhouse was very cold and still, with nothing to be heard but the storm outside. Suddenly the great change came. I heard a feeble call of “Sarah,” my mother’s name, as the dying eyes were turned upon me, followed by a curious breathing and in place of the face familiar from my earliest childhood and associated with homely household cares, there lay upon the pillow strange, august features, stern and withdrawn from all the small affairs of life. That sense of solitude, of being unsheltered in a wide world of relentless and elemental forces which is at the basis of childhood’s timidity and which is far from outgrown at fifteen, seized me irresistibly before I could reach the narrow stairs and summon the family from below.

===Chinese===

然而，当我真正祈祷的时候，眼前浮现的是一幅放肆至极的画，它印在主日学用的一本歌本上：主坐在宝座上，四周一圈又一圈的圣徒和天使，全笼在一团模糊的黄色里。说出来叫人惭愧：直到多大的年纪，那幅画才不再出现在我眼前——尤其是在恐怖的时刻逼得我向天上的能力求庇护的时候。

十五岁那年同死亡的第一次直接接触，我记得清清楚楚：波莉是一位老保姆，照看过我母亲，又跟着她来到边疆的伊利诺伊，帮着拉扯第二代孩子。她一直住在我们家，只是每年去村北几英里的农庄上看望她的表亲。一次探望期间，一个星期日的晚上传来话，说波莉要不行了；因为种种缘故，全家只有我一个人能去。我离开灯光明亮的温暖的家，乘车在遮天蔽日的风雪里走了四英里——每分钟都有新的雪加到本已高高的雪堆上——心里怀着一种踏上重大使命的感觉。我到后一个钟头，表亲全家都下楼吃晚饭去了，只留我一个人守着波莉。孤零零的农舍里那间四四方方的老式卧房又冷又静，除了外面的风雪，什么声音也没有。大变故突然来了。我听见一声微弱的呼唤——“萨拉”，我母亲的名字——垂死的眼睛转向了我；接着是一种古怪的呼吸；枕头上一不再是我自幼熟悉、同家常照料连在一起的那张脸，躺着的是一张陌生、庄严的面容，严峻，退出了生活里一切琐细的事务。那种孤独感，那种在无情的基本力量的广大世界里无处遮蔽的感觉——它正是童年怯懦的底子，而且十五岁远没有长过它——不容抗拒地攫住了我，我几乎来不及走到窄窄的楼梯口，把楼下的一家人叫上来。

===Original===

As I was driven home in the winter storm, the wind through the trees seemed laden with a passing soul and the riddle of life and death pressed hard; once to be young, to grow old and to die, everything came to that, and then a mysterious journey out into the Unknown. Did she mind faring forth alone? Would the journey perhaps end in something as familiar and natural to the aged and dying as life is to the young and living? Through all the drive and indeed throughout the night these thoughts were pierced by sharp worry, a sense of faithlessness because I had forgotten the text Polly had confided to me long before as the one from which she wished her funeral sermon to be preached. My comfort as usual finally came from my father, who pointed out what was essential and what was of little avail even in such a moment as this, and while he was much too wise to grow dogmatic upon the great theme of death, I felt a new fellowship with him because we had discussed it together.

Perhaps I may record here my protest against the efforts, so often made, to shield children and young people from all that has to do with death and sorrow, to give them a good time at all hazards on the assumption that the ills of life will come soon enough. Young people themselves often resent this attitude on the part of their elders; they feel set aside and belittled as if they were denied the common human experiences. They too wish to climb steep stairs and to eat their bread with tears, and they imagine that the problems of existence which so press upon them in pensive moments would be less insoluble in the light of these great happenings.

===Chinese===

坐车冒着冬天的风雪回家，一路上风穿过树林，仿佛载着一缕正在离去的魂灵；生与死之谜重重地压过来。一朝年轻，一朝衰老，一朝死去——一切都归结到这里，随后是一段走进未知的神秘旅程。她一个人上路，介意吗？这段旅程的终点，会不会落在某种对老人和垂死者说来同生活之于年轻人和活着的人一样家常、一样自然的东西上？一路上，乃至整夜里，这些念头不断被一阵阵尖锐的自责扎穿——我觉得自己不忠，因为我忘了波莉多年前托付给我的那节经文，她说她希望葬礼上的布道就讲那一节。我的安慰照例最后来自父亲：他指明，即便在这样的时刻，什么是紧要的，什么是无济于事的；他太明智了，不会在死亡这个大题目上变得教条；而我因为同他一起讨论过它，感到同他有了新的伙伴之谊。

或许我可以在这里记下我的抗议——人们常常那样煞费苦心，把儿童和青年同一切与死亡、悲伤有关的东西隔开，不惜一切代价让他们快活，理由是人生的祸患来得已经够快了。年轻人自己倒常常怨恨长辈的这种态度；他们觉得被撇在一旁、被小看，仿佛被剥夺了共同的人类经验。他们也渴望登上陡峭的楼梯，含着眼泪吃他们的面包；他们想象，那些在沉思的时刻紧紧压迫着他们的生存问题，放在这些大事情的光里，也许就没那么无解了。

===Original===

An incident which stands out clearly in my mind as an exciting suggestion of the great world of moral enterprise and serious undertakings must have occurred earlier than this, for in 1872, when I was not yet twelve years old, I came into my father’s room one morning to find him sitting beside the fire with a newspaper in his hand, looking very solemn; and upon my eager inquiry what had happened, he told me that Joseph Mazzini was dead. I had never even heard Mazzini’s name, and after being told about him I was inclined to grow argumentative, asserting that my father did not know him, that he was not an American, and that I could not understand why we should be expected to feel badly about him. It is impossible to recall the conversation with the complete breakdown of my cheap arguments, but in the end I obtained that which I have ever regarded as a valuable possession, a sense of the genuine relationship which may exist between men who share large hopes and like desires, even though they differ in nationality, language, and creed; that those things count for absolutely nothing between groups of men who are trying to abolish slavery in America or to throw off Hapsburg oppression in Italy. At any rate, I was heartily ashamed of my meager notion of patriotism, and I came out of the room exhilarated with the consciousness that impersonal and international relations are actual facts and not mere phrases. I was filled with pride that I knew a man who held converse with great minds and who really sorrowed and rejoiced over happenings across the sea. I never recall those early conversations with my father, nor a score of others like them, but there comes into my mind a line from Mrs. Browning in which a daughter describes her relations with her father:﻿—

“He wrapt me in his large

Man’s doublet, careless did it fit or no.”

===Chinese===

有一件事在我心里清清楚楚地凸着，它像一扇令人兴奋的窗，通向道德事业与严肃工作的广大世界；它必定发生得比这更早，因为1872年——我还不满十二岁——一天早晨我走进父亲的房间，见他手拿报纸坐在炉边，神色极为凝重；我急切地问出了什么事，他告诉我：朱塞佩·马志尼去世了。我连马志尼的名字都没听过；听完介绍，我还打算抬杠，一口咬定父亲并不认识他，他不是美国人，我不明白凭什么指望我们为他难过。父亲如何把我那些廉价的论据一一击垮，这段对话已无从追忆，但最终我得到了一样东西——我一向视为宝贵的财富：一种对人与人之间真实纽带的感觉——只要他们怀着同样的宏愿和同样的渴望，纵使国籍、语言、信仰各异，这纽带也真实存在；对美国要废除蓄奴的人、对意大利要挣脱哈布斯堡压迫的人，那一切在他们中间一文不值。总之，我为自己那点贫乏的爱国观念羞愧不已，走出房门时振奋地意识到：超乎个人的、国际间的关系是实实在在的事实，不是空话。我满心骄傲，因为我认识一个同伟大心灵交谈的人，一个真正为海外发生的事悲伤和欢喜的人。我一回想同父亲的这些早期谈话——还有另外几十次类似的谈话——心里就浮起布朗宁夫人的一行诗，做女儿的用它描写她同父亲的关系：——

“他用他那宽大的

男人的罩袍把我裹住，合不合身全不在乎。”

## II / 二
===Original===

Influence of Lincoln

I suppose all the children who were born about the time of the Civil War have recollections quite unlike those of the children who are living now. Although I was but four and a half years old when Lincoln died, I distinctly remember the day when I found on our two white gateposts American flags companioned with black. I tumbled down on the harsh gravel walk in my eager rush into the house to inquire what they were “there for.” To my amazement I found my father in tears, something that I had never seen before, having assumed, as all children do, that grownup people never cried. The two flags, my father’s tears, and his impressive statement that the greatest man in the world had died, constituted my initiation, my baptism, as it were, into the thrilling and solemn interests of a world lying quite outside the two white gateposts. The great war touched children in many ways: I remember an engraved roster of names, headed by the words “Addams’ Guard,” and the whole surmounted by the insignia of the American eagle clutching many flags, which always hung in the family living-room. As children we used to read this list of names again and again. We could reach it only by dint of putting the family Bible on a chair and piling the dictionary on top of it; using the Bible to stand on was always accompanied by a little thrill of superstitious awe, although we carefully put the dictionary above that our profane feet might touch it alone. Having brought the roster within reach of our eager fingers﻿—fortunately it was glazed﻿—we would pick out the names of those who “had fallen on the field” from those who “had come back from the war,” and from among the latter those whose children were our schoolmates. When drives were planned, we would say, “Let us take this road,” that we might pass the farm where a soldier had once lived; if flowers from the garden were to be given away, we would want them to go to the mother of one of those heroes whose names we knew from the “Addams’ Guard.” If a guest should become interested in the roster on the wall, he was at once led by the eager children to a small picture of Colonel Davis which hung next the opposite window, that he might see the brave Colonel of the Regiment.

===Chinese===

林肯的影响

我想，凡在南北战争前后出生的孩子，他们的记忆必与今日的孩子大不相同。林肯去世时我才四岁半，可我分明记得那一天：我们家两根白门柱上，星条旗伴着黑纱。我急匆匆冲进屋去问这是「干什么用的」，一头摔在粗粝的砾石道上。叫我大为惊愕的是，我看见父亲在流泪——这是我从没见过的事，因为我和所有的孩子一样，一向以为大人是从不哭的。那两面旗，父亲的眼泪，和他那句郑重的话——世上最伟大的人死了——构成了我的入教礼，我的受洗，可以说，把我领进了那两根白门柱之外一个世界的激动人心而庄严的事务里。

大战从许多方面触及孩子：我记得一张刻印的名册，抬头是「亚当斯卫队」（Addams' Guard）几个字，顶上是一枚攥着多面旗帜的美国鹰徽，一直挂在家里堂屋。小时候我们总是一遍又一遍读那份名单。要够得着它，非得把家里的《圣经》搁在椅子上、再把字典摞上去不可；踩着《圣经》垫脚，总伴着一阵迷信的敬畏，好在我们小心地把字典放在上面，让渎神的脚只碰它。把名册够到急切的手指底下——幸好它罩着玻璃——我们便从「打仗回来了的」里头挑出「阵亡疆场的」，再从前者里挑出孩子同我们同学的人家。每逢计划驾车出游，我们总说「走这条路吧」，好经过某个当过兵的人住过的农庄；园子里的花若要送人，我们总想让它们送到一位英雄的母亲手里——她儿子的名字，我们从「亚当斯卫队」的名册上认得。客人若对墙上那张名册起了兴致，孩子们立刻热切地领他到对面窗口旁挂着的一幅小像前，那是戴维斯上校（Colonel Davis），好让他见见本团这位英勇的上校。

===Original===

The introduction to the picture of the one-armed man seemed to us a very solemn ceremony, and long after the guest was tired of listening, we would tell each other all about the local hero, who at the head of his troops had suffered wounds unto death. We liked very much to talk to a gentle old lady who lived in a white farmhouse a mile north of the village. She was the mother of the village hero, Tommy, and used to tell us of her long anxiety during the spring of ’62; how she waited day after day for the hospital to surrender up her son, each morning airing the white homespun sheets and holding the little bedroom in immaculate readiness. It was after the battle of Fort Donelson that Tommy was wounded and had been taken to the hospital at Springfield; his father went down to him and saw him getting worse each week, until it was clear that he was going to die; but there was so much red tape about the department, and affairs were so confused, that his discharge could not be procured. At last the hospital surgeon intimated to his father that he should quietly take him away; a man as sick as that, it would be all right; but when they told Tommy, weak as he was, his eyes flashed, and he said, “No, sir; I will go out of the front door or I’ll die here.” Of course after that every man in the hospital worked for it, and in two weeks he was honorably discharged. When he came home at last, his mother’s heart was broken to see him so wan and changed. She would tell us of the long quiet days that followed his return, with the windows open so that the dying eyes might look over the orchard slope to the meadow beyond where the younger brothers were mowing the early hay. She told us of those days when his school friends from the Academy flocked in to see him, their old acknowledged leader, and of the burning words of earnest patriotism spoken in the crowded little room, so that in three months the Academy was almost deserted and the new Company who marched away in the autumn took as drummer boy Tommy’s third brother, who was only seventeen and too young for a regular. She remembered the still darker days that followed, when the bright drummer boy was in Andersonville prison, and little by little she learned to be reconciled that Tommy was safe in the peaceful home graveyard.

John H. Addams.

===Chinese===

给客人引见那幅独臂人的画像，在我们看来是极庄严的仪式；客人早听腻了，我们还要彼此讲个不停，讲这位本地英雄，他率队冲锋，身受致命的创伤。我们顶喜欢同一位温存的老太太说话，她住在村北一英里一座白色农舍里。她是村里那位英雄汤米（Tommy）的母亲，常给我们讲她在1862年春天漫长的煎熬：她一天天等着医院交还她的儿子，每天早晨晾晒白色的家织床单，把那间小卧室拾掇得一尘不染，随时预备着。

汤米是在唐纳尔森堡（Fort Donelson）之战后负的伤，被送进了斯普林菲尔德的医院；他父亲赶去看他，眼见他一星期比一星期糟，眼见他要死了；可是衙门里官样文章太多，时局又乱，退伍手续就是办不下来。最后，医院的外科医生向他父亲暗示：不如悄悄把他带走，病成那样的人，没人会追究；可他们把这话说给汤米听时，他虚弱成那样，眼睛却亮了，说：「不行，长官。我要从正门走出去，不然就死在这儿。」从那以后，医院里人人都为他奔走，两个星期，他光荣退役。他终于回家时，母亲的心都碎了——他那么憔悴，那么变了样。她常给我们讲他回来之后那些漫长的安静日子：窗子开着，好让那双垂死的眼睛越过果园的坡，望见坡外弟弟们割头茬干草的草地。她给我们讲那些日子，他在中学的老同学们——他素来公认的领袖——成群来探他，那间挤挤的小屋里说出的一句句滚烫的爱国话；三个月后，那所学校几乎空了，秋天开拔的新连队收下的鼓手，正是汤米的第三个弟弟——他只有十七岁，当正规军年纪太小。她还记得随后更黑暗的日子：那个漂亮的小鼓手关进了安德森维尔（Andersonville）监狱；于是她一点点学会了宽解自己——汤米好歹安然躺在家里安静的墓园里。

约翰·H·亚当斯（John H. Addams）

===Original===

However much we were given to talk of war heroes, we always fell silent as we approached an isolated farmhouse in which two old people lived alone. Five of their sons had enlisted in the Civil War, and only the youngest had returned alive in the spring of 1865. In the autumn of the same year, when he was hunting for wild ducks in a swamp on the rough little farm itself, he was accidently shot and killed, and the old people were left alone to struggle with the half-cleared land as best they might. When we were driven past this forlorn little farm our childish voices always dropped into speculative whisperings as to how the accident could have happened to this remaining son out of all the men in the world, to him who had escaped so many chances of death! Our young hearts swelled in first rebellion against that which Walter Pater calls “the inexplicable shortcoming or misadventure on the part of life itself”; we were overwhelmingly oppressed by that grief of things as they are, so much more mysterious and intolerable than those griefs which we think dimly to trace to man’s own wrongdoing.

It was well perhaps that life thus early gave me a hint of one of her most obstinate and insoluble riddles, for I have sorely needed the sense of universality thus imparted to that mysterious injustice, the burden of which we are all forced to bear and with which I have become only too familiar.

My childish admiration for Lincoln is closely associated with a visit made to the war eagle, Old Abe, who, as we children well knew, lived in the state capital of Wisconsin, only sixty-five miles north of our house, really no farther than an eagle could easily fly! He had been carried by the Eighth Wisconsin Regiment through the entire war, and now dwelt an honored pensioner in the state building itself.

===Chinese===

尽管我们说起战争英雄来滔滔不绝，一走近那座孤零零的农舍，我们总会安静下来，屋里只住着两位老人。他们的五个儿子都应征参加了南北战争，活着的只有最小的一个，1865年春天回来的。同年秋天，他就在自家那块粗陋的小农场的沼地里猎野鸭，被误枪打死，两位老人从此独自挣扎，同那片半开垦的地尽力周旋。每逢车马经过这荒凉的小农庄，我们孩子的声音总要低成猜疑的耳语：天下这么多人，这样的事怎么会偏偏落在仅存的儿子身上，落在从那么多死机会里逃出来的人身上！我们小小的心头第一次鼓起反叛，反叛沃尔特·佩特（Walter Pater）所说的「生命本身那无从解释的亏缺或厄运」；我们被「事物本相」的悲苦压得喘不过气——它比那些我们隐隐约约能追溯到人类自身罪过的悲苦，神秘得多，也难堪得多。

生活这样早便让我窥见她最顽固、最无解的一道谜题，也许倒是好事，因为从此我对那桩神秘的冤屈有了一种人人皆然之感——这副担子我们人人都被迫肩负，而我已对它熟悉得不能再熟。

我对林肯的童稚倾慕，同一次探访紧密相连——去看那只军鹰「老艾伯」（Old Abe）。我们孩子都知道，它住在威斯康星州的州府，就在我家以北六十五英里，其实不过是一只鹰轻易就能飞到的距离！整个战争期间，它由威斯康星第八团带着走，如今栖身州议会大厦里，是一位领津贴的荣誉居民。

===Original===

Many times, standing in the north end of our orchard, which was only twelve miles from that mysterious line which divided Illinois from Wisconsin, we anxiously scanned the deep sky, hoping to see Old Abe fly southward right over our apple trees, for it was clearly possible that he might at any moment escape from his keeper, who, although he had been a soldier and a sentinel, would have to sleep sometimes. We gazed with thrilled interest at one speck after another in the flawless sky, but although Old Abe never came to see us, a much more incredible thing happened, for we were at last taken to see him.

We started one golden summer’s day, two happy children in the family carriage, with my father and mother and an older sister to whom, because she was just home from boarding school, we confidently appealed whenever we needed information. We were driven northward hour after hour, past harvest fields in which the stubble glinted from bronze to gold and the heavy-headed grain rested luxuriously in rounded shocks, until we reached that beautiful region of hills and lakes which surrounds the capital city of Wisconsin.

But although Old Abe, sitting sedately upon his high perch, was sufficiently like an uplifted ensign to remind us of a Roman eagle, and although his veteran keeper, clad in an old army coat, was ready to answer all our questions and to tell us of the thirty-six battles and skirmishes which Old Abe had passed unscathed, the crowning moment of the impressive journey came to me later, illustrating once more that children are as quick to catch the meaning of a symbol as they are unaccountably slow to understand the real world about them.

===Chinese===

多少回，我们站在果园北头——那里离伊利诺伊与威斯康星之间那条神秘的界线只有十二英里——焦灼地扫视深邃的天空，盼望看见老艾伯径直飞过我们的苹果树往南来。因为事情明摆着：它随时可能从看守人手里逃走——那人虽当过兵、站过岗，总有睡觉的时候。我们屏着气盯住那万里无云的天上一个又一个黑点。老艾伯始终没来看我们，可一件更难置信的事发生了：我们终于被带去看它了。

一个金色的夏日，我们出发了：两个快活的孩子坐在自家马车里，跟着父亲、母亲，还有一位刚从寄宿学校回来的姐姐——凡有要打听的事，我们尽管放心找她。马车一小时又一小时地向北去，经过一片片收割过的田野，茬子从青铜色闪到金色，沉甸甸的谷穗雍容地卧在一垛垛圆堆里，直到我们抵达威斯康星州府四周那片湖山秀美的地带。

老艾伯端坐在高栖上，确乎像一面擎起的军旗，叫我们想起罗马的鹰徽；看管它的老兵穿着一件旧军大衣，有问必答，讲老艾伯毫发无损地经历过的三十六场战役和小规模交锋。然而这趟难忘旅程的顶点，我是后来才体会到的——这再一次说明，孩子捕捉一个象征的意义何等敏捷，理解身周的真实世界又何等莫明其妙地迟钝。

===Original===

The entire journey to the veteran war eagle had itself symbolized that search for the heroic and perfect which so persistently haunts the young; and as I stood under the great white dome of Old Abe’s stately home, for one brief moment the search was rewarded. I dimly caught a hint of what men have tried to say in their world-old effort to imprison a space in so divine a line that it shall hold only yearning devotion and high-hearted hopes. Certainly the utmost rim of my first dome was filled with the tumultuous impression of soldiers marching to death for freedom’s sake, of pioneers streaming westward to establish self-government in yet another sovereign state. Only the great dome of St. Peter’s itself has ever clutched my heart as did that modest curve which had sequestered from infinitude in a place small enough for my child’s mind, the courage and endurance which I could not comprehend so long as it was lost in “the void of unresponsible space” under the vaulting sky itself. But through all my vivid sensations there persisted the image of the eagle in the corridor below and Lincoln himself as an epitome of all that was great and good. I dimly caught the notion of the martyred President as the standard bearer to the conscience of his countrymen, as the eagle had been the ensign of courage to the soldiers of the Wisconsin regiment.

Thirty-five years later, as I stood on the hill campus of the University of Wisconsin with a commanding view of the capitol building a mile directly across the city, I saw again the dome which had so uplifted my childish spirit. The University, which was celebrating its fiftieth anniversary, had honored me with a doctor’s degree, and in the midst of the academic pomp and the rejoicing, the dome again appeared to me as a fitting symbol of the state’s aspiration even in its high mission of universal education.

===Chinese===

去看那只老兵军鹰的整趟旅程，本身就象征着年轻人心里挥之不去的对英雄与完美的追寻；而当我站在老艾伯那座庄严居所的巨大白色穹顶下时，有一刹那，这番追寻得了报偿。我朦朦胧胧捉到了一点人们想说的东西——他们世世代代努力把一方空间囚进一道如此神圣的弧线里，让它只盛仰慕的挚诚和昂扬的希望。当然，我这第一座穹顶的最外缘，充盈着纷乱的印象：士兵为自由向死亡进军，拓荒者川流西去，要在又一个主权州建立自治。此后只有圣彼得大教堂（St. Peter's）的大穹顶本身，才那样攫住过我的心；而当年抓住我的是那道朴素的弧线——它从无穷之中划出一方小得容得下我孩子心灵的天地，把勇气和坚忍圈在里面；这些东西散落在穹苍之下「不负责任的虚空」里时，我原是领悟不了的。但在我全部鲜活的感受中，始终萦绕着楼下走廊里那只鹰的形象，和林肯本人的形象——一切伟大与善良的缩影。我朦朦胧胧领会到：这位殉难的总统，是他同胞良知的旗手，正如那只鹰是威斯康星团士兵们勇气的军旗。

三十五年后，我站在威斯康星大学的山间校园里，居高望远，正对着城里一英里开外的州议会大厦，又看见了当年那样鼓舞过我童心的穹顶。大学正在庆祝五十周年校庆，授了我一个博士学位。在学袍的盛仪和欢庆之中，那座穹顶再次显现为我眼中恰切的象征——象征这个州的抱负，哪怕在普及教育这项崇高使命里。

===Original===

Thousands of children in the sixties and seventies, in the simplicity which is given to the understanding of a child, caught a notion of imperishable heroism when they were told that brave men had lost their lives that the slaves might be free. At any moment the conversation of our elders might turn upon these heroic events; there were red-letter days, when a certain general came to see my father, and again when Governor Oglesby, whom all Illinois children called “Uncle Dick,” spent a Sunday under the pine trees in our front yard. We felt on those days a connection with the great world so much more heroic than the village world which surrounded us through all the other days. My father was a member of the state senate for the sixteen years between 1854 and 1870, and even as a little child I was dimly conscious of the grave march of public affairs in his comings and goings at the state capital.

He was much too occupied to allow time for reminiscence, but I remember overhearing a conversation between a visitor and himself concerning the stirring days before the war, when it was by no means certain that the Union men in the legislature would always have enough votes to keep Illinois from seceding. I heard with breathless interest my father’s account of the trip a majority of the legislators had made one dark day to St. Louis, that there might not be enough men for a quorum, and so no vote could be taken on the momentous question until the Union men could rally their forces.

===Chinese===

六七十年代成千上万的孩子，凭着一副孩子式简单的领悟，一听说勇敢的人们为使奴隶获得自由而献出了生命，便抓住了一种不朽英雄主义的观念。长辈的谈话随时可能转到这些英雄事迹上；还有些红字喜庆的日子：某位将军来看望父亲啦，或者奥格尔比州长（Governor Oglesby）——伊利诺伊的孩子都叫他「迪克伯伯」——在我们前院的松树下过一个星期日啦。那样的日子，我们觉得自己同那个伟大的世界有了联系，它比平日包围着我们的村庄世界，英雄得多。父亲在1854到1870这十六年间任州参议员，我还是个小孩子时，就朦胧地意识到他在州府来来去去中公务的庄重步伐。

他实在忙，抽不出工夫怀旧；可我记得偷听过一位客人和他谈战前那些激动人心的日子——那时议会里的联邦派议员能否始终握有足够的票数、保住伊利诺伊不脱离联邦，全无把握。我屏息听父亲讲，某个阴暗的日子，议会里多数议员如何跑到圣路易斯去了，弄得凑不足法定人数，那桩重大问题便无从表决，直等到联邦派重新纠集起人马来。

===Original===

My father always spoke of the martyred President as Mr. Lincoln, and I never heard the great name without a thrill. I remember the day﻿—it must have been one of comparative leisure, perhaps a Sunday﻿—when at my request my father took out of his desk a thin packet marked “Mr. Lincoln’s Letters,” the shortest one of which bore unmistakable traces of that remarkable personality. These letters began, “My dear Double-D’ed Addams,” and to the inquiry as to how the person thus addressed was about to vote on a certain measure then before the legislature, was added the assurance that he knew that this Addams “would vote according to his conscience,” but he begged to know in which direction the same conscience “was pointing.” As my father folded up the bits of paper I fairly held my breath in my desire that he should go on with the reminiscence of this wonderful man, whom he had known in his comparative obscurity, or better still, that he should be moved to tell some of the exciting incidents of the Lincoln-Douglas debates. There were at least two pictures of Lincoln that always hung in my father’s room, and one in our old-fashioned upstairs parlor, of Lincoln with little Tad. For one or all of these reasons I always tend to associate Lincoln with the tenderest thoughts of my father.

I recall a time of great perplexity in the summer of 1894, when Chicago was filled with federal troops sent there by the President of the United States, and their presence was resented by the governor of the state, that I walked the wearisome way from Hull House to Lincoln Park﻿—for no cars were running regularly at that moment of sympathetic strikes﻿—in order to look at and gain magnanimous counsel, if I might, from the marvelous St. Gaudens statue which had been but recently been placed at the entrance of the park. Some of Lincoln’s immortal words were cut into the stone at his feet, and never did a distracted town more sorely need the healing of “with charity towards all” than did Chicago at that moment, and the tolerance of the man who had won charity for those on both sides of “an irrepressible conflict.”

===Chinese===

父亲说起那位殉难的总统，从来只称林肯先生；这伟大的名字，我每听一次，心头便激动一次。我记得有一天——想必是个清闲日子，也许是星期日——应我的请求，父亲从书桌里取出一个薄薄的纸包，上面标着「林肯先生来信」；其中最短的一封，也明明白白带着那个非凡人格的印记。这些信开头写着「我亲爱的双D亚当斯」，在探问收信人对当时议会里某项议案打算如何投票之后，还添上一句放心话：他知道这位亚当斯「一定凭良心投票」，只是恳请告知，那同一个良心「此刻指向哪边」。父亲把那几张纸折起来时，我简直屏住了气，巴望他继续讲这位奇妙的人——父亲认识他时，他还相当默默无闻——或者更好，讲讲林肯—道格拉斯辩论（Lincoln-Douglas debates）里那些叫人屏息的场面。父亲的房里始终挂着至少两幅林肯像，我们楼上老式客厅里还有一幅，是林肯和小塔德（Tad）。由于这一桩或所有这些缘故，我总倾向于把林肯同对父亲最温柔的怀念连在一起。

我记得1894年夏天一段极度惶惑的日子：美国总统派联邦军队进驻芝加哥，州长又对驻军心存愤懑。我从赫尔馆一路步行，疲惫不堪地走到林肯公园——同情罢工正酣，电车全都时开时停——只为去看那尊新近安放在公园入口的圣–高登斯（St. Gaudens）杰作，倘若可能，向它讨一点宽厚的忠告。像座脚下的石头上，刻着林肯几句不朽的话。一座六神无主的城市，从来没有比此刻的芝加哥更迫切地需要「对一切人怀慈善」的抚慰，需要那位为「一场无可遏抑的冲突」双方赢得过慈善的人的宽容。

===Original===

Of the many things written of my father in that sad August in 1881, when he died, the one I cared for most was written by an old political friend of his who was then editor of a great Chicago daily. He wrote that while there were doubtless many members of the Illinois legislature who during the great contracts of the war time and the demoralizing reconstruction days that followed, had never accepted a bribe, he wished to bear testimony that he personally had known but this one man who had never been offered a bribe because bad men were instinctively afraid of him.

I feel now the hot chagrin with which I recalled this statement during those early efforts of Illinois in which Hull House joined, to secure the passage of the first factory legislation. I was told by the representatives of an informal association of manufacturers that if the residents of Hull House would drop this nonsense about a sweatshop bill, of which they knew nothing, certain business men would agree to give fifty thousand dollars within two years to be used for any of the philanthropic activities of the Settlement. As the fact broke upon me that I was being offered a bribe, the shame was enormously increased by the memory of this statement. What had befallen the daughter of my father that such a thing could happen to her? The salutary reflection that it could not have occurred unless a weakness in myself had permitted it, withheld me at least from an historic display of indignation before the two men making the offer, and I explained as gently as I could that we had no ambition to make Hull House “the largest institution on the West Side,” but that we were much concerned that our neighbors should be protected from untoward conditions of work, and﻿—so much heroics, youth must permit itself﻿—if to accomplish this the destruction of Hull House was necessary, that we would cheerfully sing a Te Deum on its ruins. The good friend who had invited me to lunch at the Union League Club to meet two of his friends who wanted to talk over the sweat shop bill here kindly intervened, and we all hastened to cover the awkward situation by that scurrying away from ugly morality which seems to be an obligation of social intercourse.

===Chinese===

1881年8月他去世那个悲伤的月份，报章写了许多关于父亲的话；我最珍视的一篇，出自他的一位老政友之手，那人当时是芝加哥一家大日报的主笔。他写道：在战争年代的大宗承包和其后败坏人心的重建时期，伊利诺伊议会里从未收受贿赂的议员无疑大有人在；可他愿作证——他生平认识的人里，唯独这一位，连贿赂都从没有人来送过，因为坏人本能地怕他。

如今我还能感到当年那种火辣辣的羞愤：伊利诺伊州为通过第一批工厂立法而奔走的初期，赫尔馆也参与其事，就在那时，我想起了上面那段话。一个非正式的制造商协会的代表对我说，倘若赫尔馆的同人肯撒手别管什么血汗工厂法案——这种事她们懂什么——某些商人愿意答应在两年之内拿出五万美元，供定居所（Settlement）任何慈善事业之用。当我明白过来自己是在被人行贿，那份耻辱因记起上面那段话而加倍沉重。父亲的女儿遭了什么孽，这样的事竟落到她头上？幸而有一个有益的反省拦住了我：这事若不是我自己身上有什么软弱开了门，原不会发生——它至少抑住了我，没让我在两位来使面前演出一幕载入史册的义愤。我尽量温和地解释说，我们并无雄心把赫尔馆办成「西区最大的机构」，我们深切关心的只是让我们的街坊得到保护、免于恶劣的劳动条件；而且——年轻人总得容许自己说几句豪言——倘为做到这一点非得毁掉赫尔馆，我们甘愿在它的废墟上高唱一首《感恩赞》（Te Deum）。那位好心朋友本来约我去联邦联盟俱乐部（Union League Club）午饭、见两位想谈谈血汗工厂法案的朋友——此时殷勤地打了圆场，于是大家赶紧从难看的道德面前仓皇溜开，这似乎是社交往来的一项义务。

===Original===

Of the many old friends of my father who kindly came to look up his daughter in the first days of Hull House, I recall none with more pleasure than Lyman Trumbull, whom we used to point out to members of the Young Citizen’s Club as the man who had for days held in his keeping the Proclamation of Emancipation until his friend President Lincoln was ready to issue it. I remember the talk he gave at Hull House on one of our early celebrations of Lincoln’s birthday, his assertion that Lincoln was no cheap popular hero, that the “common people” would have to make an effort if they would understand his greatness, as Lincoln painstakingly made a long effort to understand the greatness of the people. There was something in the admiration of Lincoln’s contemporaries, or at least of those men who had known him personally, which was quite unlike even the best of the devotion and reverent understanding which has developed since. In the first place, they had so large a fund of common experience; they too had pioneered in a western country, and had urged the development of canals and railroads in order that the raw prairie crops might be transported to market; they too had realized that if this last tremendous experiment in self-government failed here, it would be the disappointment of the centuries and that upon their ability to organize self-government in state, county, and town depended the verdict of history. These men also knew, as Lincoln himself did, that if this tremendous experiment was to come to fruition, it must be brought about by the people themselves; that there was no other capital fund upon which to draw. I remember an incident occurring when I was about fifteen years old, in which the conviction was driven into my mind that the people themselves were the great resource of the country. My father had made a little address of reminiscence at a meeting of “the old settlers of Stephenson County,” which was held every summer in the grove beside the mill, relating his experiences in inducing the farmers of the county to subscribe for stock in the Northwestern Railroad, which was the first to penetrate the county and make a connection with the Great Lakes at Chicago.

===Chinese===

赫尔馆创办之初，父亲的许多老友都热心来看望他的女儿；回忆起来，最令我愉快的莫过于莱曼·特朗布尔（Lyman Trumbull）。我们常把他指给「青年公民俱乐部」的会员们看，说这位就是当年把《解放宣言》在自己手里保藏了许多天、直到他的朋友林肯总统准备颁布的那个人。我记得他在赫尔馆一次早期林肯诞辰纪念会上的讲话：他断言林肯绝不是廉价的通俗英雄，「平民百姓」要想理解他的伟大，得下一番功夫，正像林肯曾下苦功、花长时间去理解人民的伟大。

林肯同时代人——至少那些亲眼见过他的人——对他的崇敬里，有某种东西，同后世发展出来的最好的挚爱与虔敬的理解也大不一样。首先，他们有一大笔共同经历的财富：他们也都在西部拓过荒，也为开运河、修铁路奔走过，好让荒原上的庄稼运得出去上市；他们也明白，这场空前的自治大实验倘若在此地失败，将是几个世纪的失望，而历史将怎样裁决，全系于他们组织州、县、镇自治的能力。这些人也同林肯本人一样明白：这场大实验要结出果实，必须由人民自己来完成；此外再没有别的资本可动用。

我记得十五岁上下发生的一件事，从那件事起，「人民自身就是国家最大的资源」这一信念被砸进了我的头脑。史蒂文森县（Stephenson County）的「老拓荒者」每年夏天在磨坊旁的树林里聚会，父亲在会上做了一段小小的怀旧发言，讲他当年怎样劝县里的农夫认购西北铁路（Northwestern Railroad）的股票——那是第一条通进县境、在芝加哥接上五大湖的铁路。

===Original===

Many of the Pennsylvania German farmers doubted the value of “the whole newfangled business,” and had no use for any railroad, much less for one in which they were asked to risk their hard-earned savings. My father told of his despair in one farmers’ community dominated by such prejudice which did not in the least give way under his argument, but finally melted under the enthusiasm of a high-spirited German matron who took a share to be paid for “out of butter and egg money.” As he related his admiration of her, an old woman’s piping voice in the audience called out: “I’m here today, Mr. Addams, and I’d do it again if you asked me.” The old woman, bent and broken by her seventy years of toilsome life, was brought to the platform and I was much impressed by my father’s grave presentation of her as “one of the public-spirited pioneers to whose heroic fortitude we are indebted for the development of this country.” I remember that I was at that time reading with great enthusiasm Carlyle’s *Heroes and Hero Worship*, but on the evening of “Old Settlers’ Day,” to my surprise, I found it difficult to go on. Its sonorous sentences and exaltation of the man who “can” suddenly ceased to be convincing. I had already written down in my commonplace book a resolution to give at least twenty-five copies of this book each year to noble young people of my acquaintance. It is perhaps fitting in this chapter that the very first Christmas we spent at Hull House, in spite of exigent demands upon my slender purse for candy and shoes, I gave to a club of boys twenty-five copies of the then-new Carl Schurz’s “Appreciation of Abraham Lincoln.”

===Chinese===

许多宾夕法尼亚德裔农夫疑心「这整套新玩意儿」的价值，什么铁路都不要，更别说要他们拿血汗积蓄去冒风险的这条了。父亲讲到他在一个被这种成见笼罩的农夫聚落里的绝望：道理讲尽，成见分毫不为所动，末了却消融在一位心气高昂的德国主妇的热情里——她认购了一份，说钱「从黄油和鸡蛋钱里出」。父亲正讲着对她的钦佩，听众里一个老妇人尖细的嗓音喊起来：「亚当斯先生，我今天就在这儿呢！你如今再开口，我还认一份。」这位老妇人，七十年劳苦的日子把她压弯了、压垮了，被搀上台来。父亲郑重地把她介绍给大家，称她为「我们所以有今日的开拓，全靠他们英勇坚韧的公众精神的拓荒者之一」——这给我极深的印象。

我记得那时我正狂热地读卡莱尔的《英雄与英雄崇拜》（*Heroes and Hero Worship*）；可「老拓荒者日」的那个晚上，我竟惊讶地发现，这本书读不下去了。它铿锵的句子，它对那「能」人的颂扬，忽然都失了说服力。我已经在摘抄本里立下决心：每年至少送二十五册这本书给我认识的高尚青年。也许正该在这一章里记下：我们在赫尔馆过的头一个圣诞节，尽管买糖果和鞋子的开销苦苦逼着我那只瘪钱包，我还是送给一个少年俱乐部二十五册卡尔·舒尔茨（Carl Schurz）新出的《林肯礼赞》（Appreciation of Abraham Lincoln）。

===Original===

In our early effort at Hull House to hand on to our neighbors whatever of help we had found for ourselves, we made much of Lincoln. We were often distressed by the children of immigrant parents who were ashamed of the pit whence they were digged, who repudiated the language and customs of their elders, and counted themselves successful as they were able to ignore the past. Whenever I held up Lincoln for their admiration as the greatest American, I invariably pointed out his marvelous power to retain and utilize past experiences; that he never forgot how the plain people in Sangamon County thought and felt when he himself had moved to town; that this habit was the foundation for his marvelous capacity for growth; that during those distracting years in Washington it enabled him to make clear beyond denial to the American people themselves, the goal towards which they were moving. I was sometimes bold enough to add that proficiency in the art of recognition and comprehension did not come without effort, and that certainly its attainment was necessary for any successful career in our conglomerate America.

===Chinese===

赫尔馆早期，我们努力把自己寻得的助力转赠给街坊，其中很看重林肯。移民父母的儿女往往以自己的出身之地为耻，唾弃长辈的语言和习俗，越能把过去忘个干净，越自以为有出息——这常常让我们痛心。每当我举出林肯、请他们钦佩这位最伟大的美国人时，我必定指出他那保存旧日经验、加以运用的惊人本领：他进城之后，从不忘掉桑加蒙县（Sangamon County）的平民当初怎样想、怎样感受；正是这个习惯，奠定了他惊人的成长能力；华盛顿那些纷乱的年头里，也正是它，使他能向美国人民自己无可辩驳地讲明他们正走向的目标。有时我还敢添上一句：这种体察与理解的本领，不花功夫是到不了家的；而在这五方杂处的美国，要谋任何成功的事业，这份本领断不可少。

===Original===

An instance of the invigorating and clarifying power of Lincoln’s influence came to me many years ago in England. I had spent two days in Oxford under the guidance of Arnold Toynbee’s old friend Sidney Ball of St. John’s College, who was closely associated with the group of scholars we all identify with the beginnings of the Settlement movement. It was easy to claim the philosophy of Thomas Hill Green, the road-building episode of Ruskin, the experimental living in the east end by Frederick Maurice, the London Workingman’s College of Edward Dennison, as foundations laid by university men for the establishment of Toynbee Hall. I was naturally much interested in the beginnings of the movement whose slogan was “Back to the People,” and which could doubtless claim the Settlement as one of its manifestations. Nevertheless the processes by which so simple a conclusion as residence among the poor in East London was reached, seemed to me very involved and roundabout. However inevitable these processes might be for class-conscious Englishmen, they could not but seem artificial to a western American who had been born in a rural community where the early pioneer life had made social distinctions impossible. Always on the alert lest American Settlements should become mere echoes and imitations of the English movement, I found myself assenting to what was shown me only with that part of my consciousness which had been formed by reading of English social movements, while at the same time the rustic American looked on in detached comment.

===Chinese===

林肯影响的振奋与澄澈之力，多年前我在英国亲身领教过一例。我在牛津盘桓了两日，由阿诺德·汤因比（Arnold Toynbee）的故友、圣约翰学院的西德尼·鲍尔（Sidney Ball）做向导——他同那一群学者过从甚密，而世人正把定居所运动的开端归在他们名下。托马斯·希尔·格林（Thomas Hill Green）的哲学、罗斯金（Ruskin）修路的那段故事、弗雷德里克·莫里斯（Frederick Maurice）在东区的试验性同居共处、爱德华·丹尼森（Edward Dennison）的伦敦工人学院——把这些说成大学人者为建立汤因比馆（Toynbee Hall）打下的基业，是很容易的。那场以「回到人民中间去」为口号的运动，无疑可以认定居所为其表现之一，我对它的发端自然深感兴趣。然而，要得出「住到伦敦东区的穷人中间去」这样一个简单的结论，所经历的推演在我看来却极其迂曲缭绕。这些过程对有阶级意识的英国人也许在所难免，对一个生在美国西部乡下的人却不能不做作——在我的出生地，早期拓荒的生活早使社会分野无从谈起。我一向警惕美国各家定居所沦为英国运动的回声与仿制品；于是我发现，自己应允眼前所见时，用的只是意识中由阅读英国社会运动养成的那个部分，而那个乡下美国人同时在一旁冷眼旁观，超然地评头论足。

===Original===

Why should an American be lost in admiration of a group of Oxford students because they went out to mend a disused road, inspired thereto by Ruskin’s teaching for the bettering of the common life, when all the country roads in America were mended each spring by self-respecting citizens, who were thus carrying out the simple method devised by a democratic government for providing highways. No humor penetrated my high mood even as I somewhat uneasily recalled certain spring thaws when I had been mired in roads provided by the American citizen. I continued to fumble for a synthesis which I was unable to make until I developed that uncomfortable sense of playing two roles at once. It was therefore almost with a dual consciousness that I was ushered, during the last afternoon of my Oxford stay, into the drawing-room of the Master of Balliol. Edward Caird’s *Evolution of Religion*, which I had read but a year or two before, had been of unspeakable comfort to me in the labyrinth of differing ethical teachings and religious creeds which the many immigrant colonies of our neighborhood presented. I remember that I wanted very much to ask the author himself how far it was reasonable to expect the same quality of virtue and a similar standard of conduct from these diverse people. I was timidly trying to apply his method of study to those groups of homesick immigrants huddled together in strange tenement houses, among whom I seemed to detect the beginnings of a secular religion or at least of a wide humanitarianism evolved out of the various exigencies of the situation; somewhat as a household of children, whose mother is dead, out of their sudden necessity perform unaccustomed offices for each other and awkwardly exchange consolations, as children in happier households never dream of doing. Perhaps Mr. Caird could tell me whether there was any religious content in this

“Faith to each other; this fidelity

Of fellow wanderers in a desert place.”

But when tea was over and my opportunity came for a talk with my host, I suddenly remembered, to the exclusion of all other associations, only Mr. Caird’s fine analysis of Abraham Lincoln, delivered in a lecture two years before.

===Chinese===

一群牛津学生受罗斯金「改善共同生活」的教导感召，出城去修一条废弃的路——一个美国人何必为此五体投地？美国的乡间道路，年年春天都由自尊的公民修整，他们不过是在执行民主政府为兴办公路而设计的那套简单办法。这层幽默透不进我高昂的情绪，虽然我也有些不安地记起几个春融时节，自己陷在公民修的路上的泥泞里。我继续摸索一种综合，却总也综合不成，直到生出那种一人分饰两角的不自在感来。于是，在牛津停留的最后一个下午，被人引进贝利奥尔学院（Balliol）院长客厅的我，几乎带着双重意识。

爱德华·凯尔德（Edward Caird）的《宗教的演进》（*Evolution of Religion*），我一两年前才读过；在邻近众多移民聚落呈现出的伦理教条与宗教信条的重重迷宫里，它给过我难以言喻的安慰。我记得我很想问问作者本人：期望这些形形色色的人们有同等品质的德性、相近的行为标准，究竟合理到什么程度。我怯生生地想把他那套研究方法用于一群群挤在陌生出租屋里想家的移民；在这些人中间，我仿佛觉察出一种世俗宗教、至少是一种广被的人道主义的萌芽，它是从处境的种种急难里生长出来的；有点像一屋死了母亲的孩子，出于突然的必要，彼此做起素不惯做的服侍，笨拙地互相劝慰——幸福人家的孩子做梦也想不到这些。也许凯尔德先生能告诉我，这里面有没有宗教的内核——

「彼此相信；这荒漠之地

同路人之间的忠贞。」

可是茶罢，轮到我同主人攀谈时，其他种种联想霎时全部退尽，我只想起凯尔德先生两年前一次讲演中对亚伯拉罕·林肯的精彩剖析。

===Original===

The memory of Lincoln, the mention of his name, came like a refreshing breeze from off the prairie, blowing aside all the scholarly implications in which I had become so reluctantly involved, and as the philosopher spoke of the great American “who was content merely to dig the channels through which the moral life of his countrymen might flow,” I was gradually able to make a natural connection between this intellectual penetration at Oxford and the moral perception which is always necessary for the discovery of new methods by which to minister to human needs. In the unceasing ebb and flow of justice and oppression we must all dig channels as best we may, that at the propitious moment somewhat of the swelling tide may be conducted to the barren places of life.

Gradually a healing sense of well-being enveloped me and a quick remorse for my blindness, as I realized that no one among his own countrymen had been able to interpret Lincoln’s greatness more nobly than this Oxford scholar had done, and that vision and wisdom as well as high motives must lie behind every effective stroke in the continuous labor for human equality; I remembered that another Master of Balliol, Jowett himself, had said that it was fortunate for society that every age possessed at least a few minds, which, like Arnold Toynbee’s, were “perpetually disturbed over the apparent inequalities of mankind.” Certainly both the English and American settlements could unite in confessing to that disturbance of mind.

Traces of this Oxford visit are curiously reflected in a paper I wrote soon after my return at the request of the American Academy of Political and Social Science. It begins as follows:﻿—

“The word ‘settlement,’ which we have borrowed from London, is apt to grate a little upon American ears. It is not, after all, so long ago that Americans who settled were those who had adventured into a new country, where they were pioneers in the midst of difficult surroundings. The word still implies migrating from one condition of life to another totally unlike it, and against this implication the resident of an American settlement takes alarm.

===Chinese===

对林肯的记忆，对他名字的一提，像草原上一阵清爽的风，把我勉力缠身的那一切学究气吹到一旁。听这位哲学家讲那位伟大的美国人——「他甘愿做的，不过是开凿沟渠，让他同胞的道德生活得以流淌」——我渐渐能把牛津这边智识上的洞见，同那种发现济人之急的新方法所永远必需的道德直觉，自然地接在一起。在正义与压迫不息的潮起潮落里，我们都须尽力开凿沟渠，好待时机来到，把上涨的潮水引些到生命的荒芜之处。

一种疗愈的安宁渐渐把我裹住，同时我为自己的盲目生出急切的懊悔：原来把林肯的伟大阐发得如此高贵的，他的同胞中竟无一人赶得上这位牛津学者；原来在争取人类平等的持续劳作里，每一记有效的出手，背后都必须有见识与智慧，同高尚的动机并存。我又想起贝利奥尔的另一位院长乔伊特（Jowett）本人说过：每个时代至少总有几个头脑，「为人类表面的不平等而永不安宁」，一如阿诺德·汤因比，这是社会之幸。英美的各家定居所，确实可以齐声承认自己就有这种心绪不宁。

这次牛津之行的痕迹，古怪地映在一篇论文里——回国后不久，我应美国政治与社会科学学会（American Academy of Political and Social Science）之请写的。文章开头是这样：——

「『定居所』（settlement）一词，我们借自伦敦，在美国人耳朵里总有些硌耳。毕竟为时未远：那时『定居下来的』美国人，是闯进一片新国土的人，在艰难的环境中做拓荒者。这个词至今仍意味着从一种生活境况迁入另一种全然不同的境况；对这层含义，美国定居所的住驻者是存有戒心的。

===Original===

We do not like to acknowledge that Americans are divided into two nations, as her prime minister once admitted of England. We are not willing, openly and professedly, to assume that American citizens are broken up into classes, even if we make that assumption the preface to a plea that the superior class has duties to the inferior. Our democracy is still our most precious possession, and we do well to resent any inroads upon it, even though they may be made in the name of philanthropy.”

Is it not Abraham Lincoln who has cleared the title to our democracy? He made plain, once for all, that democratic government, associated as it is with all the mistakes and shortcomings of the common people, still remains the most valuable contribution America has made to the moral life of the world.

===Chinese===

我们不愿承认美国人分成了两个国度，尽管英国的首相一度这样承认过英国。我们不肯公开地、明言地认定美国公民裂成了阶级，哪怕拿这个认定去做一篇请愿的序言，说上层阶级对下层负有义务。民主仍旧是我们最珍贵的产业，我们对任何侵犯它的行径报以愤慨，都是对的——哪怕那侵犯是打着慈善的旗号。」

为我们民主的地契清除了权利瑕疵的，不正是亚伯拉罕·林肯吗？他一劳永逸地讲明：民主政府纵然同平民百姓的一切错误和缺陷联系在一起，仍是美国对世界道德生活最宝贵的贡献。

## III / 三
===Original===

Boarding-School Ideals

As my three older sisters had already attended the seminary at Rockford, of which my father was trustee, without any question I entered there at seventeen, with such meager preparation in Latin and algebra as the village school had afforded. I was very ambitious to go to Smith College, although I well knew that my father’s theory in regard to the education of his daughters implied a school as near at home as possible, to be followed by travel abroad in lieu of the wider advantages which the eastern college is supposed to afford. I was much impressed by the recent return of my sister from a year in Europe, yet I was greatly disappointed at the moment of starting to humdrum Rockford. After the first weeks of homesickness were over, however, I became very much absorbed in the little world which the boarding school in any form always offers to its students.

The school at Rockford in 1877 had not changed its name from seminary to college, although it numbered, on its faculty and among its alumnae, college women who were most eager that this should be done, and who really accomplished it during the next five years. The school was one of the earliest efforts for women’s higher education in the Mississippi Valley, and from the beginning was called “The Mount Holyoke of the West.”

It reflected much of the missionary spirit of that pioneer institution, and the proportion of missionaries among its early graduates was almost as large as Mount Holyoke’s own. In addition there had been thrown about the founders of the early western school the glamour of frontier privations, and the first students, conscious of the heroic self-sacrifice made in their behalf, felt that each minute of the time thus dearly bought must be conscientiously used. This inevitably fostered an atmosphere of intensity, a fever of preparation which continued long after the direct making of it had ceased, and which the later girls accepted, as they did the campus and the buildings, without knowing that it could have been otherwise.

===Chinese===

寄宿学校的理想

我的三个姐姐都已上过罗克福德的女子中学部——父亲是那里的校董——因此十七岁那年，我毫无异议地也进了这所学校，拉丁文和代数的底子，只是乡村学堂所能给的那一点点。我一心想上史密斯学院，虽然我心里很清楚，父亲关于女儿教育的理论意味着：学校离家越近越好，之后再出国游历，以替代东部女子学院据说能提供的那些更宽广的好处。姐姐刚从欧洲游学一年归来，给我印象极深；可当真要动身去平平无奇的罗克福德时，我还是大失所望。不过，最初几个礼拜的想家过去之后，我便深深迷上了寄宿学校以任何形式提供给学生的那个小天地。

1877 年的罗克福德学校还没有把「中学部」（seminary）的名称改成「学院」（college），尽管它的教师和校友里就有最热切盼望此举的学院女子——这件事也真的在此后五年之内办成了。这所学校是密西西比河谷妇女高等教育最早的尝试之一，从一开始就有「西部芒特霍利约克」之称。

它带着那所拓荒先行者院校浓厚的传教精神，早期毕业生中传教士的比例，几乎同芒特霍利约克本校一样高。此外，西部早期学校的创办人身上又披着一层边疆艰辛的光环，头几批学生知道自己享受到的是别人舍身的代价，便觉得这来之不易的每一分钟都必须对得起良心。这就不可避免地养出一种绷紧的气氛、一种奋发的热病——在直接制造它的年代过去很久之后仍在延续，后来的姑娘们接受它，就同接受校园和校舍一样，从不知道本可以不是这样。

===Original===

There was, moreover, always present in the school a larger or smaller group of girls who consciously accepted this heritage and persistently endeavored to fulfill its obligation. We worked in those early years as if we really believed the portentous statement from Aristotle which we found quoted in Boswell’s Johnson and with which we illuminated the wall of the room occupied by our Chess Club; it remained there for months, solely out of reverence, let us hope, for the two ponderous names associated with it; at least I have enough confidence in human nature to assert that we never really believed that “There is the same difference between the learned and the unlearned as there is between the living and the dead.” We were also too fond of quoting Carlyle to the effect, “ ’Tis not to taste sweet things, but to do noble and true things that the poorest son of Adam dimly longs.”

As I attempt to reconstruct the spirit of my contemporary group by looking over many documents, I find nothing more amusing than a plaint registered against life’s indistinctness, which I imagine more or less reflected the sentiments of all of us. At any rate here it is for the entertainment of the reader if not for his edification: “So much of our time is spent in preparation, so much in routine, and so much in sleep, we find it difficult to have any experience at all.” We did not, however, tamely accept such a state of affairs, for we made various and restless attempts to break through this dull obtuseness.

At one time five of us tried to understand De Quincey’s marvelous “Dreams” more sympathetically, by drugging ourselves with opium. We solemnly consumed small white powders at intervals during an entire long holiday, but no mental reorientation took place, and the suspense and excitement did not even permit us to grow sleepy. About four o’clock on the weird afternoon, the young teacher whom we had been obliged to take into our confidence, grew alarmed over the whole performance, took away our De Quincey and all the remaining powders, administrated an emetic to each of the five aspirants for sympathetic understanding of all human experience, and sent us to our separate rooms with a stern command to appear at family worship after supper “whether we were able to or not.”

===Chinese===

而且，学校里始终有那么一群或大或小的姑娘，自觉领受这份遗产，锲而不舍地要履行它的义务。那些年我们用功之勤，仿佛真信了我们从鲍斯威尔的《约翰逊传》里摘出来、抄去装点国际象棋俱乐部那间屋墙上的亚里士多德那句大话。它在墙上挂了几个月，但愿纯粹是出于对连在一起的那两个沉甸甸的名字的敬畏；至少我对人性还有足够的信心，敢断言我们从不真信「学者与不学者之间的分别，一如生者与死者之间的分别」。我们也太爱引卡莱尔那句话：「亚当最穷的儿子所昏昏渴求的，也不是尝到甜美的东西，而是做出高贵而真实的事情。」

如今我要凭借许多故纸来重构当年那一群人的精神，发现最有趣味的，莫过于我们登记在案的一桩对人生晦暗不清的抱怨——我想它多少反映了我们所有人的心绪。无论如何，把它录在这里，纵然不能益人心智，也可博读者一粲：「我们的时间，这么多花在预备上，这么多花在例行事务上，又这么多花在睡眠上，我们简直难有任何经验可言。」不过我们并没有驯顺地接受这种局面，我们作了各种不安分的尝试，要冲破这层沉滞的钝。

有一回，我们五个人为了更体贴入微地领会德·昆西那奇妙的《梦》，竟拿鸦片来麻醉自己。整整一个漫长的假日里，我们隔一阵便严肃地吞下小小的一包白粉，然而心境并未重开新天，那种紧张亢奋甚至不容我们发困。那个古怪下午的四点钟光景，那位被我们不得不拉进秘密的年轻教师对整场把戏惊慌起来，没收了我们的德·昆西和剩下的全部药粉，给这五位渴望同情地理解人类一切经验的候补者每人灌了一剂催吐药，把我们赶回各自的房间，厉声命令晚饭后「不管行不行」都得出席家庭礼拜。

===Original===

Whenever we had a chance to write, we took, of course, large themes, usually from the Greek because they were the most stirring to the imagination. The Greek oration I gave at our Junior Exhibition was written with infinite pains and taken to the Greek professor in Beloit College that there might be no mistakes, even after the Rockford College teacher and the most scholarly clergyman in town had both passed upon it. The oration upon Bellerophon and his successful fight with the Chimera contended that social evils could only be overcome by him who soared above them into idealism, as Bellerophon mounted upon the winged horse Pegasus, had slain the earthy dragon.

There were practically no Economics taught in women’s colleges﻿—at least in the freshwater ones﻿—thirty years ago, although we painstakingly studied “Mental” and “Moral” Philosophy, which, though far from dry in the classroom, became the subject of more spirited discussion outside, and gave us a clue for animated rummaging in the little college library. Of course we read a great deal of Ruskin and Browning, and liked the most abstruse parts the best; but like the famous gentleman who talked prose without knowing it, we never dreamed of connecting them with our philosophy. My genuine interest was history, partly because of a superior teacher, and partly because my father had always insisted upon a certain amount of historic reading ever since he had paid me, as a little girl, five cents a “Life” for each Plutarch hero I could intelligently report to him and twenty-five cents for every volume of Irving’s “Life of Washington.”

===Chinese===

一有动笔的机会，我们当然都拣大题目，多半取自希腊，因为那最能搅动想象。我在低年级展览会上发表的希腊语演说，写得字斟句酌，还专门送到贝洛伊特学院的希腊文教授那里过目，为的是不出一个错——虽然罗克福德学院的老师和城里最有学问的牧师都已先后审定过。那篇关于柏勒洛丰及其战胜客迈拉的演说，论证的是：社会之恶，唯有飞升其上、进入理想主义的人才能克服，正如柏勒洛丰骑着生翼的神马珀伽索斯，斩杀了那条土里的巨龙。

三十年前的女子学院里几乎不教经济学——至少内陆的这些不教——虽然我们下苦功攻读「心理」哲学和「道德」哲学；这两门课在课堂上远不枯燥，到了课外更成了激烈争论的题目，引得我们在小小的学院图书馆里翻箱倒柜。当然，罗斯金和勃朗宁我们读了很多，而且最喜欢最深奥的部分；可是就像那位著名的说了半辈子散文自己却毫不知情的先生一样，我们做梦也没想过把它们同我们的哲学连在一起。我真正倾心的是历史，一半因为有一位出类拔萃的老师，一半因为父亲一贯要求我读一定分量的史书——早在我还是个小姑娘时，每向他条理清楚地复述一位普鲁塔克笔下的英雄，他就付我五美分一「传」；每读完一卷欧文的《华盛顿传》，就付二十五美分。

===Original===

When we started for the long vacations, a little group of five would vow that during the summer we would read all of Motley’s *Dutch Republic* or, more ambitious still, all of Gibbon’s *Decline and Fall of the Roman Empire*. When we returned at the opening of school and three of us announced we had finished the latter, each became skeptical of the other two.
