寻访安身的街区时，我们同义务教育科的官员们、同城市传教士们、同报馆记者们一起走街串巷。我记得那些记者比人们通常想到的这个行当的人要老成一辈；也或许，只是他们打发年长些的陪我出门，去干一件他们必定都视为堂吉诃德式的差事。冬末的一个星期日下午，一位记者带我去看一所所谓无政府主义者的主日学——这样的学校，城西北一带有好几处。主持的年轻人是德国学生那一类型，领着孩子们唱克尔纳（Koerner）的诗时，脸上泛着热情。那位不懂德文的记者问我，他们唱的是什么不得了的货色；可我把那几句朴素歌词译出来，他似乎并不满意，阴沉沉地暗示这些是「话里有话」，多半把我也「蒙」了。我答道，克尔纳是一位热忱的德国诗人，他的歌曾鼓舞同胞抵抗拿破仑的侵凌，他装订成册的诗集就摆在最体面的藏书室里；他闻言斜眼看我。就在那时那地，我头一次领教：把一个被人称作无政府主义者的芝加哥人，当寻常公民一样看待，就等于把自己摊开在深重的疑心之下。

初春的又一个星期日下午，我们乘一位创办人的马车去一所波希米亚传教站，路过一座漂亮的老公馆：它远远退离街道，三面围着宽阔的游廊，廊柱是木制的，科林斯式的形制与比例纯正得出奇。那房子叫我心动不已，第二天就动身去访；可任凭当时寻、之后连寻数日，都找不到它，最后只得万分不情愿地罢手。

===Original===

Three weeks later, with the advice of several of the oldest residents of Chicago, including the ex-mayor of the city, Colonel Mason, who had from the first been a warm friend to our plans, we decided upon a location somewhere near the junction of Blue Island Avenue, Halsted Street, and Harrison Street. I was surprised and overjoyed on the very first day of our search for quarters to come upon the hospitable old house, the quest for which I had so recently abandoned. The house was of course rented, the lower part of it used for offices and storerooms in connection with a factory that stood back of it. However, after some difficulties were overcome, it proved to be possible to sublet the second floor and what had been a large drawing-room on the first floor.

The house had passed through many changes since it had been built in 1856 for the homestead of one of Chicago’s pioneer citizens, Mr. Charles J. Hull, and although battered by its vicissitudes, was essentially sound. Before it had been occupied by the factory, it had sheltered a secondhand furniture store, and at one time the Little Sisters of the Poor had used it for a home for the aged. It had a half-skeptical reputation for a haunted attic, so far respected by the tenants living on the second floor that they always kept a large pitcher full of water on the attic stairs. Their explanation of this custom was so incoherent that I was sure it was a survival of the belief that a ghost could not cross running water, but perhaps that interpretation was only my eagerness for finding folklore.

===Chinese===

三个星期后，听了几位芝加哥最老住户的建议——包括本市前市长梅森上校（Colonel Mason），他从一开始就是我们计划的热心朋友——我们把地点定在蓝岛大道、霍尔斯特德街与哈里森街交汇处的左近。找房子的头一天，我竟同那座好客的老宅不期而遇——正是我不久前才放弃寻找的那座——又是惊又是喜。房子自然已经租了出去，下层用作办公楼和库房，同房后的一座工厂配套。不过，克服了几重困难之后，转租二楼连同一楼原是一间大客厅的屋子，还是办成了。

这座房子建于1856年，本是为芝加哥一位拓荒市民查尔斯·J·赫尔先生（Mr. Charles J. Hull）修的宅邸；此后历尽变迁，虽然饱经风霜，骨架却还完好。工厂进驻之前，它开过旧家具店，济贫小姊妹会（Little Sisters of the Poor）还一度拿它办过养老院。它还有个半真半假的名声：顶楼闹鬼。二楼房客对此倒也敬畏，总是在顶楼楼梯上摆一大罐满的水。他们对这习俗的解释颠三倒四，我便认定，这是「鬼不能渡活水」那条古老信仰的遗存——不过，这个解释，也许只是我急于采风的心作祟罢了。

===Original===

The fine old house responded kindly to repairs, its wide hall and open fireplace always ensuring it a gracious aspect. Its generous owner, Miss Helen Culver, in the following spring gave us a free leasehold of the entire house. Her kindness has continued through the years until the group of thirteen buildings, which at present comprises our equipment, is built largely upon land which Miss Culver has put at the service of the Settlement which bears Mr. Hull’s name. In those days the house stood between an undertaking establishment and a saloon. “Knight, Death and the Devil,” the three were called by a Chicago wit, and yet any mock heroics which might be implied by comparing the Settlement to a knight quickly dropped away under the genuine kindness and hearty welcome extended to us by the families living up and down the street.

We furnished the house as we would have furnished it were it in another part of the city, with the photographs and other impedimenta we had collected in Europe, and with a few bits of family mahogany. While all the new furniture which was bought was enduring in quality, we were careful to keep it in character with the fine old residence. Probably no young matron ever placed her own things in her own house with more pleasure than that with which we first furnished Hull House. We believed that the Settlement may logically bring to its aid all those adjuncts which the cultivated man regards as good and suggestive of the best of the life of the past.

On the 18th of September, 1889, Miss Starr and I moved into it, with Miss Mary Keyser, who began performing the housework, but who quickly developed into a very important factor in the life of the vicinity as well as that of the household, and whose death five years later was most sincerely mourned by hundreds of our neighbors.

In our enthusiasm over “settling,” the first night we forgot not only to lock but to close a side door opening on Polk Street, and we were much pleased in the morning to find that we possessed a fine illustration of the honesty and kindliness of our new neighbors.

===Chinese===

这座漂亮的老宅经得起修缮，一修便焕发容光；宽阔的门厅和敞开的壁炉，永远使它保有一副雍容的气象。它慷慨的主人海伦·卡尔弗小姐（Miss Helen Culver）第二年春天把整座宅子免费租给了我们。她的厚意延续多年，如今构成我们全部家当的那十三座建筑，大部分就立在卡尔弗小姐提供给这座以赫尔先生命名的定居所使用的地皮上。那些日子里，这宅子夹在一家殡仪馆和一家酒馆之间。一位芝加哥的打趣人管它们仨叫「骑士、死神与魔鬼」；可是，把定居所比作骑士而可能带出的那点滑稽英雄气，在满街住户向我们表达的真心善意和热诚欢迎面前，很快便消散了。

布置屋子，我们就像它坐落在城里任何别的地段那样布置：用我们在欧洲收集的照片和种种行头，加上家里几件红木旧物。新置的家具虽然都拣了结实的，我们也小心让它们配得上这座精致的老宅的风骨。只怕没有哪位年轻主妇往自己家里安置自己的东西，比我们头一回布置赫尔馆更欢喜。我们相信：凡是受过教养的人视为美好、视为往昔生活之精粹的暗示的种种附属之物，定居所都可以名正言顺地拿来为己所用。

1889年9月18日，斯塔尔小姐和我搬了进去，同来的还有玛丽·凯泽小姐（Miss Mary Keyser）。她起初操持家务，很快便成长为一根顶梁柱——无论对这个家，还是对这一带的生活；五年后她去世，几百位街坊为我们由衷地哀恸。

我们沉浸于「定居」的兴奋里，头一夜不但忘了上锁，连那扇开向波尔克街（Polk Street）的侧门都忘了关。早晨起来我们发现，这倒为我们新街坊的正直与善良，提供了一个绝好的例证，心中大慰。

===Original===

Our first guest was an interesting young woman who lived in a neighboring tenement, whose widowed mother aided her in the support of the family by scrubbing a downtown theater every night. The mother, of English birth, was well bred and carefully educated, but was in the midst of that bitter struggle which awaits so many strangers in American cities who find that their social position tends to be measured solely by the standards of living they are able to maintain. Our guest has long since married the struggling young lawyer to whom she was then engaged, and he is now leading his profession in an eastern city. She recalls that month’s experience always with a sense of amusement over the fact that the succession of visitors who came to see the new Settlement invariably questioned her most minutely concerning “these people” without once suspecting that they were talking to one who had been identified with the neighborhood from childhood. I at least was able to draw a lesson from the incident, and I never addressed a Chicago audience on the subject of the Settlement and its vicinity without inviting a neighbor to go with me, that I might curb any hasty generalization by the consciousness that I had an auditor who knew the conditions more intimately than I could hope to do.

Halsted Street has grown so familiar during twenty years of residence that it is difficult to recall its gradual changes﻿—the withdrawal of the more prosperous Irish and Germans, and the slow substitution of Russian Jews, Italians, and Greeks. A description of the street such as I gave in those early addresses still stands in my mind as sympathetic and correct.

===Chinese===

我们的第一位客人是个有意思的年轻女子，住在隔壁一栋出租屋里；她守寡的母亲为了帮着养家，每天夜里到市中心一家剧院擦洗地板。母亲生在英国，教养良好，受过完整的教育，却正陷在那场苦斗里——美国城市里多少异乡人都在等着这场苦斗：他们发现，自己的社会地位，渐渐只按他们撑得起的生活排场来衡量了。我们的这位客人，后来嫁给了当时与她订婚的那位挣扎中的年轻律师；他如今在东部一座城里执本业之牛耳。她回忆那一个月的经历时总带着好笑：络绎来访新定居所的客人们，无一例外地向她细细打听「这些人」如何如何，却一次也没有想到，同他们说话的这位，本来就是打小长在这一带的人。至少我从这件事里学到了一课：此后凡在芝加哥当众讲定居所和它的街区，我必请一位街坊同去；想到听众席上坐着一位对这里的一切比我可能知道的更知根知底的人，任何轻率的以偏概全便收了场。

住满二十年，霍尔斯特德街已熟得不能再熟，它一点点的变化反倒难忆起——较殷实的爱尔兰人和德国人陆续迁走，俄国犹太人、意大利人、希腊人慢慢补进来。我当年在那些早期讲演里对这条街的一段描述，如今在我心里仍是贴切而准确的。

===Original===

“Halsted Street is thirty-two miles long, and one of the great thoroughfares of Chicago; Polk Street crosses it midway between the stockyards to the south and the shipbuilding yards on the north branch of the Chicago River. For the six miles between these two industries the street is lined with shops of butchers and grocers, with dingy and gorgeous saloons, and pretentious establishments for the sale of ready-made clothing. Polk Street, running west from Halsted Street, grows rapidly more prosperous; running a mile east to State Street, it grows steadily worse, and crosses a network of vice on the corners of Clark Street and Fifth Avenue. Hull House once stood in the suburbs, but the city has steadily grown up around it and its site now has corners on three or four foreign colonies. Between Halsted Street and the river live about ten thousand Italians﻿—Neapolitans, Sicilians, and Calabrians, with an occasional Lombard or Venetian. To the south on Twelfth Street are many Germans, and side streets are given over almost entirely to Polish and Russian Jews. Still farther south, these Jewish colonies merge into a huge Bohemian colony, so vast that Chicago ranks as the third Bohemian city in the world. To the northwest are many Canadian-French, clannish in spite of their long residence in America, and to the north are Irish and first-generation Americans. On the streets directly west and farther north are well-to-do English speaking families, many of whom own their own houses and have lived in the neighborhood for years; one man is still living in his old farmhouse.

===Chinese===

「霍尔斯特德街长三十二英里，是芝加哥的大干道之一；波尔克街与它相交，恰在南面的牲畜围场和北面芝加哥河北支的造船厂之间。在这两项产业之间的六英里路段，街两旁排满肉铺、杂货铺，排满或昏暗或花哨的酒馆，还有卖成衣的铺面堂皇的大字号。波尔克街从霍尔斯特德街往西，渐渐殷实；往东一英里到州街，则一路败坏下去，在克拉克街和第五大道的街角上穿过一张罪恶的网。赫尔馆从前立在郊野，城市却在它四周稳步长起，如今它占地的一角，同三四个移民聚落搭界。霍尔斯特德街与河之间住着大约一万意大利人——那不勒斯人、西西里人、卡拉布里亚人，偶有伦巴第人或威尼斯人。往南，第十二街上有许多德国人；一些横街几乎全让给了波兰和俄国的犹太人。再往南，这些犹太聚落并进一个庞大的波希米亚人聚落——大得芝加哥在世界上排得上第三大波希米亚城市。西北面有许多法裔加拿大人，尽管侨居美国多年依旧抱团；北面是爱尔兰人和第一代美国出生者。正西和再往北的街上，住着殷实的英语人家，许多有自家的房子，在这一带住了多年；有一位至今还住在他当年的农舍里。

===Original===

“The policy of the public authorities of never taking an initiative, and always waiting to be urged to do their duty, is obviously fatal in a neighborhood where there is little initiative among the citizens. The idea underlying our self-government breaks down in such a ward. The streets are inexpressibly dirty, the number of schools inadequate, sanitary legislation unenforced, the street lighting bad, the paving miserable and altogether lacking in the alleys and smaller streets, and the stables foul beyond description. Hundreds of houses are unconnected with the street sewer. The older and richer inhabitants seem anxious to move away as rapidly as they can afford it. They make room for newly arrived immigrants who are densely ignorant of civic duties. This substitution of the older inhabitants is accomplished industrially also, in the south and east quarters of the ward. The Jews and Italians do the finishing for the great clothing manufacturers, formerly done by Americans, Irish, and Germans, who refused to submit to the extremely low prices to which the sweating system has reduced their successors. As the design of the sweating system is the elimination of rent from the manufacture of clothing, the ‘outside work’ is begun after the clothing leaves the cutter. An unscrupulous contractor regards no basement as too dark, no stable loft too foul, no rear shanty too provisional, no tenement room too small for his workroom, as these conditions imply low rental. Hence these shops abound in the worst of the foreign districts where the sweater easily finds his cheap basement and his home finishers.

===Chinese===

「公共当局那种从不带头、总要等人催逼才肯尽职的做派，在居民几乎无人带头的街区里，分明是致命的。我们自治制度所依据的理念，在这样的选区里失灵了。街道脏得没法说，学校数量不足，卫生法令无人执行，街灯昏暗，路面糟糕，小巷小街干脆连路面都没有，马厩肮脏得难以形容。几百所房子没接街上的下水道。年长和有钱些的住户，似乎只求尽早搬走，能走多早走多早。他们腾出的地方，给了对公民义务一无所知的新到移民。老住户的这种更替，在本选区的南部和东部也体现在行当上：犹太人和意大利人替大服装制造商做整烫收尾——这活从前是美国人、爱尔兰人、德国人做的，他们不肯接受血汗制度压到后来者头上的那种极低价钱。血汗制度的设计，本就是要把房租从服装制造中剔除出去，所以「外发活计」等衣料一离开裁剪台便开始了。心黑的包工头眼里，没有一间地下室嫌太暗，没有一层马厩阁楼嫌太臭，没有一间后院棚屋嫌太将就，没有一间出租屋嫌太小——凡此种种都意味着低房租。所以这类作坊在最糟的移民区里比比皆是，血汗包工头在那儿轻易就找得到他便宜的地下室和他家里做活的缝工。

===Original===

“The houses of the ward, for the most part wooden, were originally built for one family and are now occupied by several. They are after the type of the inconvenient frame cottages found in the poorer suburbs twenty years ago. Many of them were built where they now stand; others were brought thither on rollers, because their previous sites had been taken by factories. The fewer brick tenement buildings which are three or four stories high are comparatively new, and there are few large tenements. The little wooden houses have a temporary aspect, and for this reason, perhaps, the tenement-house legislation in Chicago is totally inadequate. Rear tenements flourish; many houses have no water supply save the faucet in the back yard, there are no fire escapes, the garbage and ashes are placed in wooden boxes which are fastened to the street pavements. One of the most discouraging features about the present system of tenement houses is that many are owned by sordid and ignorant immigrants. The theory that wealth brings responsibility, that possession entails at length education and refinement, in these cases fails utterly. The children of an Italian immigrant owner may shine shoes in the street, and his wife may pick rags from the street gutter, laboriously sorting them in a dingy court. Wealth may do something for her self-complacency and feeling of consequence; it certainly does nothing for her comfort or her children’s improvement nor for the cleanliness of anyone concerned. Another thing that prevents better houses in Chicago is the tentative attitude of the real estate men. Many unsavory conditions are allowed to continue which would be regarded with horror if they were considered permanent. Meanwhile, the wretched conditions persist until at least two generations of children have been born and reared in them.

===Chinese===

「本选区的房子大半是木头的，原为一家一户所建，如今住着好几家。样式是二十年前穷郊区常见的那种不方便的木板小屋。许多本来就在原地起建；另一些是垫着滚木运来的——原先的地基让工厂占了。为数不多的三四层砖楼出租屋比较新，大型出租屋很少。这些小木房一副临时样子，也许正因如此，芝加哥的出租屋立法形同虚设。后院出租屋兴旺得很；许多房子除了后院一只水龙头再无水源；没有防火梯；垃圾炉灰装在钉死在街面上的木箱里。现今出租屋制度最叫人灰心的一点是：许多房东就是邋遢而无知的移民。财富带来责任、产业终将带来教养与文雅——这套理论，在这些例子里彻底失灵。一位意大利移民房东的孩子可以在街上给人擦皮鞋，他的妻子可以满街沟里拾破布，在昏暗的院子里费力分拣。财富或许能给她的自满和身价感添点什么；对她的舒适、对儿女的长进、对相关人等的清洁，却实在毫无贡献。芝加哥房子好不起来的另一个原因，是地产商们的观望态度。许多不体面的状况就这样被容许延续着——倘被视为长久之计，人们准会惊骇。与此同时，悲惨的境况就这样拖着，至少拖过两代孩子在其中出生、长大。

===Original===

“In every neighborhood where poorer people live, because rents are supposed to be cheaper there, is an element which, although uncertain in the individual, in the aggregate can be counted upon. It is composed of people of former education and opportunity who have cherished ambitions and prospects, but who are caricatures of what they meant to be﻿—‘hollow ghosts which blame the living men.’ There are times in many lives when there is a cessation of energy and loss of power. Men and women of education and refinement come to live in a cheaper neighborhood because they lack the ability to make money, because of ill health, because of an unfortunate marriage, or for other reasons which do not imply criminality or stupidity. Among them are those who, in spite of untoward circumstances, keep up some sort of an intellectual life; those who are ‘great for books,’ as their neighbors say. To such the Settlement may be a genuine refuge.”

In the very first weeks of our residence Miss Starr started a reading party in George Eliot’s *Romola*, which was attended by a group of young women who followed the wonderful tale with unflagging interest. The weekly reading was held in our little upstairs dining room, and two members of the club came to dinner each week, not only that they might be received as guests, but that they might help us wash the dishes afterwards and so make the table ready for the stacks of Florentine photographs.

Our “first resident,” as she gaily designated herself, was a charming old lady who gave five consecutive readings from Hawthorne to a most appreciative audience, interspersing the magic tales most delightfully with recollections of the elusive and fascinating author. Years before she had lived at Brook Farm as a pupil of the Ripleys, and she came to us for ten days because she wished to live once more in an atmosphere where “idealism ran high.” We thus early found the type of class which through all the years has remained most popular﻿—a combination of a social atmosphere with serious study.

===Chinese===

「凡是穷人聚居的街区——因为据说那里房租便宜些——总有一种人：作为个人固然说不准，合起来却算得准。他们由这样一些人组成：从前受过教育、有过机会，也怀抱过抱负和前程，如今却成了当初志愿的漫画——『怨怪活人的空心鬼影』。人的一生中总有精力停摆、力量流失的时候。有教养的男女住进便宜的街区，或因没有挣钱的本事，或因体弱多病，或因一段不幸的婚姻，或因别的什么——总归不意味着犯罪或愚笨。他们中间，有那身处逆境仍维持着某种智识生活的人，有街坊们所说的『书迷』。对这样的人，定居所或许是一座真正的庇护所。」

住进去的头几个星期，斯塔尔小姐办起一个《罗慕拉》（*Romola*）读书会。参加的是一群年轻女子，跟着那个美妙的故事，兴致从无衰减。每周的读书会设在我们楼上的小餐厅里；读书会的两位会员每周来吃晚饭——不光是把他们当客人待，也为了饭后帮我们洗碗，好腾出桌子摆那一摞摞佛罗伦萨照片。

我们那位「第一位住客」——她自己笑吟吟这样称呼自己——是位可爱的老太太。她连讲五场霍桑，听众知音极了；讲那些奇妙的故事之间，还穿插着对那位捉摸不定、魅力无穷的作者的种种回忆，令人心醉。多年以前，她曾在布鲁克农庄（Brook Farm）做里普利夫妇的学生；她来我们这儿住十天，只为再过一回「理想主义高涨」的日子。我们就这样早早发现了那种此后多年一直最受欢迎的课的形式——社交的气氛同严肃的研习相结合。

===Original===

Volunteers to the new undertaking came quickly; a charming young girl conducted a kindergarten in the drawing room, coming regularly every morning from her home in a distant part of the North Side of the city. Although a tablet to her memory has stood upon a mantel shelf in Hull House for five years, we still associate her most vividly with the play of little children, first in her kindergarten and then in her own nursery, which furnished a veritable illustration of Victor Hugo’s definition of heaven﻿—“a place where parents are always young and children always little.” Her daily presence for the first two years made it quite impossible for us to become too solemn and self-conscious in our strenuous routine, for her mirth and buoyancy were irresistible and her eager desire to share the life of the neighborhood never failed, although it was often put to a severe test. One day at luncheon she gaily recited her futile attempt to impress temperance principles upon the mind of an Italian mother, to whom she had returned a small daughter of five sent to the kindergarten “in quite a horrid state of intoxication” from the wine-soaked bread upon which she had breakfasted. The mother, with the gentle courtesy of a South Italian, listened politely to her graphic portrayal of the untimely end awaiting so immature a wine bibber; but long before the lecture was finished, quite unconscious of the incongruity, she hospitably set forth her best wines, and when her baffled guest refused one after the other, she disappeared, only to quickly return with a small dark glass of whisky, saying reassuringly, “See, I have brought you the true American drink.” The recital ended in seriocomic despair, with the rueful statement that “the impression I probably made on her darkened mind was, that it was the American custom to breakfast children on bread soaked in whisky instead of light Italian wine.”

===Chinese===

新事业的志愿者来得很快。一位可爱的姑娘在客厅里办起幼儿园，每天早晨从城北远处她家里准点赶来。虽然赫尔馆壁炉架上纪念她的铜牌已经立了五年，我们想起她，最鲜活的还是她同小孩子们玩耍的样子——先在她的幼儿园里，后在她自己的育儿室里。那间育儿室，真真给雨果给天国下的定义做了一个注脚——「一个父母永远年轻、孩子永远幼小的地方」。头两年她天天在，我们那紧张的一摊子事务就没法变得太严肃、太自觉；她的欢快和轻盈没法抗拒，她分享街坊生活的热望从未衰减，尽管常常受到严峻的考验。一天午饭时，她笑嘻嘻地讲起自己向一位意大利母亲灌输戒酒道理的徒劳：那家五岁的小姑娘被送来幼儿园时「醉得一塌糊涂」——早饭吃的面包浸透了酒。那位母亲怀着南意大利人的温良客气，礼貌地听她绘声绘色地描绘，这样年幼的一个酒鬼等着怎样的早夭下场；可训导远没讲完，她全然不觉其中的不协调，殷勤地摆出了自家最好的几样酒；客人一样样推辞，她不见了，转眼又端来一小杯深色的威士忌，宽慰道：「瞧，我给您拿真正的美国酒来了。」这段叙述以又悲又喜的绝望收场，末了是那句懊恼的话：「我在她那颗阴暗的头脑里留下的印象，多半是：美国人的规矩，是拿威士忌泡面包给孩子当早饭，而不是拿清淡的意大利酒。」

===Original===

That first kindergarten was a constant source of education to us. We were much surprised to find social distinctions even among its lambs, although greatly amused with the neat formulation made by the superior little Italian boy who refused to sit beside uncouth little Angelina because “we eat our macaroni this way”﻿—imitating the movement of a fork from a plate to his mouth﻿—“and she eat her macaroni this way,” holding his hand high in the air and throwing back his head, that his wide-open mouth might receive an imaginary cascade. Angelina gravely nodded her little head in approval of this distinction between gentry and peasant. “But isn’t it astonishing that merely table manners are made such a test all the way along﻿—” was the comment of their democratic teacher. Another memory which refuses to be associated with death, which came to her all too soon, is that of the young girl who organized our first really successful club of boys, holding their fascinated interest by the old chivalric tales, set forth so dramatically and vividly that checkers and jackstraws were abandoned by all the other clubs on Boys’ Day, that their members might form a listening fringe to “The Young Heros.”

I met a member of the latter club one day as he flung himself out of the House in the rage by which an emotional boy hopes to keep from shedding tears. “There is no use coming here any more, Prince Roland is dead,” he gruffly explained as we passed. We encouraged the younger boys in tournaments and dramatics of all sorts, and we somewhat fatuously believed that boys who were early interested in adventurers or explorers might later want to know the lives of living statesmen and inventors. It is needless to add that the boys quickly responded to such a program, and that the only difficulty lay in finding leaders who were able to carry it out. This difficulty has been with us through all the years of growth and development in the Boys’ Club until now, with its five-story building, its splendid equipment of shops, of recreation and study rooms, that group alone is successful which commands the services of a resourceful and devoted leader.

===Chinese===

那第一个幼儿园对我们是一所不落的学堂。我们大为惊讶地发现，连它的羔羊中间也有等级之分——虽然那位神气的小意大利男孩的干净表述把我们逗坏了：他不肯同粗手粗脚的小安杰利娜（Angelina）坐在一起，因为「我们吃通心粉是这样吃的」——学的是叉子从盘子到嘴的动作——「她吃通心粉是那样吃的」，手高高举在空中，头往后一仰，好让张大的嘴接住一道想象中的瀑布。安杰利娜郑重地点点小脑袋，赞成上等人和乡下人之间这一条界线。「可是多奇怪呀，从头到尾，怎么单拿吃相来当试金石——」她们的民主派老师这样评论。另一段不肯同死亡沾边的记忆——死亡来得她太早了——是那位组织起我们第一个真正成功的少年俱乐部的姑娘：她用古老的骑士故事牢牢拴住孩子们的心，讲得那样富于戏剧性、那样鲜活，以致男孩子们活动日那天，别的俱乐部连跳棋和挑棍都撂下了，会员们围成听话的边圈，听「少年英雄们」。

有一天我遇见那俱乐部的一名会员，他正从馆里冲出来，满腔怒火——感情丰富的男孩，指望靠这怒火把眼泪逼回去。「再来也没用了，罗兰王子死了。」我们擦肩而过时他粗声粗气地解释。我们鼓励年纪小些的男孩搞比武会、演各样的戏；我们还颇为自得地相信，早对冒险家、探险家发生兴趣的孩子，往后多半会想知道在世的政治家和发明家的生平。不必说，男孩们对这套安排响应得飞快；唯一的难处，在于找得到领得起这摊子的人。这个难处跟着少年俱乐部一路的成长与发展，直到今天——如今它有了五层的大楼、齐备的工场、文娱和自习房间——只有那些请得着一位足智多谋、全心全意的领袖的小组，才能成功。

===Original===

The dozens of younger children who from the first came to Hull House were organized into groups which were not quite classes and not quite clubs. The value of these groups consisted almost entirely in arousing a higher imagination and in giving the children the opportunity which they could not have in the crowded schools, for initiative and for independent social relationships. The public schools then contained little hand work of any sort, so that naturally any instruction which we provided for the children took the direction of this supplementary work. But it required a constant effort that the pressure of poverty itself should not defeat the educational aim. The Italian girls in the sewing classes would count the day lost when they could not carry home a garment, and the insistence that it should be neatly made seemed a super-refinement to those in dire need of clothing.

As these clubs have been continued during the twenty years they have developed classes in the many forms of handicraft which the newer education is so rapidly adapting for the delight of children; but they still keep their essentially social character and still minister to that large number of children who leave school the very week they are fourteen years old, only too eager to close the schoolroom door forever on a tiresome task that is at last well over. It seems to us important that these children shall find themselves permanently attached to a House that offers them evening clubs and classes with their old companions, that merges as easily as possible the school life into the working life and does what it can to find places for the bewildered young things looking for work. A large proportion of the delinquent boys brought into the juvenile court in Chicago are the oldest sons in large families whose wages are needed at home. The grades from which many of them leave school, as the records show, are piteously far from the seventh and eighth where the very first introduction in manual training is given, nor have they been caught by any other abiding interest.

===Chinese===

从一开始就涌到赫尔馆来的、成打的更小孩子，被编成一些组：算不得班，也算不得俱乐部。这些组的益处，几乎全在唤起更高的想象力，给孩子一个机会——拥挤的学校给不了的机会——去主动做事、去建立独立的交往。当年的公立学校几乎没有手工课，所以我们给孩子提供的任何教学，自然都朝这种补充性功课的方向去。可要叫贫穷自身的重压不致挫败教育的目的，需要不断的努力。缝纫班上的意大利小姑娘，哪天不能带一件衣裳回家，便算那天白过了；而对于急需衣裳的人，非要把针脚做齐整的坚持，未免是一种过分的讲究。

这些俱乐部延续的二十年里，发展出了多种手工艺的班——新教育正飞快地把它们改造成孩子们的乐趣；但它们仍保持着本质上社交的品格，仍照管着那一大群孩子：他们一满十四岁，当礼拜就离了学校，巴不得把教室的门对一件总算熬到头的苦差永远关上。在我们看来，要紧的是让这些孩子发现自己同一个馆结下长久的联系：那里有同老伙伴一起的晚间俱乐部和课，能把学校生活尽可能平顺地融进劳动生活，还能尽力替那些找工作的、六神无主的小家伙寻摸去处。芝加哥被送上少年法庭的问题男孩，一大部分是大家庭的长子，家里等着他们的工钱过活。记录显示，他们离校时的年级，离最初开设手工训练入门的七、八年级，差得叫人心酸；除这以外，也没有任何别的持久的兴趣把他们留住过。

===Original===

In spite of these flourishing clubs for children early established at Hull House, and the fact that our first organized undertaking was a kindergarten, we were very insistent that the Settlement should not be primarily for the children, and that it was absurd to suppose that grown people would not respond to opportunities for education and social life. Our enthusiastic kindergartner herself demonstrated this with an old woman of ninety who, because she was left alone all day while her daughter cooked in a restaurant, had formed such a persistent habit of picking the plaster off the walls that one landlord after another refused to have her for a tenant. It required but a few week’s time to teach her to make large paper chains, and gradually she was content to do it all day long, and in the end took quite as much pleasure in adorning the walls as she had formally taken in demolishing them. Fortunately the landlord had never heard the aesthetic principle that exposure of basic construction is more desirable than gaudy decoration. In course of time it was discovered that the old woman could speak Gaelic, and when one or two grave professors came to see her, the neighborhood was filled with pride that such a wonder lived in their midst. To mitigate life for a woman of ninety was an unfailing refutation of the statement that the Settlement was designed for the young.

On our first New Year’s Day at Hull House we invited the older people in the vicinity, sending a carriage for the most feeble and announcing to all of them that we were going to organize an Old Settlers’ Party.

===Chinese===

尽管赫尔馆早早办起了这些红火的儿童俱乐部，我们头一项有组织的事业又是幼儿园，我们却一口咬定：定居所不该首先是为孩子的；以为成年人不会响应受教育与社交生活的机会，那才荒唐。我们那位热情的幼儿园教师，就用一位九十岁的老太太亲身证明了这一点。老太太的女儿在餐馆掌灶，她整日独自在家，养成一个雷打不动的习惯：抠墙皮。房东一个接一个，都拒她于门外。教会她做纸链子，不过用了几个星期；渐渐地，她满足于整天做纸链，末了，装饰墙壁的兴致，竟同当初拆毁墙壁的兴致一样高。所幸房东从未听过那条美学原则——裸露的构造比花哨的装饰更可取。后来又发现老太太会说盖尔语（Gaelic）；一两位庄重的教授来看她时，这一带充满自豪：这样的奇人就住在他们中间。为一位九十岁的老妇纾解人生，是对「定居所是为年轻人办的」那句话最不动摇的反驳。

在赫尔馆的头一个新年，我们请来了邻近的老人家，最衰弱的还派马车去接，并向他们全体宣布：我们要办一个「老拓荒者聚会」。

===Original===

Every New Year’s Day since, older people in varying numbers have come together at Hull House to relate early hardships, and to take for the moment the place in the community to which their pioneer life entitles them. Many people who were formerly residents of the vicinity, but whom prosperity has carried into more desirable neighborhoods, come back to these meetings and often confess to each other that they have never since found such kindness as in early Chicago when all its citizens came together in mutual enterprises. Many of these pioneers, so like the men and women of my earliest childhood that I always felt comforted by their presence in the house, were very much opposed to “foreigners,” whom they held responsible for a depreciation of property and a general lowering of the tone of the neighborhood. Sometimes we had a chance for championship; I recall one old man, fiercely American, who had reproached me because we had so many “foreign views” on our walls, to whom I endeavored to set forth our hope that the pictures might afford a familiar island to the immigrants in a sea of new and strange impressions. The old settler guest, taken off his guard, replied, “I see; they feel as we did when we saw a Yankee notion from Down East,”﻿—thereby formulating the dim kinship between the pioneer and the immigrant, both “buffeting the waves of a new development.” The older settlers as well as their children throughout the years have given genuine help to our various enterprises for neighborhood improvement, and from their own memories of earlier hardships have made many shrewd suggestions for alleviating the difficulties of that first sharp struggle with untoward conditions.

===Chinese===

此后每个新年，数目不等的老人都在赫尔馆聚会，讲早年的艰辛，暂时享一享他们的拓荒生涯在社区里应得的位置。许多从前住在这一带、后来发迹搬进更好街区的人，也回来赴会，还常常彼此坦言：从此再没见过早期芝加哥那样的善意——那时全城公民同舟共济。这些拓荒者，同我最早童年里的那些男女那么相像，他们一在馆里，我心里总是踏实。他们许多人对「外国人」深恶痛绝，认定产业跌价、街面风气一落千丈，全是外国人闹的。有时我们也有机会当一回护卫。我记得一位火爆的美国老头，责备我们墙上挂了那么多「外国风景」，我努力向他陈述我们的指望：这些画，也许能为移民们在一片新奇印象的大海里，提供一座熟悉的小岛。这位老拓荒者客人一时不防，答道：「我明白了；他们瞅着这些，就同我们当年瞅见从东边新英格兰捎来的洋玩意儿一样。」——一句话，把拓荒者和移民之间那层朦胧的亲缘道了出来：两者都「在同一场新发展的风浪里搏击」。这些老住户连同他们的儿女，多年来为我们各项改善街区的计划出过实实在在的力；凭着对早年艰辛的记忆，也为缓和新环境里第一场尖锐搏斗的难处，出过许多精明的主意。

===Original===

In those early days we were often asked why we had come to live on Halsted Street when we could afford to live somewhere else. I remember one man who used to shake his head and say it was “the strangest thing he had met in his experience,” but who was finally convinced that it was “not strange but natural.” In time it came to seem natural to all of us that the Settlement should be there. If it is natural to feed the hungry and care for the sick, it is certainly natural to give pleasure to the young, comfort to the aged, and to minister to the deep-seated craving for social intercourse that all men feel. Whoever does it is rewarded by something which, if not gratitude, is at least spontaneous and vital and lacks that irksome sense of obligation with which a substantial benefit is too often acknowledged.

In addition to the neighbors who responded to the receptions and classes, we found those who were too battered and oppressed to care for them. To these, however, was left that susceptibility to the bare offices of humanity which raises such offices into a bond of fellowship.

From the first it seemed understood that we were ready to perform the humblest neighborhood services. We were asked to wash the newborn babies, and to prepare the dead for burial, to nurse the sick, and to “mind the children.”

Occasionally these neighborly offices unexpectedly uncovered ugly human traits. For six weeks after an operation we kept in one of our three bedrooms a forlorn little baby who, because he was born with a cleft palate, was most unwelcome even to his mother, and we were horrified when he died of neglect a week after he was returned to his home; a little Italian bride of fifteen sought shelter with us one November evening to escape her husband who had beaten her every night for a week when he returned home from work, because she had lost her wedding ring; two of us officiated quite alone at the birth of an illegitimate child because the doctor was late in arriving, and none of the honest Irish matrons would “touch the likes of her”; we ministered at the deathbed of a young man, who during a long illness of tuberculosis had received so many bottles of whisky through the mistaken kindness of his friends, that the cumulative effect produced wild periods of exultation, in one of which he died.

===Chinese===

早年常有人问我们：明明住得起别处，干吗搬到霍尔斯特德街来住。我记得有一个人，总摇着头说，这是「他平生遇到的最怪的事」；可他终于被说服了：这事「不怪，反而自然」。日子久了，我们自己也觉得定居所立在那儿是再自然不过。倘若给饥饿者饭吃、看护病人是自然的，那么给年轻人快乐、给老年人安慰、应答人人都感到的那种根深蒂固的社交渴望，自然也是自然的。做这事的人所得的报偿，纵然不是感激，至少也是自发的、有生气的，没有那种沉重的亏欠感——一份实实在在的好处受人认领时，往往就带着那股叫人不自在的味道。

除了那些来赴会、来上课的街坊，我们还遇见了被生活打磨压迫得无心于此的人。不过，他们身上偏偏还留着一份对人际间最朴素的相助的感受力——正是这份感受力，把这样的相助升华为一条团契的纽带。

从一开始，大家似乎就心照不宣：街坊里最卑微的差事，我们随叫随到。人家请我们给新生儿洗身，给死者抹洗装裹，护理病人，「照看孩子」。

这些街坊的差事，偶尔也会出其不意地揭出人性丑陋的一面。手术后，一个孤苦的小婴儿在我们三间卧室的一间里养了六个星期——他生下来就是豁嘴，连他母亲都嫌他；送回家一个星期后，他竟因疏于照料而死，我们惊骇不已。一个十一月的傍晚，一个十五岁的意大利小新娘到我们这儿避难，逃她的丈夫：就因为她丢了结婚戒指，那男人下班回家，一连一礼拜夜夜打她。一个私生子降生时，医生迟迟不到，正经的爱尔兰主妇们谁也不肯「碰她那号人」，只我们两个独自接生。我们还守过一个年轻人的临终：他肺痨缠身日久，朋友们错付的好心送来那么多瓶威士忌，积少成多，激出一阵阵狂喜的发作——他就在其中一阵里死了。

===Original===

We were also early impressed with the curious isolation of many of the immigrants; an Italian woman once expressed her pleasure in the red roses that she saw at one of our receptions in surprise that they had been “brought so fresh all the way from Italy.” She would not believe for an instant that they had been grown in America. She said that she had lived in Chicago for six years and had never seen any roses, whereas in Italy she had seen them every summer in great profusion. During all that time, of course, the woman had lived within ten blocks of a florist’s window; she had not been more than a five-cent car ride away from the public parks; but she had never dreamed of faring forth for herself, and no one had taken her. Her conception of America had been the untidy street in which she lived and had made her long struggle to adapt herself to American ways.

But in spite of some untoward experiences, we were constantly impressed with the uniform kindness and courtesy we received. Perhaps these first days laid the simple human foundations which are certainly essential for continuous living among the poor; first, genuine preference for residence in an industrial quarter to any other part of the city, because it is interesting and makes the human appeal; and second, the conviction, in the words of Canon Barnett, that the things that make men alike are finer and better than the things that keep them apart, and that these basic likenesses, if they are properly accentuated, easily transcend the less essential differences of race, language, creed, and tradition.

Perhaps even in those first days we made a beginning toward that object which was afterwards stated in our charter: “To provide a center for higher civic and social life; to institute and maintain educational and philanthropic enterprises, and to investigate and improve the conditions in the industrial districts of Chicago.”

A view from a Hull House window.

===Chinese===

许多移民那种奇特的封闭，也早早印在我们心上。一位意大利妇人曾在一次招待会上对着红玫瑰表示欢喜，惊讶它们「从意大利一路捎来，还这么新鲜」。她一刻也不肯相信这花是美国种的。她说她在芝加哥住了六年，从没见过玫瑰；在意大利，年年夏天都开得漫山遍野。这六年里，这位妇人当然一直住在离一家花店橱窗十个街区之内，离公园也不过五分钱电车票的路程；可她自己从没想过出门走走，也没有人带她去。她心目中的美国，就是她住的那条邋遢街道，就是她为适应美国方式做的那场漫长的挣扎。

可是，尽管有过种种不快的经历，我们时时感念所受到的一律的善意与礼遇。也许，正是这最初的日子，打下了长住穷人中间必不可少的那两样简单的人性根基：其一，是真心更愿意住在工业区，胜过城里任何别处——因为这里有意思，因为它发出人的呼唤；其二，是用巴尼特牧师（Canon Barnett）的话说的那个信念：使人相像的东西，比使人隔开的东西更精、更好；这些根本的相似，只要摆正了重心，轻易便越过了种族、语言、信仰、传统那些次要的差异。

也许，即使在那些最初的日子里，我们已朝着后来写进章程的那个目标迈出了头一步：「提供一个更高的公民生活与社会生活的中心；创办并维持教育与慈善事业，调查并改善芝加哥工业区的状况。」

从赫尔馆窗口望出去的一景。

## VI / 六
===Original===

Subjective Necessity for Social Settlements

The Ethical Culture Societies held a summer school at Plymouth, Massachusetts, in 1892, to which they invited several people representing the then-new Settlement movement, that they might discuss with others the general theme of Philanthropy and Social Progress.

I venture to produce here parts of a lecture I delivered in Plymouth, both because I have found it impossible to formulate with the same freshness those early motives and strivings, and because, when published with other papers given that summer, it was received by the Settlement people themselves as a satisfactory statement.

I remember one golden summer afternoon during the sessions of the summer school that several of us met on the shores of a pond in a pine wood a few miles from Plymouth, to discuss our new movement. The natural leader of the group was Robert A. Woods. He had recently returned from a residence in Toynbee Hall, London, to open Andover House in Boston, and had just issued a book, *English Social Movements*, in which he had gathered together and focused the many forms of social endeavor preceding and contemporaneous with the English Settlements. There were Miss Vida D. Scudder and Miss Helena Dudley from the College Settlement Association, Miss Julia C. Lathrop and myself from Hull House. Some of us had numbered our years as far as thirty, and we all carefully avoided the extravagance of statement which characterizes youth, and yet I doubt if anywhere on the continent that summer could have been found a group of people more genuinely interested in social development or more sincerely convinced that they had found a clue by which the conditions in crowded cities might be understood and the agencies for social betterment developed.

===Chinese===

社会定居所的主观必要性

1892年，伦理文化学会（Ethical Culture Societies）在马萨诸塞州的普利茅斯办了一所暑期学校，请了几位代表当时新兴的定居所运动的人，好让大家一同讨论「慈善与社会进步」这个总题。

我在这里冒昧引录当年在普利茅斯所做讲演的一部分，一是因为我发现，那些早期的动机与奋斗，如今再不可能带着当初的新鲜感表述出来了；二是因为那篇讲稿同那年夏天的其他论文一并刊出时，定居所的人们自己都认可它是一份满意的陈述。

我记得暑期学校上课期间一个金色的夏日午后，我们几个人聚在离普利茅斯几英里一片松林里的池塘边，讨论我们的新运动。这一群人天生的领袖是罗伯特·A·伍兹（Robert A. Woods）。他刚从伦敦汤因比馆住了回来，去开办波士顿的安多弗馆（Andover House），又刚出版了一本《英国社会运动》（*English Social Movements*），把英国定居所之前和同时的种种社会努力汇聚拢来、聚成焦点。在场的还有学院定居所协会的维达·D·斯卡德小姐（Miss Vida D. Scudder）和海伦娜·达德利小姐（Miss Helena Dudley），赫尔馆的朱莉娅·C·拉斯罗普小姐（Miss Julia C. Lathrop）和我。我们中有人已数到三十岁，大家都很小心地避开口无遮拦的青年腔；可我怀疑，那年夏天，整个美洲大陆上还能不能找到另一群人，比我们更真切地关切社会的发展，更笃信自己找到了一条线索，凭着它可以理解拥挤城市的境况、培育社会改善的机关。

===Original===

We were all careful to avoid saying that we had found a “life work,” perhaps with an instinctive dread of expending all our energy in vows of constancy, as so often happens; and yet it is interesting to note that of all the people whom I have recalled as the enthusiasts at that little conference have remained attached to Settlements in actual residence for longer or shorter periods each year during the eighteen years that have elapsed since then, although they have also been closely identified as publicists or governmental officials with movements outside. It is as if they had discovered that the Settlement was too valuable as a method as a way of approach to the social question to abandoned, although they had long since discovered it was not a “social movement” in itself. This, however, is anticipating the future, whereas the following paper on “The Subjective Necessity for Social Settlements” should have a chance to speak for itself. It is perhaps too late in the day to express regret for its stilted title.

===Chinese===

我们都很小心，不说自己找到了「终身的事业」——也许是出于一种本能的畏惧，怕把全部精力都耗在山盟海誓上，这种事太常见了。不过有意思的是：我回忆得起的那次小聚会的热忱者，此后十八年里，个个每年都以或长或短的时段实际住在定居所里，尽管同时也以政论家或政府官员的身份深深参与外面的事业。仿佛他们发现：作为一种方法、一条接近社会问题的路径，定居所宝贵得叫人割舍不下——尽管他们早就发现，它本身并不是一场「社会运动」。不过这已是预支未来；下面那篇《社会定居所的主观必要性》，还该让它自己说话。为它那副书呆子气的题目表示遗憾，如今恐怕为时已晚。

===Original===

This paper is an attempt to analyze the motives which underlie a movement based, not only upon conviction, but upon genuine emotion, wherever educated young people are seeking an outlet for that sentiment for universal brotherhood, which the best spirit of our times is forcing from an emotion into a motive. These young people accomplish little toward the solution of this social problem, and bear the brunt of being cultivated into unnourished, oversensitive lives. They have been shut off from the common labor by which they live which is a great source of moral and physical health. They feel a fatal want of harmony between their theory and their lives, a lack of coordination between thought and action. I think it is hard for us to realize how seriously many of them are taking to the notion of human brotherhood, how eagerly they long to give tangible expression to the democratic ideal. These young men and women, longing to socialize their democracy, are animated by certain hopes which may be thus loosely formulated; that if in a democratic country nothing can be permanently achieved save through the masses of the people, it will be impossible to establish a higher political life than the people themselves crave; that it is difficult to see how the notion of a higher civic life can be fostered save through common intercourse; that the blessings which we associate with a life of refinement and cultivation can be made universal and must be made universal if they are to be permanent; that the good we secure for ourselves is precarious and uncertain, is floating in midair, until it is secured for all of us and incorporated into our common life. It is easier to state these hopes than to formulate the line of motives, which I believe to constitute the trend of the subjective pressure toward the Settlement. There is something primordial about these motives, but I am perhaps overbold in designating them as a great desire to share the race life. We all bear traces of the starvation struggle which for so long made up the life of the race. Our very organism holds memories and glimpses of that long life of our ancestors, which still goes on among so many of our contemporaries.

===Chinese===

本文试图剖析一场运动的动机。这场运动不仅基于信念，也基于真实的情感——凡有受过教育的年轻人在寻求为四海同胞之情寻找出路的地方，这场运动就存在；我们时代最好的精神，正把这情感催逼成动机。这些年轻人为社会问题的解决出力甚少，却首当其冲，被养成了一种缺乏滋养、过分敏感的生活。他们被隔绝在借以维生的共同劳动之外——那本是道德与健康的一大源泉。他们痛感理论与生活之间致命的失谐，思想与行动之间失调。我想，我们很难体会到，他们中许多人把人类的手足之义看得多么郑重，多么急切地渴望给民主理想一个有形的表达。这些渴望使民主社会化的青年男女，被几种希望推动着；不妨粗略表述如下：在民主国家，凡不能通过民众而成就的事，终不能长久——那么，比民众自身渴求的更高的政治生活，便无从建立；很难想象，更高的公民生活的观念，能不经共同的交往而养成；我们同雅致与教养的生活相联的种种福分，能够普及，也必须普及，否则便不能长久；我们为自己谋得的善，是悬空的、靠不住的，直到它为我们所有人谋得、织进我们共同的生活里才算数。陈述这些希望容易；要理清那一条动机的线索——我相信它构成了推向定居所的主观压力的走向——就难了。这些动机里有某种太初的东西；把它们径直称作「分担种族生活的巨大渴望」，我或许胆子太大。我们人人都带着饥饿挣扎的痕迹——它长久以来就是种族的生活。我们的身体本身，就藏着对我们祖先漫长生活的记忆与一瞥；那种生活，如今仍在我们的许多同代人中间继续着。

===Original===

Nothing so deadens the sympathies and shrivels the power of enjoyment as the persistent keeping away from the great opportunities for helpfulness and a continual ignoring of the starvation struggle which makes up the life of at least half the race. To shut one’s self away from that half of the race life is to shut one’s self away from the most vital part of it; it is to live out but half the humanity to which we have been born heir and to use but half our faculties. We have all had longings for a fuller life which should include the use of these faculties. These longings are the physical complement of the “Intimations of Immortality,” on which no ode has yet been written. To portray these would be the work of a poet, and it is hazardous for any but a poet to attempt it.

===Chinese===

再没有什么比这更能麻木同情、枯萎享受的能力了：一味躲开施以援手的种种大好机会，一贯无视那至少构成半数人类生活的饥饿挣扎。把自己关在这半种族生活之外，就是把自己关在它最有生气的部分之外；就是只活了我们所承继的人性的一半，只用了一半的官能。我们都曾渴望一种更完整的生活，把这些官能都用上。这些渴望，是《不朽的征兆》（"Intimations of Immortality"）在身体上的对偶——还没有人为它写过颂歌。描画它们是诗人的活计；非诗人而想一试，是冒险。

===Original===

You may remember the forlorn feeling which occasionally seizes you when you arrive early in the morning a stranger in a great city: the stream of laboring people goes past you as you gaze through the plate-glass window of your hotel; you see hard working men lifting great burdens; you hear the driving and jostling of huge carts and your heart sinks with a sudden sense of futility. The door opens behind you and you turn to the man who brings you in your breakfast with a quick sense of human fellowship. You find yourself praying that you may never lose your hold on it all. A more poetic prayer would be that the great mother breasts of our common humanity, with its labor and suffering and its homely comforts, may never be withheld from you. You turn helplessly to the waiter and feel that it would be almost grotesque to claim from him the sympathy you crave because civilization has placed you apart, but you resent your position with a sudden sense of snobbery. Literature is full of portrayals of these glimpses: they come to shipwrecked men on rafts; they overcome the differences of an incongruous multitude when in the presence of a great danger or when moved by a common enthusiasm. They are not, however, confined to such moments, and if we were in the habit of telling them to each other, the recital would be as long as the tales of children are, when they sit down on the green grass and confide to each other how many times they have remembered that they lived once before. If these childish tales are the stirring of inherited impressions, just so surely is the other the striving of inherited powers.

===Chinese===

你也许记得那种偶尔袭来的凄惶：清晨，你只身初到一座大城。你隔着旅馆的厚玻璃窗望去，上工的人流从你眼前淌过；你看见卖力气的人扛着巨大的重负；你听见大车的驱赶和碰撞，心猛地一沉，沉进一阵突然的徒劳之感。身后门开了，你朝端早餐进来的人转过身去，一股人间的伙伴之情油然而生。你发觉自己在祈祷：但愿永远不松开对这一切的把握。更诗意一点的祷告是：愿我们共同人性的伟大母怀——它的劳苦、它的苦难、它家常的安慰——永远不向你关闭。你无可奈何地转向侍者，又觉得向他讨要你渴求的同情未免近乎荒唐，因为文明已把你隔开了；可你愤恨自己的地位，忽然觉出其中一股势利味。文学作品里满是这类一瞥的描画：它们降临给木筏上的遇难者；在大危险面前、在同一场热情的激荡下，它们消弥一个驳杂人群的差异。但它们并不限于这样的时刻；倘若我们惯于彼此讲说，那讲述会像孩子们的故事一样长——孩子们坐在绿草地上，彼此交心，说自己想起前世活过的次数有多少回。如果说这些童言是遗传印象的萌动，那么那些一瞥，便确凿是遗传力量的奋起。

===Original===

“It is true that there is nothing after disease, indigence and a sense of guilt, so fatal to health and to life itself as the want of a proper outlet for active faculties.” I have seen young girls suffer and grow sensibly lowered in vitality in the first years after they leave school. In our attempt then to give a girl pleasure and freedom from care we succeed, for the most part, in making her pitifully miserable. She finds “life” so different from what she expected it to be. She is besotted with innocent little ambitions, and does not understand this apparent waste of herself, this elaborate preparation, if no work is provided for her. There is a heritage of noble obligation which young people accept and long to perpetuate. The desire for action, the wish to right wrong and alleviate suffering haunts them daily. Society smiles at it indulgently instead of making it of value to itself. The wrong to them begins even farther back, when we restrain the first childish desires for “doing good,” and tell them that they must wait until they are older and better fitted. We intimate that social obligation begins at a fixed date, forgetting that it begins at birth itself. We treat them as children who, with strong-growing limbs, are allowed to use their legs but not their arms, or whose legs are daily carefully exercised that after a while their arms may be put to high use. We do this in spite of the protest of the best educators, Locke and Pestalozzi. We are fortunate in the meantime if their unused members do not weaken and disappear. They do sometimes. There are a few girls who, by the time they are “educated,” forget their old childish desires to help the world and to play with poor little girls “who haven’t playthings.” Parents are often inconsistent: they deliberately expose their daughters to knowledge of the distress in the world; they send them to hear missionary addresses on famines in India and China; they accompany them to lectures on the suffering in Siberia; they agitate together over the forgotten region of East London. In addition to this, from babyhood the altruistic tendencies of these daughters are persistently cultivated. They are taught to be self-forgetting and self-sacrificing, to consider the good of the whole before the good of the ego.

===Chinese===

「千真万确：除了疾病、穷困和罪疚之感，再没有比活跃的官能找不到正当出路更危害健康乃至生命本身的了。」我见过年轻姑娘们离校后的头几年怎样受苦，生命的活力怎样眼见着低落。我们一心要给姑娘快乐、让她免于操心，结果多半只是把她弄得可怜兮兮地不幸。她发现「生活」同她指望的如此不同。她怀着一脑袋无辜的小小抱负，想不通这对她自己的这场显而易见的浪费——这般精心的预备——若到头来没有一份工作派给她，又是为了什么。年轻人承继着一份高贵义务的遗产，领受它，渴望把它延续下去。行动的欲望、匡正错误减轻苦难的愿望，日日萦绕着他们。社会对它宽容地一笑，却不知借此为自己谋利。对他们的亏欠开始得还要更早：早在我们按住孩子头一桩「做好事」的愿望、叫他们等着长大些、条件好些的时候。我们暗示：社会义务从一个固定的日子才开始，忘了它从出生那一刻就开始了。我们待他们，就像待那种孩子：四肢正长得结实，却只许用腿不许用胳膊；或者天天仔细锻炼他的腿，指望过些日子好让胳膊派上大用场。我们这样做，全不顾最优秀的教育家洛克（Locke）和裴斯泰洛齐（Pestalozzi）的抗议。这些日子里，那些闲置的肢体若不衰弱、不消失，就算我们走运。有时它们真会消失。有少数姑娘，到「受了教育」的时候，已经忘了儿时帮助世界、同「没有玩具的」穷苦小姑娘一起玩的旧愿。做父母的常常自相矛盾：他们存心让女儿接触世上的苦难；送她们去听关于印度和中国饥荒的传教演讲；陪她们去听关于西伯利亚苦难的讲座；一家人为被遗忘的东伦敦地区愤愤不平。不止如此，从襁褓起，这些女儿身上的利他倾向就被孜孜不倦地培育。她们被教得忘我、教得自我牺牲，教得把整体的善放在自我的善之前。

===Original===

But when all this information and culture show results, when the daughter comes back from college and begins to recognize her social claim to the “submerged tenth,” and to evince a disposition to fulfill it, the family claim is strenuously asserted; she is told that she is unjustified, ill-advised in her efforts. If she persists, the family too often are injured and unhappy unless the efforts are called missionary and the religious zeal of the family carry them over their sense of abuse. When this zeal does not exist, the result is perplexing. It is a curious violation of what we would fain believe a fundamental law﻿—that the final return of the deed is upon the head of the doer. The deed is that of exclusiveness and caution, but the return, instead of falling upon the head of the exclusive and cautious, falls upon a young head full of generous and unselfish plans. The girl loses something vital out of her life to which she is entitled. She is restricted and unhappy; her elders meanwhile, are unconscious of the situation and we have all the elements of a tragedy.

===Chinese===

可是，当这满腹的见闻和文化结出果子，当女儿从学院归来，开始认领她对「沉沦的十分之一」的社会债权，并显出要履行它的意思时，家族的债权便被狠狠祭了出来；人家告诉她：她这些努力没有道理，欠考虑。她若坚持，家族多半要受伤、要不快活——除非这些努力被称作传教，靠着一家的宗教热忱，才能咽下这口气。这份热忱若不存在，结局便叫人错愕。这是对一条我们乐于信为根本法则的古怪违犯：行为的最终报应落在行为者自己头上。这里的行为是排外与谨慎的行为，可报应不落在排外者与谨慎者头上，却落在一颗装满慷慨无私的计划的年轻的头上。姑娘失去了她生命中一样有权利得到的东西，一样攸关性命的东西。她受着钳制，郁郁不乐；长辈们却对此毫无觉察——一场悲剧的所有要素，全齐了。

===Original===

We have in America a fast-growing number of cultivated young people who have no recognized outlet for their active faculties. They hear constantly of the great social maladjustment, but no way is provided for them to change it, and their uselessness hangs about them heavily. Huxley declares that the sense of uselessness is the severest shock which the human system can sustain, and that if persistently sustained, it results in atrophy of function. These young people have had advantages of college, of European travel, and of economic study, but they are sustaining this shock of inaction. They have pet phrases, and they tell you that the things that make us all alike are stronger than the things that make us different. They say that all men are united by needs and sympathies far more permanent and radical than anything that temporarily divides them and sets them in opposition to each other. If they affect art, they say that the decay in artistic expression is due to the decay in ethics, that art when shut away from the human interests and from the great mass of humanity is self-destructive. They tell their elders with all the bitterness of youth that if they expect success from them in business or politics or in whatever lines their ambition for them has run, they must let them consult all of humanity; that they must let them find out what the people want and how they want it. It is only the stronger young people, however, who formulate this. Many of them dissipate their energies in so-called enjoyment. Others not content with that, go on studying and go back to college for their second degrees; not that they are especially fond of study, but because they want something definite to do, and their powers have been trained in the direction of mental accumulation. Many are buried beneath this mental accumulation with lowered vitality and discontent. Walter Besant says they have had the vision that Peter had when he saw the great sheet let down from heaven, wherein was neither clean nor unclean. He calls it the sense of humanity. It is not philanthropy nor benevolence, but a thing fuller and wider than either of these.

===Chinese===

在美国，我们有一批增长飞快的有教养的年轻人，他们活跃的官能没有公认的出路。社会的大失调，他们时时听人说起，却没有一条路给他们去改变它；无所用处沉沉地压在他们身上。赫胥黎（Huxley）断言：无用之感是人体所能承受的最严酷的打击；若持续承受，便导致功能的萎缩。这些年轻人享过学院、游欧、经济学的种种好处，此刻却正承受着这无所作为的打击。他们有口头禅。他们告诉你：使我们相像的东西，强于使我们相异的东西。他们说：把一切人联在一起的，是需求与同情——它们远比任何暂时分开人、使人彼此对立的东西更持久、更根本。他们中倾心艺术的说：艺术表现的衰败，源于伦理的衰败；艺术一旦关在人类利益和人类大众之外，便是自我毁灭。他们带着青年的全部辛辣对长辈说：若指望他们在商业、政治、或在长辈为他们铺好的任何一条道上成功，就须容他们请教全人类；须容他们弄清人民要什么、怎么个要法。不过，能把这层意思说成话的，只有较坚强的青年。许多人把精力耗散在所谓的享乐里。另一些不满足于此，接着读、回学院去拿第二个学位——并非特别爱读书，而是想要一件确定的事做，而他们的能力，又只受过去朝知识积累的方向训练。许多人就埋在这知识积累底下，活力低落，心怀不满。沃尔特·贝赞特（Walter Besant）说，他们见过的，正是彼得见过的那个异象：一块大布从天而降，里面洁净的与不洁净的俱有。他称之为人类之感。它不是慈善，也不是仁惠，而是比两者都更丰满、更宽阔的东西。

===Original===

This young life, so sincere in its emotion and good phrases and yet so undirected, seems to me as pitiful as the other great mass of destitute lives. One is supplementary to the other, and some method of communication can surely be devised. Mr. Barnett, who urged the first Settlement﻿—Toynbee Hall, in East London﻿—recognized this need of outlet for the young men of Oxford and Cambridge, and hoped that the Settlement would supply the communication. It is easy to see why the Settlement movement originated in England, where the years of education are more constrained and definite than they are here, where class distinctions are more rigid. The necessity of it was greater there, but we are fast feeling the pressure of the need and meeting the necessity for Settlements in America. Our young people feel nervously the need of putting theory into action, and respond quickly to the Settlement form of activity.

Other motives which I believe make toward the Settlement are the result of a certain renaissance going forward in Christianity. The impulse to share the lives of the poor, the desire to make social service, irrespective of propaganda, express the spirit of Christ, is as old as Christianity itself. We have no proof from the records themselves that the early Roman Christians, who strained their simple art to the point of grotesqueness in their eagerness to record a “good news” on the walls of the catacombs, considered this good news a religion. Jesus had no set of truths labeled “Religious.” On the contrary, his doctrine was that all truth is one, that the appropriation of it is freedom. His teaching had no dogma to mark it off from truth and action in general. He himself called it a revelation﻿—a life. These early Roman Christians received the Gospel message, a command to love all men, with a certain joyous simplicity. The image of the Good Shepherd is blithe and gay beyond the gentlest shepherd of Greek mythology; the hart no longer pants, but rushes to the water brooks. The Christians looked for the continuous revelation, but believed what Jesus said, that this revelation, to be retained and made manifest, must be put into terms of action; that action is the only medium man has for receiving and appropriating truth; that the doctrine must be known through the will.

===Chinese===

这一群年轻的生命，情感和漂亮话都那样真挚，又那样没有方向；在我眼里，同另一大群赤贫的生命一样可怜。一边正补另一边的缺，两者之间，必能想出一种相通的办法。敦促创办第一家定居所——东伦敦的汤因比馆——的巴尼特先生，早看出牛津剑桥的青年有这份对出路的需要，指望定居所来充当这条通路。定居所运动为何发端于英国，不难明白：那里受教育的年头比我们这里更受拘束、更一板一眼，阶级的分野更僵硬。在那边，定居所的必要更迫切；可我们也在飞快地感到这需要的压力，迎上美国对定居所的必要。我们的青年焦灼地感到须把理论化为行动，对定居所这种形式的活动，响应得飞快。

另一些我相信把人推向定居所的动机，来自基督教内部正在推进的一场复兴。分担穷人生活的冲动，使社会服务——不问布道与否——体现基督精神的愿望，同基督教本身一样古老。从记载本身，我们找不到证据说，那些把朴拙的艺术绷到近乎怪诞、急切要把一个「好消息」录在地下墓窟墙上的早期罗马基督徒，把这个好消息当成了一门宗教。耶稣没有一套贴着「宗教」标签的真理。恰恰相反，他的学说是：一切真理是一，得着它便是自由。他的教导没有一条教义，把它同一般的真理和行动划分开来。他自己称它为启示——一个生命。这些早期罗马基督徒以一种欢快的质朴，领受了福音的信息，一条爱一切人的诫命。好牧人的形象，比希腊神话里最温柔的牧人更轻快欢畅；鹿不再喘息干渴，而是直奔溪水。基督徒期待那持续的启示，却相信耶稣的话：这启示要留住、要显明，必须化成行动的语言；行动是人领受真理、取得真理的唯一媒介；教义必须经由意志来认识。

===Original===

That Christianity has to be revealed and embodied in the line of social progress is a corollary to the simple proposition, that man’s action is found in his social relationships in the way in which he connects with his fellows; that his motives for action are the zeal and affection with which he regards his fellows. By this simple process was created a deep enthusiasm for humanity; which regarded man as at once the organ and the object of revelation; and by this process came about the wonderful fellowship, the true democracy of the early Church, that so captivates the imagination. The early Christians were preeminently nonresistant. They believed in love as a cosmic force. There was no iconoclasm during the minor peace of the Church. They did not yet denounce nor tear down temples, nor preach the end of the world. They grew to a mighty number, but it never occurred to them, either in their weakness or in their strength, to regard other men for an instant as their foes or as aliens. The spectacle of the Christians loving all men was the most astounding Rome had ever seen. They were eager to sacrifice themselves for the weak, for children, and for the aged; they identified themselves with slaves and did not avoid the plague; they longed to share the common lot that they might receive the constant revelation. It was a new treasure which the early Christians added to the sum of all treasures, a joy hitherto unknown in the world﻿—the joy of finding the Christ which lieth in each man, but which no man can unfold save in fellowship. A happiness ranging from the heroic to the pastoral enveloped them. They were to possess a revelation as long as life had new meaning to unfold, new action to propose.

I believe that there is a distinct turning among many young men and women toward this simple acceptance of Christ’s message. They resent the assumption that Christianity is a set of ideas which belong to the religious consciousness, whatever that may be. They insist that it cannot be proclaimed and instituted apart from the social life of the community and that it must seek a simple and natural expression in the social organism itself. The Settlement movement is only one manifestation of that wider humanitarian movement which throughout Christendom, but preeminently in England, is endeavoring to embody itself, not in a sect, but in society itself.

===Chinese===

基督教必须在社会进步的路线上启示出来、体现出来——这是一条简单命题的推论：人的行动见于他的社会关系、见于他同同胞相连的方式；他行动的动机，是他看待同胞的那份热忱与情爱。经这道简单的工夫，生出了一种对人类的深沉热情，它把人看作启示的器官，也看作启示的对象；经这道工夫，也成就了那奇妙团契、早期教会的真民主——至今还这样攫住人的想象。早期基督徒最突出的品格是不抵抗。他们信爱是一种宇宙的力。在教会小平安的日子里，没有捣毁圣像的事。他们还不咒骂、还不拆毁神庙，也不宣讲世界末日。他们长成了浩大的一群，可无论弱时强时，他们连一瞬间也不曾想过把旁人看作仇敌或外人。基督徒爱一切人的景象，是罗马从未见过的奇观。他们甘愿为弱者、为孩子、为老人牺牲自己；他们同奴隶认同，不避瘟疫；他们渴望分担共同的命运，好领受那不断的启示。这是早期基督徒添进万般珍宝总账的一件新珍宝，一种世上前所未闻的喜乐——发现那躺卧在每个人里面的基督的喜乐；这基督，唯有在团契里才能展开。一种从英雄气到田园气的幸福笼罩着他们。只要生命还有新意可展、新行可倡，他们便拥有一份启示。

我相信，许多青年男女正明显地转向这番对基督信息素朴的领受。他们愤慨于这样一个假定：基督教是一套观念，属于所谓的宗教意识——随那是什么东西。他们坚持：它不能脱开共同体的社会生活来宣讲、来确立；它必须在社会组织本身之中，寻找一种简单而自然的表达。定居所运动，只是那场更广阔的人道主义运动的一种表现——这场运动遍及整个基督教世界，尤以英国为著，正努力把自己体现出来——不是体现在一个宗派里，而是体现在社会本身之中。

===Original===

I believe that this turning, this renaissance of the early Christian humanitarianism, is going on in America, in Chicago, if you please, without leaders who write or philosophize, without much speaking, but with a bent to express in social service and in terms of action the spirit of Christ. Certain it is that spiritual force is found in the Settlement movement, and it is also true that this force must be evoked and must be called into play before the success of any Settlement is assured. There must be the overmastering belief that all that is noblest in life is common to men as men, in order to accentuate the likenesses and ignore the differences which are found among the people whom the Settlement constantly brings into juxtaposition. It may be true, as the Positivists insist, that the very religious fervor of man can be turned into love for his race, and his desire for a future life into content to live in the echo of his deeds; Paul’s formula of seeking for the Christ which lieth in each man and founding our likenesses on him, seems a simpler formula to many of us.

===Chinese===

我相信，这转向，这早期基督教人道主义的复兴，正在美国进行——就在芝加哥，你若愿意这么说——没有著书立说的领袖，没有多少言说，只有一股心劲，要在社会服务里、在行动的语言里，表达基督的精神。可以确定的是，定居所运动里有精神的力量；同样确实的是：任何一家定居所未获成功之前，这力量必须先被唤起、被调动起来。必须有一种压倒一切的信念：人生中一切最高贵的东西，是人之为人的共同所有——好去突显那些相似、忽略那些差异；定居所正是不断把带着这些异同的人聚到一处。或许真如实证主义者所坚持的：人的宗教热忱可以转成对种族的爱，对来生的渴望可以转成甘愿活在自己事迹的回声里；可保罗的公式——寻索那躺卧在每个人里面的基督，把我们的相似建立在他上面——在我们许多人看来，似乎是一条更简单的公式。

===Original===

In a thousand voices singing the Hallelujah Chorus in Handel’s “Messiah,” it is possible to distinguish the leading voices, but the differences of training and cultivation between them and the voices in the chorus, are lost in the unity of purpose and in the fact that they are all human voices lifted by a high motive. This is a weak illustration of what a Settlement attempts to do. It aims, in a measure, to develop whatever of social life its neighborhood may afford, to focus and give form to that life, to bring to bear upon it the results of cultivation and training; but it receives in exchange for the music of isolated voices the volume and strength of the chorus. It is quite impossible for me to say in what proportion or degree the subjective necessity which led to the opening of Hull House combined the three trends: first, the desire to interpret democracy in social terms; secondly, the impulse beating at the very source of our lives, urging us to aid in the race progress; and, thirdly, the Christian movement toward humanitarianism. It is difficult to analyze a living thing; the analysis is at best imperfect. Many more motives may blend with the three trends; possibly the desire for a new form of social success due to the nicety of imagination, which refuses worldly pleasures unmixed with the joys of self-sacrifice; possibly a love of approbation, so vast that it is not content with the treble clapping of delicate hands, but wishes also to hear the bass notes from toughened palms, may mingle with these.

===Chinese===

一千个人同唱亨德尔《弥赛亚》（"Messiah"）里的哈利路亚大合唱时，领唱的声音分辨得出；可他们同合唱队之间训练与教养的差别，都消融在目标的统一里，消融在「那都是被一个高尚动机托起的人声」这一事实里。这是定居所所想做之事的一个蹩脚的比方。它多少志在于：培植街坊所能提供的那一点社会生活，使它聚焦、赋它以形，把教养和训练的成果施加于它；而它收到的回报，是合唱的音量与力量，换掉了独唱的孤音。促使赫尔馆开办的那份主观必要，究竟以何种比例、何种程度调和了三种趋向——第一，用社会的语言诠释民主的愿望；第二，在我们生命源头搏动、催我们襄助种族进步的冲动；第三，基督教走向人道主义的运动——我实在说不上来。剖析一件活物是难的；剖析得再好也不完全。还有更多的动机可能同这三种趋向相混：也许是一种对新型社会成功的向往，源于想象的细腻，不肯要不掺自我牺牲之乐的世俗享乐；也许是一种对赞许的贪爱，大得不知足于纤手的清脆鼓掌，还想听见老茧巴掌打出的低音——这些也可能掺在其中。

===Original===

The Settlement then, is an experimental effort to aid in the solution of the social and industrial problems which are engendered by the modern conditions of life in a great city. It insists that these problems are not confined to any one portion of a city. It is an attempt to relieve, at the same time, the overaccumulation at one end of society and the destitution at the other; but it assumes that this overaccumulation and destitution is most sorely felt in the things that pertain to social and educational privileges. From its very nature it can stand for no political or social propaganda. It must, in a sense, give the warm welcome of an inn to all such propaganda, if perchance one of them be found an angel. The only thing to be dreaded in the Settlement is that it lose its flexibility, its power of quick adaptation, its readiness to change its methods as its environment may demand. It must be open to conviction and must have a deep and abiding sense of tolerance. It must be hospitable and ready for experiment. It should demand from its residents a scientific patience in the accumulation of facts and the steady holding of their sympathies as one of the best instruments for that accumulation. It must be grounded in a philosophy whose foundation is on the solidarity of the human race, a philosophy which will not waver when the race happens to be represented by a drunken woman or an idiot boy. Its residents must be emptied of all conceit of opinion and all self-assertion, and ready to arouse and interpret the public opinion of their neighborhood. They must be content to live quietly side by side with their neighbors, until they grow into a sense of relationship and mutual interests. Their neighbors are held apart by differences of race and language which the residents can more easily overcome. They are bound to see the needs of their neighborhood as a whole, to furnish data for legislation, and to use their influence to secure it. In short, residents are pledged to devote themselves to the duties of good citizenship and to the arousing of the social energies which too largely lie dormant in every neighborhood given over to industrialism. They are bound to regard the entire life of their city as organic, to make an effort to unify it, and to protest against its over-differentiation.

===Chinese===

那么，定居所就是这样一种实验性的努力：帮助解决大都市现代生活条件所孕育的社会问题和工业问题。它坚持：这些问题并不限于城市的任何一个局部。它试图同时缓解社会一端的过度积聚与另一端的赤贫；而它假定，这过度积聚与赤贫，痛感最深之处，在于那些关乎社会特权与教育特权的东西。凭它的天性，它不能代表任何政治的或社会的主张。在某种意义上，它必须像客栈一样，对一切这类主张敞开暖热的欢迎——万一其中一位竟是天使呢。定居所里唯一可惧的事，是它失掉弹性、失掉迅速应变的能力、失掉随环境要求而改换方法的现成。它必须乐于服理，必须有一种深厚而持久的宽容意识。它必须好客，必须乐于实验。它应当要求住驻者们拿出科学家的耐心来积累事实，并把自己的同情稳定地保持住，作为积累事实的最好工具之一。它必须立定在一种哲学上——这哲学的地基是人类的休戚与共；哪怕这个种族碰巧由一个醉妇或一个痴童来代表，这哲学也不动摇。它的住驻者必须倒空一切意见的自负和一切自行其是，随时准备唤醒并阐释街坊的舆论。他们必须安于同街坊安安静静比邻而居，直到长出一份相关相通、利害与共之感。街坊们被种族和语言的差异隔开，这些差异，住驻者们更容易跨越。他们责无旁贷，要看到整个街区的需要，要为立法提供数据，要用自身的影响促成立法。一句话：住驻者立誓献身于良善公民的本分，献身于唤醒那些社会能量——在每一个交给工业主义的街区，它们大都沉睡着。他们责无旁贷，要把全城的生活看作有机体，努力使之统一，抗议它的过度分化。

===Original===

It is always easy to make all philosophy point one particular moral and all history adorn one particular tale; but I may be forgiven the reminder that the best speculative philosophy sets forth the solidarity of the human race; that the highest moralists have taught that without the advance and improvement of the whole, no man can hope for any lasting improvement in his own moral or material individual condition; and that the subjective necessity for Social Settlements is therefore identical with that necessity, which urges us on toward social and individual salvation.

===Chinese===

叫一切哲学都指向一条特定的寓意、叫一切历史都装点一个特定的故事，从来都容易；但请恕我提醒一句：最好的思辨哲学陈述的，正是人类的休戚与共；最崇高的道德家们教导过：没有整体的进步与改善，任何人也别指望自己个人的道德境况或物质境况得到长久的改善。因此，社会定居所的主观必要，同那催我们走向社会与个人得救的必要，本是同一个。

## VII / 七
===Original===

Some Early Undertakings at Hull House

If the early American Settlements stood for a more exigent standard in philanthropic activities, insisting that each new undertaking should be preceded by carefully ascertained facts, then certainly Hull House held to this standard in the opening of our new coffeehouse first started as a public kitchen. An investigation of the sweatshops had disclosed the fact, that sewing women during the busy season paid little attention to the feeding of their families, for it was only by working steadily through the long day that the scanty pay of five, seven, or nine cents for finishing a dozen pairs of trousers could be made into a day’s wage; and they bought from the nearest grocery the canned goods that could be most quickly heated, or gave a few pennies to the children with which they might secure a lunch from a neighboring candy shop.

One of the residents made an investigation, at the instance of the United States Department of Agriculture, into the food values of the dietaries of the various immigrants, and this was followed by an investigation made by another resident, for the United States Department of Labor, into the foods of the Italian colony, on the supposition that the constant use of imported products bore a distinct relation to the cost of living. I recall an Italian who, coming into Hull House one day as we were sitting at the dinner table, expressed great surprise that Americans ate a variety of food, because he believed that they partook only of potatoes and beer. A little inquiry showed that this conclusion was drawn from the fact that he lived next to an Irish saloon and had never seen anything but potatoes going in and beer coming out.

===Chinese===

赫尔会所的几桩早期事业

如果说美国早期的各睦邻会所代表着慈善活动里一种更严格的标准——坚持每一项新事业都必须以仔细查明的事实为先导——那么在开设我们的新咖啡屋（最初是作为公共厨房创办的）这件事上，赫尔会所确实守住了这个标准。一次对血汗工厂的调查披露：缝衣女工在旺季里几乎无暇顾及一家人的饭食，因为只有整日不停地干活，做一打裤子的五、七或九美分的微薄计件钱才能凑成一日工资；她们从最近的杂货店买那些最快加热的罐头，或者塞给孩子几个铜子，让他们去邻近糖果店换一顿午饭。

一位居民受美国农业部委托，调查了各族移民日常饮食的营养价值；随后另一位居民又受美国劳工部委托，调查意大利侨民聚居区的食品，立论的前提是：持续使用进口食品同生活费用有明确关联。我记得有个意大利人，有一天趁我们围坐饭桌时走进赫尔会所，对美国人吃多种多样的食物表示大为惊讶，因为他原以为美国人只吃土豆、只喝啤酒。稍一询问就真相大白：他住在一爿爱尔兰人酒馆隔壁，只见过土豆运进去、啤酒抬出来，于是得出了这个结论。

===Original===

At that time the New England kitchen was comparatively new in Boston, and Mrs. Richards, who was largely responsible for its foundation, hoped that cheaper cuts of meat and simpler vegetables, if they were subjected to slow and thorough processes of cooking, might be made attractive and their nutritive value secured for the people who so sadly needed more nutritious food. It was felt that this could be best accomplished in public kitchens, where the advantage of scientific training and careful supervision could be secured. One of the residents went to Boston for a training under Mrs. Richards, and when the Hull House kitchen was fitted under her guidance and direction, our hopes ran high for some modification of the food of the neighborhood. We did not reckon, however, with the wide diversity in nationality and inherited tastes, and while we sold a certain amount of the carefully prepared soups and stews in the neighboring factories﻿—a sale which has steadily increased throughout the years﻿—and were also patronized by a few households, perhaps the neighborhood estimate was best summed up by the woman who frankly confessed, that the food was certainly nutritious, but that she didn’t like to eat what was nutritious, that she liked to eat “what she’d ruther.”

If the dietetics were appreciated but slowly, the social value of the coffeehouse and the gymnasium, which were in the same building, were quickly demonstrated. At that time the saloon halls were the only places in the neighborhood where the immigrant could hold his social gatherings, and where he could celebrate such innocent and legitimate occasions as weddings and christenings.

===Chinese===

那时，「新英格兰厨房」在波士顿还算是新事物，对它的创立负主要责任的理查兹夫人希望：较贱部位的肉和较平常的蔬菜，只要经过缓慢而充分的烹煮，也可以变得诱人，其营养价值也能为那些极需更富营养食物的人们所享用。人们认为这件事在公共厨房里最能办成，因为那里有科学训练与严格监管之利。会所派了一位居民去波士顿受教于理查兹夫人门下；赫尔会所的厨房在她的指教下装备起来时，我们对改造街坊的饮食寄予了厚望。然而我们没有料到民族与口味的根性之深。虽然我们在附近工厂里售出了一定数量精心制作的汤和炖菜——这笔买卖逐年稳步增长——也赢得了少数人家的光顾，街坊的评语却恐怕以一位妇女说得最透彻：她坦白承认，这些食物无疑有营养，可她不爱吃有营养的，她爱吃「她宁可吃的」。

如果说营养学被接受得很慢，那么同楼的咖啡屋和健身房的社会价值，却很快显了出来。那时，酒馆的厅堂是这一带移民唯一能举行聚会的地方，也是他能庆祝婚礼、洗礼这类正当而无辜的场合的唯一去处。

===Original===

These halls were rented very cheaply with the understanding that various sums of money should be “passed across the bar,” and it was considered a mean host or guest who failed to live up to this implied bargain. The consequence was that many a reputable party ended with a certain amount of disorder, due solely to the fact that the social instinct was traded upon and used as a basis for money making by an adroit host. From the beginning the young people’s clubs had asked for dancing, and nothing was more popular than the increased space for parties offered by the gymnasium, with the chance to serve refreshments in the room below. We tried experiments with every known “soft drink,” from those extracted from an expensive soda water fountain to slender glasses of grape juice, but so far as drinks were concerned we never became a rival to the saloon, nor indeed did anyone imagine that we were trying to do so. I remember one man who looked about the cozy little room and said, “This would be a nice place to sit in all day if one could only have beer.” But the coffeehouse gradually performed a mission of its own and became something of a social center to the neighborhood as well as a real convenience. Business men from the adjacent factories and school teachers from the nearest public schools, used it increasingly. The Hull House students and club members supped together in little groups or held their reunions and social banquets, as, to a certain extent, did organizations from all parts of the town. The experience of the coffeehouse taught us not to hold to preconceived ideas of what the neighborhood ought to have, but to keep ourselves in readiness to modify and adapt our undertakings as we discovered those things which the neighborhood was ready to accept.

===Chinese===

这些厅堂租金极廉，条件是各种款项须「从吧台上递过去」；哪个主人或宾客不履行这层默契，便被视为小气。结果便是：多少体面的聚会以一场乱子收场，而祸根只在一点——社交的天性被人算计，被精明的主人拿去当了摇钱的由头。青年俱乐部从一开始就要求跳舞；健身房提供了宽敞些的聚会场地，楼下还能备茶点，没有比这更叫座的了。每一种叫得出名目的「软饮料」我们都试过——从昂贵的汽水机里打出来的，到细细一玻璃杯的葡萄汁——但就饮料而论，我们始终没能成为酒馆的对手，其实也从来没有人当真以为我们要抢酒馆的生意。我记得有个男人环顾这间舒适的小屋说：「要是能有啤酒，这儿倒是个能坐一整天的好地方。」但咖啡屋渐渐担起了它自己的使命，既成了这一带某种社交中心，也成了一处真正的便利。附近工厂的生意人、最近几家公立学校的教师，来这里的一天多似一天。赫尔会所的学生和会员们三五成群地在此共进晚餐，或在这里开联欢会、办交谊宴会；全城各处的团体，也在一定程度上如此。咖啡屋的经验教会我们：不要抱着先入之见，认定街坊「应该」要什么；要随时准备修正、变通我们的事业，随着我们发现街坊「乐于」接受什么。

更佳的食物固然需要，更有吸引力、更安全的聚会场所也固然需要；而街坊准备好了接受后者，没有准备好接受前者。那时芝加哥还没有半点后来十五年里将要建立的小公园的影子——那里面有跳舞的厅堂，有自己的饭馆，青年人爱好欢乐与结社的天性，可以在那里安然满足。但即使在那个早期，赫尔会所男子俱乐部的一位会员被委任为道格拉斯公园主管后，就在那里办起了第一座公共游泳池，他的会友们引以为荣。

===Original===

Better food was doubtless needed, but more attractive and safer places for social gatherings were also needed, and the neighborhood was ready for one and not for the other. We had no hint then in Chicago of the small parks which were to be established fifteen years later, containing the halls for dancing and their own restaurants in buildings where the natural desire of the young for gayety and social organization, could be safely indulged. Yet even in that early day a member of the Hull House Men’s Club who had been appointed superintendent of Douglas Park had secured there the first public swimming pool, and his fellow club members were proud of the achievement.

There was in the earliest undertakings at Hull House a touch of the artist’s enthusiasm when he translates his inner vision through his chosen material into outward form. Keenly conscious of the social confusion all about us and the hard economic struggle, we at times believed that the very struggle itself might become a source of strength. The devotion of the mothers to their children, the dread of the men lest they fail to provide for the family dependent upon their daily exertions, at moments seemed to us the secret stores of strength from which society is fed, the invisible array of passion and feeling which are the surest protectors of the world. We fatuously hoped that we might pluck from the human tragedy itself a consciousness of a common destiny which should bring its own healing, that we might extract from life’s very misfortunes a power of cooperation which should be effective against them.

Of course there was always present the harrowing consciousness of the difference in economic condition between ourselves and our neighbors. Even if we had gone to live in the most wretched tenement, there would have always been an essential difference between them and ourselves, for we should have had a sense of security in regard to illness and old age and the lack of these two securities are the specters which most persistently haunt the poor. Could we, in spite of this, make their individual efforts more effective through organization and possibly complement them by small efforts of our own?

===Chinese===

赫尔会所早期的事业里，带着一点艺术家把内心视像经由所选的材料译成外在形制时的那种热忱。我们痛感周遭社会的紊乱和经济斗争的严酷，有时却相信这场斗争本身或许正是力量的源泉。母亲们对孩子的挚爱，男人们唯恐养不活仰赖他们每日劳作的一家的忧惧，在我们看来，某些时刻正是社会赖以滋养的秘密力源，是守护世界最可靠的激情与情感的看不见的阵列。我们痴心妄想地希望：从人类悲剧本身里，能拔出一种共同命运的觉悟，它自带疗愈；从生活的不幸里，能榨出一种协作的力量，足以对抗不幸。

当然，那种揪心的自觉始终在场：我们同邻人经济境况的悬殊。哪怕我们住进最破败的经济公寓，彼此之间也永远有一层本质的分别，因为我们对疾病和老年怀着安全感，而正是这两种安全感的缺失，最执拗地萦绕着穷人的魂灵。尽管如此，我们能不能借组织使他们的个人努力更见成效，或许再以我们自己的微小努力加以补充？

===Original===

Some such vague hope was in our minds when we started the Hull House Cooperative Coal Association, which led a vigorous life for three years, and developed a large membership under the skillful advice of its one paid officer, an English workingman who had had experience in cooperative societies at “ ’ome.” Some of the meetings of the association, in which people met to consider together their basic dependence upon fire and warmth, had a curious challenge of life about them. Because the cooperators knew what it meant to bring forth children in the midst of privation and to see the tiny creatures struggle for life, their recitals cut a cross section, as it were, in that world-old effort﻿—the “dying to live” which so inevitably triumphs over poverty and suffering. And yet their very familiarity with hardship may have been responsible for that sentiment which traditionally ruins business, for a vote of the cooperators that the basket buyers be given one basket free out of every six, that the presentation of five purchase tickets should entitle the holders to a profit in coal instead of stock “because it would be a shame to keep them waiting for the dividend,” was always pointed to by the conservative quarter-of-a-ton buyers as the beginning of the end. At any rate, at the close of the third winter, although the Association occupied an imposing coal yard on the southeast corner of the Hull House block and its gross receipts were between three and four hundred dollars a day, it became evident that the concern could not remain solvent if it continued its philanthropic policy, and the experiment was terminated by the cooperators taking up their stock in the remaining coal.

Our next cooperative experiment was much more successful, perhaps because it was much more spontaneous.

===Chinese===

我们心里怀着某种这样模糊的希望，办起了赫尔会所合作煤业协会。它兴旺地活了三年，在那位唯一受薪干事的老练指导下发展出庞大的会员队伍——他是个英国工人，在「老家」有过合作社的经验。协会的一些集会——人们聚在一起共同思量他们对火与温暖的基本仰赖——有一种奇异的直面人生的气息。因为会员们深知在匮乏中养育儿女、看小小生命挣扎求活是什么滋味，他们的讲述像是给那场古老的努力——那终将战胜贫困与苦难的「向死而生」——切了一道横断面。然而，也许正是他们对苦难的这份熟稔，酿成了那桩传统上毁掉生意的事：会员们表决通过，每买六筐煤赠一筐；凭五张购煤券即可换煤利的分红而不换股金，「因为叫人家等分红，太难为情了」。保守的四分之一吨买主们，始终指着这两条说是末日的开端。无论如何，第三个冬天结束时，协会虽然占据着赫尔会所街区东南角一处气派的煤场，日进毛收入三四百美元，事情已很明显：这买卖若继续执行它的慈善方针，便无从维持资债相抵。试验就此收场，会员们把剩余的煤折成股金分领了事。

我们的下一个合作试验成功得多，也许因为它自发得多。

===Original===

At a meeting of working girls held at Hull House during a strike in a large shoe factory, the discussions made it clear that the strikers who had been most easily frightened, and therefore first to capitulate, were naturally those girls who were paying board and were afraid of being put out if they fell too far behind. After a recital of a case of peculiar hardship one of them exclaimed: “Wouldn’t it be fine if we had a boarding club of our own, and then we could stand by each other in a time like this?” After that events moved quickly. We read aloud together Beatrice Potter’s little book on “Cooperation,” and discussed all the difficulties and fascinations of such an undertaking, and on the first of May, 1891, two comfortable apartments near Hull House were rented and furnished. The Settlement was responsible for the furniture and paid the first month’s rent, but beyond that the members managed the club themselves. The undertaking “marched,” as the French say, from the very first, and always on its own feet. Although there were difficulties, none of them proved insurmountable, which was a matter for great satisfaction in the face of a statement made by the head of the United States Department of Labor, who, on a visit to the club when it was but two years old, said that his department had investigated many cooperative undertakings, and that none founded and managed by women had ever succeeded. At the end of the third year the club occupied all of the six apartments which the original building contained, and numbered fifty members.

It was in connection with our efforts to secure a building for the Jane Club, that we first found ourselves in the dilemma between the needs of our neighbors and the kindhearted response upon which we had already come to rely for their relief. The adapted apartments in which the Jane Club was housed were inevitably more or less uncomfortable, and we felt that the success of the club justified the erection of a building for its sole use.

===Chinese===

一家大制鞋厂罢工期间，赫尔会所召开了一次做工姑娘的会，讨论让大家看清：最先被吓住、因而最先屈服的罢工者，自然是那些要付膳宿费的姑娘——她们怕欠账太多被房东撵出去。听完一桩格外凄惨的讲述后，其中一位喊道：「咱们要有自己的寄宿俱乐部该多好，遇上这种时候就能互相撑住了！」此后事态进展飞快。我们一起朗读比阿特丽斯·波特那本谈「合作」的小书，讨论这项事业的一切难处与迷人处；1891 年 5 月 1 日，赫尔会所附近两套舒适的公寓租下并布置停当。会所担保了家具，付了头一个月的房租，除此之外，俱乐部由会员们自己管理。这项事业从第一天起便如法国人所说「在行进中」，而且始终靠自己的双脚。困难固然有，却没有一样证明是不可逾越的——面对美国劳工部首长的一句话，这尤其令人欣慰：他在这俱乐部两岁时来访，说他的部里调查过许多合作事业，凡由女性创办并管理的，从来没有成功过。第三年年底，俱乐部占满了原楼的全部六套公寓，会员达五十人。

正是在为简俱乐部（Jane Club）筹措一座专用楼房的努力中，我们头一回陷进了两难：一边是邻人的需要，一边是我们已经倚惯了的好心人的慷慨响应。简俱乐部所住的这些改装公寓难免多少不舒适，我们认为俱乐部的成功足以为它建一座专用楼房张目。

===Original===

Up to that time, our history had been as the minor peace of the early Church. We had had the most generous interpretation of our efforts. Of course, many people were indifferent to the idea of the Settlement; others looked on with tolerant and sometimes cynical amusement which we would often encounter in a good story related at our expense; but all this was remote and unreal to us, and we were sure that if the critics could but touch “the life of the people,” they would understand.

The situation changed markedly after the Pullman strike, and our efforts to secure factory legislation later brought upon us a certain amount of distrust and suspicion; until then we had been considered merely a kindly philanthropic undertaking whose new form gave us a certain idealistic glamour. But sterner tests were coming, and one of the first was in connection with the new building for the Jane Club.
