那第一个冬天距亨利·乔治（Henry George）在纽约的竞选不过三年，他的拥护者正在全国推进一场得手而有效的宣传。亨利·乔治本人一个星期日下午来到赫尔馆时，体操馆里早已挤满了来听亨廷顿神父（Father Huntington）讲「自由思想者为何应当信基督」的人，此时这位伟大领袖受到的经久而热烈的掌声几乎使它连地基都在晃动，掌声不断打断他那振奋人心的讲演——同他所有的演说一样，满蓄着高昂的道德热情与人道的义愤。在世界博览会（World's Fair）前后举办的那些著名大会中，也许要数单一税拥护者们发起的几场最富于生命的热情。有一件事也许意味深长：社会科学各科的讨论都得由各派党徒分作独立的团体来组织。连我们那个由芝加哥市民组成的社会科学委员会——我也是其一——都一周一周地改组，因为党派成员伤了感情，嫌他们的事业没有得到「应有的承认」。而在同一座建筑里，东方西方最形形色色的宗教信条的拥护者们却友善亲睦地相聚。这也许说明，他们对人生永恒问题的表述用的是一副更古老、更不敏感的模子，不像这种以社会经验作出的表述；抑或不如说，新的社会科学当时还根本不是一门科学，只是一个名目，我们借它作掩护来讨论工业局势中那些令人困惑的问题？我们委员会的难处当然还因为：当时新兴的社会学尚未划定自己的领域。世博会前一年才开办的芝加哥大学（University of Chicago），是第一所设立社会学系的大型学府。

===Original===

In the meantime the Hull House Social Science Club grew in numbers and fervor as various distinguished people who were visiting the World’s Fair came to address it. I recall a brilliant Frenchwoman who was filled with amazement because one of the shabbiest men reflected a reading of Schopenhauer. She considered the statement of another member most remarkable﻿—that when he saw a carriage driving through the streets occupied by a capitalist who was no longer even an entrepreneur, he felt quite as sure that his days were numbered and that his very lack of function to society would speedily bring him to extinction, as he did when he saw a drunkard reeling along the same street.

The club at any rate convinced the residents that no one so poignantly realizes the failures in the social structure as the man at the bottom, who has been most directly in contact with those failures and has suffered most. I recall the shrewd comments of a certain sailor who had known the disinherited in every country; of a Russian who had served his term in Siberia; of an old Irishman who called himself an atheist but who in moments of excitement always blamed the good Lord for “setting supinely” when the world was so horribly out of joint.

It was doubtless owing largely to this club that Hull House contracted its early reputation for radicalism. Visitors refused to distinguish between the sentiments expressed by its members in the heat of discussion and the opinions held by the residents themselves. At that moment in Chicago the radical of every shade of opinion was vigorous and dogmatic; of the sort that could not resign himself to the slow march of human improvement; of the type who knew exactly “in what part of the world Utopia standeth.”

During this decade Chicago seemed divided into two classes; those who held that “business is business” and who were therefore annoyed at the very notion of social control, and the radicals, who claimed that nothing could be done to really moralize the industrial situation until society should be reorganized.

===Chinese===

其间，随着前来参观世博会的各路名流到社会科学俱乐部讲演，俱乐部的人数与热忱一同增长。我记得一位光彩照人的法国女人，因为一个衣着最寒酸的会员谈吐间显出读过叔本华（Schopenhauer）而惊讶不已。她认为另一位会员的话最了不起——他说，每当看见一辆马车驶过街头，里头坐着一个早已算不上企业家的资本家，他就同看见一个醉汉在同一条街上踉跄而行一样确信：此人的日子屈指可数，他对社会之全无功用很快会把他带向灭绝。

无论如何，这个俱乐部使居民们信服：没有人比社会底层的人更痛切地意识到社会结构中的种种失败，他与这些失败接触最直接，受苦最深。我记得某个精明水手的评论，他在每一个国家都结识过被剥夺者们；一个在西伯利亚服满刑期的俄国人；一个自称无神论者的爱尔兰老人，可一到激动时总怪好上帝在世界乱得如此可怕时还「安然稳坐」。

赫尔馆早年落下的激进名声，无疑大半要归功于这个俱乐部。来访的客人们不肯区分会员们在辩论火头上表露的情绪与居民们自己的主张。那时的芝加哥，每一种色彩的激进派都劲头十足、教条十足；都是那种不甘心人类改善的缓慢进程的人；都是确切知道「乌托邦立在世界哪一方」的那一类人。

那十年间，芝加哥仿佛分成两类人：一类主张「生意就是生意」，因而一听到社会控制的说法就恼火；一类是激进派，声言不把社会重新组织起来，就休想真正使工业局面道德化。

===Original===

A Settlement is above all a place for enthusiasms, a spot to which those who have a passion for the equalization of human joys and opportunities are early attracted. It is this type of mind which is in itself so often obnoxious to the man of conquering business faculty, to whom the practical world of affairs seems so supremely rational that he would never vote to change the type of it even if he could. The man of social enthusiasm is to him an annoyance and an affront. He does not like to hear him talk and considers him per se “unsafe.” Such a business man would admit, as an abstract proposition, that society is susceptible of modification and would even agree that all human institutions imply progressive development, but at the same time he deeply distrusts those who seek to reform existing conditions. There is a certain commonsense foundation for this distrust, for too often the reformer is the rebel who defies things as they are, because of the restraints which they impose upon his individual desires rather than because of the general defects of the system. When such a rebel poses for a reformer, his shortcomings are heralded to the world, and his downfall is cherished as an awful warning to those who refuse to worship “the god of things as they are.”

And yet as I recall the members of this early club, even those who talked the most and the least rationally, seem to me to have been particularly kindly and “safe.” The most pronounced anarchist among them has long since become a convert to a religious sect, holding Buddhistic tenets which imply little food and a distrust of all action; he has become a wraith of his former self but he still retains his kindly smile.

===Chinese===

定居所首先是一个热情的场所，是那些怀着人类欢乐与机会均等之激情的人们早早被吸引来的地方。正是这种心智，本身就往往惹恼那种有征服性商业才干的人；在后者看来，实务的世界如此至尊地合乎理性，即便他能够，他也决不会投票改变它的样式。怀着社会热情的人在他是桩烦恼和冒犯。他不爱听这种人讲话，认为他本身就是「不安全」的。这样的生意人可以承认——作为一个抽象命题——社会是可以修改的，甚至同意一切人类制度都蕴含着进步的发展；但同时他深深不信任那些谋求改革现状的人。这种不信任自有其常识的根基，因为改革者往往其实是叛逆者，他之所以反抗事物的现状，与其说是因为制度有种种一般的缺陷，不如说是因为现状给他个人的欲望加上了种种约束。这样的叛逆者一旦冒充改革者，他的短处便被宣扬于世，他的垮台被珍视为对一切拒绝礼拜「事物现状之神」的人的可怕鉴戒。

然而我回想这个早期俱乐部的会员们，连那些讲得最多、讲得最不讲理的，在我看来都格外和善、「安全」。他们中间最旗帜鲜明的无政府主义者，早已皈依了一个宗教派别，信持着佛教味的教义——少食，不信任一切行动。他已成了从前自己的一个幽灵，但那副和善的笑容还留着。

===Original===

In the discussion of these themes, Hull House was of course quite as much under the suspicion of one side as the other. I remember one night when I addressed a club of secularists, which met at the corner of South Halsted and Madison streets, a rough-looking man called out: “You are all right now, but, mark my words, when you are subsidized by the millionaires, you will be afraid to talk like this.” The defense of free speech was a sensitive point with me, and I quickly replied that while I did not intend to be subsidized by millionaires, neither did I propose to be bullied by workingmen, and that I should state my honest opinion without consulting either of them. To my surprise, the audience of radicals broke into applause, and the discussion turned upon the need of resisting tyranny wherever found, if democratic institutions were to endure. This desire to bear independent witness to social righteousness often resulted in a sense of compromise difficult to endure, and at many times it seemed to me that we were destined to alienate everybody. I should have been most grateful at that time to accept the tenets of socialism, and I conscientiously made my effort, both by reading and by many discussions with the comrades. I found that I could easily give an affirmative answer to the heated question “Don’t you see that just as the hand mill created a society with a feudal lord, so the steam mill creates a society with an industrial capitalist?” But it was a little harder to give an affirmative reply to the proposition that the social relation thus established proceeds to create principles, ideas and categories as merely historical and transitory products.

Of course I use the term “socialism” technically and do not wish to confuse it with the growing sensitiveness which recognizes that no personal comfort, nor individual development can compensate a man for the misery of his neighbors, nor with the increasing conviction that social arrangements can be transformed through man’s conscious and deliberate effort. Such a definition would not have been accepted for a moment by the Russians, who then dominated the socialist party in Chicago and among whom a crude interpretation of the class conflict was the test of faith.

===Chinese===

在这些题目的讨论中，赫尔馆自然一面受猜疑，另一面也同样受猜疑。我记得一天夜里，我在南哈尔斯特德街与麦迪逊街（South Halsted and Madison streets）拐角一个世俗主义者俱乐部讲演，一个面目粗犷的男人喊道：「你现在还没事，可记住我的话，等百万富翁们给你津贴，你就不敢这么讲了。」捍卫言论自由是我的一个敏感处，我立刻回答：我不打算受百万富翁津贴，也同样不打算受工人们威吓，我要不请教他们任何一方，照样说出自己诚实的意见。出乎我的意料，满场的激进派鼓起掌来，讨论随即转到：民主制度若要长存，就必须反抗任何地方的暴政。这种要为社会正义作独立见证的愿望，常常造成一种难以忍受的妥协感，有许多次我觉得我们注定要把所有人都疏远掉。那时若能接受社会主义的信条，我本会感激不尽；我也确实认真下过功夫，靠阅读，也靠与同志们的多次讨论。对那个火辣辣的问题——「你难道看不出，正如手推磨造就了有封建领主的社会，蒸汽磨也造就了有工业资本家的社会吗？」——我发现自己满可以轻易地答一声「是」。可是要给另一个命题也答一声「是」就难了些：由此确立的社会关系进而创造出原则、观念和范畴，而它们只是历史的、暂时的产物。

当然，我是在技术意义上使用「社会主义」这个词的，不想把它同那种日益增长的敏感混为一谈——那敏感承认，任何个人的舒适或个人的发展，都补偿不了邻人的苦难；也不把它同那种日益增强的信念混为一谈——社会安排可以经由人自觉而审慎的努力得到改造。这样的定义，当时把持着芝加哥社会党的俄国人是一刻也不会接受的；在他们中间，一种粗陋的阶级斗争解释便是信仰的试金石。

===Original===

During those first years on Halsted Street nothing was more painfully clear than the fact that pliable human nature is relentlessly pressed upon by its physical environment. I saw nowhere a more devoted effort to understand and relieve that heavy pressure than the socialists were making, and I should have been glad to have had the comradeship of that gallant company had they not firmly insisted that fellowship depends upon identity of creed. They repudiated similarity of aim and social sympathy as tests which were much too loose and wavering as they did that vague socialism which for thousands has come to be a philosophy or rather religion embodying the hope of the world and the protection of all who suffer.

I also longed for the comfort of a definite social creed, which should afford at one and the same time an explanation of the social chaos and the logical steps towards its better ordering. I came to have an exaggerated sense of responsibility for the poverty in the midst of which I was living and which the socialists constantly forced me to defend. My plight was not unlike that which might have resulted in my old days of skepticism regarding foreordination, had I then been compelled to defend the confusion arising from the clashing of free wills as an alternative to an acceptance of the doctrine. Another difficulty in the way of accepting this economic determinism, so baldly dependent upon the theory of class consciousness, constantly arose when I lectured in country towns and there had opportunities to read human documents of prosperous people as well as those of my neighbors who were crowded into the city. The former were stoutly unconscious of any classes in America, and the class consciousness of the immigrants was fast being broken into by the necessity for making new and unprecedented connections in the industrial life all about them.

===Chinese===

在哈尔斯特德街（Halsted Street）度过的那些最初年头里，没有什么比这个事实更令人痛切地明白：柔韧的人性正被它的物质环境无情地挤压。我在任何地方都看不到比社会主义者们正在做出的更专注的努力，去理解并缓解那沉重的压力；倘若他们不坚决主张情谊取决于信条的同一，我本会乐于与那一彪勇敢的人马为伍。他们拒绝把目标相近与社会同情当作标准，嫌那标准太松、太摇摆，正如他们拒绝那种对千万人来说已成了一种哲学、毋宁说是宗教的模糊社会主义——体现着世界的希望和对一切受苦者的护佑。

我也渴望一种明确的社会信条所带来的安慰，它应当一举既解释社会的混乱，又给出通往较好秩序的逻辑步骤。我渐渐对身陷其中的贫穷生出一种夸大了的责任感，而社会主义者们又不断逼我为这贫穷辩护。我的处境，颇似我早年怀疑宿命论的日子里、倘若有人逼我把自由意志相互冲撞所致的混乱当作接受该教义的替代而加以辩护时会陷入的境地。接受这种经济决定论的另一个障碍——它赤裸裸地依赖阶级意识学说——在我到乡镇讲演、因而有机会阅读兴旺人家同我那些挤在城里的邻居一样的人的文献时，不断出现。前者死活不承认美国有什么阶级，而移民们的阶级意识，正在被他们在四周工业生活中建立新的、空前的联系的必要性迅速打破。

===Original===

In the meantime, although many men of many minds met constantly at our conferences, it was amazing to find the incorrigible good nature which prevailed. Radicals are accustomed to hot discussion and sharp differences of opinion and take it all in the day’s work. I recall that the secretary of the Hull House Social Science Club at the anniversary of the seventh year of its existence read a report in which he stated that, so far as he could remember, but twice during that time had a speaker lost his temper, and in each case it had been a college professor who “wasn’t accustomed to being talked back to.”

He also added that but once had all the club members united in applauding the same speaker; only Samuel Jones, who afterwards became the “golden rule” mayor of Toledo, had been able to overcome all their dogmatic differences, when he had set forth a plan of endowing a group of workingmen with a factory plant and a working capital for experimentation in hours and wages, quite as groups of scholars are endowed for research.

Chicago continued to devote much time to economic discussion and remained in a state of youthful glamour throughout the nineties. I recall a young Methodist minister who, in order to free his denomination from any entanglement in his discussion of the economic and social situation, moved from his church building into a neighboring hall. The congregation and many other people followed him there, and he later took to the street corners because he found that the shabbiest men liked that best. Professor Herron filled to overflowing a downtown hall every noon with a series of talks entitled “Between Caesar and Jesus”﻿—an attempt to apply the teachings of the Gospel to the situations of modern commerce. A half dozen publications edited with some ability and much moral enthusiasm have passed away, perhaps because they represented pamphleteering rather than journalism and came to a natural end when the situation changed. Certainly their editors suffered criticism and poverty on behalf of the causes which they represented.

===Chinese===

其间，虽然许多持许多种见解的人不断在我们的会议上相聚，弥漫全场的却是一种不可救药的好脾气，令人称奇。激进派惯于热烈的讨论和尖锐的分歧，把这一切都当作分内之事。我记得赫尔馆社会科学俱乐部的书记，在俱乐部成立七周年时宣读一份报告，其中说，据他记忆所及，那些年里讲演人只发过两次脾气，而每次都是一位「不习惯被人回嘴」的大学教授。

他还补充说，只有过一次全体会员一致为同一位讲演人鼓掌；唯有塞缪尔·琼斯（Samuel Jones）——后来的托莱多（Toledo）「金律市长」——曾克服他们全部的教条分歧，当时他提出一项计划：给一群工人配备一座工厂和一笔运营资本，供他们在工时与工资上作实验，正如学者群体受资助作研究一样。

整个九十年代，芝加哥继续把大量时间用于经济讨论，始终保持着一种青春的光彩。我记得一位年轻的卫理公会牧师，为了使他的教派不致缠进他对经济与社会局势的讨论，从教堂搬进了邻近的一座会堂。会众和许多别的人跟到那里，后来他索性走上街角，因为他发现衣着最寒酸的人最喜欢那样。赫伦教授（Professor Herron）每天中午把市中心一座会堂挤得水泄不通，讲一个题为「在恺撒与耶稣之间」的系列——试图把福音书的教导应用到现代商业的种种境况上去。半打编得颇有才具、满含道德热情的刊物都已消逝，也许因为它们代表的是小册子笔仗而非新闻事业，时局一变便自然告终。它们的编辑们无疑为他们所代表的事业受过批评、挨过贫穷。

===Original===

Trades-unionists, unless they were also socialists, were not prominent in those economic discussions, although they were steadily making an effort to bring order into the unnecessary industrial confusion. They belonged to the second of the two classes into which Mill divides all those who are dissatisfied with human life as it is, and whose feelings are wholly identified with its radical amendment. He states that the thoughts of one class are in the region of ultimate aims, of “the highest ideals of human life,” while the thoughts of the other are in the region of the “immediately useful, and practically attainable.”

The meetings of our Social Science Club were carried on by men of the former class, many of them with a strong religious bias who constantly challenged the Church to assuage the human spirit thus torn and bruised “in the tumult of a time disconsolate.” These men were so serious in their demand for religious fellowship, and several young clergymen were so ready to respond to the appeal, that various meetings were arranged at Hull House, in which a group of people met together to consider the social question, not in a spirit of discussion, but in prayer and meditation. These clergymen were making heroic efforts to induce their churches to formally consider the labor situation, and during the years which have elapsed since then, many denominations of the Christian Church have organized labor committees; but at that time there was nothing of the sort beyond the society in the established Church of England “to consider the conditions of labor.”

===Chinese===

工会会员们，除非同时也是社会主义者，在这些经济讨论中并不出头，尽管他们正稳步努力给不必要的工业混乱带来秩序。他们属于穆勒（Mill）所划分的两类人中的第二类——凡对人生现状不满、其情感完全系于人生之彻底改良的人，穆勒把他们分作两类。他说，前一类的思想处于终极目的的地带，处于「人生的最高理想」之中；后一类的思想则处于「直接有用的、实际可得的」地带。

我们社会科学俱乐部的集会是由前一类人主持的，其中许多人怀着强烈的宗教偏向，不断挑战教会去抚慰那在「令人怅惘的时代喧嚣中」被撕裂、被擦伤的人的心灵。这些人对宗教情谊的要求如此恳切，几位年轻教士又如此乐于响应这一呼吁，于是赫尔馆安排了多次集会，一群人聚在一起考虑社会问题，不是以讨论的精神，而是以祈祷与默想。这些教士们正做着英雄式的努力，促使他们的教会正式考虑劳工局势；自那时以来，基督教会的许多教派都已组织了劳工委员会；但在当时，除英国国教里那个「考虑劳动状况」的社团之外，尚无此类组织。

===Original===

During that decade even the most devoted of that pioneer church society failed to formulate the fervid desire for juster social conditions into anything more convincing than a literary statement, and the Christian Socialists, at least when the American branch held its annual meeting at Hull House, afforded but a striking portrayal of that “between-age mood” in which so many of our religious contemporaries are forced to live. I remember that I received the same impression when I attended a meeting called by the canon of an English cathedral to discuss the relation of the Church to labor. The men quickly indicted the cathedral for its uselessness, and the canon asked them what in their minds should be its future. The men promptly replied that any new social order would wish, of course, to preserve beautiful historic buildings, that although they would dismiss the bishop and all the clergy, they would want to retain one or two scholars as custodians and interpreters. “And what next?” the imperturbable ecclesiastic asked. “We would democratize it,” replied the men. But when it came to a more detailed description of such an undertaking, the discussion broke down into a dozen bits, although illuminated by much shrewd wisdom and affording a clue, perhaps as to the destruction of the bishop’s palace by the citizens of this same town, who had attacked it as a symbol of swollen prosperity during the bread riots of the earlier part of the century.

===Chinese===

在那十年里，连那个先锋教会团体中最热忱的人，也未能把对更公正社会状况的炽热愿望表述成比一纸文学声明更有说服力的东西；而基督教社会主义者们——至少当美国分部在赫尔馆举行年会时——只是提供了一幅触目的写照，画出我们许多宗教同代人被迫生活于其中的那种「中间时代的心境」。我记得我出席一次由一座英国座堂的法政牧师（canon）召集、讨论教会与劳工关系的会议时，得到过同样的印象。人们很快指控座堂无用，那位牧师问他们，在他们的心目中它应当有怎样的将来。人们立刻回答：任何新的社会秩序当然都愿意保存美丽的历史建筑；他们虽然会辞退主教和全体教士，却愿意留下一两位学者作看守人和阐释者。「然后呢？」这位不动声色的教士问。「我们要把它民主化，」人们回答。可是一谈到这样一项事业的更具体的描绘，讨论便散作十几片碎块，尽管其间闪烁着许多精明的智慧，也提供了一条线索——也许可以说明本城的市民们何以在本世纪早些年的面包暴动中捣毁主教宫，他们曾把它当作臃肿的繁荣的象征来攻击。

===Original===

On the other hand the workingmen who continue to demand help from the Church thereby acknowledge their kinship, as does the son who continues to ask bread from the father who gives him a stone. I recall an incident connected with a prolonged strike in Chicago on the part of the typographical unions for an eight-hour day. The strike had been conducted in a most orderly manner and the union men, convinced of the justice of their cause, had felt aggrieved because one of the religious publishing houses in Chicago had constantly opposed them. Some of the younger clergymen of the denominations who were friendly to the strikers’ cause came to a luncheon at Hull House, where the situation was discussed by the representatives of all sides. The clergymen, becoming much interested in the idealism with which an officer of the State Federation of Labor presented the cause, drew from him the story of his search for fraternal relation: he said that at fourteen years of age he had joined a church, hoping to find it there; he had later become a member of many fraternal organizations and mutual benefit societies, and, although much impressed by their rituals, he was disappointed in the actual fraternity. He had finally found, so it seemed to him, in the cause of organized labor, what these other organizations had failed to give him﻿—an opportunity for sacrificial effort.

Chicago thus took a decade to discuss the problems inherent in the present industrial organization and to consider what might be done, not so much against deliberate aggression as against brutal confusion and neglect; quite as the youth of promise passed through a mist of rose-colored hope before he settles in the land of achievement where he becomes all too dull and literal minded. And yet as I hastily review the decade in Chicago which followed this one given over to discussion, the actual attainment of these early hopes, so far as they have been realized at all, seem to have come from men of affairs rather than from those given to speculation. Was the whole decade of discussion an illustration of that striking fact which has been likened to the changing of swords in Hamlet; that the abstract minds at length yield to the inevitable or at least grow less ardent in their propaganda, while the concrete minds, dealing constantly with daily affairs, in the end demonstrate the reality of abstract notions?

===Chinese===

另一方面，继续向教会求助的工人们由此承认了这份亲缘，正如那继续向给他石头的父亲要饼的儿子一样。我记得芝加哥排字工各工会为八小时工作日长期罢工期间的一件事。罢工进行得极有秩序，工会会员们自信事业正当，却因芝加哥一家宗教出版机构不断反对他们而心怀委屈。几个对罢工者的事业持友好态度的教派的年轻教士，来赫尔馆赴一次午餐会，各方代表在席间讨论局势。教士们对州劳工联合会（State Federation of Labor）一位官员陈述事业时所表现的理想主义大感兴趣，引出了他寻求兄弟情谊的故事：他说他十四岁上加入过一座教会，希望在那里找到它；后来又加入过许多友爱组织和互济会，虽然对它们的仪式印象颇深，却在实际的兄弟情谊上失望了。他终于——在他看来——在有组织的劳工事业中找到了别的组织未能给他的东西：一个作出牺牲性努力的机会。

芝加哥就这样用了十年时间讨论现行工业组织固有的种种问题，考虑能做些什么——与其说是对付蓄意的侵凌，不如说是对付粗暴的混乱与忽视；正像一个有出息的青年在定居到成就之邦、变得过分迟钝刻板以前，要先穿过一片玫瑰色希望的雾。然而当我匆匆回顾芝加哥继这讨论的十年之后的十年，这些早期希望的实际成就——就其确已实现者而言——似乎得自实务家而非耽于思辨的人。这整十年的讨论，是不是正例证了那个被人比作《哈姆雷特》中换剑的惊人事实：抽象的心智终于向必不可免者让步，或至少在宣传上不那么热烈了，而具体的心智，终日与日常事务打交道，最终实证了抽象观念的现实性？

===Original===

I remember when Frederick Harrison visited Hull House that I was much disappointed to find that the Positivists had not made their ardor for humanity a more potent factor in the English social movement, as I was surprised during a visit from John Morley to find that he, representing perhaps the type of man whom political life seemed to have pulled away from the ideals of his youth, had yet been such a champion of democracy in the full tide of reaction. My observations were much too superficial to be of value and certainly both men were well grounded in philosophy and theory of social reform and had long before carefully formulated their principles, as the new English Labor Party, which is destined to break up the reactionary period, is now being created by another set of theorists. There were certainly moments during the heated discussions of this decade when nothing seemed so important as right theory: this was borne in upon me one brilliant evening at Hull House when Benjamin Kidd, author of the much-read *Social Evolution*, was pitted against Victor Berger of Milwaukee, even then considered a rising man in the Socialist Party.

At any rate the residents of Hull House discovered that while their first impact with city poverty allied them to groups given over to discussion of social theories, their sober efforts to heal neighborhood ills allied them to general public movements which were without challenging creeds. But while we discovered that we most easily secured the smallest of much-needed improvements by attaching our efforts to those of organized bodies, nevertheless these very organizations would have been impossible, had not the public conscience been aroused and the community sensibility quickened by these same ardent theorists.

===Chinese===

我记得弗雷德里克·哈里森（Frederick Harrison）访问赫尔馆时，我很失望地发现实证主义者们没有使他们对人道的热忱成为英国社会运动中一股更有力的因素；同样，约翰·莫利（John Morley）来访时我惊讶地发现，他也许代表着被政治生活从青年理想上拖开的那一类人，却曾在反动的高潮中做过民主的一名斗士。我的观察太肤浅，不足为凭；何况两人都植根于哲学与社会改革的理论，早已仔细表述过自己的原则——正如注定要打破反动时期的新英国劳工党，此刻正由另一批理论家缔造着。在那十年激烈讨论的某些时刻，确实没有什么比正确的理论更要紧：赫尔馆一个晴朗的晚上，我痛感这一点——那晚，《社会进化》（*Social Evolution*）的作者、拥有广大读者的本杰明·基德（Benjamin Kidd）与密尔沃基的维克托·伯杰（Victor Berger）对垒，后者当时已被视为社会党里一个正在升起的人物。

无论如何，赫尔馆的居民们发现：他们与城市贫穷的第一次撞击，使他们同那些专心讨论社会理论的团体结成了同盟；而他们医治邻里疾苦的踏实努力，使他们同没有挑衅性信条的一般公众运动结成了同盟。不过我们也发现，把我们的努力挂靠到有组织的团体的努力上，最容易赢得哪怕最小的急需改善；然而正是这些组织，倘若不是这些热心的理论家唤醒了公众良心、擦亮了社会的敏感，原本是不可能存在的。

===Original===

As I review these very first impressions of the workers in unskilled industries, living in a depressed quarter of the city, I realize how easy it was for us to see exceptional cases of hardship as typical of the average lot, and yet, in spite of alleviating philanthropy and labor legislation, the indictment of Tolstoy applied to Moscow thirty years ago still fits every American city: “Wherever we may live, if we draw a circle around us of a hundred thousand, or a thousand, or even of ten miles circumference, and look at the lives of those men and women who are inside our circle, we shall find half-starved children, old people, pregnant women, sick and weak persons, working beyond their strength, who have neither food nor rest enough to support them, and who, for this reason, die before their time; we shall see others, full grown, who are injured and needlessly killed by dangerous and hurtful tasks.”

As the American city is awakening to self-consciousness, it slowly perceives the civic significance of these industrial conditions, and perhaps Chicago has been foremost in the effort to connect the unregulated overgrowth of the huge centers of population, with the astonishingly rapid development of industrial enterprises; quite as Chicago was foremost to carry on the preliminary discussion through which a basis was laid for like-mindedness and the coordination of diverse wills. I remember an astute English visitor, who had been a guest in a score of American cities, observed that it was hard to understand the local pride he constantly encountered; for in spite of the boasting on the part of leading citizens in the western, eastern, and southern towns, all American cities seemed to him essentially alike and all equally the results of an industry totally unregulated by well-considered legislation.

===Chinese===

如今回顾我对这些非熟练行业工人的最初印象——他们住在城市里一个萧条的街区——我明白，我们多么容易把特殊的苦难看作寻常命运的典型；然而尽管有缓解性的慈善与劳工立法，托尔斯泰（Tolstoy）三十年前加于莫斯科的控诉仍然切合每一座美国城市：「无论我们住在哪里，只要在我们周围画一个十万人的圈子，或是一千人，甚至方圆十英里的圈子，看看圈子里面那些男人女人的生活，我们就会看见半饥半饱的孩子、老人、孕妇、病弱的人，做着超出体力的工作，既没有足以支撑他们的食物，也没有足够的休息，因此未老先死；我们还会看见另一些成年的人，被危险而有害的工作弄伤残、无谓地断送性命。」

随着美国城市渐渐觉醒到自我意识，它慢慢察觉这些工业状况的市政意义；也许在把人口巨中心的失控的过度增长与工业企业的惊人快速发展联系起来的努力上，芝加哥走在最前；正如在开展那场初步讨论、为同心同德与多样意志的协调奠基上，芝加哥也走在最前。我记得一位精明的英国访客，他在二十来座美国城市做过客；他说他很难理解他不断遇到的那种地方自豪感；因为尽管西部、东部、南部的头面市民们自夸不休，在他看来，所有美国城市本质上都彼此相像，全都同样是全然未经深思熟虑的立法加以规范的工业所结出的结果。

===Original===

I am inclined to think that perhaps all this general discussion was inevitable in connection with the early Settlements, as they in turn were the inevitable result of theories of social reform, which in their full enthusiasm reached America by way of England, only in the last decade of the century. There must have been tough fiber somewhere; for, although the residents of Hull House were often baffled by the radicalism within the Social Science Club and harassed by the criticism from outside, we still continued to believe that such discussion should be carried on, for if the Settlement seeks its expression through social activity, it must learn the difference between mere social unrest and spiritual impulse.

The group of Hull House residents, which by the end of the decade comprised twenty-five, differed widely in social beliefs, from the girl direct from the country who looked upon all social unrest as mere anarchy, to the resident, who had become a socialist when a student in Zurich, and who had long before translated from the German Engel’s *Conditions of the Working Class in England*, although at this time she had been read out of the Socialist Party because the Russian and German Impossibilists suspected her fluent English, as she always lightly explained. Although thus diversified in social beliefs, the residents became solidly united through our mutual experience in an industrial quarter, and we became not only convinced of the need for social control and protective legislation but also of the value of this preliminary argument.

This decade of discussion between 1890 and 1900 already seems remote from the spirit of Chicago of today. So far as I have been able to reproduce this earlier period, it must reflect the essential provisionality of everything; “the perpetual moving on to something future which shall supersede the present,” that paramount impression of life itself, which affords us at one and the same time, ground for despair and for endless and varied anticipation.

===Chinese===

我倾向于认为，这一切泛泛的讨论与早期定居所的联系也许是不可避免的，正如定居所本身又是社会改革种种理论不可避免的结果——那些理论在满怀的热忱中，于本世纪最后一个十年方才取道英国抵达美国。这里头某处必有坚韧的纤维；因为赫尔馆的居民虽然常被社会科学俱乐部内部的激进主义所窘，又被外界的批评所扰，我们仍然相信这样的讨论应当继续下去；因为定居所既然要在社会活动中寻求它的表达，它就必须学会分辨单纯的社会不安与精神的冲动。

到那十年之末已增至二十五人的赫尔馆居民群体，社会信念分歧很大：从把一切社会不安都看作无非无政府状态的、刚从乡下来的姑娘，到在苏黎世（Zurich）做学生时便成了社会主义者、早已从德文翻译过恩格斯《英国工人阶级状况》（*Conditions of the Working Class in England*）的那位居民——虽然这时她已被社会党除名，因为俄国和德国的不可能派疑心她那口流利的英语，她总这样轻轻带过。社会信念尽管如此多样，居民们却因我们在一个工业街区的共同经历而牢固地团结在一起；我们不仅确信社会控制与保护性立法的必要，也确信这场先行论证的价值。

1890到1900这讨论的十年，在今天芝加哥的精神面前已显得遥远。就我所能重现的这个较早时期而言，它必定反映出一切事物本质上都是暂时的；「永远向着某个行将取代现在的未来移动」，那是生命本身给我们的至深印象，它同时给了我们绝望的根据，也给了我们无穷而多样的期待。

## X / 十
===Original===

Pioneer Labor Legislation in Illinois

Our very first Christmas at Hull House, when we as yet knew nothing of child labor, a number of little girls refused the candy which was offered them as part of the Christmas good cheer, saying simply that they “worked in a candy factory and could not bear the sight of it.” We discovered that for six weeks they had worked from seven in the morning until nine at night, and they were exhausted as well as satiated. The sharp consciousness of stern economic conditions was thus thrust upon us in the midst of the season of good will.

During the same winter three boys from a Hull House club were injured at one machine in a neighboring factory for lack of a guard which would have cost but a few dollars. When the injury of one of these boys resulted in his death, we felt quite sure that the owners of the factory would share our horror and remorse, and that they would do everything possible to prevent the recurrence of such a tragedy. To our surprise they did nothing whatever, and I made my first acquaintance then with those pathetic documents signed by the parents of working children, that they will make no claim for damages resulting from “carelessness.”

Sweatshop workers.

The visits we made in the neighborhood constantly discovered women sewing upon sweatshop work, and often they were assisted by incredibly small children. I remember a little girl of four who pulled out basting threads hour after hour, sitting on a stool at the feet of her Bohemian mother, a little bunch of human misery. But even for that there was no legal redress, for the only child-labor law in Illinois, with any provision for enforcement, had been secured by the coal miners’ unions, and was confined to children employed in mines.

===Chinese===

伊利诺伊州早期劳动立法

我们在赫尔会所过的头一个圣诞节，那时我们对童工还一无所知，好些小女孩拒绝了当作圣诞喜气分给她们的糖果，只简单地说她们「在糖果厂做工，见了它就受不了」。我们发现，六个星期里她们从早上七点一直做到晚上九点，人既累坏也吃腻了。就这样，在友善的季节当中，严酷经济状况的尖锐意识硬塞到了我们眼前。

同一个冬天，赫尔会所一个俱乐部的三个男孩，因为邻近一家工厂一台机器上缺一个只值几块钱的防护罩而受了伤。其中一个男孩因伤死去之后，我们满以为厂主们会同我们一样惊骇悔恨，会千方百计防止这样的惨剧重演。出乎意料，他们什么也没有做；我那时头一回见到那种可怜的文件——做工孩子的父母签字画押，声明对「疏忽」造成的伤害概不索赔。

血汗作坊的工人。

我们在街坊里串门，不断发现女人在做血汗作坊的活，帮手的常常是小得叫人不敢相信的孩子。我记得一个四岁的小姑娘，一小时又一小时地抽着疏缝线，坐在她波希米亚母亲的脚边一张小凳上，一小团人间的苦楚。可就连这个也没有法律救济的办法，因为伊利诺伊州唯一带有执行条款的童工法，是煤矿工人的工会争来的，只适用于矿上雇用的孩子。

===Original===

We learned to know many families in which the working children contributed to the support of their parents, not only because they spoke English better than the older immigrants and were willing to take lower wages, but because their parents gradually found it easy to live upon their earnings. A South Italian peasant who has picked olives and packed oranges from his toddling babyhood cannot see at once the difference between the outdoor healthy work which he had performed in the varying seasons, and the long hours of monotonous factory life which his child encounters when he goes to work in Chicago. An Italian father came to us in great grief over the death of his eldest child, a little girl of twelve, who had brought the largest wages into the family fund. In the midst of his genuine sorrow he said: “She was the oldest kid I had. Now I shall have to go back to work again until the next one is able to take care of me.” The man was only thirty-three and had hoped to retire from work at least during the winters. No foreman cared to have him in a factory, untrained and unintelligent as he was. It was much easier for his bright, English-speaking little girl to get a chance to paste labels on a box than for him to secure an opportunity to carry pig iron. The effect on the child was what no one concerned thought about, in the abnormal effort she made thus prematurely to bear the weight of life. Another little girl of thirteen, a Russian-Jewish child employed in a laundry at a heavy task beyond her strength, committed suicide, because she had borrowed three dollars from a companion which she could not repay unless she confided the story to her parents and gave up an entire week’s wages﻿—but what could the family live upon that week in case she did! Her child mind, of course, had no sense of proportion, and carbolic acid appeared inevitable.

While we found many pathetic cases of child labor and hard-driven victims of the sweating system who could not possibly earn enough in the short busy season to support themselves during the rest of the year, it became evident that we must add carefully collected information to our general impression of neighborhood conditions if we would make it of any genuine value.

===Chinese===

我们认识了许许多多这样的人家：做工的孩子赡养着父母——不只因为他们英语说得比年长的移民好、又肯拿更低的工钱，还因为他们的父母渐渐发现，靠孩子的工钱过日子很容易。一个南意大利农民，从蹒跚学步起就摘橄榄、装橙子，一下子看不出两样的分别：他自己在四季流转里做的是户外健康的活，而他的孩子到芝加哥进厂做工，遇到的是单调乏味的漫长工时。一位意大利父亲来找我们，为大孩子的死悲恸不已——那是个十二岁的小姑娘，往家里的钱罐里带进的工钱最多。他真心悲痛之中却说：「她是我最大的一个孩子。如今我只得再回去做工，做到下一个能养活我的时候。」这人不过三十三岁，原指望至少冬天能歇工。他没受过训练，人又不灵光，没有哪个工头愿意要他进厂。他那伶俐会讲英语的小姑娘要谋一个往盒子上贴标签的机会，比他谋一个扛生铁的机会容易得多。孩子身上落下的后果——她那样早熟地拼命挑起生活的重担——却是所有相关的人谁也没有想过的。另一个十三岁的小姑娘，一个俄裔犹太孩子，在一家洗衣店里干着远超她力气的重活，自杀了：她向同伴借了三块钱，除非把这件事向父母坦白、搭进去整整一个星期的工钱，否则还不上——可那个星期一家人靠什么过呢！她那颗孩子的心当然没有轻重之分，石炭酸便成了免不了的结局。

我们看到许多童工的惨例，也看到血汗制下被榨得极苦的受害者——旺季短短一截挣的钱，怎么也撑不起一年里其余的日子。事情变得很明显：要让我们对街坊状况的笼统印象真有点价值，就得给它添上细心收集的材料。

===Original===

There was at that time no statistical information on Chicago industrial conditions, and Mrs. Florence Kelley, an early resident of Hull House, suggested to the Illinois State Bureau of Labor that they investigate the sweating system in Chicago with its attendant child labor. The head of the Bureau adopted this suggestion and engaged Mrs. Kelley to make the investigation. When the report was presented to the Illinois Legislature, a special committee was appointed to look into the Chicago conditions. I well recall that on the Sunday the members of this commission came to dine at Hull House, our hopes ran high, and we believed that at last some of the worst ills under which our neighbors were suffering would be brought to an end.

===Chinese===

那时关于芝加哥工业状况没有任何统计材料，赫尔会所早期居民弗洛伦斯·凯利太太（Mrs. Florence Kelley）向伊利诺伊州劳工局建议，调查芝加哥的血汗制及其附带的童工。劳工局首长采纳了这个建议，并聘请凯利太太来做这次调查。报告呈交伊利诺伊州议会后，指定了一个特别委员会来查究芝加哥的情形。我清楚地记得那个星期天，委员会的成员们来赫尔会所吃饭，我们的希望升得高高的，相信街坊们身受的最坏的苦难，终于有一些要结束了。

===Original===

As a result of its investigations, this committee recommended to the Legislature the provisions which afterward became those of the first factory law of Illinois, regulating the sanitary conditions of the sweatshop and fixing fourteen as the age at which a child might be employed. Before the passage of the law could be secured, it was necessary to appeal to all elements of the community, and a little group of us addressed the open meetings of trades-unions and of benefit societies, church organizations, and social clubs literally every evening for three months. Of course the most energetic help as well as intelligent understanding came from the trades-unions. The central labor body of Chicago, then called the Trades and Labor Assembly, had previously appointed a committee of investigation to inquire into the sweating system. This committee consisted of five delegates from the unions and five outside their membership. Two of the latter were residents of Hull House, and continued with the unions in their well-conducted campaign until the passage of Illinois’s first Factory Legislation was secured, a statute which has gradually been built upon by many public-spirited citizens until Illinois stands well among the States, at least in the matter of protecting her children. The Hull House residents that winter had their first experience in lobbying. I remember that I very much disliked the word and still more the prospect of the lobbying itself, and we insisted that well-known Chicago women should accompany this first little group of Settlement folk who with trades-unionists moved upon the state capitol in behalf of factory legislation. The national or, to use its formal name, The General Federation of Woman’s Clubs had been organized in Chicago only the year before this legislation was secured. The Federation was then timid in regard to all legislation because it was anxious not to frighten its new membership, although its second president, Mrs. Henrotin, was most untiring in her efforts to secure this law.

===Chinese===

这个委员会调查的结果，向议会提出了后来成为伊利诺伊州第一部工厂法条款的建议：规管血汗作坊的卫生条件，并把十四岁定为可以雇用儿童的年纪。要在法律通过之前办成这件事，必须向各界吁求；我们一小群人接连三个月，当真每天晚上都去工会和互助会、教会团体、社交俱乐部的公开集会上演讲。当然，最卖力的帮助和最明白的理解都来自工会。芝加哥的劳工中央机构，当时叫行业与劳工大会（Trades and Labor Assembly），此前已指定了一个调查委员会查究血汗制。这个委员会由五名工会代表和五名工会外的人组成。后者中有两位是赫尔会所的居民，他们同工会一道，把那场办得有声有色的运动坚持到伊利诺伊州第一部工厂立法通过为止；这部法令，经许多热心公益的公民逐步增筑，使伊利诺伊在各州之间站得住脚——至少在保护儿童这一项上。那年冬天，赫尔会所的居民们头一遭有了游说的经历。我记得我很不喜欢「游说」这个词，更不喜欢游说这件事本身的前景；我们坚持，务必请几位芝加哥的名妇人陪同我们这第一小队定居所的人——他们同工会员一道，为工厂立法向州议会大厦进发。全国性的组织——用它的正式名称，妇女俱乐部总联合会（The General Federation of Woman's Clubs）——就在这项立法通过的前一年才成立于芝加哥。总联合会那时对一切立法都缩手缩脚，因为它生怕吓着新会员，尽管它的第二任会长亨罗廷太太（Mrs. Henrotin）为促成这项法律不遗余力。

===Original===

It was, perhaps, a premature effort, though certainly founded upon a genuine need, to urge that a clause limiting the hours of all women working in factories or workshops to eight a day, or forty-eight a week, should be inserted in the first factory legislation of the State. Although we had lived at Hull House but three years when we urged this legislation, we had known a large number of young girls who were constantly exhausted by night work; for whatever may be said in defense of night work for men, few women are able to endure it. A man who works by night sleeps regularly by day, but a woman finds it impossible to put aside the household duties which crowd upon her, and a conscientious girl finds it hard to sleep with her mother washing and scrubbing within a few feet of her bed. One of the most painful impressions of those first years is that of pale, listless girls, who worked regularly in a factory of the vicinity which was then running full night time. These girls also encountered a special danger in the early morning hours as they returned from work, debilitated and exhausted, and only too easily convinced that a drink and a little dancing at the end of the balls in the saloon dance halls, was what they needed to brace them. One of the girls whom we then knew, whose name, Chloe, seemed to fit her delicate charm, craving a drink to dispel her lassitude before her tired feet should take the long walk home, had thus been decoyed into a saloon, where the soft drink was followed by an alcoholic one containing “knockout drops,” and she awoke in a disreputable rooming house﻿—too frightened and disgraced to return to her mother.

===Chinese===

在州的头一部工厂立法里加进一条，限定所有在工厂或作坊做工的妇女每日八小时、每周四十八小时——这或许是一场为时过早的努力，尽管它确实根于真实的需要。虽然我们促成这项立法时住进赫尔会所才三年，却已经认识许许多多叫夜班拖得常年疲惫不堪的年轻姑娘；因为男人上夜班会有什么可辩的且不说，能受得了夜班的女人寥寥无几。男人夜里做工，白天还能按时睡觉；女人却甩不开扑上来的家务，一个有良心的姑娘，母亲就在离床几尺的地方洗洗刷刷，她怎么睡得着。头几年最痛心的印象之一，便是那些苍白倦怠的姑娘们，她们在附近一家通宵开工的工厂里按时做工。这些姑娘凌晨下班回家的路上还遇到特别的危险——人已虚弱疲惫，很容易就信了：酒馆舞厅的舞会散场时来上一杯、跳上一阵，正是提神所需要的。我们那时认识的一个姑娘，名字叫克洛伊（Chloe），恰好配她那娇柔的可人劲儿；疲惫的双脚要走长长的回家路之前，她想喝上一口解解乏，就这样被诱进了酒馆——软饮料之后跟上的是一杯掺了「蒙汗药」的酒，等她醒来，人已经在一间名声扫地的寄宿屋里——又惊又羞，不敢回母亲那里去了。

===Original===

Thus confronted by that old conundrum of the interdependence of matter and spirit, the conviction was forced upon us that long and exhausting hours of work are almost sure to be followed by lurid and exciting pleasures; that the power to overcome temptation reaches its limit almost automatically with that of physical resistance. The eight-hour clause in this first factory law met with much less opposition in the Legislature than was anticipated, and was enforced for a year before it was pronounced unconstitutional by the Supreme Court of Illinois. During the halcyon months when it was a law, a large and enthusiastic Eight-Hour Club of working women met at Hull House, to read the literature on the subject and in every way to prepare themselves to make public sentiment in favor of the measure which meant so much to them. The adverse decision in the test case, the progress of which they had most intelligently followed, was a matter of great disappointment. The entire experience left on my mind a mistrust of all legislation which was not preceded by full discussion and understanding. A premature measure may be carried through a legislature by perfectly legitimate means and still fail to possess vitality and a sense of maturity. On the other hand, the administration of an advanced law acts somewhat as a referendum. The people have an opportunity for two years to see the effects of its operation. If they choose to reopen the matter at the next General Assembly, it can be discussed with experience and conviction; the very operation of the law has performed the function of the “referendum” in a limited use of the term.

===Chinese===

就这样迎面撞上物质与精神互相依存的那道老难题，我们被迫认定：漫长耗人的工时之后，跟着来的几乎必然是刺激兴奋的享乐；抵抗诱惑的本领，几乎随体力一起自动到了极限。这第一部工厂法里的八小时条款，在议会里遇到的反对远比预想的少，施行了一年，才被伊利诺伊州最高法院判为违宪。在它还是法律的那些好日子里，一个庞大的、热气腾腾的八小时俱乐部（Eight-Hour Club）的劳动妇女在赫尔会所集会，读这方面的文献，想方设法为这项对她们意义重大的措施准备舆论。测试案的败诉——它的进程她们一直极明白地追着——是巨大的失望。这一整段经历在我心里留下一条戒备：凡不是经过充分讨论和理解便出台的立法，都不可轻信。一项为时过早的措施，可以全凭正当的手段在议会里通过，却依然缺乏生命力、缺乏成熟的气象。反过来，一部先进的法律，其施行倒有点像一次公民复决：民众有机会在两年里看看它运行的效果；如果他们愿意在下一届州议会重提此事，讨论起来便有了经验和信念；单就这个词有限的用法说，法律的运行本身已经履行了「复决」的职能。

===Original===

Founded upon some such compunction, the sense that the passage of the child labor law would in many cases work hardship, was never absent from my mind during the earliest years of its operation. I addressed as many mothers’ meetings and clubs among working women as I could, in order to make clear the object of the law and the ultimate benefit to themselves as well as to their children. I am happy to remember that I never met with lack of understanding among the hardworking widows, in whose behalf many prosperous people were so eloquent. These widowed mothers would say, “Why, of course, that is what I am working for﻿—to give the children a chance. I want them to have more education than I had”; or another, “That is why we came to America, and I don’t want to spoil his start, even although his father is dead”; or “It’s different in America. A boy gets left if he isn’t educated.” There was always a willingness, even among the poorest women, to keep on with the hard night scrubbing or the long days of washing for the children’s sake.

The bitterest opposition to the law came from the large glass companies, who were so accustomed to use the labor of children that they were convinced the manufacturing of glass could not be carried on without it.

Fifteen years ago the State of Illinois, as well as Chicago, exhibited many characteristics of the pioneer country in which untrammeled energy and an “early start” were still the most highly prized generators of success. Although this first labor legislation was but bringing Illinois into line with the nations in the modern industrial world, which “have long been obliged for their own sakes to come to the aid of the workers by which they live﻿—that the child, the young person and the woman may be protected from their own weakness and necessity?” nevertheless from the first it ran counter to the instinct and tradition, almost to the very religion of the manufacturers of the state, who were for the most part self-made men.

===Chinese===

大约就是出于这样一种不安——童工法在许多人家会引起困窘这个感觉，在它施行的最初几年里从没有离开过我的心。我尽量多地到劳动妇女的母亲会和俱乐部去讲话，好讲清法律的目的，讲清它最终对她们自己、对她们的孩子都有好处。我高兴地记得，在操劳的寡妇们中间我从不曾遇到不理解——多少境况优裕的人曾为她们说得那样动听。这些寡母会说：「哎呀，当然啦，我做工不就为的这个——给孩子一个机会。我要他们受的教育比我多。」另一位说：「我们到美国来就为的这个；哪怕他爹死了，我也不想耽误他的起步。」或者说：「美国不一样。男孩子不受教育，就要落下。」哪怕最穷的女人也总愿意为了孩子，把夜里吃力的擦洗、白天漫长的洗衣一天天做下去。

对这项法律最激烈的反对来自几家大玻璃公司，他们用童工用惯了，坚信没有童工玻璃就造不成。

十五年前的伊利诺伊州，也同芝加哥一样，带着开拓时代的种种特征：不受拘束的干劲和「抢先起跑」仍是人人最看重的成功本钱。虽然这头一部劳动立法不过是要让伊利诺伊跟上现代工业世界的各国——它们「为了自己的缘故，早就不得不救助它们赖以为生的工人，使儿童、少年和妇女得免于自身的软弱与穷迫」——它从一开始就逆着本州制造商的本能与传统，几乎逆着他们的宗教；这些人多半是白手起家的。

===Original===

This first attempt in Illinois for adequate factory legislation also was associated in the minds of businessmen with radicalism, because the law was secured during the term of Governor Altgeld and was first enforced during his administration. While nothing in its genesis or spirit could be further from “anarchy” than factory legislation, and while the first law in Illinois was still far behind Massachusetts and New York, the fact that Governor Altgeld pardoned from the state’s prison the anarchists who had been sentenced there after the Haymarket riot, gave the opponents of this most reasonable legislation a quickly utilized opportunity to couple it with that detested word; the State document which accompanied Governor Altgeld’s pardon gave these ungenerous critics a further opportunity, because a magnanimous action was marred by personal rancor, betraying for the moment the infirmity of a noble mind. For all of these reasons this first modification of the undisturbed control of the aggressive captains of industry could not be enforced without resistance marked by dramatic episodes and revolts. The inception of the law had already become associated with Hull House, and when its ministration was also centered there, we inevitably received all the odium which these first efforts entailed. Mrs. Kelley was appointed the first factory inspector with a deputy and a force of twelve inspectors to enforce the law. Both Mrs. Kelley and her assistant, Mrs. Stevens, lived at Hull House; the office was on Polk Street directly opposite, and one of the most vigorous deputies was the president of the Jane Club. In addition, one of the early men residents, since dean of a state law school, acted as prosecutor in the cases brought against the violators of the law.

===Chinese===

伊利诺伊这头一次争取像样的工厂立法的尝试，在商人心里还同激进主义拴在一起，因为这项法律是在阿尔特吉尔德州长（Governor Altgeld）任内争得的，又是在他的行政期间开始执行的。尽管就其起源与精神而言，没有什么比工厂立法离「无政府主义」更远的了，尽管伊利诺伊的第一部法律还远远落在马萨诸塞和纽约后面，可是阿尔特吉尔德州长赦免了干草市场暴乱（Haymarket riot）后被判入州监狱的无政府主义者，这件事给这项最合乎情理的立法的反对者们一个立刻用上手的机会，把它同那个遭人痛恨的字眼拴在了一起；伴随赦免的州公文又给这些不厚道的批评者添了一个机会，因为一桩宽宏大量的行动叫私人的怨毒败坏了，一时间显出了高贵心灵的软弱处。由于这一切，要对进取的实业巨头们不受约束的统治作这第一次修正，不经过带戏剧性插曲和反抗的抵抗，是推行不开的。这项法律的发端早已同赫尔会所连在一起，等到它的执行也集中到那里，这些初步努力招来的一切骂名，自然都落到我们头上。凯利太太被任命为第一任工厂督察员，配有一名副手和十二名督察员来执行法律。凯利太太和她的助手史蒂文斯太太（Mrs. Stevens）都住在赫尔会所；办事处就在正对面的波尔克街上，而最干练的副督察之一就是简俱乐部（Jane Club）的主席。此外，一位早期的男居民——如今是一所州立法学院院长——担任了起诉违法者的案件的公诉人。

===Original===

Chicago had for years been notoriously lax in the administration of law, and the enforcement of an unpopular measure was resented equally by the president of a large manufacturing concern and by the former victim of a sweatshop who had started a place of his own. Whatever the sentiments toward the new law on the part of the employers, there was no doubt of its enthusiastic reception by the trades-unions, as the securing of the law had already come from them, and through the years which have elapsed since, the experience of the Hull House residents would coincide with that of an English statesman who said that “a common rule for the standard of life and the condition of labor may be secured by legislation, but it must be maintained by trades unionism.”

This special value of the trades-unions first became clear to the residents of Hull House in connection with the sweating system. We early found that the women in the sewing trades were sorely in need of help. The trade was thoroughly disorganized, Russian and Polish tailors competing against English-speaking tailors, unskilled Bohemian and Italian women competing against both. These women seem to have been best helped through the use of the label when unions of specialized workers in the trade are strong enough to insist that the manufacturers shall “give out work” only to those holding union cards. It was certainly impressive when the garment makers themselves in this way finally succeeded in organizing six hundred of the Italian women in our immediate vicinity, who had finished garments at home for the most wretched and precarious wages. To be sure, the most ignorant women only knew that “you couldn’t get clothes to sew” from the places where they paid the best, unless “you had a card,” but through the veins of most of them there pulsed the quickened blood of a new fellowship, a sense of comfort and aid which had been laid out to them by their fellow-workers.

===Chinese===

芝加哥的执法松懈多年来是出了名的，一项不得人心的措施的执行，大制造公司的总裁固然怨恨，从前血汗作坊的受害者自己开了铺子的，也一样怨恨。不论雇主们对新法律怀什么心思，工会对它的欢迎是毫无疑问的，因为法律的得来本就出于他们；此后这些年里，赫尔会所居民的经历恰好印证了一位英国政治家的话：「生活的水准与劳动的条件，可以靠立法立下共同的规矩，却必须靠工会去维持。」

工会的这一特殊价值，赫尔会所的居民最早是在血汗制上弄明白的。我们早就发现，缝纫业的女人急需帮助。这一行彻底地散了伙：俄国裁缝和波兰裁缝同讲英语的裁缝竞相压价，没有手艺的波希米亚女人、意大利女人又同她们所有人对压。这些女人受助最见效的路子，看来是工会标章——当这一行里各专门工种的工会足够强大，能坚持要制造商只把活「发给」持工会会员证的人。成衣工人们后来就用这个法子，终于把我们紧邻的六百名意大利妇女组织了起来——她们一直在家里缝制成衣，挣的是最悲惨、最朝不保夕的工钱——那场面确实动人。诚然，最无知的女人只知道「没有证」，就「领不着工钱开得最好的地方的活」，可在她们大多数人的血管里，搏动着的是一种新团契的热血，一种由工友为她们铺排出来的、有依靠有帮手的意识。

===Original===

During the fourth year of our residence at Hull House we found ourselves in a large mass meeting ardently advocating the passage of a Federal measure called the Sulzer Bill. Even in our short struggle with the evils of the sweating system it did not seem strange that the center of the effort had shifted to Washington, for by that time we had realized that the sanitary regulation of sweatshops by city officials, and a careful enforcement of factory legislation by state factory inspectors will not avail, unless each city and State shall be able to pass and enforce a code of comparatively uniform legislation. Although the Sulzer Act failed to utilize the Interstate Commerce legislation for its purpose, many of the national representatives realized for the first time that only by federal legislation could their constituents in remote country places be protected from contagious diseases raging in New York or Chicago, for many country doctors testify as to the outbreak of scarlet fever in rural neighborhoods after the children have begun to wear the winter overcoats and cloaks which have been sent from infected city sweatshops.

Through our efforts to modify the sweating system, the Hull House residents gradually became committed to the fortunes of the Consumers’ League, an organization which for years has been approaching the question of the underpaid sewing woman from the point of view of the ultimate responsibility lodged in the consumer. It becomes more reasonable to make the presentation of the sweatshop situation through this League, as it is more effectual to work with them for the extension of legal provisions in the slow upbuilding of that code of legislation which is alone sufficient to protect the home from the dangers incident to the sweating system.

===Chinese===

住进赫尔会所的第四年，我们发现自己置身于一个盛大的群众大会，热烈鼓吹通过一项叫苏尔泽法案（Sulzer Bill）的联邦议案。哪怕同血汗制的罪恶只短短周旋过一阵，斗争的中心移到华盛顿去也不显得奇怪，因为那时我们已经明白：靠市府官员规管血汗作坊的卫生、靠州工厂督察员认真执行工厂法，是不中用的，除非每座城市、每个州都能通过并执行一套大体划一的立法。苏尔泽法案虽未能借州际商务立法达成目的，许多国会议员却头一回明白了：只有联邦立法，才能保护他们偏远乡间的选民，免遭纽约或芝加哥肆虐的传染病——多少乡间医生作证说，孩子们一穿上从城里疫病作坊发来的冬季大衣斗篷，乡下就闹起猩红热。

经由改造血汗制的种种努力，赫尔会所的居民渐渐同消费者联盟（Consumers' League）的命运结在了一起；这个组织多年来一直从消费者所负最终责任的角度，去接近低薪缝纫女工的问题。透过这个联盟来呈示血汗作坊的境况，理由更充分；同它们一道，去一步步筑起那唯一足以保护家庭免遭血汗制之害的法典，逐步扩充法律条款，也更有成效。

===Original===

The Consumers’ League seems to afford the best method of approach for the protection of girls in department stores; I recall a group of girls from a neighboring “emporium” who applied to Hull House for dancing parties on alternate Sunday afternoons. In reply to our protest they told us they not only worked late every evening, in spite of the fact that each was supposed to have “two nights a week off,” and every Sunday morning, but that on alternate Sunday afternoons they were required “to sort the stock.” Over and over again, meetings called by the Clerks Union and others have been held at Hull House protesting against these incredibly long hours. Little modification has come about, however, during our twenty years of residence, although one large store in the Bohemian quarter closes all day on Sunday and many of the others for three nights a week. In spite of the Sunday work, these girls prefer the outlying department stores to those downtown; there is more social intercourse with the customers, more kindliness and social equality between the saleswomen and the managers, and above all the girls have the protection naturally afforded by friends and neighbors and they are free from that suspicion which so often haunts the girls downtown, that their fellow workers may not be “nice girls.”

In the first years of Hull House we came across no trades-unions among the women workers, and I think, perhaps, that only one union, composed solely of women, was to be found in Chicago then﻿—that of the bookbinders. I easily recall the evening when the president of this pioneer organization accepted an invitation to take dinner at Hull House. She came in rather a recalcitrant mood, expecting to be patronized, and so suspicious of our motives that it was only after she had been persuaded to become a guest of the house for several weeks in order to find out about us for herself, that she was convinced of our sincerity and of the ability of “outsiders” to be of any service to working women. She afterward became closely identified with Hull House, and her hearty cooperation was assured until she moved to Boston and became a general organizer for the American Federation of Labor.

===Chinese===

消费者联盟看来也为保护百货商店的姑娘们提供了最好的路径。我记得附近一家「大商场」的一群姑娘，隔周的星期天下午来赫尔会所申请跳舞会。我们提出异议，她们回答说：且不说每人本该「一星期歇两个晚上」、外加每个星期天上午，她们每天晚上都做到很晚，隔周的星期天下午还被要求「理货」。店员工会（Clerks Union）等团体在赫尔会所开过一次又一次的会，抗议这些长得叫人不敢相信的工时。然而我们住的这二十年里，改变寥寥；只有波希米亚区的一家大商店星期天全天关门，另有几家一星期歇三个晚上。尽管要做礼拜日的工，这些姑娘们还是喜欢外围的百货店，不喜欢市中心的：同顾客的交往多些，女售货员同经理之间厚道些、平等些，尤其是姑娘们有亲友近邻天然的保护，而且免了那种常常纠缠着市中心姑娘们的疑心——怕同事未必是「正经姑娘」。

赫尔会所的头几年里，我们在劳动妇女中间没有见到过什么工会；我想，那时全芝加哥恐怕只有一个纯由妇女组成的工会——装订工的工会。我轻易记得那个晚上，这个开路组织的主席应邀来赫尔会所吃饭。她来时带着一肚子别扭，料定要受人恩赐，又那样疑心我们的动机，直到劝得她做了会所几个星期的客人、亲自把我们打量个明白，她才信了我们的诚意，也信了「外头人」真能帮得上劳动妇女。后来她同赫尔会所关系密切，衷心合作，直到她迁居波士顿，当了美国劳工联合会（American Federation of Labor）的巡回组织者。

===Original===

The women shirt makers and the women cloak makers were both organized at Hull House as was also the Dorcas Federal Labor Union, which had been founded through the efforts of a working woman, then one of the residents. The latter union met once a month in our drawing room. It was composed of representatives from all the unions in the city which included women in their membership and also received other women in sympathy with unionism. It was accorded representation in the central labor body of the city, and later it joined its efforts with those of others to found the Woman’s Union Label League. In what we considered a praiseworthy effort to unite it with other organizations, the president of a leading Woman’s Club applied for membership. We were so sure of her election that she stood just outside of the drawing-room door, or, in trades-union language, “the wicket gate,” while her name was voted upon. To our chagrin, she did not receive enough votes to secure her admission, not because the working girls, as they were careful to state, did not admire her, but because she “seemed to belong to the other side.” Fortunately, the big-minded woman so thoroughly understood the vote and her interest in working women was so genuine that it was less than a decade afterward when she was elected to the presidency of the National Woman’s Trades Union League. The incident and the sequel registers, perhaps, the change in Chicago toward the labor movement, the recognition of the fact that it is a general social movement concerning all members of society and not merely a class struggle.

===Chinese===

做衬衫的女工和做斗篷的女工都是在赫尔会所组织起来的，还有多卡斯联邦劳工工会（Dorcas Federal Labor Union），它由一位劳动妇女出力创办，那时也是会所的居民。后一个工会每月在我们的客厅里开一次会。它由全城所有兼收妇女的工会各派代表组成，也接纳其他赞同工会的妇女。它在市的劳工中央机构里得到了席位，后来又同人合力创办了妇女工会标章同盟（Woman's Union Label League）。在我们自以为值得称许的一次努力中——想使它同别的团体联合——一家重要妇女俱乐部的主席申请入会。我们对她的当选那样有把握，让她就站在客厅门外——用工会的话说，「小门闸」外——听着表决她的名字。叫我们难堪的是，她的票数不够，没进来；倒不是如那些姑娘们特意说明的，她们不敬佩她，而是因为她「好像站在另一边」。所幸这位胸怀宽大的女人完全懂得这一票的意思，她对劳动妇女的关切又那样真，不到十年之后，她当选为全国妇女工会同盟（National Woman's Trades Union League）的主席。这桩事和它的后话，也许正标志着芝加哥对劳工运动的看法的转变：承认它是一场关乎社会全体成员的普遍社会运动，而不仅仅是阶级斗争。

===Original===

Some such public estimate of the labor movement was brought home to Chicago during several conspicuous strikes; at least labor legislation has twice been inaugurated because its need was thus made clear. After the Pullman strike various elements in the community were unexpectedly brought together that they might soberly consider and rectify the weakness in the legal structure which the strike had revealed. These citizens arranged for a large and representative convention to be held in Chicago on Industrial Conciliation and Arbitration. I served as secretary of the committee from the new Civic Federation having the matter in charge, and our hopes ran high when, as a result of the agitation, the Illinois legislature passed a law creating a State Board of Conciliation and Arbitration. But even a state board cannot accomplish more than public sentiment authorizes and sustains, and we might easily have been discouraged in those early days could we have foreseen some of the industrial disturbances which have since disgraced Chicago. This law embodied the best provisions of the then existing laws for the arbitration of industrial disputes. At the time the word “arbitration” was still a word to conjure with, and many Chicago citizens were convinced, not only of the danger and futility involved in the open warfare of opposing social forces, but further believed that the search for justice and righteousness in industrial relations was made infinitely more difficult thereby.

===Chinese===

劳工运动的这样一种公众评价，是在几次引人注目的大罢工中落到芝加哥人心上的；至少劳动立法有过两回，正是因为需要这样才开了头。普尔曼罢工（Pullman strike）之后，社会各界意想不到地聚到一起，要冷静地考量并修补罢工暴露出来的法律结构的弱点。这些公民安排了一次在芝加哥举行的大型而有代表性的大会，讨论工业调停与仲裁。我担任新成立的市民联合会（Civic Federation）主管此事的委员会的秘书；由于这一番鼓动，伊利诺伊州议会通过法律设立州调停仲裁委员会（State Board of Conciliation and Arbitration）时，我们的希望升得高高的。可是州委员会能做到的，超不过公众情感所授权、所支持的那一点；倘若早预见得到此后那些玷污芝加哥的工业骚动，那些年头我们很可能会灰心。这部法律汇入了当时已有的工业争议仲裁法里最好的条款。那个时候，「仲裁」还是一个有魔力的字眼，许多芝加哥市民不仅确信对立社会力量公开交战的危险与徒劳，更相信对工业关系中的公义正道的寻求，会因此难上无数倍。

===Original===

The Pullman strike afforded much illumination to many Chicago people. Before it, there had been nothing in my experience to reveal that distinct cleavage of society, which a general strike at least momentarily affords. Certainly, during all those dark days of the Pullman strike, the growth of class bitterness was most obvious. The fact that the Settlement maintained avenues of intercourse with both sides seemed to give it opportunity for nothing but a realization of the bitterness and division along class lines. I had known Mr. Pullman and had seen his genuine pride and pleasure in the model town he had built with so much care; and I had an opportunity to talk to many of the Pullman employees during the strike when I was sent from a so-called “Citizens’ Arbitration Committee” to their first meetings held in a hall in the neighboring village of Kensington, and when I was invited to the modest supper tables laid in the model houses. The employees then expected a speedy settlement and no one doubted but that all the grievances connected with the “straw bosses” would be quickly remedied and that the benevolence which had built the model town would not fail them. They were sure that the “straw bosses” had misrepresented the state of affairs, for this very first awakening to class consciousness bore many traces of the servility on one side and the arrogance on the other which had so long prevailed in the model town. The entire strike demonstrated how often the outcome of far-reaching industrial disturbances is dependent upon the personal will of the employer or the temperament of a strike leader. Those familiar with strikes know only too well how much they are influenced by poignant domestic situations, by the troubled consciences of the minority directors, by the suffering women and children, by the keen excitement of the struggle, by the religious scruples sternly suppressed but occasionally asserting themselves, now on one side and now on the other, and by that undefined psychology of the crowd which we understand so little. All of these factors also influence the public and do much to determine popular sympathy and judgment.

===Chinese===

普尔曼罢工给许多芝加哥人很大的启发。在它之前，我的经历里还没有什么能显出社会的这种清楚裂痕——一场总罢工至少能片刻之间把它摆出来。当然，普尔曼罢工那些阴暗的日子里，阶级怨毒的增长是最显眼的。定居所同两边都维持着交往的渠道，这似乎只给了它一个机会：看清阶级界线两侧的怨毒与分裂。我认识普尔曼先生，见过他对自己精心建造的模范镇那份由衷的骄傲与欢喜；罢工期间我又有机会同许多普尔曼的雇工谈话——那时我受一个所谓「市民仲裁委员会」的委派，去他们在邻村肯辛顿（Kensington）一家会堂里开的头几次会，又应邀到模范住宅里摆开的朴素饭桌上。雇工们当时指望迅速解决，谁都不怀疑同「小工头」们有关的种种委屈会很快矫正，建造模范镇的恩主不会亏待他们。他们认定是「小工头」把情形报歪了；因为这头一回阶级意识的觉醒，还带着模范镇里久已盛行的这边的卑屈、那边的倨傲的许多痕迹。整场罢工表明：影响深远的工业骚动，其结局多么经常地系于雇主的个人意志或罢工领袖的脾性。熟悉罢工的人都太清楚：罢工受着多少影响——揪心的家事，少数派董事们不安的良心，受苦的女人孩子，斗争的尖锐紧张，被死死压住却不时冒头的宗教顾虑——忽而在这边，忽而在那边——还有那种我们知之甚少的、说不清的群众心理。这些因素也都左右着公众，大大决定着民众的同情与判断。

===Original===

In the early days of the Pullman strike, as I was coming down in the elevator of the Auditorium hotel from one of the futile meetings of the Arbitration Committee, I met an acquaintance, who angrily said “that the strikers ought all to be shot.” As I had heard nothing so bloodthirsty as this either from the most enraged capitalist or from the most desperate of the men, and was interested to find the cause of such a senseless outbreak, I finally discovered that the first ten thousand dollars which my acquaintance had ever saved, requiring, he said, years of effort from the time he was twelve years old until he was thirty, had been lost as the result of a strike; he clinched his argument that he knew what he was talking about, with the statement that “no one need expect him to have any sympathy with strikers or with their affairs.”

A very intimate and personal experience revealed, at least to myself, my constant dread of the spreading ill will. At the height of the sympathetic strike my oldest sister, who was convalescing from a long illness in a hospital near Chicago, became suddenly very much worse. While I was able to reach her at once, every possible obstacle of a delayed and blocked transportation system interrupted the journey of her husband and children who were hurrying to her bedside from a distant state. As the end drew nearer and I was obliged to reply to my sister’s constant inquiries that her family had not yet come, I was filled with a profound apprehension lest her last hours should be touched with resentment toward those responsible for the delay; lest her unutterable longing should at the very end be tinged with bitterness. She must have divined what was in my mind, for at last she said each time after the repetition of my sad news: “I don’t blame anyone, I am not judging them.” My heart was comforted and heavy at the same time; but how many more such moments of sorrow and death were being made difficult and lonely throughout the land, and how much would these experiences add to the lasting bitterness, that touch of self-righteousness which makes the spirit of forgiveness well-nigh impossible.

===Chinese===

普尔曼罢工初期，我乘礼堂饭店（Auditorium Hotel）的电梯从仲裁委员会一次毫无结果的会议上下来，遇见一位相识的人，他怒气冲冲地说「罢工的人都该枪毙」。这话我在最恼怒的资本家嘴里、在最绝望的工人嘴里都不曾听到过，血腥到这个地步；我有兴致寻一寻这场无谓发作的根由，最后发现：我这相识的人平生攒下的头一个一万元——照他说，从十二岁攒到三十三岁，费了多少年的力气——在一回罢工里丢了；他把「谁也别指望他同情罢工的人或罢工的事」这句话一放，算是把「我知道自己在说什么」钉死了。

一段切身的经历，至少向我本人，显出了我时刻惧怕的那种蔓延开来的恶感。同情罢工闹到最凶的时候，我大姐——她在芝加哥附近一家医院里养一场大病——突然沉重起来。我倒是马上到了她身边，可她丈夫孩子从远处一个州赶来，却被耽误的、瘫掉的运输系统一道道阻住。临终愈近，我不得不一回又一回回答姐姐的追问：家里人还没有到；我心里充满深深的忧惧，怕她最后的时辰染上对耽误的人的怨恨，怕她难以言说的渴望到了尽头掺进苦味。她一定猜着了我的心思，因为后来每逢我重复这悲哀的消息，她总说：「我不怪谁，我不论断他们。」我的心又得安慰又沉重；可全国还有多少这样的死别与哀伤的时刻，正被弄得艰难而孤单；这些经历又会给那久久不散的怨毒添上多少东西——添上那一点自以为是，叫宽恕的心几乎成了不可能。

===Original===

When I returned to Chicago from the quiet country I saw the Federal troops encamped about the post office; almost everyone on Halsted Street wearing a white ribbon, the emblem of the strikers’ side; the residents at Hull House divided in opinion as to the righteousness of this or that measure; and no one able to secure any real information as to which side was burning the cars. After the Pullman strike I made an attempt to analyze in a paper which I called “The Modern King Lear” the inevitable revolt of human nature against the plans Mr. Pullman had made for his employees, the miscarriage of which appeared to him such black ingratitude. It seemed to me unendurable not to make some effort to gather together the social implications of the failure of this benevolent employer and its relation to the demand for a more democratic administration of industry. Doubtless the paper represented a certain “excess of participation,” to use a gentle phrase of Charles Lamb’s in preference to a more emphatic one used by Mr. Pullman himself. The last picture of the Pullman strike which I distinctly recall was three years later when one of the strike leaders came to see me. Although out of work for most of the time since the strike, he had been undisturbed for six months in the repair shops of a streetcar company, under an assumed name, but he had at that moment been discovered and dismissed. He was a superior type of English workingman, but as he stood there, broken and discouraged, believing himself so blacklisted that his skill could never be used again, filled with sorrow over the loss of his wife who had recently died after an illness with distressing mental symptoms, realizing keenly the lack of the respectable way of living he had always until now been able to maintain, he seemed to me an epitome of the wretched human waste such a strike implies. I fervently hoped that the new arbitration law would prohibit in Chicago forever more such brutal and ineffective methods of settling industrial disputes. And yet even as early as 1896, we found the greatest difficulty in applying the arbitration law to the garment workers’ strike, although it was finally accomplished after various mass meetings had urged it. The cruelty and waste of the strike as an implement for securing the most reasonable demands came to me at another time, during the long strike of the clothing cutters.

===Chinese===

我从安静的乡间回到芝加哥，看见联邦军队围着邮局扎营；哈尔斯特德街（Halsted Street）上几乎人人佩着白缎带，罢工一方的标记；赫尔会所的居民们为这个措施那个措施谁是谁非意见分歧；谁也拿不到真消息，说不清烧车厢的是哪一边。普尔曼罢工之后，我试着在一篇题为《现代的李尔王》（“The Modern King Lear”）的文章里分析：人性对普尔曼先生为雇工作下的安排必然的反叛——这安排的落空，在他看来是那样的恩将仇报。要是不作一番努力，把这位好心的雇主败绩的社会含义拢起来、同产业更民主的管理的要求联系起来，我觉得简直受不住。无疑，这篇文章表现出某种「介入过头」——借用查尔斯·兰姆（Charles Lamb）一句温和的话，不用普尔曼先生自己那句更重口的。普尔曼罢工我记得清楚的最后一幅画，是三年后一位罢工领袖来看我。罢工以来他大部分时间没有活干，曾用假名在一家电车公司的修理厂安然做了六个月，可那时节他被查了出来，撵走了。他是英国工人里上等的那一类人；可当他站在那里，垮了、灰了，相信自己已被列入黑名单，一身手艺再没有用武之地；痛惜新近去世的妻子——她病了一场，带着折磨人的精神症状走了；又痛切地感到，此前一直守得住的那份体面日子如今保不住了——在我眼里，他成了这样一场罢工所意味着的人力浪费的一个缩影。我热切希望新的仲裁法能让芝加哥从此根绝这种解决工业争议的残忍而无效的手段。然而早在1896年，我们就发现把仲裁法用到成衣工人的罢工上困难重重，虽然经各次群众大会力劝，最终用上了。罢工作为一种手段，连最合情理的要求也要靠它去争——它的残忍与浪费，我另有一次领教，是在裁剪工人漫长的罢工期间。

===Original===

They had protested, not only against various wrongs of their own, but against the fact that the tailors employed by the custom merchants were obliged to furnish their own workshops and thus bore a burden of rent which belonged to the employer. One of the leaders in this strike, whom I had known for several years as a sober, industrious, and unusually intelligent man, I saw gradually break down during the many trying weeks and at last suffer a complete moral collapse.

===Chinese===

他们抗议的，不仅有自身的种种委屈，还有这样一条：受雇于定制商的裁缝必须自备工场，从而负担了本该雇主负担的房租。这场罢工的一位领袖，我认识他好几年了，是个清醒、勤勉、见识出众人一等的人；那些难熬的星期里，我眼看他渐渐垮下去，末了整个人格崩塌了。

===Original===

He was a man of sensitive organization under the necessity, as is every leader during a strike, to address the same body of men day after day with an appeal sufficiently emotional to respond to their sense of injury; to receive callers at any hour of the day or night; to sympathize with all the distress of the strikers who see their families daily suffering; he must do it all with the sickening sense of the increasing privation in his own home, and in this case with the consciousness that failure was approaching nearer each day. This man, accustomed to the monotony of his workbench and suddenly thrown into a new situation, showed every sign of nervous fatigue before the final collapse came. He disappeared after the strike and I did not see him for ten years, but when he returned he immediately began talking about the old grievances which he had repeated so often that he could talk of nothing else. It was easy to recognize the same nervous symptoms which the broken-down lecturer exhibits who has depended upon the exploitation of his own experiences to keep himself going. One of his stories was indeed pathetic. His employer, during the busy season, had met him one Sunday afternoon in Lincoln Park whither he had taken his three youngest children, one of whom had been ill. The employer scolded him for thus wasting his time and roughly asked why he had not taken home enough work to keep himself busy through the day. The story was quite credible because the residents of Hull House have had many opportunities to see the worker driven ruthlessly during the season and left in idleness for long weeks afterward. We have slowly come to realize that periodical idleness as well as the payment of wages insufficient for maintenance of the manual worker in full industrial and domestic efficiency, stand economically on the same footing with the “sweated” industries, the overwork of women, and employment of children.

===Chinese===

他是个神经敏感的人。罢工期间，凡是领袖都免不了的本分压在他身上：同一群人，日复一日地演讲，情绪要挑得够高，才配得上他们受的委屈；白天黑夜任何时辰接见来客；同情罢工者眼见家人天天受苦的种种悲苦——而他做这一切的时候，心里带着自家境况一日紧过一日的恶心感觉，在这一件里，还带着失败一天天逼近的自觉。这个惯于工作台的单调、猛然被抛进新境地的人，在最后的崩溃到来之前，早已显尽神经疲惫的征象。罢工过后他不见了，我一连十年没有见到他；可他一回来，立刻又讲起那些老委屈，那些他重复过千百遍、以至于别的什么也讲不出的话。同样的神经症状不难认出：正像垮掉的讲演者，全靠咀嚼自己的经历撑着往前走。他有一段故事确实凄惨。旺季里的一个星期天下午，他的雇主在林肯公园碰到他——他带着三个最小的孩子上那儿去，其中一个在生病。雇主骂他这样糟蹋光阴，粗声粗气地问他为什么不带够活计回家，好把一天填满。这故事完全可信，因为赫尔会所的居民有多少机会看到：旺季里工人被无情驱使，过后又是长长几个星期的赋闲。我们慢慢懂得：周期性的失业，同付给手工工人的工资不足以维持其充分工业效能与家庭效能，在经济上同「血汗」行业、女人的过劳、童工的雇用站在同一个立足点上。

===Original===

But of all the aspects of social misery nothing is so heartbreaking as unemployment, and it was inevitable that we should see much of it in a neighborhood where low rents attracted the poorly paid worker and many newly arrived immigrants who were first employed in gangs upon railroad extensions and similar undertakings. The sturdy peasants eager for work were either the victims of the padrone who fleeced them unmercifully, both in securing a place to work and then in supplying them with food, or they became the mere sport of unscrupulous employment agencies. Hull House made an investigation both of the padrone and of the agencies in our immediate vicinity, and the outcome confirming what we already suspected, we eagerly threw ourselves into a movement to procure free employment bureaus under State control until a law authorizing such bureaus and giving the officials entrusted with their management power to regulate private employment agencies, passed the Illinois Legislature in 1899. The history of these bureaus demonstrates the tendency we all have to consider a legal enactment in itself an achievement and to grow careless in regard to its administration and actual results; for an investigation into the situation ten years later discovered that immigrants were still shamefully imposed upon. A group of Bulgarians were found who had been sent to work in Arkansas where their services were not needed; they walked back to Chicago only to secure their next job in Oklahoma and to pay another railroad fare as well as another commission to the agency. Not only was there no method by which the men not needed in Arkansas could know that there was work in Oklahoma unless they came back to Chicago to find it out, but there was no certainty that they might not be obliged to walk back from Oklahoma because the Chicago agency had already sent out too many men.

===Chinese===

但在社会苦难的一切面向里，最令人心碎的莫过于失业；而在这样一个街坊里，低廉的房租既招来低薪工人和许多新到的移民——他们先是一伙一伙地被雇去修铁路延长线之类的工程——失业我们就必然见得多。急着找活的壮实农民，或者落进包工头（padrone）手里被狠命盘剥——找活要剥一层，供给伙食再剥一层——或者干脆成了无良职业介绍所的玩物。赫尔会所对我们紧邻的包工头和介绍所都做过调查，结果证实了我们已经疑心的；我们一头扎进一场运动，要求设州管的免费职业介绍所，直到1899年伊利诺伊州议会通过法律，授权设立这样的介绍所，并授权受托管理的官员规管私营职业介绍所。这些介绍所的历史证明了我们大家共有的毛病：把法律条文的通过本身当作成就，对它的执行和实效漫不经心；因为十年后的一项调查发现，移民照旧被可耻地作弄。发现有一群保加利亚人被送去阿肯色做工，那边根本用不着他们；他们步行回到芝加哥，结果寻到的下一个工却在俄克拉何马——又付一笔车钱，又付一笔介绍费。不但阿肯色用不着的人没有办法知道俄克拉何马有活干——除非回到芝加哥来打听；而且谁也不能担保他们不会又从俄克拉何马走回来，因为芝加哥的介绍所已经送出去太多的人。

===Original===

This investigation of the employment bureau resources of Chicago was undertaken by the League for the Protection of Immigrants, with whom it is possible for Hull House to cooperate whenever an investigation of the immigrant colonies in our immediate neighborhood seems necessary, as was recently done in regard to the Greek colonies of Chicago. The superintendent of this League, Miss Grace Abbott, is a resident of Hull House and all of our later attempts to secure justice and opportunity for immigrants are much more effective through the League, and when we speak before a congressional committee in Washington concerning the needs of Chicago immigrants, we represent the League as well as our own neighbors.

===Chinese===

芝加哥职业介绍资源的这次调查，是保护移民同盟（League for the Protection of Immigrants）做的；每逢需要对我们紧邻的移民聚落做调查——像最近对芝加哥希腊侨民聚落做的那样——赫尔会所都能同它合作。这个同盟的总干事格雷斯·阿博特小姐（Miss Grace Abbott）就是赫尔会所的居民；我们后来为移民争公道、争机会的一切努力，透过同盟去做都见效得多；我们在华盛顿向国会委员会陈述芝加哥移民的需要时，代表的既是同盟，也是我们自己的街坊。

===Original===

It is in connection with the first factory employment of newly arrived immigrants and the innumerable difficulties attached to their first adjustment that some of the most profound industrial disturbances in Chicago have come about. Under any attempt at classification these strikes belong more to the general social movement than to the industrial conflict, for the strike is an implement used most rashly by unorganized labor who, after they are in difficulties, call upon the trades-unions for organization and direction. They are similar to those strikes which are inaugurated by the unions on behalf of unskilled labor. In neither case do the hastily organized unions usually hold after the excitement of the moment has subsided, and the most valuable result of such strikes is the expanding consciousness of the solidarity of the workers. This was certainly the result of the Chicago stockyard strike in 1905, inaugurated on behalf of the immigrant laborers and so conspicuously carried on without violence that, although twenty-two thousand workers were idle during the entire summer, there were fewer arrests in the stockyards district than the average summer months afford. However, the story of this strike should not be told from Hull House, but from the University of Chicago Settlement, where Miss Mary McDowell performed such signal public service during that trying summer. It would be interesting to trace how much of the subsequent exposure of conditions and attempts at governmental control of this huge industry had their genesis in this first attempt of the unskilled workers to secure a higher standard of living. Certainly the industrial conflict when epitomized in a strike, centers public attention on conditions as nothing else can do. A strike is one of the most exciting episodes in modern life, and as it assumes the characteristics of a game, the entire population of a city becomes divided into two cheering sides. In such moments the fair-minded public, who ought to be depended upon as a referee, practically disappears. Anyone who tries to keep the attitude of nonpartisanship, which is perhaps an impossible one, is quickly under suspicion by both sides. At least that was the fate of a group of citizens appointed by the mayor of Chicago to arbitrate during the stormy teamsters’ strike which occurred in 1905.

===Chinese===

正是在新到移民头一次进厂做工、头一回适应环境的数不清的难处上，芝加哥几场最深刻的工业骚动发生了。不管怎么归类，这些罢工属于普遍的社会运动多过属于工业冲突：这是没有组织的工人最莽撞地使用的工具，闯了祸之后，才叫工会来组织和指挥。它们同工会为无技术劳工发动的罢工相似。两种情形里，匆匆组成的工会通常都撑不过一时激动的消退，而这类罢工最可贵的结果，是工人团结意识的扩张。1905年芝加哥牲畜围场罢工肯定是这样：为移民劳工而发动，进行得那样显著地不带暴力，以致两万二千工人闲了整整一个夏天，围场区的拘捕反而少于寻常夏季月份。不过这场罢工的故事，不该从赫尔会所讲，该从芝加哥大学定居所（University of Chicago Settlement）讲——在那个难熬的夏天里，玛丽·麦克道尔小姐（Miss Mary McDowell）在那里立下了那样显著的公益功劳。倘能追溯一下，后来对这庞大产业状况的揭露、政府管控的种种尝试，有多少是发端于无技术工人争取更高生活水准的这头一回努力，倒是件有意思的事。工业冲突凝结成一场罢工时，确实把公众的注意钉在了境况上，别的什么都办不到这一点。罢工是现代生活中最富刺激的事件之一；它一带上比赛的性质，全城人便分成了两支呐喊的队伍。这样的时刻，本该倚仗来当裁判的公道的公众，实际上不见了。谁想持不偏不倚的态度——这态度也许根本不可能——很快就两边一齐犯疑。至少芝加哥市长指派来调停1905年那场风暴般的卡车司机罢工的一群公民，落的正是这个下场。

===Original===

We sat through a long Sunday afternoon in the mayor’s office in the City Hall, talking first with the labor men and then with the group of capitalists. The undertaking was the more futile in that we were all practically the dupes of a new type of “industrial conspiracy” successfully inaugurated in Chicago by a close compact between the coal teamsters’ union and the coal team owners’ association, who had formed a kind of monopoly hitherto new to a monopoly-ridden public.

===Chinese===

市政厅市长办公室里，我们熬过一个漫长的星期天下午，先同劳工方面的人谈，再同那群资本家谈。这番功夫愈发徒劳，因为我们大家其实都是一桩新型「工业阴谋」的受骗者——运煤马车夫工会同运煤马车车主协会紧密勾结，在芝加哥把它办成了；他们结成的那路垄断，对受尽垄断之苦的公众来说都是新鲜的。

===Original===

The stormy teamsters’ strike, ostensibly undertaken in defense of the garment workers, but really arising from causes so obscure and dishonorable that they have never yet been made public, was the culmination of a type of trades-unions which had developed in Chicago during the preceding decade in which corruption had flourished almost as openly as it had previously done in the City Hall. This corruption sometimes took the form of grafting after the manner of Samuel Parks in New York; sometimes that of political deals in the “delivery of the labor vote”; and sometimes that of a combination between capital and labor hunting together. At various times during these years the better type of trades-unionists had made a firm stand against this corruption and a determined effort to eradicate it from the labor movement, not unlike the general reform effort of many American cities against political corruption. This reform movement in the Chicago Federation of Labor had its martyrs, and more than one man nearly lost his life through the “slugging” methods employed by the powerful corruptionists. And yet even in the midst of these things were found touching examples of fidelity to the earlier principles of brotherhood totally untouched by the corruption. At one time the scrubwomen in the downtown office buildings had a union of their own affiliated with the elevator men and the janitors. Although the union was used merely as a weapon in the fight of the coal teamsters against the use of natural gas in downtown buildings, it did not prevent the women from getting their first glimpse into the fellowship and the sense of protection which is the great gift of trades-unionism to the unskilled, unbefriended worker. I remember in a meeting held at Hull House one Sunday afternoon, that the president of a “local” of scrubwomen stood up to relate her experience. She told first of the long years in which the fear of losing her job and the fluctuating pay were harder to bear than the hard work itself, when she had regarded all the other women who scrubbed in the same building merely as rivals and was most afraid of the most miserable, because they offered to work for less and less as they were pressed harder and harder by debt.

===Chinese===

那场风暴般的卡车司机罢工，名义上是为声援成衣工人，实际上根由暧昧而不体面到从来没有公开过；它是一种工会类型的顶点——那种工会在芝加哥滋长了前此的十年，其间腐败得几乎同从前市政厅里的腐败一样公开。这腐败有时取纽约塞缪尔·帕克斯（Samuel Parks）式的敲诈；有时是「交付劳工选票」的政治交易；有时是资本与劳工合伙的围猎。这些年里，较好的那类工会员一再坚决顶住这种腐败，下狠心要把它从劳工运动里除掉，同许多美国城市反政治腐败的改革运动并无二致。芝加哥劳工联合会（Chicago Federation of Labor）里的这场改革运动有它的殉道者，不止一人险些丧命于有权有势的腐败分子使出的「闷棍」手段。然而就在这样的事当中，也还有动人的例子：对早年兄弟情义的信守，一点没有被腐败沾污。有一阵子，市中心写字楼里的擦洗女工有一个自己的工会，同开电梯的人和楼房杂工联合在一起。尽管这个工会不过被当作运煤马车夫反对市中心楼房用天然气这场斗殴里的一件兵器，它到底没有拦住女工们头一回窥见工会的团契与保障感——那是工联主义送给无技术的、无亲无友的工人的大礼物。记得一个星期天下午在赫尔会所开的会上，一个擦洗女工「分会」的主席站起来讲自己的经历。她先讲那些漫长的年头：怕丢活计、工钱起起落落，比活重本身还难熬；那时她把在同一栋楼里擦洗的所有别的女人只看作对头，最怕的是最苦的那几个——因为她们被债逼得越紧，肯做的工钱就越低。

===Original===

Then she told of the change that had come when the elevator men and even the lordly janitors had talked to her about an organization and had said that they must all stand together.
