She told how gradually she came to feel sure of her job and of her regular pay, and she was even starting to buy a house now that she could “calculate” how much she “could have for sure.” Neither she nor any of the other members knew that the same combination which had organized the scrubwomen into a union later destroyed it during a strike inaugurated for their own purposes.

That a Settlement is drawn into the labor issues of its city can seem remote to its purpose only to those who fail to realize that so far as the present industrial system thwarts our ethical demands, not only for social righteousness but for social order, a Settlement is committed to an effort to understand and, as far as possible, to alleviate it. That in this effort it should be drawn into fellowship with the local efforts of trades-unions is most obvious. This identity of aim apparently commits the Settlement in the public mind to all the faiths and works of actual trades-unions. Fellowship has so long implied similarity of creed that the fact that the Settlement often differs widely from the policy pursued by trades-unionists and clearly expresses that difference does not in the least change public opinion in regard to its identification. This is especially true in periods of industrial disturbance, although it is exactly at such moments that the trades-unionists themselves are suspicious of all but their “own kind.” It is during the much longer periods between strikes that the Settlement’s fellowship with trades-unions is most satisfactory in the agitation for labor legislation and similar undertakings. The first officers of the Chicago Woman’s Trades Union League were residents of Settlements, although they can claim little share in the later record the League made in securing the passage of the Illinois Ten-Hour Law for Women and in its many other fine undertakings.

===Chinese===

接着她讲起了变化：开电梯的人、连神气的杂工都跟她谈组织，说大家必须站在一起。她讲自己怎样渐渐对活计有了底、对固定的工钱有了底；如今她既然能「算出」自己「稳稳能有」多少，居然开始买房子了。她同其他会员谁都不知道：把擦洗女工组织成工会的那个联合体，后来又在一场为着自己目的发动的罢工里把它毁掉了。

一座定居所被卷进本城的劳工问题，只有那些不明白下面这层道理的人，才会觉得这离它的宗旨很远：只要现在的工业制度挫折着我们的伦理要求——不只社会公义的要求，还有社会秩序的要求——定居所就担起了一份努力：去理解它，并尽其可能去缓解它。在这份努力里，它同工会的本地努力结成伙伴，是最自然不过的。目标的一致，显然使定居所在公众心里同现实工会的一切信条与作为拴在了一起。「伙伴」久已意味着信条的一致，所以尽管定居所常常同工会员奉行的方针大相径庭，并且明白说出这不同，公众眼里它们是一体，分毫也改不过来。工业骚动期间尤其如此，尽管恰恰在这样的时刻，工会员自己除了「自己人」谁都疑心。倒是罢工之间长得多的时期里，定居所同工会的伙伴关系最见成效——在劳动立法的鼓动和诸如此类的事业上。芝加哥妇女工会同盟的头几任干事都是各定居所的居民，虽说后来同盟促成伊利诺伊州妇女十小时法（Illinois Ten-Hour Law for Women）通过、办成许多别的好事，这份功劳她们认领的份很小。

===Original===

Nevertheless the reaction of strikes upon Chicago Settlements affords an interesting study in social psychology. For whether Hull House is in any wise identified with the strike or not, makes no difference. When “Labor” is in disgrace we are always regarded as belonging to it and share the opprobrium. In the public excitement following the Pullman strike Hull House lost many friends; later the teamsters’ strike caused another such defection, although my office in both cases had been solely that of a duly appointed arbitrator.

There is, however, a certain comfort in the assumption I have often encountered that wherever one’s judgment might place the justice of a given situation, it is understood that one’s sympathy is not alienated by wrongdoing, and that through this sympathy one is still subject to vicarious suffering. I recall an incident during a turbulent Chicago strike which brought me much comfort. On the morning of the day of a luncheon to which I had accepted an invitation, the waitress, whom I did not know, said to my prospective hostess that she was sure I could not come. Upon being asked for her reason she replied that she had seen in the morning paper that the strikers had killed a “scab” and she was sure that I would feel quite too badly about such a thing to be able to keep a social engagement. In spite of the confused issues, she evidently realized my despair over the violence in a strike quite as definitely as if she had been told about it. Perhaps that sort of suffering and the attempt to interpret opposing forces to each other will long remain a function of the Settlement, unsatisfactory and difficult as the role often becomes.

===Chinese===

不过罢工加在芝加哥各定居所身上的反应，倒是社会心理学上一个有趣的研究题。因为赫尔会所同罢工有没有一点干系，全不相干：只要「劳工」蒙羞，我们就总被算作它一边，同担骂名。普尔曼罢工之后的公众激愤里，赫尔会所失掉了许多朋友；后来卡车司机罢工又造成一批这样的疏远，尽管这两案里我的身份都只是奉命任命的仲裁人。

不过有一种假定倒给人某种安慰——我常遇到这种假定：不管你对某一情势的是非判断站在哪一边，人们总认定你的同情不会因为人做了错事就断绝，而凭着这份同情，你仍免不了代人受苦。我记得芝加哥一场乱纷纷的罢工里的一件小事，给了我很大安慰。那天上午我原定赴一个午餐会，请客的东道主人家里的女侍——我并不认识她——对女主人说，我准来不了。问她缘故，她答道：晨报上登了，罢工的人打死了一个「工贼」；这样的事我一定难过得很，一定赴不了什么社交的约会。尽管是非纠缠不清，她显然同别人告诉她一样真切地明白，我对罢工中的暴力是何等绝望。也许这一种受苦、这一种把敌对双方互相诠释给对方的努力，还会长久是定居所的一种职分——尽管这个角色常常吃力不讨好。

===Original===

There has gradually developed between the various Settlements of Chicago a warm fellowship founded upon a like-mindedness resulting from similar experiences, quite as identity of interest and endeavor develop an enduring relation between the residents of the same Settlement. This sense of comradeship is never stronger than during the hardships and perplexities of a strike of unskilled workers revolting against the conditions which drag them even below the level of their European life. At such time the residents in various Settlements are driven to a standard of life argument running somewhat in this wise﻿—that as the very existence of the State depends upon the character of its citizens, therefore if certain industrial conditions are forcing the workers below the standard of decency, it becomes possible to deduce the right of State regulation. Even as late as the stockyard strike this line of argument was denounced as “socialism” although it has since been confirmed as wise statesmanship by a decision of the Supreme Court of the United States which was apparently secured through the masterly argument of the Brandeis brief in the Oregon ten-hour case.

In such wise the residents of an industrial neighborhood gradually comprehend the close connection of their own difficulties with national and even international movements. The residents in the Chicago Settlements became pioneer members in the American branch of the International League for Labor Legislation, because their neighborhood experiences had made them only too conscious of the dire need for protective legislation. In such a league, with its ardent members in every industrial nation of Europe, with its encouraging reports of the abolition of all night work for women in six European nations, with its careful observations on the results of employer’s liability legislation and protection of machinery, one becomes identified with a movement of worldwide significance and manifold manifestation.

===Chinese===

芝加哥各定居所之间，渐渐生发出一种温暖的伙伴情谊，它立在因经历相似而来的心意相通上，正如利益与事业的相同，使同一所定居所的居民之间结成经久的关系。这种同志之感，最强烈的时刻莫过于无技术工人反抗把他们拖到连欧洲生活水准都不如的境况的罢工的艰难与困顿之中。这种时候，各定居所的居民被逼到一条生活水准的论证上，大致是这样：既然国家的存在本身系于其公民的品性，那么如果某些工业境况正把工人压到体面以下，就可以推出国家规管的权利。迟至牲畜围场罢工的时候，这条论证还被骂作「社会主义」，可后来美国联邦最高法院的一项判决证实了它是明智的治国之才——那判决看来是靠布兰代斯诉讼摘要（Brandeis brief）在俄勒冈十小时案（Oregon ten-hour case）里的雄辩赢来的。

就这样，一个工业区街坊的居民渐渐懂得：自家的难处同全国性乃至国际性的运动紧相连。芝加哥各定居所的居民成了国际劳动立法同盟（International League for Labor Legislation）美国分会最早的成员，因为街坊的经历使他们太明白保护性立法的急迫需要。在这样一个同盟里——欧洲每个工业国里都有它热心的成员，有它那六国废除妇女一切夜间劳动的振奋人心的报告，有它对雇主责任法与机器防护成效的细心观察——一个人便同一桩具有世界意义、千姿百态的运动结成了一体。

## XI / 十一
===Original===

Immigrants and Their Children

From our very first months at Hull House we found it much easier to deal with the first generation of crowded city life than with the second or third, because it is more natural and cast in a simpler mold. The Italian and Bohemian peasants who live in Chicago still put on their bright holiday clothes on a Sunday and go to visit their cousins. They tramp along with at least a suggestion of having once walked over plowed fields and breathed country air. The second generation of city poor too often have no holiday clothes and consider their relations a “bad lot.” I have heard a drunken man in a maudlin stage babble of his good country mother and imagine he was driving the cows home, and I knew that his little son who laughed loud at him would be drunk earlier in life and would have no pastoral interlude to his ravings. Hospitality still survives among foreigners, although it is buried under false pride among the poorest Americans. One thing seemed clear in regard to entertaining immigrants; to preserve and keep whatever of value their past life contained and to bring them in contact with a better type of Americans. For several years, every Saturday evening the entire families of our Italian neighbors were our guests. These evenings were very popular during our first winters at Hull House. Many educated Italians helped us, and the house became known as a place where Italians were welcome and where national holidays were observed. They come to us with their petty lawsuits, sad relics of the vendetta, with their incorrigible boys, with their hospital cases, with their aspirations for American clothes, and with their needs for an interpreter.

An editor of an Italian paper made a genuine connection between us and the Italian colony, not only with the Neapolitans and the Sicilians of the immediate neighborhood, but with the educated *connazionali* throughout the city, until he went south to start an agricultural colony in Alabama, in the establishment of which Hull House heartily cooperated.

===Chinese===

移民与他们的子女

在赫尔馆（Hull House）的最初几个月里我们就发现，同拥挤城市生活的第一代打交道，比同第二代、第三代容易得多，因为第一代更自然，铸型也更简单。住在芝加哥的意大利和波希米亚农民，星期天仍会穿上鲜艳的节日衣裳去走亲戚。他们走路的样子，至少还留着一点当年走过犁过的田垄、呼吸过乡间空气的意思。城市贫民的第二代却往往没有节日衣裳，还把亲戚看作「一伙坏种」。我听过一个醉汉在伤感的阶段里絮絮叨叨说他乡下善良的母亲，想象自己正赶牛回家；我知道他那大声笑话他的小儿子，会更早喝醉，而且他的胡话中间不会有牧歌的插曲。好客之风在外国人中间还活着，尽管在美国最穷的人中间它被虚假的自尊埋没了。在款待移民这件事上，有一点似乎是清楚的：保住并留住他们过去生活中一切有价值的东西，让他们接触到更好的一类美国人。有几年，每逢星期六晚上，我们意大利邻居的全家都是我们的客人。这些晚会在赫尔馆的头几个冬天很受欢迎。许多受过教育的意大利人来帮我们，这座馆渐渐有了名声：一个欢迎意大利人的地方，一个过他们国庆节的地方。他们带着他们小小的官司——家族世仇的可悲遗风——带着他们不可救药的男孩、他们的医院病例、他们对美国衣裳的渴望、他们对译员的需要，来找我们。

一家意大利报纸的编辑在我们与意大利侨民聚居地之间搭起了真正的联系，不但联系着近邻的那不勒斯人和西西里人，也联系着全城受过教育的 *connazionali*（同胞），直到他南下到阿拉巴马去开办一个农业垦殖区——在那件事的创办上，赫尔馆是衷心合作的。

===Original===

Possibly the South Italians more than any other immigrants represent the pathetic stupidity of agricultural people crowded into city tenements, and we were much gratified when thirty peasant families were induced to move upon the land which they knew so well how to cultivate. The starting of this colony, however, was a very expensive affair in spite of the fact that the colonists purchased the land at two dollars an acre; they needed much more than raw land, and although it was possible to collect the small sums necessary to sustain them during the hard time of the first two years, we were fully convinced that undertakings of this sort could be conducted properly only by colonization societies such as England has established, or, better still, by enlarging the functions of the Federal Department of Immigration.

An evening similar in purpose to the one devoted to the Italians was organized for the Germans, in our first year. Owing to the superior education of our Teutonic guests and the clever leading of a cultivated German woman, these evenings reflected something of that cozy social intercourse which is found in its perfection in the fatherland. Our guests sang a great deal in the tender minor of the German folksong or in the rousing spirit of the Rhine, and they slowly but persistently pursued a course in German history and literature, recovering something of that poetry and romance which they had long since resigned with other good things. We found strong family affection between them and their English-speaking children, but their pleasures were not in common, and they seldom went out together. Perhaps the greatest value of the Settlement to them was in placing large and pleasant rooms with musical facilities at their disposal, and in reviving their almost forgotten enthusiams. I have seen sons and daughters stand in complete surprise as their mother’s knitting needles softly beat time to the song she was singing, or her worn face turned rosy under the hand-clapping as she made an old-fashioned curtsy at the end of a German poem. It was easy to fancy a growing touch of respect in her children’s manner to her, and a rising enthusiasm for German literature and reminiscence on the part of all the family, an effort to bring together the old life and the new, a respect for the older cultivation, and not quite so much assurance that the new was the best.

===Chinese===

也许南意大利人比任何别的移民都更能代表农业人口被塞进城市出租屋时那种令人心酸的愚顽；当三十户农民家庭被劝说到他们深知如何耕种的土地上去时，我们大为欣慰。不过这个垦殖区的创办是件非常费钱的事，尽管垦殖者们以每英亩两美元的价钱买了地；他们需要的远不止生地，虽然头两年艰难时期里维持他们所需的小笔款项还凑得出来，我们却深信：这类事业要办得妥当，只能靠英国已建立的那类殖民协会，或者——更好——靠扩大联邦移民部的职能。

在我们第一年里，为德国人办了一个与意大利人之夜目的相似的晚会。由于我们条顿客人教养较高，又有一位有教养的德国妇女的巧妙主持，这些晚会映出了一点故乡那种以完美形态存在的温馨社交。我们的客人大量唱歌，唱德国民歌柔婉的小调，或莱茵河畔昂扬的曲子；他们缓慢而不间断地修完了德国历史与文学的一门课程，找回了一点他们早已连同别的好东西一并舍弃的诗与传奇。我们发现他们与说英语的儿女之间感情深厚，但他们的乐趣并不相通，也很少一同出门。定居所对他们最大的价值，也许在于把宽大而愉快的、备有音乐设备的房间交他们使用，并唤醒他们几乎遗忘的热情。我见过做儿女的全然惊讶地站在一旁，看着母亲的织针随她唱的歌轻轻打拍子，看她念完一首德国诗、行一个老式的屈膝礼时，那张憔悴的脸在掌声里泛出红来。不难想象儿女对她添了几分敬意，全家对德国文学和旧事升起了一阵热情：一种把旧生活与新生活拉到一起的努力，一种对上一代教养的尊重，以及不再那么笃定新的就是最好的。

===Original===

This tendency upon the part of the older immigrants to lose the amenities of European life without sharing those of America has often been deplored by keen observers from the home countries. When Professor Masurek of Prague gave a course of lectures in the University of Chicago, he was much distressed over the materialism into which the Bohemians of Chicago had fallen. The early immigrants had been so stirred by the opportunity to own real estate, an appeal perhaps to the Slavic land hunger, and their energies had become so completely absorbed in moneymaking that all other interests had apparently dropped away. And yet I recall a very touching incident in connection with a lecture Professor Masurek gave at Hull House, in which he had appealed to his countrymen to arouse themselves from this tendency to fall below their home civilization and to forget the great enthusiasm which had united them into the Pan-Slavic Movement. A Bohemian widow who supported herself and her two children by scrubbing, hastily sent her youngest child to purchase, with the twenty-five cents which was to have supplied them with food the next day, a bunch of red roses which she presented to the lecturer in appreciation of his testimony to the reality of the things of the spirit.

===Chinese===

老一辈移民丢掉欧洲生活的种种美好、却又不曾分享美国的，这种倾向常被来自母国的敏锐观察家们痛心疾首地提及。布拉格的马萨雷克教授（Professor Masurek）在芝加哥大学（University of Chicago）讲学时，为芝加哥的波希米亚人陷进的物质主义深感痛心。早期移民被置产的机会搅动得如此厉害——这也许是对斯拉夫土地渴望的召唤——他们的精力又被赚钱完全吸干，以致别的一切兴味显然都掉了。然而我记得与马萨雷克教授在赫尔馆一次讲演有关的一件很动人的事，那次他呼吁同胞们从这种落到本国文明之下的倾向中振作起来，不要忘记曾把他们团结进泛斯拉夫运动的伟大热情。一个靠擦洗养活自己和两个孩子的波希米亚寡妇，急忙打发她最小的孩子拿上原本要供他们第二天吃饭的两毛五分钱，去买了一束红玫瑰献给讲演人，感谢他见证了精神事物之真实。

===Original===

An overmastering desire to reveal the humbler immigrant parents to their own children lay at the base of what has come to be called the Hull House Labor Museum. This was first suggested to my mind one early spring day when I saw an old Italian woman, her distaff against her homesick face, patiently spinning a thread by the simple stick spindle so reminiscent of all southern Europe. I was walking down Polk Street, perturbed in spirit, because it seemed so difficult to come into genuine relations with the Italian women and because they themselves so often lost their hold upon their Americanized children. It seemed to me that Hull House ought to be able to devise some educational enterprise which should build a bridge between European and American experiences in such wise as to give them both more meaning and a sense of relation. I meditated that perhaps the power to see life as a whole is more needed in the immigrant quarter of a large city than anywhere else, and that the lack of this power is the most fruitful source of misunderstanding between European immigrants and their children, as it is between them and their American neighbors; and why should that chasm between fathers and sons, yawning at the feet of each generation, be made so unnecessarily cruel and impassable to these bewildered immigrants? Suddenly I looked up and saw the old woman with her distaff, sitting in the sun on the steps of a tenement house. She might have served as a model for one of Michelangelo’s Fates, but her face brightened as I passed and, holding up her spindle for me to see, she called out that when she had spun a little more yarn, she would knit a pair of stockings for her goddaughter. The occupation of the old woman gave me the clue that was needed. Could we not interest the young people working in the neighborhood factories in these older forms of industry, so that, through their own parents and grandparents, they would find a dramatic representation of the inherited resources of their daily occupation. If these young people could actually see that the complicated machinery of the factory had been evolved from simple tools, they might at least make a beginning toward that education which Dr. Dewey defines as “a continuing reconstruction of experience.” They might also lay a foundation for reverence of the past which Goethe declares to be the basis of all sound progress.

===Chinese===

把卑微的移民父母揭示给他们自己的子女看——这个压倒一切的愿望，便是后来所谓赫尔馆劳工博物馆（Labor Museum）的根基。这念头最初浮上我心头，是在早春的一天，我看见一个意大利老太太，纺杆贴着她思乡的脸，用那根令人想起整个南欧的简朴纺锤耐心地纺着线。我正沿着波尔克街（Polk Street）走，心里烦乱，因为同意大利妇女建立真正的关系显得如此之难，而她们自己又常常抓不住她们美国化的儿女。在我看来，赫尔馆应当能设计出某种教育事业，在欧洲经验与美国经验之间架一座桥，让两者都多得一点意义和关联感。我暗想，把生活当作整体来看的能力，也许在大城市的移民区比在任何别的地方都更需要；这种能力的欠缺，是欧洲移民与他们子女之间——正如与他们美国邻居之间——误解的最肥沃的源泉；而那道横在父子之间、在每一代人脚下张着口的鸿沟，为什么偏要弄得对这些惶惑的移民如此不必要地残忍、不可逾越？忽然我抬起头，看见那个拿着纺杆的老妇人，坐在一座出租屋的台阶上晒太阳。她大可以当米开朗琪罗（Michelangelo）命运三女神之一的模特，可她见我走过便喜色满面，举起纺锤给我看，喊道等她再多纺一点纱，就要给她的教女织一双袜子。老妇人的活计给了我需要的线索。我们能不能让在附近工厂做工的年轻人对这些更老的工业形态发生兴趣，好让他们通过自己的父母祖辈，找到他们日常职业所承继的资源的戏剧化呈现？这些年轻人如果真能看到工厂里复杂的机器是由简单的工具演进而来，他们至少可以开始接受杜威博士（Dr. Dewey）定义为「经验的不断重构」的那种教育。他们也可以为歌德（Goethe）所说的一切健全进步之基础——对过去的敬意——打下根基。

===Original===

My exciting walk on Polk Street was followed by many talks with Dr. Dewey and with one of the teachers in his school who was a resident at Hull House. Within a month a room was fitted up to which we might invite those of our neighbors who were possessed of old crafts and who were eager to use them.

We found in the immediate neighborhood at least four varieties of these most primitive methods of spinning and three distinct variations of the same spindle in connection with wheels. It was possible to put these seven into historic sequence and order and to connect the whole with the present method of factory spinning. The same thing was done for weaving, and on every Saturday evening a little exhibit was made of these various forms of labor in the textile industry. Within one room a Syrian woman, a Greek, an Italian, a Russian, and an Irishwoman enabled even the most casual observer to see that there is no break in orderly evolution if we look at history from the industrial standpoint; that industry develops similarly and peacefully year by year among the workers of each nation, heedless of differences in language, religion, and political experiences.

And then we grew ambitious and arranged lectures upon industrial history. I remember that after an interesting lecture upon the industrial revolution in England and a portrayal of the appalling conditions throughout the weaving districts of the north, which resulted from the hasty gathering of the weavers into the new towns, a Russian tailor in the audience was moved to make a speech. He suggested that whereas time had done much to alleviate the first difficulties in the transition of weaving from hand work to steam power, that in the application of steam to sewing we are still in our first stages, illustrated by the isolated woman who tries to support herself by hand needlework at home until driven out by starvation, as many of the hand weavers had been.

===Chinese===

波尔克街上那场令我激动的散步之后，是同杜威博士以及他学校里一位住在赫尔馆的教师的多次谈话。不出一个月，一间屋子装备起来，我们可以邀请邻居中那些怀有旧手艺、又乐于使用它们的人。

我们在紧邻的街区就找到了至少四种最原始的纺纱方法，以及与纺车相连的同一纺锤的三种不同变体。这七样可以排进历史的序列和秩序里去，整个又能同工厂纺纱的现行方法接起来。织布也照此办理，每逢星期六晚上就把这些纺织工业的各种劳动形态小小展览一回。在一间屋子里，一个叙利亚女人、一个希腊人、一个意大利人、一个俄国人、一个爱尔兰女人，使最不经意的旁观者也能看出：从工业的观点看历史，秩序井然的演进没有断裂；工业在每一国工人中年复一年相似而和平地发展着，全不理会语言、宗教和政治经历的差异。

后来我们胆子大了，安排起工业史的讲演来。我记得，一次关于英国工业革命的有趣讲演之后——其中描绘了织工们被匆匆赶进新城镇后北方织布区骇人的状况——听众里一个俄国裁缝起来发言。他说，织布从手工过渡到蒸汽，时间已经大大缓和了最初的困难；而蒸汽应用于缝纫，我们还在最初阶段，其例证便是那个孤零零的女人，靠在家里做针线活养活自己，直到被饥饿赶出去，正如许多手工织工曾被赶出去一样。

===Original===

The historical analogy seemed to bring a certain comfort to the tailor, as did a chart upon the wall showing the infinitesimal amount of time that steam had been applied to manufacturing processes compared to the centuries of hand labor. Human progress is slow and perhaps never more cruel than in the advance of industry, but is not the worker comforted by knowing that other historical periods have existed similar to the one in which he finds himself, and that the readjustment may be shortened and alleviated by judicious action; and is he not entitled to the solace which an artistic portrayal of the situation might give him? I remember the evening of the tailor’s speech that I felt reproached because no poet or artist has endeared the sweaters’ victim to us as George Eliot has made us love the belated weaver, Silas Marner. The textile museum is connected directly with the basket weaving, sewing, millinery, embroidery, and dressmaking constantly being taught at Hull House, and so far as possible with the other educational departments; we have also been able to make a collection of products, of early implements, and of photographs which are full of suggestion. Yet far beyond its direct educational value, we prize it because it so often puts the immigrants into the position of teachers, and we imagine that it affords them a pleasant change from the tutelage in which all Americans, including their own children, are so apt to hold them. I recall a number of Russian women working in a sewing room near Hull House, who heard one Christmas week that the House was going to give a party to which they might come. They arrived one afternoon, when, unfortunately, there was no party on hand and, although the residents did their best to entertain them with impromptu music and refreshments, it was quite evident that they were greatly disappointed. Finally it was suggested that they be shown the Labor Museum﻿—where gradually the thirty sodden, tired women were transformed. They knew how to use the spindles and were delighted to find the Russian spinning frame. Many of them had never seen the spinning wheel, which has not penetrated to certain parts of Russia, and they regarded it as a new and wonderful invention.

===Chinese===

这个历史的类比似乎给了裁缝某种安慰，墙上那张图表也是如此——它显示出，与几百年的手工劳动相比，蒸汽应用于制造过程的时间何其短促。人类的进步是缓慢的，也许从没有比在工业的前进中更残酷的了；可是，知道自己所处的时期在历史上另有相似的时期存在、知道凭着审慎的行动可以缩短和减轻那种重新调整，工人不就得了安慰吗？对这处境作一番艺术化描绘所能给他的慰藉，他不也有权享受吗？我记得裁缝发言那晚，我感到一种责备，因为还没有诗人或艺术家使我们爱上血汗制度的牺牲者，像乔治·艾略特（George Eliot）使我们爱上那个迟到的织工西拉斯·马南（Silas Marner）那样。纺织博物馆直接连着赫尔馆不断在教的编篮、缝纫、女帽、刺绣和裁剪，并尽可能连着其他教育部门；我们还收集了一批产品、早期器具和照片，满含启发。然而远超其直接教育价值的，是我们珍视它，因为它常常把移民放到教师的位置上；我们可以想象，这给了他们一个愉快的转变——从一切美国人（包括他们自己的儿女）都太容易把他们置于其中的被监护地位里转出来。我记得一批在赫尔馆附近一间缝纫房做工的俄国女人，圣诞节的那个星期听说馆里要办一个她们可以来的聚会。她们一个下午到了，不巧当时并没有聚会；居民们虽然尽力用即兴的音乐和茶点招待她们，她们显然大失所望。最后有人提议带她们去看劳工博物馆——在那里，三十个疲惫麻木的女人渐渐变了样。她们会用那些纺锤，又惊喜地发现了俄国纺架。她们中许多人从未见过纺车——它还没有深入俄国的某些地区——她们把它看作一项新奇的伟大发明。

===Original===

They turned up their dresses to show their homespun petticoats; they tried the looms; they explained the difficulty of the old patterns; in short, from having been stupidly entertained, they themselves did the entertaining. Because of a direct appeal to former experiences, the immigrant visitors were able for the moment to instruct their American hostesses in an old and honored craft, as was indeed becoming to their age and experience.

In some such ways as these have the Labor Museum and the shops pointed out the possibilities which Hull House has scarcely begun to develop, of demonstrating that culture is an understanding of the long-established occupations and thoughts of men, of the arts with which they have solaced their toil. A yearning to recover for the household arts something of their early sanctity and meaning arose strongly within me one evening when I was attending a Passover Feast to which I had been invited by a Jewish family in the neighborhood, where the traditional and religious significance of the woman’s daily activity was still retained. The kosher food the Jewish mother spread before her family had been prepared according to traditional knowledge and with constant care in the use of utensils; upon her had fallen the responsibility to make all ready according to Mosaic instructions that the great crisis in a religious history might be fittingly set forth by her husband and son. Aside from the grave religious significance in the ceremony, my mind was filled with shifting pictures of woman’s labor with which travel makes one familiar; the Indian women grinding grain outside of their huts as they sing praises to the sun and rain; a file of white-clad Moorish women whom I had once seen waiting their turn at a well in Tangiers; south Italian women kneeling in a row along the stream and beating their wet clothes against the smooth white stones; the milking, the gardening, the marketing in thousands of hamlets, which are such direct expressions of the solicitude and affection at the basis of all family life.

===Chinese===

她们撩起裙子给看自家织的衬裙；她们试了织机；她们讲解旧花样的难处；总之，本来是被呆板地款待的，这会儿是她们自己来款待人了。由于直接诉诸昔日的经验，这些移民来客一时间竟能在一门古老而受人敬重的手艺上教导她们的女主人，这本来才配得上她们的年纪与阅历。

劳工博物馆和那些工场正是以这样一些方式，指出了赫尔馆几乎还没着手开发的种种可能：证明文化就是对人类久已确立的职业和思想的了解，对他们借以慰藉劳苦的种种艺术的了解。一天晚上，我应邀出席邻里一户犹太人家的逾越节宴，那里女人日常活动的传统与宗教意义还保留着；当晚，为家务艺术找回一点其早期神圣性和意义的渴望，在我心里强烈升起。犹太母亲摆在全家面前的洁净食物，是按传统知识备制的，器具的使用也处处经心；落在她身上的责任，是按摩西的训示把一切备妥，好让宗教历史上的那场大危机由她的丈夫和儿子合宜地讲述出来。除了仪式本身庄严的宗教意义，我脑子里满是旅行让人熟识的妇人劳动的种种变换的画面：印第安女人在棚屋外磨谷，一面唱着赞美太阳和雨的歌；一列白衣的摩尔女人，我曾在丹吉尔（Tangiers）的井边见她们排队等候；南意大利的女人跪成一排沿着溪流，把湿衣裳在光滑的白石头上捶打；还有千百个村落里的挤奶、种园、赶集——它们都是一切家庭生活根基上那种挂念与深情的直接表达。

===Original===

There has been some testimony that the Labor Museum has revealed the charm of woman’s primitive activities. I recall a certain Italian girl who came every Saturday evening to a cooking class in the same building in which her mother spun in the Labor Museum exhibit; and yet Angelina always left her mother at the front door while she herself went around to a side door because she did not wish to be too closely identified in the eyes of the rest of the cooking class with an Italian woman who wore a kerchief over her head, uncouth boots, and short petticoats. One evening, however, Angelina saw her mother surrounded by a group of visitors from the School of Education who much admired the spinning, and she concluded from their conversation that her mother was “the best stick-spindle spinner in America.” When she inquired from me as to the truth of this deduction, I took occasion to describe the Italian village in which her mother had lived, something of her free life, and how, because of the opportunity she and the other women of the village had to drop their spindles over the edge of a precipice, they had developed a skill in spinning beyond that of the neighboring towns. I dilated somewhat on the freedom and beauty of that life﻿—how hard it must be to exchange it all for a two-room tenement, and to give up a beautiful homespun kerchief for an ugly department store hat. I intimated it was most unfair to judge her by these things alone, and that while she must depend on her daughter to learn the new ways, she also had a right to expect her daughter to know something of the old ways.

That which I could not convey to the child, but upon which my own mind persistently dwelt, was that her mother’s whole life had been spent in a secluded spot under the rule of traditional and narrowly localized observances, until her very religion clung to local sanctities﻿—to the shrine before which she had always prayed, to the pavement and walls of the low vaulted church﻿—and then suddenly she was torn from it all and literally put out to sea, straight away from the solid habits of her religious and domestic life, and she now walked timidly but with poignant sensibility upon a new and strange shore.

===Chinese===

有一些证据表明，劳工博物馆显示出了女人原始活动的魅力。我记得一个意大利姑娘，每星期六晚上来上烹调课，课就在她母亲做纺纱展示的劳工博物馆所在的那座楼里；可是安杰莉娜（Angelina）总是把母亲留在前门，自己绕到侧门进去，因为她不愿在烹调班其他人的眼里，同这样一个意大利女人太紧密地认在一起——头包三角巾，脚登粗笨的靴子，衬裙短短的。可是有一个晚上，安杰莉娜看见她母亲被一群教育学校（School of Education）来的参观者围住，他们对纺纱大为赞赏，她从他们的谈话里断定，她母亲是「美国最好的纺锤纺纱能手」。她来问我这推断是真是假，我趁势描述了她母亲住过的那个意大利村庄，说了一点那种自由的生活，又说因为她和村里别的女人有机会把纺锤垂到悬崖边上纺，她们练出了超过邻镇的手艺。我又稍稍铺陈了那种生活的自由与美好——要拿这一切去换一间两居室的出租屋、拿一条漂亮的手织头巾去换一顶难看的百货公司帽子，该多么难；我暗示，只凭这些东西评判她母亲是最不公平的，她固然要靠女儿学会新的方式，她同样有权指望女儿懂得一点旧的方式。

我无法向这孩子传达、自己心里却盘桓不去的是：她母亲的一生都消磨在一个僻塞的角落，处在传统而狭隘的地方性仪轨之下，直到她的宗教本身都黏着在当地的神圣之物上——黏着她一向祈祷的神龛，黏着那低矮穹顶教堂的地面和墙壁——然后她忽然被从这一切里扯出来，简直是放逐出海，径直离开了她宗教生活和家庭生活的坚实习惯，如今她怯生生、却带着尖锐的感受力，行走在一片崭新而陌生的岸上。

===Original===

It was easy to see that the thought of her mother with any other background than that of the tenement was new to Angelina, and at least two things resulted; she allowed her mother to pull out of the big box under the bed the beautiful homespun garments which had been previously hidden away as uncouth; and she openly came into the Labor Museum by the same door as did her mother, proud at least of the mastery of the craft which had been so much admired.

A club of necktie workers formerly meeting at Hull House persistently resented any attempt on the part of their director to improve their minds. The president once said that she “wouldn’t be caught dead at a lecture,” that she came to the club “to get some fun out of it,” and indeed it was most natural that she should crave recreation after a hard day’s work. One evening I saw the entire club listening to quite a stiff lecture in the Labor Museum and to my rather wicked remark to the president that I was surprised to see her enjoying a lecture, she replied that she did not call this a lecture, she called this “getting next to the stuff you work with all the time.” It was perhaps the sincerest tribute we have ever received as to the success of the undertaking.

The Labor Museum continually demanded more space as it was enriched by a fine textile exhibit lent by the Field Museum, and later by carefully selected specimens of basketry from the Philippines. The shops have finally included a group of three or four women, Irish, Italian, Danish, who have become a permanent working force in the textile department which has developed into a self-supporting industry through the sale of its homespun products.

===Chinese===

不难看出，母亲还有出租屋之外的任何背景这个想法，对安杰莉娜是新鲜的，而且至少结出了两个果实：她允许母亲从床底的大箱子里，把那些先前当作粗鄙而藏起来的漂亮手织衣裳翻出来；她公开地同母亲走同一道门进出劳工博物馆，为那门大受赞赏的手艺的娴熟，至少感到骄傲。

一个从前在赫尔馆聚会的领带女工俱乐部，一味怨恨指导人想要提高她们心智的任何企图。会长有一次说，她「死也不会让人逮着去听讲演」，她来俱乐部是「为了找点乐子」；辛苦一天之后渴望消遣，这原是最自然的。一天晚上我看见整个俱乐部在劳工博物馆听一场相当艰深的讲演，我对会长说了句相当刻薄的话，说想不到她竟听得津津有味；她答道，她不把这叫讲演，她把这叫「弄明白你成天打交道的那些东西」。这也许是我们得到的、关于这项事业成功与否的最真诚的赞辞。

劳工博物馆不断要求更多的空间，因为它陆续得到菲尔德博物馆（Field Museum）借展的一批精美纺织品，后来又有菲律宾的精选编篮标本。那些工场最后留住了一小群三四个女人——爱尔兰人、意大利人、丹麦人——成了纺织部的固定工班；这个部门靠出售手织产品，已发展成一门自给的营生。

===Original===

These women and a few men, who come to the museum to utilize their European skill in pottery, metal, and wood, demonstrate that immigrant colonies might yield to our American life something very valuable, if their resources were intelligently studied and developed. I recall an Italian, who had decorated the doorposts of his tenement with a beautiful pattern he had previously used in carving the reredos of a Neapolitan church, who was “fired” by his landlord on the ground of destroying property. His feelings were hurt, not so much that he had been put out of his house, as that his work had been so disregarded; and he said that when people traveled in Italy they liked to look at wood carvings but that in America “they only made money out of you.”

Sometimes the suppression of the instinct of workmanship is followed by more disastrous results. A Bohemian whose little girl attended classes at Hull House, in one of his periodic drunken spells had literally almost choked her to death, and later had committed suicide when in delirium tremens. His poor wife, who stayed a week at Hull House after the disaster until a new tenement could be arranged for her, one day showed me a gold ring which her husband had made for their betrothal. It exhibited the most exquisite workmanship, and she said that although in the old country he had been a goldsmith, in America he had for twenty years shoveled coal in a furnace room of a large manufacturing plant; that whenever she saw one of his “restless fits,” which preceded his drunken periods, “coming on,” if she could provide him with a bit of metal and persuade him to stay at home and work at it, he was all right and the time passed without disaster, but that “nothing else would do it.” This story threw a flood of light upon the dead man’s struggle and on the stupid maladjustment which had broken him down. Why had we never been told? Why had our interest in the remarkable musical ability of his child blinded us to the hidden artistic ability of the father? We had forgotten that a long-established occupation may form the very foundations of the moral life, that the art with which a man has solaced his toil may be the salvation of his uncertain temperament.

===Chinese===

这些女人，加上几个来馆里利用他们欧洲技艺做陶、做金属、做木工的男人，证明了移民聚居区倘使他们的资源得到明智的研究和开发，原可以向我们的美国生活贡献非常宝贵的东西。我记得一个意大利人，他用先前雕刻那不勒斯一座教堂祭坛后屏时用过的美丽花样，装饰了自己出租屋的门柱，却被房东以毁坏财物为由「炒」了出去。他伤心，倒不怎么因为被赶出了屋子，而是因为他的手艺如此不受理会；他说，人们在意大利旅行时喜欢看木雕，可是在美国「人家只会从你身上赚钱」。

有时，手艺本能的被压制接着来的是更悲惨的结果。一个波希米亚人，他的小女孩在赫尔馆上课；他一阵周期性的酒狂发作，真的差点把她掐死，后来又在震颤性谵妄中自尽了。他可怜的妻子，在那场灾祸之后在赫尔馆住了一个星期，直到替她安排好新的住处；有一天她给我看一枚她丈夫为订婚打的金戒指。那手工精美绝伦，她说，虽然在故国他是个金匠，在美国他却在一家大制造厂的锅炉房铲了二十年煤；又说，每当他酒疯之前那种「烦躁劲儿」「要来」的时候，她只要能找一小块金属、劝他待在家里做一做，他就没事，那段日子就能平安过去，可是「别的什么都不管用」。这个故事把那死去的男人的挣扎、把击垮他的那种愚蠢的失调，照得通亮。为什么早没有人告诉我们？为什么我们对他孩子出众音乐才能的关切，弄瞎了我们的眼，看不见父亲深藏的艺术才能？我们忘记了：一种久已确立的职业可以正是道德生活的根基；一个人借以慰藉劳苦的艺术，可以正是他动荡气质的救星。

===Original===

There are many examples of touching fidelity to immigrant parents on the part of their grown children; a young man who day after day attends ceremonies which no longer express his religious convictions and who makes his vain effort to interest his Russian Jewish father in social problems; a daughter who might earn much more money as a stenographer could she work from Monday morning till Saturday night, but who quietly and docilely makes neckties for low wages because she can thus abstain from work Saturdays to please her father; these young people, like poor Maggie Tulliver, through many painful experiences have reached the conclusion that pity, memory, and faithfulness are natural ties with paramount claims.

This faithfulness, however, is sometimes ruthlessly imposed upon by immigrant parents who, eager for money and accustomed to the patriarchal authority of peasant households, hold their children in a stern bondage which requires a surrender of all their wages and concedes no time or money for pleasures.

There are many convincing illustrations that this parental harshness often results in juvenile delinquency. A Polish boy of seventeen came to Hull House one day to ask a contribution of fifty cents “towards a flower piece for the funeral of an old Hull House club boy.” A few questions made it clear that the object was fictitious, whereupon the boy broke down and half-defiantly stated that he wanted to buy two twenty-five cent tickets, one for his girl and one for himself, to a dance of the Benevolent Social Twos; that he hadn’t a penny of his own although he had worked in a brass foundry for three years and had been advanced twice, because he always had to give his pay envelope unopened to his father; “just look at the clothes he buys me” was his concluding remark.

===Chinese===

成年的子女对移民父母表现出动人的忠诚，这样的例子很多：一个小伙子，日复一日出席那些已不再表达他宗教信念的仪式，徒劳地想让他俄国犹太人的父亲对种种社会问题发生兴趣；一个女儿，倘若从星期一早晨干到星期六夜里，本可以挣多得多的速记员薪水，却温顺安静地做着低工资的领带，因为这样她可以星期六不干活，讨父亲的欢心；这些年轻人，同可怜的玛吉·塔利弗（Maggie Tulliver）一样，经过许多痛苦的经历得出了结论：怜悯、记忆和忠诚是带有至高要求的天性纽带。

然而这种忠诚有时被移民父母无情地利用；他们渴望金钱，又惯于农家家长的权威，把孩子攥在严酷的束缚里：工资必须悉数交出，玩乐的时间和金钱一概不给。

有许多有说服力的事例说明，这种父母的严酷常常造成少年犯罪。一个十七岁的波兰男孩有一天来赫尔馆，求捐五毛钱，「给一个从前赫尔馆俱乐部小伙子的葬礼凑个花圈」。几个问题一问就明白那名目是编的，男孩于是垮下来，半带挑衅地说，他想买两张两毛五的票，一张给他女朋友、一张给他自己，去参加「仁爱社交二人会」的舞会；他在黄铜铸造厂干了三年、还加过两次薪，却一个子儿也没有，因为他的工资袋总是原封不动交给他父亲；「看看他给我买的衣裳吧」是他收尾的话。

===Original===

Perhaps the girls are held even more rigidly. In a recent investigation of two hundred working girls it was found that only five percent had the use of their own money and that sixty-two percent turned in all they earned, literally every penny, to their mothers. It was through this little investigation that we first knew Marcella, a pretty young German girl who helped her widowed mother year after year to care for a large family of younger children. She was content for the most part although her mother’s old-country notions of dress gave her but an infinitesimal amount of her own wages to spend on her clothes, and she was quite sophisticated as to proper dressing because she sold silk in a neighborhood department store. Her mother approved of the young man who was showing her various attentions and agreed that Marcella should accept his invitation to a ball, but would allow her not a penny toward a new gown to replace one impossibly plain and shabby. Marcella spent a sleepless night and wept bitterly, although she well knew that the doctor’s bill for the children’s scarlet fever was not yet paid. The next day as she was cutting off three yards of shining pink silk, the thought came to her that it would make her a fine new waist to wear to the ball. She wistfully saw it wrapped in paper and carelessly stuffed into the muff of the purchaser, when suddenly the parcel fell upon the floor. No one was looking and quick as a flash the girl picked it up and pushed it into her blouse. The theft was discovered by the relentless department store detective who, for “the sake of example,” insisted upon taking the case into court. The poor mother wept bitter tears over this downfall of her “*frommes Madchen*” and no one had the heart to tell her of her own blindness.

===Chinese===

姑娘们也许被攥得更紧。在最近一次对两百个职业少女的调查中发现，只有百分之五能支配自己挣的钱，百分之六十二把挣来的钱分文不差地上交给母亲。正是通过这次小小的调查我们认识了马尔切拉（Marcella），一个漂亮的德国姑娘，年复一年帮着她守寡的母亲照料一大家年幼的弟妹。她大体上知足，尽管母亲旧大陆的衣着观念，只让她自己工资里极小的一点花在衣裳上；而她对穿着其实相当在行，因为她在附近一家百货公司卖绸缎。母亲认可那个对她献殷勤的年轻人，也同意马尔切拉接受他舞会的邀请，却一分钱也不肯给她添置新衣，去替换那件素得没法穿、旧得没法看的。马尔切拉彻夜未眠，哭得很伤心，尽管她明知道孩子们的猩红热的医生账单还没有付。第二天，她正剪下三码闪光的粉红绸缎，忽然起了一个念头：这足可给自己做一件赴舞会的新衣。她怅然看着它被纸包好、被买主随手塞进手筒，忽然那包裹掉在了地上。没有人看，那姑娘闪电一样把它拾起来，塞进了自己的罩衫。偷窃被毫不留情的百货公司侦探发现了；为了「以儆效尤」，他坚持把案子送进法庭。可怜的母亲为她那「*frommes Madchen*」（虔诚的姑娘）的堕落流下苦泪，谁也不忍告诉她，她自己是如何盲目。

===Original===

I know a Polish boy whose earnings were all given to his father who gruffly refused all requests for pocket money. One Christmas his little sisters, having been told by their mother that they were too poor to have any Christmas presents, appealed to the big brother as to one who was earning money of his own. Flattered by the implication, but at the same time quite impecunious, the night before Christmas he nonchalantly walked through a neighboring department store and stole a manicure set for one little sister and a string of beads for the other. He was caught at the door by the house detective as one of those children whom each local department store arrests in the weeks before Christmas at the daily rate of eight to twenty. The youngest of these offenders are seldom taken into court but are either sent home with a warning or turned over to the officers of the Juvenile Protective Association. Most of these premature law breakers are in search of Americanized clothing and others are only looking for playthings. They are all distracted by the profusion and variety of the display, and their moral sense is confused by the general air of openhandedness.

===Chinese===

我认识一个波兰男孩，收入全交给父亲，父亲对零花钱的请求一律粗声回绝。有一年圣诞节，他的小妹妹们听母亲说家里太穷、置办不起圣诞礼物，便去求大哥，把他当成一个自己挣着钱的人。他被这层意思捧得高兴，同时又身无分文，圣诞前夜他若无其事地走过邻近一家百货公司，偷了一套指甲修具给一个小妹妹、一串珠子给另一个。他在门口被店探逮住，成了圣诞前几个星期里各家本地百货公司每天以八到二十的数目逮捕的那些孩子之一。这些犯事的孩子里年纪最小的很少被送进法庭，或者被警告一顿放回家，或者交给少年保护协会（Juvenile Protective Association）的官员。这些早熟的犯法者，多数要的是美国化的衣裳，另一些不过想要玩具。他们全被陈列品的丰饶多样弄得心神迷乱，他们的道德感又被那种到处大方的气派搅糊涂了。

===Original===

These disastrous efforts are not unlike those of many younger children who are constantly arrested for petty thieving because they are too eager to take home food or fuel which will relieve the distress and need they so constantly hear discussed. The coal on the wagons, the vegetables displayed in front of the grocery shops, the very wooden blocks in the loosened street paving are a challenge to their powers to help out at home. A Bohemian boy who was out on parole from the old detention home of the Juvenile Court itself, brought back five stolen chickens to the matron for Sunday dinner, saying that he knew the Committee were “having a hard time to fill up so many kids and perhaps these fowl would help out.” The honest immigrant parents, totally ignorant of American laws and municipal regulations, often send a child to pick up coal on the railroad tracks or to stand at three o’clock in the morning before the side door of a restaurant which gives away broken food, or to collect grain for the chickens at the base of elevators and standing cars. The latter custom accounts for the large number of boys arrested for breaking the seals on grain freight cars. It is easy for a child thus trained to accept the proposition of a junk dealer to bring him bars of iron stored in freight yards. Four boys quite recently had thus carried away and sold to one man two tons of iron.

Four fifths of the children brought into the Juvenile Court in Chicago are the children of foreigners. The Germans are the greatest offenders, Polish next. Do their children suffer from the excess of virtue in those parents so eager to own a house and lot? One often sees a grasping parent in the court, utterly broken down when the Americanized youth who has been brought to grief clings as piteously to his peasant father as if he were still a frightened little boy in the steerage.

===Chinese===

这些倒霉的企图，同许多更小的孩子没有什么两样——那些孩子不断因小偷小摸被捕，因为他们太急切地想带回食物或燃料，好纾解他们成天听见大人议论的困苦与匮乏。运煤车上的煤，杂货店门前摆的菜，连松动的路面铺的木块，都是对他们「帮衬家里」本事的挑战。一个从少年法庭旧拘留所假释在外的波希米亚男孩，带回五只偷来的鸡给女管家做星期天的正餐，说他知道委员会「正为填饱这么多孩子的肚子发愁，这些鸡也许能帮上忙」。诚实的移民父母，对美国法律和市政规章全然无知，常常打发孩子去铁路沿线捡煤，或凌晨三点去一家分发残羹的餐馆侧门口站着，或去粮仓底下和停着的车皮边给鸡捡粮食。后一种习惯解释了为什么大量男孩因撬开运粮车皮的封条被捕。这样训练出来的孩子，很容易接受收破烂的贩子的提议，把堆存在货场里的铁条搬给他。就在最近，四个男孩就这样搬走并卖给了同一个人两吨铁。

芝加哥少年法庭受理的孩子，五分之四是外国人的孩子。德国人的孩子犯事最多，波兰人次之。他们的孩子是吃了那些急着一栋房一块地的父母过分美德的亏吗？人在法庭上常看见一个贪心的家长彻底垮掉，那时闯了祸的美国化青年紧紧攀着他农民父亲，可怜巴巴的，仿佛他还是统舱里一个吓坏的小男孩。

===Original===

Many of these children have come to grief through their premature fling into city life, having thrown off parental control as they have impatiently discarded foreign ways. Boys of ten and twelve will refuse to sleep at home, preferring the freedom of an old brewery vault or an empty warehouse to the obedience required by their parents, and for days these boys will live on the milk and bread which they steal from the back porches after the early morning delivery. Such children complain that there is “no fun” at home. One little chap who was given a vacant lot to cultivate by the City Garden Association insisted upon raising only popcorn and tried to present the entire crop to Hull House “to be used for the parties,” with the stipulation that he would have “to be invited every single time.” Then there are little groups of dissipated young men who pride themselves upon their ability to live without working and who despise all the honest and sober ways of their immigrant parents. They are at once a menace and a center of demoralization. Certainly the bewildered parents, unable to speak English and ignorant of the city, whose children have disappeared for days or weeks, have often come to Hull House, evincing that agony which fairly separates the marrow from the bone, as if they had discovered a new type of suffering, devoid of the healing in familiar sorrows. It is as if they did not know how to search for the children without the assistance of the children themselves. Perhaps the most pathetic aspect of such cases is their revelation of the premature dependence of the older and wiser upon the young and foolish, which is in itself often responsible for the situation because it has given the children an undue sense of their own importance and a false security that they can take care of themselves.

===Chinese===

这些孩子中有许多是栽在他们过早冲进城市生活上，他们把父母的管束连同外国做派一起不耐烦地丢掉了。十岁十二岁的男孩会不肯在家里睡，宁愿要废弃的啤酒窖或空仓库的自由，也不要父母要求的服从；这些男孩会一连几天靠偷来的牛奶面包过活——清早送奶之后从后廊偷的。这样的孩子抱怨家里「没有乐子」。一个小家伙，城市园圃协会（City Garden Association）分给他一块空地耕种，他坚持只种爆米花玉米，还想把整季收成献给赫尔馆「拿去开聚会用」，条件是「每次都得请我」。还有一小伙一小伙的浪荡青年，以不干活也能过活的本事自负，看不起移民父母一切诚实规矩的做派。他们既是一种威胁，也是一个败坏的中心。那些不会说英语、又不识城市的惶惑父母，孩子失踪几天几星期，常常到赫尔馆来，显出那种简直要把骨髓从骨头里分离出来的痛苦，仿佛他们发现了一种新型苦难，缺了熟识的忧伤里那种疗愈。仿佛没有孩子们自己帮忙，他们连怎么寻找孩子都不知道。这类案子最令人心酸的地方，也许在于它揭示了年老而明智者对年轻而愚蠢者的过早依赖；而这种依赖本身往往正是处境的根源，因为它给了孩子们对自己重要性的过度感觉，和一种他们能照顾自己的虚假安全感。

===Original===

On the other hand, an Italian girl who has had lessons in cooking at the public school will help her mother to connect the entire family with American food and household habits. That the mother has never baked bread in Italy﻿—only mixed it in her own house and then taken it out to the village oven﻿—makes all the more valuable her daughter’s understanding of the complicated cooking stove. The same thing is true of the girl who learns to sew in the public school, and more than anything else, perhaps, of the girl who receives the first simple instruction in the care of little children﻿—that skillful care which every tenement-house baby requires if he is to be pulled through his second summer. As a result of this teaching I recall a young girl who carefully explained to her Italian mother that the reason the babies in Italy were so healthy and the babies in Chicago were so sickly, was not, as her mother had firmly insisted, because her babies in Italy had goat’s milk and her babies in America had cow’s milk, but because the milk in Italy was clean and the milk in Chicago was dirty. She said that when you milked your own goat before the door, you knew that the milk was clean, but when you bought milk from the grocery store after it had been carried for many miles in the country, you couldn’t tell whether it was fit for the baby to drink until the men from the City Hall who had watched it all the way said that it was all right.

Thus through civic instruction in the public schools, the Italian woman slowly became urbanized in the sense in which the word was used by her own Latin ancestors, and thus the habits of her entire family were modified. The public schools in the immigrant colonies deserve all the praise as Americanizing agencies which can be bestowed upon them, and there is little doubt that the fast-changing curriculum in the direction of the vacation-school experiments will react more directly upon such households.

===Chinese===

另一方面，在公立学校上过烹调课的意大利姑娘，会帮母亲把全家同美国的饮食和家庭习惯接起来。母亲在意大利从没烤过面包——只在自己家里和好面，再拿到村中的公炉去烤——这就使女儿对那复杂炉灶的了解愈加可贵。在公立学校学会缝纫的姑娘也是如此；而比一切更有过之的，也许是接受了照料幼儿最初浅训练的姑娘——那种熟练的照料，是每个出租屋的娃娃要熬过第二个夏天所必需的。作为这种教学的结果，我记得一个年轻姑娘向她意大利母亲仔细解释：意大利的娃娃那么健康、芝加哥的娃娃那么孱弱，原因不在于——像母亲一口咬定的——她意大利的娃娃喝羊奶、美国的娃娃喝牛奶，而在于意大利的奶干净、芝加哥的奶脏。她说，在门口亲手挤自家的羊奶，你知道奶是干净的；可是牛奶在乡间运了许多英里之后从杂货店买来，你就说不清它配不配给娃娃喝，直到市政厅那些一路盯着它的人说它没问题。

这样，借着公立学校的公民教导，意大利女人慢慢地在她自己拉丁祖先用这个词的意义上城市化了，她全家的习惯也因此改变了。移民区的公立学校作为美国化机关，受多少当之无愧的赞扬都不为过；而且毫无疑问，朝暑假学校实验方向快速变化的课程，将更直接地反作用于这类家庭。

===Original===

It is difficult to write of the relation of the older and most foreign-looking immigrants to the children of other people﻿—the Italians whose fruit-carts are upset simply because they are “dagoes,” or the Russian peddlers who are stoned and sometimes badly injured because it has become a code of honor in a gang of boys to thus express their derision. The members of a Protective Association of Jewish Peddlers organized at Hull House related daily experiences in which old age had been treated with such irreverence, cherished dignity with such disrespect, that a listener caught the passion of Lear in the old texts, as a platitude enunciated by a man who discovers in it his own experience thrills us as no unfamiliar phrases can possibly do. The Greeks are filled with amazed rage when their very name is flung at them as an opprobrious epithet. Doubtless these difficulties would be much minimized in America, if we faced our own race problem with courage and intelligence, and these very Mediterranean immigrants might give us valuable help. Certainly they are less conscious than the Anglo-Saxon of color distinctions, perhaps because of their traditional familiarity with Carthage and Egypt. They listened with respect and enthusiasm to a scholarly address delivered by Professor Du Bois at Hull House on a Lincoln’s birthday, with apparently no consciousness of that race difference which color seems to accentuate so absurdly, and upon my return from various conferences held in the interest of “the advancement of colored people,” I have had many illuminating conversations with my cosmopolitan neighbors.

The celebration of national events has always been a source of new understanding and companionship with the members of the contiguous foreign colonies not only between them and their American neighbors but between them and their own children. One of our earliest Italian events was a rousing commemoration of Garibaldi’s birthday, and his imposing bust, presented to Hull House that evening, was long the chief ornament of our front hall. It called forth great enthusiasm from the *connazionali* whom Ruskin calls, not the “common people” of Italy, but the “companion people” because of their power for swift sympathy.

===Chinese===

要描写年纪最老、外国相貌最重的移民同别人家孩子的关系，是很难下笔的——意大利人的水果车被掀翻，只因为他们是「dagoes」（意大利佬）；俄国货郎被扔石头，有时伤得很重，因为在一伙男孩中间这已成了一项荣誉规则，要用这种方式表达他们的轻蔑。在赫尔馆组织起来的犹太货郎保护协会（Protective Association of Jewish Peddlers）的会员们讲述着日常的经历：高龄被如此无礼相待，珍重的尊严被如此轻慢，听的人从那些老话里捉到了李尔王的激情——正如一个人在一句老生常谈里认出了自己的经历，那句老生常谈便以陌生词句绝不可能有的力量震撼我们。希腊人听见自己的名字本身被当作骂人的字眼扔过来，满腔惊讶的怒火。毫无疑问，倘若我们拿出勇气和智慧面对我们自己的种族问题，这些困难在美国会大大减少，而这些地中海移民反倒可能给我们宝贵的帮助。当然，他们对肤色的分别不如盎格鲁-撒克逊人那么在意，也许因为他们传统上同迦太基和埃及相熟。林肯诞辰那天，杜波依斯教授（Professor Du Bois）在赫尔馆作了一次学者的讲演，他们怀着敬意和热忱听讲，对肤色似乎荒谬地强加出来的那种种族差异显然毫无所觉；而当我出席了几次为「有色人种进步」而开的会议回来，同我这些世界主义的邻居们有过许多茅塞顿开的谈话。

民族事件的庆祝，一直是与毗邻各侨民聚居区成员之间新的理解与情谊的源泉——不但在他们与他们的美国邻居之间，也在他们与自己的孩子之间。我们最早的意大利活动之一，是热热闹闹纪念加里波第（Garibaldi）诞辰；那晚献给赫尔馆的他的威严半身像，长久是我们前厅的首要装饰。它引来了 *connazionali*（同胞们）的巨大热情——罗斯金（Ruskin）把他们称作意大利的「companion people」（同伴的人民）而非「common people」（平民），因为他们有迅即同情的力量。

===Original===

A huge Hellenic meeting held at Hull House, in which the achievements of the classic period were set forth both in Greek and English by scholars of well-known repute, brought us into a new sense of fellowship with all our Greek neighbors. As the mayor of Chicago was seated upon the right hand of the dignified senior priest of the Greek Church and they were greeted alternately in the national hymns of America and Greece, one felt a curious sense of the possibility of transplanting to new and crude Chicago some of the traditions of Athens itself, so deeply cherished in the hearts of this group of citizens.

The Greeks indeed gravely consider their traditions as their most precious possession and more than once in meetings of protest held by the Greek colony against the aggressions of the Bulgarians in Macedonia, I have heard it urged that the Bulgarians are trying to establish a protectorate, not only for their immediate advantage, but that they may claim a glorious history for the “barbarous country.” It is said that on the basis of this protectorate, they are already teaching in their schools that Alexander the Great was a Bulgarian and that it will be but a short time before they claim Aristotle himself, an indignity the Greeks will never suffer!

To me personally the celebration of the hundredth anniversary of Mazzini’s birth was a matter of great interest. Throughout the world that day Italians who believed in a United Italy came together. They recalled the hopes of this man who, with all his devotion to his country was still more devoted to humanity and who dedicated to the workingmen of Italy, an appeal so philosophical, so filled with a yearning for righteousness, that it transcended all national boundaries and became a bugle call for “The Duties of Man.” A copy of this document was given to every school child in the public schools of Italy on this one hundredth anniversary, and as the Chicago branch of the Society of Young Italy marched into our largest hall and presented to Hull House an heroic bust of Mazzini, I found myself devoutly hoping that the Italian youth, who have committed their future to America, might indeed become “the Apostles of the fraternity of nations” and that our American citizenship might be built without disturbing these foundations which were laid of old time.

===Chinese===

一场在赫尔馆举行的大型希腊集会，由知名学者用希腊文和英文讲述古典时代的成就，使我们同所有希腊邻居生出了新的情谊。芝加哥市长坐在希腊教会那位庄严的资深祭司右手边，美国的和希腊的国歌交替向他们致敬；人感到一种奇异的可能：把雅典本身的某些传统移栽到崭新而粗粝的芝加哥来——这些传统在这群市民心里如此深受珍视。

希腊人的确把他们的传统当作最珍贵的财产；在希腊侨民为抗议保加利亚人在马其顿的侵略而开的会上，我不止一次听人主张：保加利亚人想建立保护国，不单是为了眼前的好处，更是要替那个「野蛮国家」认领一段光荣的历史。据说在这个保护国的基础上，他们已经在学校里教亚历山大大帝（Alexander the Great）是保加利亚人，而且要不了多久就要认领亚里士多德本人了——这种羞辱希腊人是决不能忍受的！

对我个人来说，马志尼（Mazzini）诞辰一百周年的庆祝是件极有意思的事。那一天，全世界相信统一意大利的意大利人聚到了一起。他们追想这个人的希望：他对祖国的挚爱之外更爱人类；他献给意大利工人的那篇呼吁，如此富于哲理、如此充满对正义的渴慕，以至于超越了所有国界，成了「人的义务」的号角。这份文件的复本，在这次一百周年之际发给了意大利公立学校的每个学童；当青年意大利协会（Society of Young Italy）芝加哥分会列队走进我们最大的厅堂、向赫尔馆献上马志尼的半身雕像时，我不由虔诚地希望：这些已把未来托付给美国的意大利青年，真能成为「各民族友爱的使徒」；也希望我们美国的公民资格，能够在不扰动这些古时所奠基础的情况下建立起来。

## XII / 十二
===Original===

Tolstoyism

The administration of charity in Chicago during the winter following the World’s Fair had been of necessity most difficult, for, although large sums had been given to the temporary relief organization which endeavored to care for the thousands of destitute strangers stranded in the city, we all worked under a sense of desperate need and a paralyzing consciousness that our best efforts were most inadequate to the situation.

During the many relief visits I paid that winter in tenement houses and miserable lodgings, I was constantly shadowed by a certain sense of shame that I should be comfortable in the midst of such distress. This resulted at times in a curious reaction against all the educational and philanthropic activities in which I had been engaged. In the face of the desperate hunger and need, these could not but seem futile and superficial. The hard winter in Chicago had turned the thoughts of many of us to these stern matters. A young friend of mine who came daily to Hull House consulted me in regard to going into the paper warehouse belonging to her father that she might there sort rags with the Polish girls; another young girl took a place in a sweatshop for a month, doing her work so simply and thoroughly that the proprietor had no notion that she had not been driven there by need; still two others worked in a shoe factory;﻿—and all this happened before such adventures were undertaken in order to procure literary material. It was in the following winter that the pioneer effort in this direction, Walter Wyckoff’s account of his vain attempt to find work in Chicago, compelled even the sternest businessman to drop his assertion that “any man can find work if he wants it.”

The dealing directly with the simplest human wants may have been responsible for an impression which I carried about with me almost constantly for a period of two years and which culminated finally in a visit to Tolstoy﻿—that the Settlement, or Hull House at least, was a mere pretense and travesty of the simple impulse “to live with the poor,” so long as the residents did not share the common lot of hard labor and scant fare.

===Chinese===

托尔斯泰主义

世界博览会之后的那个冬天，芝加哥的慈善施济本来就必然万分艰难：虽然大笔款项给了那个设法照管滞留城中数以千计的赤贫异乡人的临时救济组织，我们都是在一种拼命的需要感和一种叫人瘫软的意识底下做事——我们最大的努力也远配不上这局势。

那个冬天，我在租屋和凄惨的寄宿处做了许多次救济访问，一种羞耻感始终跟着我：身处这样的苦难当中，我竟过得舒舒服服。这有时竟至引起一种古怪的反感，反感我此前从事的一切教育与慈善活动。面对拼命的饥饿与需要，这些不能不显得徒劳而肤浅。芝加哥这个严冬把我们许多人的心思都拨到了这些严峻的事上。我的一位年轻朋友天天来赫尔会所，为着一件事来同我商量：要不要进她父亲的纸品仓库，同波兰姑娘们一起分拣破布；另一个年轻姑娘在一家血汗作坊做了一个月的工，活做得那样简单而地道，东家压根想不到她不是被穷困逼去的；还有两位进了制鞋厂——而这一切都发生在这类冒险被用来猎取文学材料之前。也就是第二年冬天，沃尔特·威科夫（Walter Wyckoff）记述他在芝加哥找工徒劳的那部开山之作，逼得最铁石的生意人也不得不收回「谁想做工谁就找得到」那句断言。

直接对付人最简单的需要，也许要为我心头一种印象负责；这印象我随身带了差不多两年，末了归结为一次对托尔斯泰的拜访——只要居民们不分担苦役粗食的共同命运，定居所——至少赫尔会所——就只是对「同穷人一起生活」这个单纯冲动的装点与滑稽模仿。

===Original===

Actual experience had left me in much the same state of mind I had been in after reading Tolstoy’s *What to Do*, which is a description of his futile efforts to relieve the unspeakable distress and want in the Moscow winter of 1881, and his inevitable conviction that only he who literally shares his own shelter and food with the needy can claim to have served them.

Doubtless it is much easier to see “what to do” in rural Russia, where all the conditions tend to make the contrast as broad as possible between peasant labor and noble idleness, than it is to see “what to do” in the interdependencies of the modern industrial city. But for that very reason perhaps, Tolstoy’s clear statement is valuable for that type of conscientious person in every land who finds it hard, not only to walk in the path of righteousness, but to discover where the path lies.

I had read the books of Tolstoy steadily all the years since *My Religion* had come into my hands immediately after I left college. The reading of that book had made clear that men’s poor little efforts to do right are put forth for the most part in the chill of self-distrust; I became convinced that if the new social order ever came, it would come by gathering to itself all the pathetic human endeavor which had indicated the forward direction. But I was most eager to know whether Tolstoy’s undertaking to do his daily share of the physical labor of the world, that labor which is “so disproportionate to the unnourished strength” of those by whom it is ordinarily performed, had brought him peace!

I had time to review carefully many things in my mind during the long days of convalescence following an illness of typhoid fever which I suffered in the autumn of 1895. The illness was so prolonged that my health was most unsatisfactory during the following winter, and the next May I went abroad with my friend, Miss Smith, to effect if possible a more complete recovery.

The prospect of seeing Tolstoy filled me with the hope of finding a clue to the tangled affairs of city poverty. I was but one of thousands of our contemporaries who were turning toward this Russian, not as to a seer﻿—his message is much too confused and contradictory for that﻿—but as to a man who has had the ability to lift his life to the level of his conscience, to translate his theories into action.

===Chinese===

实际的经历留给我的心境，同我读完托尔斯泰的《怎么办》（*What to Do*）后的心境差不多——那本书写他救济1881年莫斯科冬天的惨绝人寰的贫困如何徒劳，写他无可避免的信念：只有把自己的居所和食物真正分给匮乏者的人，才配说服务了他们。

在俄国的乡村，「怎么办」自然容易看清：那里的种种条件，都使农民的劳苦与贵族的闲逸之间的反差宽到不能再宽；而在现代工业城市互相倚靠的千丝万缕里看清「怎么办」，就难了。但也正因为如此，托尔斯泰的清晰陈述，对各国那一类良心敏感的人才格外可贵——这种人不但难于走在正路上，也难于找到路在哪里。

自从大学毕业不久《我的宗教》（*My Religion*）到我手里之后，这些年我一直在读托尔斯泰的书。读了那本书我才明白：人那点想做对的可怜努力，大半是在自疑的寒气里使出来的；我从此相信，新的社会秩序倘若真会到来，也必定是靠把一切指示着前进方向的可贵的人的努力聚拢到自己身上来。可我最急切想知道的是：托尔斯泰承担起世上体力劳动每日的一份——那「同通常做这活的人吃不饱的力气太不相称」的劳动——可曾给他带来平安！

1895年秋我害了一场伤寒，此后长长的康复日子里，我有工夫在心里细细回顾许多事。病拖得那样久，整个随后那个冬天我的身体都很不像样；第二年五月，我便同朋友史密斯小姐（Miss Smith）出国，想尽可能将养得彻底些。

将要见到托尔斯泰的前景，使我满怀着找到城市贫困这团乱麻的线索的希望。转向这位俄国人的，同代人何止千万，我只是其中之一——不是把他当先知（他的讯息实在太混乱、太自相矛盾，当不得先知），而是把他当一个有能力把自己的生活抬到良心的高度、把理论化成行动的人。

===Original===

Our first few weeks in England were most stimulating. A dozen years ago London still showed traces of “that exciting moment in the life of the nation when its youth is casting about for new enthusiasms,” but it evinced still more of that British capacity to perform the hard work of careful research and self-examination which must precede any successful experiments in social reform. Of the varied groups and individuals whose suggestions remained with me for years, I recall perhaps as foremost those members of the new London County Council whose far-reaching plans for the betterment of London could not but enkindle enthusiasm. It was a most striking expression of that effort which would place beside the refinement and pleasure of the rich, a new refinement and a new pleasure born of the commonwealth and the common joy of all the citizens, that at this moment they prized the municipal pleasure boats upon the Thames no less than the extensive schemes for the municipal housing of the poorest people. Ben Tillet, who was then an alderman, “the docker sitting beside the duke,” took me in a rowboat down the Thames on a journey made exciting by the hundreds of dockers who cheered him as we passed one wharf after another on our way to his home at Greenwich; John Burns showed us his wonderful civic accomplishments at Battersea, the plant turning street sweepings into cement pavements, the technical school teaching boys brick laying and plumbing, and the public bath in which the children of the Board School were receiving a swimming lesson﻿—these measures anticipating our achievements in Chicago by at least a decade and a half. The new Education Bill which was destined to drag on for twelve years before it developed into the children’s charter, was then a storm center in the House of Commons. Miss Smith and I were much pleased to be taken to tea on the Parliament terrace by its author, Sir John Gorst, although we were quite bewildered by the arguments we heard there for church schools versus secular.

We heard Keir Hardie before a large audience of workingmen standing in the open square of Canning Town outline the great things to be accomplished by the then-new Labor Party, and we joined the vast body of men in the booming hymn

“When wilt Thou save the people,

O God of Mercy, when!”

===Chinese===

在英格兰的头几个星期最是振奋人心。十几年前的伦敦，还带着「民族生命史上那个青年正在四处寻找新的热忱的激动时刻」的痕迹，但更多地显出的，是英国人那种肯下苦功做细密调查与自我省察的能耐——任何社会改革的成功实验，都得先过这一关。各色团体和个人，他们给我的启发留了很多年；我记得最清楚的，恐怕还是新伦敦郡议会的那批成员，他们改造伦敦的宏图不能不点燃人的热忱。富人的精致与享乐之旁，要放上一种新的精致、新的享乐——生于全体公民的公益与同乐；这番努力有一个最触目的表现：此刻他们看重泰晤士河上的市营游船，不亚于那些为最穷的人盖市营住宅的庞大计划。本·蒂利特（Ben Tillet）当时是郡议会参事，「坐在公爵旁边的码头工人」，划着一条小船送我顺泰晤士河而下；一路码头一个接一个，成百的码头工人朝他欢呼，这趟水路走得叫人激动，直到他格林尼治的家。约翰·伯恩斯（John Burns）领我们看他在巴特西的了不起的市政成就：把扫街的垃圾烧成水泥路面的工厂，教男孩子砌砖和铅管手艺的技术学校，还有公立学校（Board School）的孩子们正在上游泳课的公共浴场——这些举措比芝加哥的成就至少早了十五年。那部注定要拖上十二年才长成「儿童宪章」的新教育法案，当时正是下议院的风暴中心。它的起草人约翰·戈斯特爵士（Sir John Gorst）请史密斯小姐和我到议会露台喝茶，我们很是欢喜，尽管在那里听到的教会学校对世俗学校的种种论辩，叫我们云里雾里。

在坎宁镇（Canning Town）的空场上，我们听基尔·哈迪（Keir Hardie）面对一大群站着的工人，历数那时新兴的工党要成就的大事；随后我们同黑压压的人群一起吼起那首响亮的赞美诗——

「你几时拯救众生，

慈悲的上帝，几时！」

===Original===

finding it hard to realize that we were attending a political meeting. It seemed that moment as if the hopes of democracy were more likely to come to pass on English soil than upon our own. Robert Blatchford’s stirring pamphlets were in everyone’s hands, and a reception given by Karl Marx’s daughter, Mrs. Aveling, to Liebknecht before he returned to Germany to serve a prison term for his *lèse majesté* speech in the Reichstag, gave us a glimpse of the old-fashioned orthodox Socialist who had not yet begun to yield to the biting ridicule of Bernard Shaw although he flamed in their midst that evening.

Octavia Hill kindly demonstrated to us the principles upon which her well-founded business of rent collecting was established, and with pardonable pride showed us the Red Cross Square with its cottages marvelously picturesque and comfortable, on two sides, and on the third a public hall and common drawing room for the use of all the tenants; the interior of the latter had been decorated by pupils of Walter Crane with mural frescoes portraying the heroism in the life of the modern workingman.

While all this was warmly human, we also had opportunities to see something of a group of men and women who were approaching the social problem from the study of economics; among others Mr. and Mrs. Sidney Webb who were at work on their Industrial Democracy; Mr. John Hobson who was lecturing on the evolution of modern capitalism.

We followed factory inspectors on a round of duties performed with a thoroughness and a trained intelligence which were a revelation of the possibilities of public service. When it came to visiting Settlements, we were at least reassured that they were not falling into identical lines of effort. Canon Ingram, who has since become Bishop of London, was then warden of Oxford House and in the midst of an experiment which pleased me greatly, the more because it was carried on by a churchman. Oxford House had hired all the concert halls﻿—vaudeville shows we later called them in Chicago﻿—which were found in Bethnal Green, for every Saturday night. The residents had censored the programs, which they were careful to keep popular, and any workingman who attended a show in Bethnal Green on a Saturday night, and thousands of them did, heard a program the better for this effort.

===Chinese===

很难相信我们是在参加一个政治集会。那一刻，民主的希望仿佛更可能落在英国的土壤上，而不是我们自己的。罗伯特·布拉奇福德（Robert Blatchford）那些激荡人心的小册子人手一册；马克思的女儿阿维林太太（Mrs. Aveling）为李卜克内西（Liebknecht）饯行——他就要回德国去，为他在国会里那篇冒犯君上的演说坐牢——那场招待让我们窥见了老派的正统社会主义者：那时他还不曾开始向萧伯纳（Bernard Shaw）的刻毒讥诮让步，虽然那晚萧伯纳就在他们中间，火焰似的说着俏皮话。

奥克塔维娅·希尔（Octavia Hill）好心地给我们演示了她的根基扎实的收租营生所依据的原则，又带着情有可原的骄傲，领我们看红十字广场：两旁的小屋妙在又入画又舒适，第三边是一座公共会堂和大家共用的客厅，供全体房客使用；客厅内部的壁画出自沃尔特·克兰（Walter Crane）的学生之手，画的是现代工人生活中的英雄气。

这一切固然是温暖的人情，我们也有机会见识另一群男女——他们从经济学的研究走向社会问题；其中有正在写《工业民主》（*Industrial Democracy*）的西德尼·韦布夫妇（Mr. and Mrs. Sidney Webb），有正在讲授现代资本主义演变的约翰·霍布森先生（Mr. John Hobson）。

我们跟着工厂督察员跑了一轮差事，那份彻底、那份训练有素的明察，把公共服务的可能性启示给我们。至于访问各定居所，我们至少放了心：它们并没有落进千篇一律的路数。英格拉姆牧师（Canon Ingram）——后来做了伦敦主教——那时是牛津馆（Oxford House）的主事，正在办一项叫我大为欢喜的实验，尤其因为办它的是一位教会中人。牛津馆把贝斯纳尔格林（Bethnal Green）所有找得到的演艺厅——我们后来在芝加哥管它们叫杂耍场——每个星期六晚上全包了下来。居民们审过了节目单，又小心保持它的大众口味；任何一个星期六晚上到贝斯纳尔格林看演出的工人——这样的工人成千上万——听到的节目，都因这番用心而更好了。

===Original===

One evening in University Hall Mrs. Humphry Ward, who had just returned from Italy, described the effect of the Italian salt tax in a talk which was evidently one in a series of lectures upon the economic wrongs which pressed heaviest upon the poor; at Browning House, at the moment, they were giving prizes to those of their costermonger neighbors who could present the best cared-for donkeys, and the warden, Herbert Stead, exhibited almost the enthusiasm of his well-known brother, for that crop of kindliness which can be garnered most easily from the acreage where human beings grow the thickest; at the Bermondsey Settlement they were rejoicing that their University Extension students had successfully passed the examinations for the University of London. The entire impression received in England of research, of scholarship, of organized public spirit, was in marked contrast to the impressions of my next visit in 1900, when the South African War had absorbed the enthusiasm of the nation and the wrongs at “the heart of the empire” were disregarded and neglected.

London, of course, presented sharp differences to Russia where social conditions were written in black and white with little shading, like a demonstration of the Chinese proverb, “Where one man lives in luxury, another is dying of hunger.”

The fair of Nijni-Novgorod seemed to take us to the very edge of civilization so remote and eastern that the merchants brought their curious goods upon the backs of camels or on strange craft riding at anchor on the broad Volga. But even here our letter of introduction to Korolenko, the novelist, brought us to a realization of that strange mingling of a remote past and a self-conscious present which Russia presents on every hand. This same contrast was also shown by the pilgrims trudging on pious errands to monasteries, to tombs, and to the Holy Land itself, with their bleeding feet bound in rags and thrust into bast sandals, and, on the other hand, by the revolutionists even then advocating a Republic which should obtain not only in political but also in industrial affairs.

===Chinese===

一天晚上，在大学会堂（University Hall），刚从意大利回来的汉弗莱·沃德太太（Mrs. Humphry Ward）讲述意大利盐税的效用；那显然是一系列讲演中的一讲，讲压在穷人身上最重的经济不义。在布朗宁馆（Browning House），人们此刻正给街坊里菜贩邻居发奖，奖的是照料得最好的驴子；主事赫伯特·斯特德（Herbert Stead）几乎拿出了他那位闻名兄长的热忱，为的是那茬善意——人长得最密的田亩上，这茬庄稼收得最容易。在伯蒙德赛定居所（Bermondsey Settlement），人们正欢庆他们的大学推广课程学生顺利通过了伦敦大学的考试。在英国得到的整个印象——研究、学问、有组织的公共精神——同我1900年第二次去时的印象恰成对照：那时南非战争吸干了全国的热忱，「帝国心脏」的种种不义遭到冷落和忽视。

伦敦同俄国自然是天差地别；俄国的社会状况黑白分明，几乎没有中间色调，像在演示中国那句古话：「一家酒肉臭，路有冻死骨。」

下诺夫哥罗德（Nijni-Novgorod）的大集市，像把我们带到了文明的边沿，那样遥远、那样东方，商人们把稀奇的货物驮在骆驼背上，或装在停泊在宽阔伏尔加河上的怪船上。可就在这里，那封引见小说家柯罗连科（Korolenko）的信，也让我们领受了俄国处处呈现的那种奇景：遥远的过去同自觉的现今掺在一处。同样的对照也显在朝圣者身上——他们拖着虔诚的差事，往修道院去，往圣墓去，往圣地去，流着血的脚裹着破布，套着树皮鞋；另一面，也显在革命者身上——他们那时已经在鼓吹一个共和国，不但行于政治，也行于产业。

===Original===

We had letters of introduction to Mr. and Mrs. Aylmer Maude of Moscow, since well known as the translators of *Resurrection* and other of Tolstoy’s later works, who at that moment were on the eve of leaving Russia in order to form an agricultural colony in South England where they might support themselves by the labor of their hands. We gladly accepted Mr. Maude’s offer to take us to Yasnaya Polyana and to introduce us to Count Tolstoy, and never did a disciple journey toward his master with more enthusiasm than did our guide. When, however, Mr. Maude actually presented Miss Smith and myself to Count Tolstoy, knowing well his master’s attitude toward philanthropy, he endeavored to make Hull House appear much more noble and unique than I should have ventured to do.

===Chinese===

我们带着引见信去莫斯科找艾尔默·莫德夫妇（Mr. and Mrs. Aylmer Maude）——他们后来以《复活》（*Resurrection*）和托尔斯泰晚期其他著作的译者闻名；那时他们正要离开俄国，去英格兰南部办一个农业移民村，好靠双手的劳动养活自己。莫德先生提出带我们去亚斯纳亚·波利亚纳（Yasnaya Polyana），把我们引见给托尔斯泰伯爵，我们欣然接受；朝自己的宗师进发，再没有哪个门徒比我们这位向导更热忱的了。可是等到莫德先生果真把史密斯小姐和我介绍给托尔斯泰伯爵时，因为深知宗师对慈善的态度，他竭力把赫尔会所说得比我胆敢自诩的高贵得多、独特得多。

===Original===

Tolstoy, standing by clad in his peasant garb, listened gravely but, glancing distrustfully at the sleeves of my traveling gown which unfortunately at that season were monstrous in size, he took hold of an edge and pulling out one sleeve to an interminable breadth, said quite simply that “there was enough stuff on one arm to make a frock for a little girl,” and asked me directly if I did not find “such a dress” a “barrier to the people.” I was too disconcerted to make a very clear explanation, although I tried to say that monstrous as my sleeves were they did not compare in size with those of the working girls in Chicago and that nothing would more effectively separate me from “the people” than a cotton blouse following the simple lines of the human form; even if I had wished to imitate him and “dress as a peasant,” it would have been hard to choose which peasant among the thirty-six nationalities we had recently counted in our ward. Fortunately the countess came to my rescue with a recital of her former attempts to clothe hypothetical little girls in yards of material cut from a train and other superfluous parts of her best gown until she had been driven to a firm stand which she advised me to take at once. But neither Countess Tolstoy nor any other friend was on hand to help me out of my predicament later, when I was asked who “fed” me, and how did I obtain “shelter”? Upon my reply that a farm a hundred miles from Chicago supplied me with the necessities of life, I fairly anticipated the next scathing question: “So you are an absentee landlord? Do you think you will help the people more by adding yourself to the crowded city than you would by tilling your own soil?” This new sense of discomfort over a failure to till my own soil was increased when Tolstoy’s second daughter appeared at the five-o’clock tea table set under the trees, coming straight from the harvest field where she had been working with a group of peasants since five o’clock in the morning, not pretending to work but really taking the place of a peasant woman who had hurt her foot. She was plainly much exhausted, but neither expected nor received sympathy from the members of a family who were quite accustomed to see each other carry out their convictions in spite of discomfort and fatigue.

===Chinese===

托尔斯泰一身农装站在旁边，神情庄重地听着；可他不放心地瞥了瞥我那件旅行裙的袖子——不巧那个年头袖子正大得吓人——捏住一边，把一只袖子拉到没边没沿的宽，很简单地说：「一只胳膊上的布，就够给小姑娘做条裙子了。」又直截了当地问我，不觉得「这样的衣裳」是「同人民之间的一道障壁」么。我狼狈得说不出个清楚的解释，虽然我试着说：我的袖子再吓人，也比不上芝加哥做工姑娘们的袖子；真要比「人民」隔得更开的，倒是那种老老实实照人身简单线条裁的布衫。就算我想学他「穿得像农民」，也不知该学哪一个——我们的选区里近来数得出三十六个民族，各穿各的装束。幸亏伯爵夫人来解了围，讲起她从前怎样把长裙的拖尾和好裙子上其余多余的部位一码码铰下来，给假想中的小姑娘们做衣裳，直到被逼得立定一个坚决的立场，还劝我立刻照办。可后来又有人问：谁「养活」我？我的「住处」又是怎么来的？这回，托尔斯泰伯爵夫人也好，别的朋友也好，谁也不在跟前替我解围了。我答道，离芝加哥一百英里的一座农庄供给我生活的必需；我简直已经料到下一句不留情面的质问：「那么你就是个坐收田租的地主？你以为把自己添进拥挤的城市，就比种自己的地更能帮助人民？」这种因为不种自己的地而生的新不安，到托尔斯泰的二女儿来到树下摆开的五点钟茶桌上时，又添了一层：她从收割地里径直过来，从早上五点钟起就同一群农民一起干活——不是装装样子，是真顶了一个伤了脚的农妇的工。她分明累得很了，却不指望、也没有得到家人的同情；这一家人看惯了彼此不管辛劳疲惫、把信念做到底。

===Original===

The martyrdom of discomfort, however, was obviously much easier to bear than that to which, even to the eyes of the casual visitor, Count Tolstoy daily subjected himself, for his study in the basement of the conventional dwelling, with its short shelf of battered books and its scythe and spade leaning against the wall, had many times lent itself to that ridicule which is the most difficult form of martyrdom.

That summer evening as we sat in the garden with a group of visitors from Germany, from England and America, who had traveled to the remote Russian village that they might learn of this man, one could not forbear the constant inquiry to one’s self, as to why he was so regarded as sage and saint that this party of people should be repeated each day of the year. It seemed to me then that we were all attracted by this sermon of the deed, because Tolstoy had made the one supreme personal effort, one might almost say the one frantic personal effort, to put himself into right relations with the humblest people, with the men who tilled his soil, blacked his boots, and cleaned his stables. Doubtless the heaviest burden of our contemporaries is a consciousness of a divergence between our democratic theory on the one hand, that working people have a right to the intellectual resources of society, and the actual fact on the other hand, that thousands of them are so overburdened with toil that there is no leisure nor energy left for the cultivation of the mind. We constantly suffer from the strain and indecision of believing this theory and acting as if we did not believe it, and this man who years before had tried “to get off the backs of the peasants,” who had at least simplified his life and worked with his hands, had come to be a prototype to many of his generation.

===Chinese===

不过，吃的这层苦同托尔斯泰伯爵天天加给自己的那层相比——连偶然来访的客人都看得见——显然容易受得多：他那间设在常规宅子地下层里的书房，一架短短的碰坏了的书，一柄镰刀一把铁锹靠在墙边，不知多少回招来讥笑；而讥笑，正是殉道诸苦里最难当的一等。

那个夏天的傍晚，我们同一群游客坐在花园里——有从德国来的，有从英国、美国来的，都为仰慕这个人，千里迢迢赶到这个偏远的俄国村子——人忍不住一遍遍自问：他凭什么被当作智者与圣者，以致一年三百六十五天，天天有这样一批人前来？在我那时看来，我们大家都被这场以身作则的布道吸住，因为托尔斯泰作成了那桩至大的个人努力——几乎可以说是孤注一掷的个人努力——去同最卑微的人、同种他的地、擦他的靴、清他的马厩的人建立正当的关系。无疑，我们同代人最重的负担，是一种撕裂的意识：一边是民主的理论，说劳动人民有权分享社会的智识财富；一边是实际，说他们成千上万被苦役压得那样重，再也留不下一点余暇与气力去滋养心灵。我们相信这理论，行事却像不信它；这紧张与摇摆我们时时在受。而这个人，多年前就试过「从农民的背脊上下来」，至少已经把生活简化了、用双手做工了，于是成了他同代许多人的一个原型。

===Original===

Doubtless all of the visitors sitting in the Tolstoy garden that evening had excused themselves from laboring with their hands upon the theory that they were doing something more valuable for society in other ways. No one among our contemporaries has dissented from this point of view so violently as Tolstoy himself, and yet no man might so easily have excused himself from hard and rough work on the basis of his genius and of his intellectual contributions to the world. So far, however, from considering his time too valuable to be spent in labor in the field or in making shoes, our great host was too eager to know life to be willing to give up this companionship of mutual labor. One instinctively found reasons why it was easier for a Russian than for the rest of us to reach this conclusion; the Russian peasants have a proverb which says: “Labor is the house that love lives in,” by which they mean that no two people nor group of people can come into affectionate relations with each other unless they carry on together a mutual task, and when the Russian peasant talks of labor he means labor on the soil, or, to use the phrase of the great peasant, Bondereff, “bread labor.” Those monastic orders founded upon agricultural labor, those philosophical experiments like Brook Farm and many another have attempted to reduce to action this same truth. Tolstoy himself has written many times his own convictions and attempts in this direction, perhaps never more tellingly than in the description of Lavin’s morning spent in the harvest field, when he lost his sense of grievance and isolation and felt a strange new brotherhood for the peasants, in proportion as the rhythmic motion of his scythe became one with theirs.

===Chinese===

那天傍晚坐在托尔斯泰花园里的客人，无疑个个都用这套道理原谅过自己不动手：他们在别的方面为社会做着更宝贵的事。同代人里，没有谁像托尔斯泰本人那样猛烈地反对过这个立场；可也没有谁比他更有资格凭天才、凭他对世界智识的贡献，把自己从粗重肮脏的活里开脱出来。然而我们这位伟大的东家，非但不觉得自己的光阴金贵得花不得在地里、花不得在打草鞋上，反倒太想懂得生活，舍不得放弃这共同劳作的情谊。人不由自主地替他找理由：俄国人得出这个结论，比我们容易；俄国的农民有句谚语：「劳动是爱居住的屋子」——意思是说，两个人也好、两群人也好，除非共同担着一件彼此的活计，否则生不出亲近的情分；而俄国农民说到劳动，指的就是地里的劳动，或者，用那位伟大的农民邦达列夫（Bondereff）的话说，「面包劳动」。那些立在农业劳动上的修道院，布鲁克农庄（Brook Farm）和许多那类哲学实验，都试过把同一条真理化成行动。托尔斯泰自己也一次次写过他在这方向上的信念与尝试，写得最动人的，恐怕莫过于拉温在收割地里过的那个早晨：随着他镰刀的节奏同农民们的合成一个，委屈与孤独感消失了，对农民生出一种新奇的手足之情。

===Original===

At the long dinner table laid in the garden were the various traveling guests, the grownup daughters, and the younger children with their governess. The countess presided over the usual European dinner served by men, but the count and the daughter, who had worked all day in the fields, ate only porridge and black bread and drank only kvass, the fare of the haymaking peasants. Of course we are all accustomed to the fact that those who perform the heaviest labor eat the coarsest and simplest fare at the end of the day, but it is not often that we sit at the same table with them while we ourselves eat the more elaborate food prepared by someone else’s labor. Tolstoy ate his simple supper without remark or comment upon the food his family and guests preferred to eat, assuming that they, as well as he, had settled the matter with their own consciences.

The Tolstoy household that evening was much interested in the fate of a young Russian spy who had recently come to Tolstoy in the guise of a country schoolmaster, in order to obtain a copy of *Life*, which had been interdicted by the censor of the press. After spending the night in talk with Tolstoy, the spy had gone away with a copy of the forbidden manuscript but, unfortunately for himself, having become converted to Tolstoy’s views he had later made a full confession to the authorities and had been exiled to Siberia. Tolstoy, holding that it was most unjust to exile the disciple while he, the author of the book, remained at large, had pointed out this inconsistency in an open letter to one of the Moscow newspapers. The discussion of this incident, of course, opened up the entire subject of nonresidence, and curiously enough I was disappointed in Tolstoy’s position in the matter. It seemed to me that he made too great a distinction between the use of physical force and that moral energy which can override another’s differences and scruples with equal ruthlessness.

===Chinese===

花园里摆开的长餐桌上，坐着各方来客、成年的女儿们，还有年幼的孩子们和他们的家庭女教师。伯爵夫人照欧洲规矩主持这顿由男仆上菜的晚饭；伯爵和干了一整天活的女儿却只吃麦粥和黑面包，只喝格瓦斯（kvass）——割草农民的饭食。干最重的活的人，一天下来吃得最粗最简单，这个我们本来都习惯了；可我们很少同他们坐在一张桌上，自己吃着别人劳动做出的更讲究的饭菜。托尔斯泰吃他简单的晚饭，对他家人和客人爱吃的东西不置一词，仿佛认定这件事，他们同他一样，都已经在各自的良心里了结了。

那天晚上，托尔斯泰一家正牵挂着一个年轻俄国密探的命运：他新近扮作乡下小学教师来到托尔斯泰这里，想弄一部被书报检查机关查禁的《生活》（*Life*）。同托尔斯泰谈了一夜之后，密探带着禁书抄本走了；不幸的是，他后来皈依了托尔斯泰的主张，向当局作了一次彻底的坦白，被流放去了西伯利亚。托尔斯泰认为，流放门徒而让写书的本人逍遥在外，极不公道，便在一封写给莫斯科一家报纸的公开信里点出了这层自相矛盾。对这件事的讨论自然把不抵抗的整个话题都打开了；说来奇怪，托尔斯泰在这个问题上的立场叫我失望。在我看来，他把动用体力和动用那种道德能量分得太开了——后者压倒别人的异议与顾忌时，可以同样不留情面。

===Original===

With that inner sense of mortification with which one finds one’s self at difference with the great authority, I recalled the conviction of the early Hull House residents; that whatever of good the Settlement had to offer should be put into positive terms, that we might live with opposition to no man, with recognition of the good in every man, even the most wretched.
