Practically nothing is done to remedy this. The people who might do it, who have the social tact and training, the large houses, and the traditions and customs of hospitality, live in other parts of the city. The club houses, libraries, galleries, and semipublic conveniences for social life are also blocks away. We find workingmen organized into armies of producers because men of executive ability and business sagacity have found it to their interests thus to organize them. But these workingmen are not organized socially; although lodging in crowded tenement houses, they are living without a corresponding social contact. The chaos is as great as it would be were they working in huge factories without foremen or superintendent. Their ideas and resources are cramped, and the desire for higher social pleasure becomes extinct. They have no share in the traditions and social energy which make for progress. Too often their only place of meeting is a saloon, their only host a bartender; a local demagogue forms their public opinion. Men of ability and refinement, of social power and university cultivation, stay away from them. Personally, I believe the men who lose most are those who thus stay away. But the paradox is here; when cultivated people do stay away from a certain portion of the population, when all social advantages are persistently withheld, it may be for years, the result itself is pointed to as a reason and is used as an argument, for the continued withholding.

===Chinese===

他听了这些熟悉的话微微一笑，还是倾向于把我们的冷漠归因于对社会状况的纯然无知。

赫尔馆整个的社会发展，同我二十年前预言的那么不一样，我于是大胆从那篇旧文字里引一段，来结束这一章。

「在我们大城市的广大区域，社会机体已经崩溃。住在那里的人很多非常贫穷，多数人没有闲暇和精力顾及生计以外的任何事。

「他们为眼前活着，比邻而居，许多人互不相识，没有情谊，没有地方传统或公共精神，没有任何形式的社会组织。实际上无人着手补救。能够做这件事的人——有社交的分寸和训练、有宽大的宅子、有款待的传统和习惯的人——住在城市的其他区域。俱乐部会所、图书馆、美术馆和社交生活的半公共设施也隔着好几个街区。我们看到工人被组织成生产者的大军，因为有执行才干和商业头脑的人发现这样组织他们于己有利。可这些工人在社交上没有组织；虽然挤住在拥挤的出租屋里，他们却没有相应的社会接触。其混乱，不亚于他们在大工厂里做工却没有工头和监工。他们的观念和资源受到压制，对更高社交快乐的欲望归于熄灭。他们没有份于促成进步的传统和社会能量。太常见的是，他们唯一的聚会场所是酒馆，唯一的东道是酒保；一个地方煽动家替他们形成舆论。有才干有教养、有社会势力和大学熏陶的人，避开他们。我个人相信，损失最大的是那些这样避开的人。可是悖论在这里：当有教养的人确实避开人口中的某一部分，当一切社会好处被持续扣着——可以一扣多年——那结果本身反倒被指作理由，被用作继续扣着的论据。」

===Original===

“It is constantly said that because the masses have never had social advantages, they do want them, that they are heavy and dull, and that it will take political or philanthropic machinery to change them. This divides a city into rich and poor; into the favored, who express their sense of the social obligation by gifts of money, and into the unfavored, who express it by clamoring for a ‘share’﻿—both of them actuated by a vague sense of justice. This division of the city would be more justifiable, however, if the people who thus isolate themselves on certain streets and use their social ability for each other, gained enough thereby and added sufficient to the sum total of social progress to justify the withholding of the pleasures and results of that progress from so many people who ought to have them. But they cannot accomplish this for the social spirit discharges itself in many forms, and no one form is adequate to its total expression.”

A Hull House studio.

===Chinese===

「人们常说，因为民众从来没有过社会的好处，所以他们并不想要；说他们迟钝沉重，要靠政治的或慈善的机器来改变。这就把一座城分成了富与穷；分成用捐钱来表达社会义务感的得宠者，和用吵嚷着要一份来表达的失宠者——两者都受一种模糊的正义感驱使。然而，这种对城市的划分若要更站得住，那些这样把自己隔离在某些街上、把自己的社交才能用在彼此身上的人，就得由此得到足够的收益、为社会进步的总数增添足够的东西，才配把那进步的快乐和成果扣着不给那么多本该享有它们的人。可他们做不到这一点，因为社交精神在许多形态里宣泄自己，没有一种形态足以穷尽它的全部表达。」

赫尔馆一间工作室。

## XVI / 十六
===Original===

Arts at Hull House

The first building erected for Hull House contained an art gallery well lighted for day and evening use, and our first exhibit of loaned pictures was opened in June, 1891, by Mr. And Mrs. Barnett of London. It is always pleasant to associate their hearty sympathy with that first exhibit, and thus to connect it with their pioneer efforts at Toynbee Hall to secure for working people the opportunity to know the best art, and with their establishment of the first permanent art gallery in an industrial quarter.

We took pride in the fact that our first exhibit contained some of the best pictures Chicago afforded, and we conscientiously insured them against fire and carefully guarded them by night and day.

We had five of these exhibits during two years, after the gallery was completed: two of oil paintings, one of old engravings and etchings, one of water colors, and one of pictures especially selected for use in the public schools. These exhibits were surprisingly well attended and thousands of votes were cast for the most popular pictures. Their value to the neighborhood of course had to be determined by each one of us according to the value he attached to beauty and the escape it offers from dreary reality into the realm of the imagination. Miss Starr always insisted that the arts should receive adequate recognition at Hull House and urged that one must always remember “the hungry individual soul which without art will have passed unsolaced and unfed, followed by other souls who lack the impulse his should have given.”

The exhibits afforded pathetic evidence that the older immigrants do not expect the solace of art in this country; an Italian expressed great surprise when he found that we, although Americans, still liked pictures, and said quite naively that he didn’t know that Americans cared for anything but dollars﻿—that looking at pictures was something people only did in Italy.

The extreme isolation of the Italian colony was demonstrated by the fact that he did not know that there was a public art gallery in the city nor any houses in which pictures were regarded as treasures.

===Chinese===

赫尔会所的艺术

为赫尔会所盖起的第一座楼里，有一间白天晚上光线都好的画廊；我们头一回借画展出，是1891年6月，由伦敦来的巴尼特夫妇（Mr. and Mrs. Barnett）揭幕。他们的热忱同那第一次画展连在一起，总叫人愉快；这也把它同他们在汤因比馆的开路努力连在一起——为劳动人民争得认识一流艺术的机会——同他们在工业区设立第一座常设画廊连在一起。

我们头一次画展里有芝加哥拿得出手的最好的一些画，我们引以为豪；也规规矩矩给它们保了火险，日夜小心看护。

画廊落成后两年里，我们办了五回这样的展览：两回油画，一回古版画与蚀刻，一回水彩，一回专为公立学校使用挑选的画。这些展览观者之多出人意料，最受欢迎的画得了几千张票。它们对街坊的价值，自然得由我们各人按各人对美的看重、对美提供的那条逃离沉闷现实、遁入想象之国的出路来定。斯塔尔小姐一向坚持，艺术在赫尔会所该得足够的承认；她常提醒人别忘了「那饥渴的个人的灵魂——没有艺术，它便无人安慰、无人喂养地过去了，跟在后面的，是那许许多多本该受它激励却没有受到的灵魂」。

这些展览也提供了叫人心酸的证据：年长的移民并不指望在这个国家得到艺术的安慰。一个意大利人发现我们虽是美国人却还喜欢画，大为惊讶，天真地说，他原不知道美国人除了钱还看重什么——看画这种事，是只有意大利人才做的。

意大利侨民与外界隔绝到什么地步，有一件事可为证：他不知道城里有公立美术馆，也不知道有人家把画当作宝贝。

===Original===

A Greek was much surprised to see a photograph of the Acropolis at Hull House because he had lived in Chicago for thirteen years and had never before met any Americans who knew about this foremost glory of the world. Before he left Greece he had imagined that Americans would be most eager to see pictures of Athens, and as he was a graduate of a school of technology, he had prepared a book of colored drawings and had made a collection of photographs which he was sure Americans would enjoy. But although from his fruit stand near one of the large railroad stations he had conversed with many Americans and had often tried to lead the conversation back to ancient Greece, no one had responded, and he had at last concluded that “the people of Chicago knew nothing of ancient times.”

The loan exhibits were continued until the Chicago Art Institute was opened free to the public on Sunday afternoons and parties were arranged at Hull House and conducted there by a guide. In time even these parties were discontinued as the galleries became better known in all parts of the city and the Art Institute management did much to make pictures popular.

From the first a studio was maintained at Hull House which has developed through the changing years under the direction of Miss Benedict, one of the residents who is a member of the faculty in the Art Institute. Buildings on the Hull House quadrangle furnish studios for artists who find something of the same spirit in the contiguous Italian colony that the French artist is traditionally supposed to discover in his beloved Latin Quarter. These artists uncover something of the picturesque in the foreign colonies, which they have reproduced in painting, etching, and lithography. They find their classes filled not only by young people possessing facility and sometimes talent, but also by older people to whom the studio affords the one opportunity of escape from dreariness; a widow with four children who supplemented a very inadequate income by teaching the piano, for six years never missed her weekly painting lesson because it was “her one pleasure”; another woman, whose youth and strength had gone into the care of an invalid father, poured into her afternoon in the studio once a week, all of the longing for self-expression which she habitually suppressed.

===Chinese===

一个希腊人看见赫尔会所里一张卫城的照片，惊讶得很：他在芝加哥住了十三年，从没遇到过知道这件天下第一荣耀的美国人。离开希腊之前，他想象美国人一定巴望着看雅典的照片；他是工科学校毕业的，还备了一册彩色画，收集了一套照片，满心以为美国人会喜欢。可是，尽管他在一个大车站旁摆水果摊，同许多美国人攀谈过，也常想把话头引回古希腊，却从没有人接茬；末了他断定「芝加哥的人对古代一无所知」。

借来展出的画一直办到芝加哥艺术学院（Chicago Art Institute）星期天下午免费向公众开放；赫尔会所组织起一批批人，由向导领着过去。后来画廊在全城各处出了名，艺术学院管理处又大力让画走近大众，连这样的结伴也停了。

赫尔会所从起初就设着一间画室，这些年在居民本尼迪克特小姐（Miss Benedict）——她是艺术学院的教师——主持下渐渐发展。赫尔会所四合院里的楼房为艺术家们提供画室；他们在紧邻的意大利侨民地里找到的，正是法国艺术家素来该在他心爱的拉丁区找到的那种气息。这些艺术家从外国移民聚落里发掘出画意，用油画、蚀刻、石版画再现出来。他们的班里不但坐着手头利落、有时颇有天分的年轻人，也坐着年纪更大的人——画室给了他们唯一逃离沉闷的机会：一位带着四个孩子的寡妇，靠教钢琴补贴入不敷出的家用，六年里一星期一堂的绘画课从不缺席，因为那是「她唯一的乐趣」；另一位女人，青春和力气都耗在照护瘫病的父亲上，每星期把一个下午倾进画室，倾进她惯常死死压住的那份自我表现的渴望。

===Original===

Perhaps the most satisfactory results of the studio have been obtained through the classes of young men who are engaged in the commercial arts, and who are glad to have an opportunity to work out their own ideas. This is true of young engravers and lithographers; of the men who have to do with posters and illustrations in various ways. The little pile of stones and the lithographer’s handpress in a corner of the studio have been used in many an experiment, as has a set of beautiful type loaned to Hull House by a bibliophile.

===Chinese===

画室最叫人满意的成果，恐怕要从那些从事商业艺术的青年的班里得来：他们巴不得有个机会做出自己的构思。年轻的雕版工、石印工如此；同海报和插图打各式各样交道的人也如此。画室角落里那一小摞石版和石印手摇机，用在一回又一回的试验里；一位藏书家借给赫尔会所的一套漂亮活字也一样。

===Original===

The work of the studio almost imperceptibly merged into the crafts and well within the first decade a shop was opened at Hull House under the direction of several residents who were also members of the Chicago Arts and Crafts Society. This shop is not merely a school where people are taught and then sent forth to use their teaching in art according to their individual initiative and opportunity, but where those who have already been carefully trained, may express the best they can in wood or metal. The Settlement soon discovers how difficult it is to put a fringe of art on the end of a day spent in a factory. We constantly see young people doing overhurried work. Wrapping bars of soap in pieces of paper might at least give the pleasure of accuracy and repetition if it could be done at a normal pace, but when paid for by the piece, speed becomes the sole requirement and the last suggestion of human interest is taken away. In contrast to this the Hull House shop affords many examples of the restorative power in the exercise of a genuine craft; a young Russian who, like too many of his countrymen, had made a desperate effort to fit himself for a learned profession, and who had almost finished his course in a night law school, used to watch constantly the work being done in the metal shop at Hull House. One evening in a moment of sudden resolve, he took off his coat, sat down at one of the benches, and began to work, obviously as a very clever silversmith. He had long concealed his craft because he thought it would hurt his efforts as a lawyer and because he imagined an office more honorable and “more American” than a shop. As he worked on during his two leisure evenings each week, his entire bearing and conversation registered the relief of one who abandons the effort he is not fitted for and becomes a man on his own feet, expressing himself through a familiar and delicate technique.

Miss Starr at length found herself quite impatient with her role of lecturer on the arts, while all the handicraft about her was untouched by beauty and did not even reflect the interest of the workman. She took a training in bookbinding in London under Mr. Cobden-Sanderson and established her bindery at Hull House in which design and workmanship, beauty and thoroughness are taught to a small number of apprentices.

===Chinese===

画室的工作几乎不知不觉地汇进了手工工艺；不出头一个十年，赫尔会所就开了一间工场，由几位居民主持——他们也是芝加哥艺术与手工协会（Chicago Arts and Crafts Society）的会员。这间工场不只是一所教人的学校，把人教完便打发出去凭各人的主动与机会去用他们的艺术；它还是让那些已经受过细心训练的人，在木头或金属上尽力做出最好的东西的地方。定居所很快便发现，要在工厂里耗掉一天之后给这一天缀上一圈艺术的边，多么难。我们不断看见年轻人做着过赶的活。给肥皂块包纸，若能按正常的速度做，至少还给人准确与重复的乐趣；可是一按件计酬，快成了唯一的要求，最后一点人的兴味也被抽走了。同这个对照，赫尔会所的工场里尽是实例，证明一门真正的手艺的操练有回天之力：一个年轻的俄国人——像他太多的同胞一样——拼命要把自己造就成一个学问职业的人，夜校法学院都快念完了，却总是站在赫尔会所金属工场的活计旁看。一天晚上，他突然下了决心，脱下外衣，在一张工作台前坐下干了起来——一望便知是个手艺极好的银匠。他一直藏着这门手艺，因为他以为它会坏了他的律师前程，又因为在他想象里，一间写字楼比一间铺子体面，「更像美国人」。此后每星期两个闲晚，他一路做下去；他整个的神态谈吐，都显出一个放下自己不配的努力、从此凭熟悉而精微的手艺自立的人的松快。

斯塔尔小姐终于对自己当艺术讲师这个角色很不耐烦了——她周身的手工物件，件件沾不上美，连做工人的兴味都照不出来。她到伦敦在科布登-桑德森先生（Mr. Cobden-Sanderson）门下学了书籍装订，在赫尔会所办起自己的装订所，把设计与手艺、美与彻底，教给一小批学徒。

===Original===

From the very first winter, concerts which are still continued were given every Sunday afternoon in the Hull House drawing-room and later, as the audiences increased, in the larger halls. For these we are indebted to musicians from every part of the city. Mr. William Tomlins early trained large choruses of adults as his assistants did of children, and the response to all of these showed that while the number of people in our vicinity caring for the best music was not large, they constituted a steady and appreciative group. It was in connection with these first choruses that a public-spirited citizen of Chicago offered a prize for the best labor song, competition to be open to the entire country. The responses to the offer literally filled three large barrels and speaking at least for myself as one of the bewildered judges, we were more disheartened by their quality than even by their overwhelming bulk. Apparently the workers of America are not yet ready to sing, although I recall a creditable chorus trained at Hull House for a large meeting in sympathy with the anthracite coal strike in which the swinging lines

“Who was it made the coal?

Our God as well as theirs.”

seemed to relieve the tension of the moment. Miss Eleanor Smith, the head of the Hull House Music School, who had put the words to music, performed the same office for the “Sweatshop” of the Yiddish poet, the translation of which presents so graphically the bewilderment and tedium of the New York shop that it might be applied to almost any other machinery industry as the first verse indicates:﻿—

“The roaring of the wheels has filled my ears,

The clashing and the clamor shut me in,

Myself, my soul, in chaos disappears,

I cannot think or feel amid the din.”

It may be that this plaint explains the lack of labor songs in this period of industrial maladjustment when the worker is overmastered by his very tools. In addition to sharing with our neighborhood the best music we could procure, we have conscientiously provided careful musical instruction that at least a few young people might understand those old usages of art; that they might master its trade secrets, for after all it is only through a careful technique that artistic ability can express itself and be preserved.

===Chinese===

从第一个冬天起，赫尔会所客厅里每个星期天下午都有音乐会，至今不辍；后来听众多了，移到大些的厅里。这些音乐会要感谢全城各处的音乐家。威廉·汤姆林斯（Mr. William Tomlins）早早训练了成人的大合唱团，他的助手们也训练了儿童的；各方面的响应表明：我们这一带关心最好音乐的人虽不多，却是一群稳定而知音的人。正是同这些最早的合唱团相连接，芝加哥一位热心公益的公民设了一个最佳劳动歌曲奖，竞赛向全国开放。应征之作当真装满了三大桶；至少拿我自己这个被弄得晕头转向的评判人来说，它们的质量比它们的吓人数量更叫我们灰心。看来美国的工人还没有到歌唱的时候，虽然我记得赫尔会所为一声援无烟煤罢工的大会练过一支像样的合唱，那摇曳的两行——

「是谁造了煤？

是我们的上帝，也是他们的。」

——仿佛当真松了松那一刻的紧张。赫尔会所音乐学校主任埃莉诺·史密斯小姐（Miss Eleanor Smith）为它谱了曲；她又为那位意第绪语诗人的《血汗作坊》做了同样的事——那篇译文把纽约作坊里的昏乱与乏味写得那样活，几乎可以挪到任何别的机器工业上，首节便是：——

「轮子的轰鸣灌满我的耳朵，

铿锵与喧嚣把我关在里面，

我自己、我的灵魂，在混沌里消没，

在喧声中我不能思考，不能感受。」

也许这首哀歌正说明了这个工业失调的年头为什么缺少劳动歌曲：工人叫他自己的工具压倒了。除了同街坊分享我们能弄到的最好的音乐，我们也认真提供细心的音乐教导，好让至少少数年轻人懂得艺术那些古老的习用；让他们掌握它的行内秘密——毕竟，艺术才能要表现自己、要保存下来，只有经过细心的技巧一途。

===Original===

From the beginning we had classes in music, and the Hull House Music School, which is housed in quarters of its own in our quieter court, was opened in 1893. The school is designed to give a thorough musical instruction to a limited number of children. From the first lessons they are taught to compose and to reduce to order the musical suggestions which may come to them, and in this wise the school has sometimes been able to recover the songs of the immigrants through their children. Some of these folk songs have never been committed to paper, but have survived through the centuries because of a touch of undying poetry which the world has always cherished; as in the song of a Russian who is digging a post hole and finds his task dull and difficult until he strikes a stratum of red sand, which in addition to making digging easy, reminds him of the red hair of his sweetheart, and all goes merrily as the song lifts into a joyous melody. I recall again the almost hilarious enjoyment of the adult audience to whom it was sung by the children who had revived it, as well as the more sober appreciation of the hymns taken from the lips of the cantor, whose father before him had officiated in the synagogue.

The recitals and concerts given by the school are attended by large and appreciative audiences. On the Sunday before Christmas the program of Christmas songs draws together people of the most diverging faiths. In the deep tones of the memorial organ erected at Hull House, we realize that music is perhaps the most potent agent for making the universal appeal and inducing men to forget their differences.

===Chinese===

从一开头我们就开音乐班；设在自家院落里清静处的赫尔会所音乐学校（Hull House Music School），是1893年开办的。学校的宗旨，是给有限数目的孩子彻底的音乐教导。从最初的课起，就教他们作曲，把来到他们心里的乐思理出个秩序；靠着这个办法，学校有时竟能借孩子们之手，把移民的歌找回来。这些民歌有的从来不曾写上纸，只因为带着一点不死诗意——世界一向珍爱的诗意——便活过了多少个世纪；比如那首俄国人挖柱坑的歌：活又闷又难，直到他挖到一层红沙——红沙不光省力，还叫他想起心上人的红头发——歌一扬起，成了欢乐的调子，一切也都顺溜了。我还记得孩子们唱起这支复活的歌时，满堂大人那近乎开怀的陶醉；也记得领唱人唇边取来的圣歌所得的更肃穆的赏识——领唱人的父亲，在他之前就在犹太会堂里司职。

学校的演奏会与音乐会，听众多而懂味。圣诞节前的星期天，圣诞歌曲的节目把信仰相去最远的人聚到一处。在赫尔会所那架纪念管风琴的低音里，我们体会到：音乐恐怕是最有力的媒介，能作普遍的诉求，能叫人忘掉彼此的分别。

===Original===

Some of the pupils in the music school have developed during the years into trained musicians and are supporting themselves in their chosen profession. On the other hand, we constantly see the most promising musical ability extinguished when the young people enter industries which so sap their vitality that they cannot carry on serious study in the scanty hours outside of factory work. Many cases indisputably illustrate this: a Bohemian girl, who, in order to earn money for pressing family needs, first ruined her voice in a six months’ constant vaudeville engagement, returned to her trade working overtime in a vain effort to continue the vaudeville income; another young girl whom Hull House had sent to the high school so long as her parents consented, because we realized that a beautiful voice is often unavailable through lack of the informing mind, later extinguished her promise in a tobacco factory; a third girl who had supported her little sisters since she was fourteen, eagerly used her fine voice for earning money at entertainments held late after her day’s work, until exposure and fatigue ruined her health as well as a musician’s future; a young man whose music-loving family gave him every possible opportunity, and who produced some charming and even joyous songs during the long struggle with tuberculosis which preceded his death, had made a brave beginning, not only as a teacher of music but as a composer. In the little service held at Hull House in his memory, when the children sang his composition, “How Sweet is the Shepherd’s Sweet Lot,” it was hard to realize that such an interpretive pastoral could have been produced by one whose childhood had been passed in a crowded city quarter.

===Chinese===

音乐学校的学生里，有几个这些年长成了受过训练的音乐家，靠选定的职业自立。另一方面，我们也不断眼见最有希望的音乐才能熄灭：年轻人进了那些吸干他们血力的行业，工厂以外那点可怜的钟头，再撑不起认真的研习。许多例子无可争辩地说明了这一点：一个波希米亚姑娘，为挣家里的急用钱，先在杂耍场连演六个月毁了嗓子，回头再干本行，加班加点，妄想留住杂耍场的进项；另一个姑娘，赫尔会所送她上中学，一直上到父母不再答应——因为我们明白，一副好嗓子缺了那个点醒它的头脑，往往白搭——后来在一座烟草厂里把前程断送了；第三个姑娘，从十四岁起养活两个小妹妹，用她那副好嗓子在收工后开到深夜的游艺会上挣钱，直到风霜与劳累毁了她的健康，也毁了音乐家的前途；一个小伙子，全家爱乐，给了他一切可能的机会，他在同肺病长久的搏斗里写出过几支迷人的、甚至欢快的歌，死前不但在教音乐上开了个好头，作曲上也开了个好头。赫尔会所为追念他开的小小礼拜上，孩子们唱了他写的《牧人的命运何其甘美》（“How Sweet is the Shepherd's Sweet Lot”）；这样一支解意的牧歌，竟出自一个在拥挤城市街区里长大的孩子之手，叫人难以相信。

===Original===

Even that bitter experience did not prepare us for the sorrowful year when six promising pupils out of a class of fifteen, developed tuberculosis. It required but little penetration to see that during the eight years the class of fifteen school children had come together to the music school, they had approximately an even chance, but as soon as they reached the legal working age only a scanty moiety of those who became self-supporting could endure the strain of long hours and bad air. Thus the average human youth, “With all the sweetness of the common dawn,” is flung into the vortex of industrial life wherein the everyday tragedy escapes us save when one of them becomes conspicuously unfortunate. Twice in one year we were compelled

“To find the inheritance of this poor child

His little kingdom of a forced grave.”

It has been pointed out many times that Art lives by devouring her own offspring and the world has come to justify even that sacrifice, but we are unfortified and unsolaced when we see the children of Art devoured, not by her, but by the uncouth stranger, Modern Industry, who, needlessly ruthless and brutal to her own children, is quickly fatal to the offspring of the gentler mother. And so schools in art for those who go to work at the age when more fortunate young people are still sheltered and educated, constantly epitomize one of the haunting problems of life; why do we permit the waste of this most precious human faculty, this consummate possession of civilization? When we fail to provide the vessel in which it may be treasured, it runs out upon the ground and is irretrievably lost.

===Chinese===

就连那样惨痛的经历，也没有让我们对随后那个伤心的年头有半点准备：一个十五人的班里，六个最有希望的学生得了肺痨。不用多少眼力也看得出来：这十五个学童一块儿来上音乐学校的八年里，他们的机会大体均等；可一到法定做工的年纪，自立了的那些人里，熬得住长工时和浊空气的，只剩可怜的一小半。就这样，寻常的少年人，「带着寻常黎明的全部甘美」，被掷进了工业生活的漩涡；里面的日常悲剧，我们一概看不见，除非哪一个遭了特别显眼的不幸。一年里我们竟两回不得不——

「眼看这可怜孩子的继承，

只是一方逼来的坟墓，做他的小小王国。」

人们早就指出过：艺术靠吞吃自己的儿女活着，世界也已经把那样的牺牲认作正当；可我们看见艺术的儿女被吞吃，吞吃它们的不是艺术，而是那个粗野的生客——现代工业——对自家儿女尚且那样无谓的狠与横，对这位温柔母亲的儿女，转眼便致命——这时我们既无遮拦，也无安慰。于是，那些在别的幸运青年还被庇护着受教育时便去做工的人的艺术学校，就不断把人生一个萦绕不去的问题缩在掌心：我们为什么容许浪费这份最宝贵的人的官能、这文明最圆满的禀赋？我们不备下收藏它的器皿，它便流到地上，再也收不回来。

===Original===

The universal desire for the portrayal of life lying quite outside of personal experience evinces itself in many forms. One of the conspicuous features of our neighborhood, as of all industrial quarters, is the persistency with which the entire population attends the theater. The very first day I saw Halsted Street a long line of young men and boys stood outside the gallery entrance of the Bijou Theater, waiting for the Sunday matinee to begin at two o’clock, although it was only high noon. This waiting crowd might have been seen every Sunday afternoon during the twenty years which have elapsed since then. Our first Sunday evening in Hull House, when a group of small boys sat on our piazza and told us “about things around here,” their talk was all of the theater and of the astonishing things they had seen that afternoon.

But quite as it was difficult to discover the habits and purposes of this group of boys because they much preferred talking about the theater to contemplating their own lives, so it was all along the line; the young men told us their ambitions in the phrases of stage heroes, and the girls, so far as their romantic dreams could be shyly put into words, possessed no others but those soiled by long use in the melodrama. All of these young people looked upon an afternoon a week in the gallery of a Halsted Street theater as their one opportunity to see life. The sort of melodrama they see there has recently been described as “the ten commandments written in red fire.” Certainly the villain always comes to a violent end, and the young and handsome hero is rewarded by marriage with a beautiful girl, usually the daughter of a millionaire, but after all that is not a portrayal of the morality of the ten commandments any more than of life itself.

===Chinese===

想把全然亲身经历之外的人生描画出来的普遍渴望，以许多形态显出来。我们街坊——正如一切工业区——最扎眼的景象之一，是全人口那样锲而不舍地上戏院。我头一回见到哈尔斯特德街的那一天，一长列小伙子和孩子站在宝石剧院（Bijou Theater）顶层楼座入口外，等下午两点开演的星期天日场——那时才正午。此后二十年间，每个星期天下午都能看到这等场的人。我们住进赫尔会所的第一个星期天晚上，一群小男孩坐在我们廊台上讲「这一带的事」，讲的净是戏院，净是他们下午看过的那些了不得的东西。

可是，正如这群男孩宁可谈戏院、不愿想一想自己的日子，弄得人难于摸清他们的习性与打算，全线也都是如此：小伙子们用舞台英雄的腔调向我们讲他们的抱负；姑娘们的浪漫梦，能羞答答说出口的，除了情节剧里用得发旧的那几套，再没有别的。这些年轻人统统把一星期一个下午、哈尔斯特德街某家戏院的顶层楼座，看作他们见识人生的唯一机会。他们在那里看到的那路情节剧，新近被人形容作「用红火写成的十诫」。不错，恶棍到头来总不得好死，年轻英俊的主人公总有美人相酬——通常是一位百万富翁的千金；可那毕竟不是十诫道德的写照，也不是人生的写照。

===Original===

Nevertheless the theater, such as it was, appeared to be the one agency which freed the boys and girls from that destructive isolation of those who drag themselves up to maturity by themselves, and it gave them a glimpse of that order and beauty into which even the poorest drama endeavors to restore the bewildering facts of life. The most prosaic young people bear testimony to this overmastering desire. A striking illustration of this came to us during our second year’s residence on Halsted Street through an incident in the Italian colony, where the men have always boasted that they were able to guard their daughters from the dangers of city life, and until evil Italians entered the business of the “white slave traffic,” their boast was well founded. The first Italian girl to go astray known to the residents of Hull House, was so fascinated by the stage that on her way home from work she always loitered outside a theater before the enticing posters. Three months after her elopement with an actor, her distracted mother received a picture of her dressed in the men’s clothes in which she appeared in vaudeville. Her family mourned her as dead and her name was never mentioned among them nor in the entire colony. In further illustration of an overmastering desire to see life as portrayed on the stage are two young girls whose sober parents did not approve of the theater and would allow no money for such foolish purposes. In sheer desperation the sisters evolved a plot that one of them would feign a toothache, and while she was having her tooth pulled by a neighboring dentist the other would steal the gold crowns from his table, and with the money thus procured they could attend the vaudeville theater every night on their way home from work. Apparently the pain and wrongdoing did not weigh for a moment against the anticipated pleasure. The plan was carried out to the point of selling the gold crowns to a pawnbroker when the disappointed girls were arrested.

===Chinese===

然而那样的戏院，似乎是唯一能把少男少女从那种孤绝里解放出来的东西——自个儿把自个儿拖扯到大的人的孤绝；它给了他们一瞥那种秩序与美：哪怕最蹩脚的戏，也在设法把人生的纷乱事实归置回秩序与美里去。再平淡无奇的年轻人，也见证着这股压倒一切的渴望。我们住在哈尔斯特德街的第二年，意大利侨民地里的一件事，给了我们一个触目的例证。那里的男人向来夸口，说他们守得住女儿，不受城市生活的危险；直到意大利的恶人也做起「白奴生意」，这海口才算落空。赫尔会所居民所知的头一个失足的意大利姑娘，就是叫舞台迷住的：下了工回家，总在一家戏院外那些诱人的海报前磨蹭。她同一个演员私奔三个月后，走投无路的母亲收到她一张相片：她穿着男人的衣裳——她就在杂耍场里那样登台。家里当她死了为她举哀，她的名字，家里人不再提，整个侨民地也不再提。想看舞台上演的人生、那股压倒一切的渴望，还有两个姑娘作证：她们持重的父母不赞成戏院，一个子儿也不肯给这等傻用途。两姐妹绝望之余定下一条计：一个装牙疼，趁邻家牙医拔牙的当口，另一个把他桌上的金冠偷了；拿这笔钱，往后每天收工回家路上都能进杂耍场。看来疼痛与罪过，在同盼望中的乐趣一比之下，片刻也没压过秤。计划一路做下去，做到把金冠卖给当铺的当口，两个失望的姑娘被抓住了。

===Original===

All this effort to see the play took place in the years before the five-cent theaters had become a feature of every crowded city thoroughfare and before their popularity had induced the attendance of two and a quarter million people in the United States every twenty-four hours. The eagerness of the penniless children to get into these magic spaces is responsible for an entire crop of petty crimes made more easy because two children are admitted for one nickel at the last performance when the hour is late and the theater nearly deserted. The Hull House residents were aghast at the early popularity of these mimic shows, and in the days before the inspection of films and the present regulations for the five-cent theaters we established at Hull House a moving picture show. Although its success justified its existence, it was so obviously but one in the midst of hundreds that it seemed much more advisable to turn our attention to the improvement of all of them or rather to assist as best we could, the successful efforts in this direction by the Juvenile Protective Association.

However, long before the five-cent theater was even heard of, we had accumulated much testimony as to the power of the drama, and we would have been dull indeed if we had not availed ourselves of the use of the play at Hull House, not only as an agent of recreation and education, but as a vehicle of self-expression for the teeming young life all about us.

Long before the Hull House theater was built we had many plays, first in the drawing-room and later in the gymnasium. The young people’s clubs never tired of rehearsing and preparing for these dramatic occasions, and we also discovered that older people were almost equally ready and talented. We quickly learned that no celebration at Thanksgiving was so popular as a graphic portrayal on the stage of the Pilgrim Fathers, and we were often put to it to reduce to dramatic effects the great days of patriotism and religion.

===Chinese===

这一切看戏的努力，都发生在五分钱戏院还没挤满每条热闹街道、还没招得全美国每二十四小时有二百二十五万人进场之前。分文全无的孩子钻进这些魔地的急切，养出了整整一茬轻罪：末一场夜深、戏院快空的时候，一个镍币放两个孩子，更给这茬罪开了方便。赫尔会所的居民眼见这类模仿戏早早走红，惊骇不已；在影片审查和如今的五分钱戏院章程还没有的日子，我们在赫尔会所办起了一场电影放映。它办得成功，存在得有理；可它分明只是几百家里的一家，与其如此，远不如把注意力转向改良它们全体——或者说，尽力去帮少年保护协会（Juvenile Protective Association）在这方面已经见效的努力。

不过，远在人们听说五分钱戏院之前，我们就攒下了许多关于戏剧的威力的证言；若不把戏剧拿来为赫尔会所所用——不但当作消遣与教育的媒介，也当作我们四周沸腾的年轻生命的自我表现的载具——那我们真是迟钝了。

赫尔会所剧场还没有盖起来之前，我们早就演过许多戏：先在客厅，后在健身房。青年们的俱乐部排练、筹备这些演戏的日子，从不知疲倦；我们又发现，年长的人也几乎同样热心、同样有才。我们很快懂得：感恩节最叫座的庆祝，莫过于把朝圣移民先祖在台上活画一遍；爱国与宗教的大日子，也常逼得我们没法，非编成戏剧效果不可。

===Original===

At one of our early Christmas celebrations Longfellow’s “Golden Legend” was given, the actors portraying it with the touch of the miracle play spirit which it reflects. I remember an old blind man, who took the part of a shepherd, said, at the end of the last performance, “Kind Heart,” a name by which he always addressed me, “it seems to me that I have been waiting all my life to hear some of these things said. I am glad we had so many performances, for I think I can remember them to the end. It is getting hard for me to listen to reading, but the different voices and all made this very plain.” Had he not perhaps made a legitimate demand upon the drama, that it shall express for us that which we have not been able to formulate for ourselves, that it shall warm us with a sense of companionship with the experiences of others; does not every genuine drama present our relations to each other and to the world in which we find ourselves in such wise as may fortify us to the end of the journey?

The immigrants in the neighborhood of Hull House have utilized our little stage in an endeavor to reproduce the past of their own nations through those immortal dramas which have escaped from the restraining bond of one country into the land of the universal.

===Chinese===

早年间的一次圣诞庆典上，演了朗费罗的《金色传奇》（“Golden Legend”），演员带着它所映照的那一点奇迹剧的神味。我记得一个演牧人的瞎眼老头，在末一场演完时说——「善心人」，他一向这样称呼我——「我好像等了一辈子，就等着听人把这些话讲出来。演这么多场我很高兴，我想我能记到底了。如今听人念书我吃力了，可这许多不同的嗓音，把话都说得清清楚楚。」他这话，岂不是对戏剧提了一个正当的要求：它该替我们说出我们自己说不成形的东西，该用一份同旁人经历作伴的暖意焐热我们；每一部真正的戏剧，把我们彼此的关系、把我们安身的这个世界的关系摆出来，不正是这样摆，才撑得住我们走到旅程尽头么？

赫尔会所街坊的移民们，借我们这座小舞台，试着再现他们各自民族的往昔——用那些挣脱了一国的拘束、进了普天之下的国土的不朽戏剧。

===Original===

A large colony of Greeks near Hull House, who often feel that their history and classic background are completely ignored by Americans, and that they are easily confused with the more ignorant immigrants from other parts of southeastern Europe, welcome an occasion to present Greek plays in the ancient text. With expert help in the difficulties of staging and rehearsing a classic play, they reproduced the *Ajax* of Sophocles upon the Hull House stage. It was a genuine triumph to the actors who felt that they were “showing forth the glory of Greece” to “ignorant Americans.” The scholar who came with a copy of Sophocles in hand and followed the play with real enjoyment, did not in the least realize that the revelation of the love of Greek poets was mutual between the audience and the actors. The Greeks have quite recently assisted an enthusiast in producing *Elektra*, while the Lithuanians, the Poles, and other Russian subjects often use the Hull House stage to present plays in their own tongue, which shall at one and the same time keep alive their sense of participation in the great Russian revolution and relieve their feelings in regard to it. There is something still more appealing in the yearning efforts the immigrants sometimes make to formulate their situation in America. I recall a play written by an Italian playwright of our neighborhood, which depicted the insolent break between Americanized sons and old country parents, so touchingly that it moved to tears all the older Italians in the audience. Did the tears of each express relief in finding that others had had the same experience as himself, and did the knowledge free each one from a sense of isolation and an injured belief that his children were the worst of all?

This effort to understand life through its dramatic portrayal, to see one’s own participation intelligibly set forth, becomes difficult when one enters the field of social development, but even here it is not impossible if a Settlement group is constantly searching for new material.

===Chinese===

赫尔会所附近的一大片希腊侨民，常常觉得美国人对他们的历史与古典底子全然视而不见，又轻易把他们同东南欧别处来的更无知的移民混为一谈；能有机会用古文原文演出希腊戏，他们求之不得。有行家帮忙解决搬演与排练古典戏的种种难处，他们在赫尔会所舞台上排出了索福克勒斯的《埃阿斯》（*Ajax*）。对演员们，这是一场真正的凯旋：他们觉得正把「希腊的荣光」展示给「无知的美国人」。那位带着一册索福克勒斯、看得津津有味的学者，全然没有觉出：对希腊诗人的爱这一层启示，观众同演员是彼此彼此。希腊人不久前还帮一位热心人演了《厄勒克特拉》（*Elektra*）；立陶宛人、波兰人和其他俄国的臣民，也常用赫尔会所的舞台演本族语的戏——既活住他们参与伟大的俄国革命之感，又抒一抒对它的胸中块垒。更动人的，是移民们有时为他们的美国处境寻求表述的苦苦努力。我记得街坊里一位意大利剧作家写的一出戏，写美国化的儿女同故国的父母之间傲慢的决裂，写得那样动人，观众里年长的意大利人个个落泪。那些泪，是不是每一双都在松一口气——原来遭过同样事的，不止我一个？这一点知道，是不是把每个人从孤绝里、从「我家孩子天下最坏」那口气苦的认定里放了出来？

想借戏剧的描画理解人生、想把自己的一份明白地摆出来，一进到社会发展这块地里就难了；可即便在这里，也不是全无可能——只要定居所的一班人肯不断寻新材料。

===Original===

A labor story appearing in the Atlantic Monthly was kindly dramatized for us by the author who also superintended its presentation upon the Hull House stage. The little drama presented the untutored effort of a trades-union man to secure for his side the beauty of self-sacrifice, the glamour of martyrdom, which so often seems to belong solely to the nonunion forces. The presentation of the play was attended by an audience of trades-unionists and employers and those other people who are supposed to make public opinion. Together they felt the moral beauty of the man’s conclusion that “it’s the side that suffers most that will win out in this war﻿—the saints is the only ones that has got the world under their feet﻿—we’ve got to do the way they done if the unions is to stand,” so completely that it seemed quite natural that he should forfeit his life upon the truth of this statement.

The dramatic arts have gradually been developed at Hull House through amateur companies, one of which has held together for more than fifteen years. The members were originally selected from the young people who had evinced talent in the plays the social clubs were always giving, but the association now adds to itself only as a vacancy occurs. Some of them have developed almost a professional ability, although contrary to all predictions and in spite of several offers, none of them have taken to a stage career. They present all sorts of plays from melodrama and comedy to those of Shaw, Ibsen, and Galsworthy. The latter are surprisingly popular, perhaps because of their sincere attempt to expose the shams and pretenses of contemporary life and to penetrate into some of its perplexing social and domestic situations. Through such plays the stage may become a pioneer teacher of social righteousness.

I have come to believe, however, that the stage may do more than teach, that much of our current moral instruction will not endure the test of being cast into a lifelike mold, and when presented in dramatic form will reveal itself as platitudinous and effete. That which may have sounded like righteous teaching when it was remote and wordy, will be challenged afresh when it is obliged to simulate life itself.

===Chinese===

《大西洋月刊》（Atlantic Monthly）上登过一篇劳动故事，作者好心地为我们改编成戏，又亲自督导它在赫尔会所舞台上的演出。小戏写一个工会里人朴质的努力：要为自家这一边争得自我牺牲之美、殉道之光——那似乎向来只属于非工会一边。看演出的，有工会员、雇主，还有那些据认为制造舆论的人。他们一同感受到那个人结论里的道德之美：「吃苦最多的一边，终归要赢——圣徒是唯一把世界踩在脚底下的人——工会要立得住，就得照他们的样子做」——感受得那样彻，以至他为这句话舍了性命，都显得顺理成章。

戏剧艺术在赫尔会所经业余剧团渐渐发展起来，其中一个剧团聚了十五年还散不了。成员起初从各社交俱乐部屡屡演的戏里显过才具的青年中挑选，如今只在出缺时才补人。他们中有几位练出了近乎职业的本事；尽管同一切预言相反，尽管有过几次邀约，谁也没有去吃舞台饭。他们演各色戏，从情节剧、喜剧到萧伯纳（Shaw）、易卜生（Ibsen）、高尔斯华绥（Galsworthy）。后一类出奇地受欢迎，也许因为他们真心想揭当代生活的虚饰与假面，想钻进它几处费解的社会与家庭情境。透过这样的戏，舞台可以成为社会正义的开路教师。

不过我渐渐相信，舞台能做的不止于教：我们许多现行的道德训条，经不起翻成活生生模子的考验；一用戏剧的形式摆出来，就露了本相——陈腐而萎顿。那些远远讲来、洋洋洒洒时听着像正训的话，一旦必须模拟人生本身，就要重新受审。

===Original===

This function of the stage, as a reconstructing and reorganizing agent of accepted moral truths, came to me with overwhelming force as I listened to the Passion Play at Oberammergau one beautiful summer’s day in 1900. The peasants who portrayed exactly the successive scenes of the wonderful Life, who used only the very words found in the accepted version of the Gospels, yet curiously modernized and reorientated the message. They made clear that the opposition to the young Teacher sprang from the merchants whose traffic in the temple He had disturbed and from the Pharisees who were dependent upon them for support. Their query was curiously familiar, as they demanded the antecedents of the Radical who dared to touch vested interests, who presumed to dictate the morality of trade, and who insulted the marts of honest merchants by calling them “a den of thieves.” As the play developed, it became clear that this powerful opposition had friends in Church and State, that they controlled influences which ramified in all directions. They obviously believed in their statement of the case and their very wealth and position in the community gave their words such weight that finally all of their hearers were convinced that the young Agitator must be done away with in order that the highest interests of society might be conserved. These simple peasants made it clear that it was the money power which induced one of the Agitator’s closest friends to betray him, and the villain of the piece, Judas himself, was only a man who was so dazzled by money, so under the domination of all it represented, that he was perpetually blind to the spiritual vision unrolling before him. As I sat through the long summer day, seeing the shadows on the beautiful mountain back of the open stage shift from one side to the other and finally grow long and pointed in the soft evening light, my mind was filled with perplexing questions. Did the dramatization of the life of Jesus set forth its meaning more clearly and conclusively than talking and preaching could possibly do as a shadowy following of the command “to do the will”?

===Chinese===

舞台的这种功能——作为公认道德真理的重构与重整者——是1900年一个晴美的夏日，我在上阿默高（Oberammergau）听耶稣受难剧（Passion Play）时，以压倒的力量来到我心上的。那些农民，一幕不差地扮演那奇妙的一生，只用福音通行本里找得到的原话，却古怪地把讯息现代化了、挪了方向。他们讲明白了：对那位年轻教师的敌意，来自那些圣殿买卖叫他搅了的商人，来自那些靠商人供养的法利赛人。他们的质问，古怪地耳熟：这胆敢碰既得利益的激进分子，出身如何？竟敢给买卖立道德！竟敢把诚实商人的市集叫作「贼窝」！戏一路演下去，越来越清楚：这股强大的反对势力，教会里、政府里都有朋友，它操控的影响四通八达。他们显然深信自己的说法；他们的财富和地位，又给他们的言辞那样的分量，到末了听众全都信了：要保全社会最高的利益，这位年轻的煽动家非除掉不可。这些淳朴的农民又讲明白：是金钱的势力，叫煽动家最贴心的朋友之一出卖了他；而戏里的反派犹大，不过是一个叫金钱晃花了眼、叫金钱所代表的一切辖制住、对眼前展开的属灵异象永远瞎了眼的人。那个长长的夏日，我坐在那里，看露天舞台背后美丽山上的影子从一边挪到另一边，末了在柔和的暮色里拉得又长又尖，心里塞满了费解的问题。耶稣生平的这场戏化，是不是比谈论与讲道更清楚、更彻底地摆出了它的意义——胜过对「行上帝旨意」那条命令影影绰绰的遵行？

===Original===

The peasant actors whom I had seen returning from mass that morning had prayed only to portray the life as He had lived it and, behold, out of their simplicity and piety arose this modern version which even Harnack was only then venturing to suggest to his advanced colleagues in Berlin. Yet the Oberammergau fold were very like thousands of immigrant men and women of Chicago, both in their experiences and in their familiarity with the hard facts of life, and throughout that day as my mind dwelt on my faraway neighbors, I was reproached with the sense of an ungarnered harvest.

===Chinese===

那天早晨我见过那些农民演员望完弥撒回来；他们求的只是照祂活过的样子演祂的一生。可看哪，从他们的淳朴与敬虔里，竟升起这样一个现代的版本——连哈纳克（Harnack）那时也不过刚敢向他在柏林的先进同僚做此暗示罢了。而上阿默高的羊群，同芝加哥成千上万的移民男女，何其相像——经历相像，对生活的硬事实的熟稔也相像。那一天，我的心思一路落在我那远方的街坊身上，一茬没有收割的庄稼，时时责备着我。

===Original===

Of course such a generally uplifted state comes only at rare moments, while the development of the little theater at Hull House has not depended upon the moods of anyone, but upon the genuine enthusiasm and sustained effort of a group of residents, several of them artists who have ungrudgingly given their time to it year after year. This group has long fostered junior dramatic associations, through which it seems possible to give a training in manners and morals more directly than through any other medium. They have learned to determine very cleverly the ages at which various types of the drama are most congruous and expressive of the sentiments of the little troupes, from the fairy plays such as *Snow White* and *Puss-in-Boots* which appeal to the youngest children, to the heroic plays of *William Tell*, *King John*, and *Wat Tyler* for the older lads, and to the romances and comedies which set forth in stately fashion the elaborated life which so many young people admire. A group of Jewish boys gave a dramatic version of the story of Joseph and his brethren and again of Queen Esther. They had almost a sense of proprietorship in the fine old lines and were pleased to bring from home bits of Talmudic lore for the stage setting. The same club of boys at one time will buoyantly give a roaring comedy and five years later will solemnly demand a drama dealing with modern industrial conditions. The Hull House theater is also rented from time to time to members of the Young People’s Socialist League who give plays both in Yiddish and English which reduce their propaganda to conversation. Through such humble experiments as the Hull House stage, as well as through the more ambitious reforms which are attempted in various parts of the country, the theatre may at last be restored to its rightful place in the community.

There have been times when our little stage was able to serve the theatre libre. A Chicago troupe, finding it difficult to break into a trust theater, used it one winter twice a week for the presentation of Ibsen and old French comedy. A visit from the Irish poet Yeats inspired us to do our share towards freeing the stage from its slavery to expensive scene setting, and a forest of stiff conventional trees against a gilt sky still remains with us as a reminder of an attempt not wholly unsuccessful, in this direction.

===Chinese===

当然，这样普遍昂扬的时刻，稀罕得很；赫尔会所小剧场的发展，从不系在任何人的心绪上，只系在一班居民真实的热情与不懈的努力上——其中几位是艺术家，年复一年，把时间慷慨地给了它。这一班人多年扶持着少年戏剧团，透过它们，似乎能把礼貌与道德教得比任何别的媒介都直接。他们学会了极乖巧地断定：哪一类型的戏同哪样年纪的小班子最相称、最见心——从最能抓住最小孩子的童话剧《白雪公主》（*Snow White*）、《穿靴子的猫》（*Puss-in-Boots*），到给大些的少年的英雄剧《威廉·退尔》（*William Tell*）、《约翰王》（*King John*）、《瓦特·泰勒》（*Wat Tyler*），再到以堂堂气派铺陈那许多年轻人艳羡的精工生活的传奇与喜剧。一群犹太男孩演过约瑟与他的兄弟们的故事改的戏，又演过以斯帖王后的。他们对着那些古雅的好台词，几乎有一份主人翁之感，还欢喜从家里带来一点塔木德的掌故做布景。同一班男孩，这时会兴冲冲演一出闹喜剧，五年后又正正经经点一出处理现代工业境况的正剧。赫尔会所的剧场还不时租给青年社会主义者同盟（Young People's Socialist League）的成员，他们用意第绪语和英语演把宣传化成谈话的戏。透过赫尔会所舞台这样朴素的试验，也透过全国各地试办的更大的改良，剧场或许终能复得它在社区里应有的位置。

也有过几回，我们的小舞台得以为自由剧场效力。芝加哥一个剧团苦于挤不进托拉斯的戏院，有一年冬天每星期两次用它演易卜生和老派的法国喜剧。爱尔兰诗人叶芝（Yeats）的一次来访，激励我们尽了自己一份力，把舞台从对昂贵布景的奴役里解放出来；一片镀金天空前僵硬呆板的树林子，至今还留在我们这里，作这个方向上一次并非全然失败的努力的纪念。

===Original===

This group of Hull House artists have filled our little foyer with a series of charming playbills and by dint of painting their own scenery and making their own costumes have obtained beguiling results in stage setting. Sometimes all the artistic resources of the House unite in a Wagnerian combination; thus, the text of the *Troll’s Holiday* was written by one resident, set to music by another; sung by the Music School, and placed upon the stage under the careful direction and training of the dramatic committee; and the little brown trolls could never have tumbled about so gracefully in their gleaming caves unless they had been taught in the gymnasium.

Some such synthesis takes place every year at the Hull House annual exhibition, when an effort is made to bring together in a spirit of holiday the nine thousand people who come to the House every week during duller times. Curiously enough the central feature at the annual exhibition seems to be the brass band of the boys’ club which apparently dominates the situation by sheer size and noise, but perhaps their fresh boyish enthusiasm expresses that which the older people take more soberly.

As the stage of our little theater had attempted to portray the heroes of many lands, so we planned one early spring seven years ago, to carry out a scheme of mural decoration upon the walls of the theater itself, which should portray those cosmopolitan heroes who have become great through identification with the common lot, in preference to the heroes of mere achievement. In addition to the group of artists living at Hull House several others were in temporary residence, and they all threw themselves enthusiastically into the plan. The series began with Tolstoy plowing his field which was painted by an artist of the Glasgow school, and the next was of the young Lincoln pushing his flatboat down the Mississippi River at the moment he received his first impression of the “great iniquity.” This was done by a promising young artist of Chicago, and the wall spaces nearest to the two selected heroes were quickly filled with their immortal sayings.

===Chinese===

这一班赫尔会所的艺术家，把我们的小门厅挂满了一套迷人的戏单；靠着自绘布景、自制行头，他们在舞台装置上得了诱人的成绩。有时全会所的艺术力量来一次瓦格纳式的合流：譬如《巨怪的节日》（*Troll's Holiday*），词是一位居民写的，曲是另一位谱的，由音乐学校演唱，在戏剧委员会细心的指导与训练下搬上舞台；而那群棕色的小巨怪，能在亮闪闪的洞穴里翻滚得那样漂亮，全亏健身房里教出来的功夫。

每年赫尔会所年展，都有这样一次综合：要把淡季里每星期来会所的九千人，怀着节日的兴致聚到一处。说来奇怪，年展中央的节目，仿佛是男孩俱乐部的铜管乐队——它显然单凭块头和声响就镇住了全场；不过，他们那一股少年的新鲜热气，也许正替年长的人说出了他们说得庄重的东西。

我们小剧场的舞台既演过许多国土的英雄，七年前一个初春，我们又计划在剧场自己的墙上做一套壁画，画那些因认同普通人的命运而伟大起来的世界主义英雄——不取单凭成就的英雄。除了住在赫尔会所的几位画家，另有几位短期驻留；大家热忱地投入了这个计划。头一幅是托尔斯泰犁他的地，出自一位格拉斯哥画派画家之手；第二幅是年轻的林肯把平底船撑下密西西比河——正是他头一回得着那「大罪孽」印象的一刻——出自一位很有前途的芝加哥青年画家；紧挨着这两位选定的英雄的墙面，很快写上了他们不朽的名言。

===Original===

A spirited discussion thereupon ensued in regard to the heroes for the two remaining large wall spaces, when to the surprise of all of us the group of twenty-five residents who had lived in unbroken harmony for more than ten years, suddenly broke up into cults and even camps of hero worship. Each cult exhibited drawings of its own hero in his most heroic moment, and of course each drawing received enthusiastic backing from the neighborhood, each according to the nationality of the hero. Thus Phidias standing high on his scaffold as he finished the heroic head of Athene; the young David dreamily playing his harp as he tended his father’s sheep at Bethlehem; St. Francis washing the feet of the leper; the young slave Patrick guiding his master through the bogs of Ireland, which he later rid of their dangers; the poet Hans Sachs cobbling shoes; Jeanne d’Arc dropping her spindle in startled wonder before the heavenly visitants, naturally all obtained such enthusiastic following from our cosmopolitan neighborhood that it was certain to give offense if any two were selected. Then there was the cult of residents who wished to keep the series contemporaneous with the two heroes already painted, and they advocated William Morris at his loom, Walt Whitman tramping the open road, Pasteur in his laboratory, or Florence Nightingale seeking the wounded on the field of battle. But beyond the socialists, few of the neighbors had heard of William Morris, and the fame of Walt Whitman was still more apocryphal; Pasteur was considered merely a clever scientist without the romance which evokes popular affection and in the provisional drawing submitted for votes, gentle Florence Nightingale was said “to look more as if she were robbing the dead than succoring the wounded.” The remark shows how high the feeling ran, and then, as something must be done quickly, we tried to unite upon strictly local heroes such as the famous fire marshal who had lived for many years in our neighborhood﻿—but why prolong this description which demonstrates once more that art, if not always the handmaid of religion, yet insists upon serving those deeper sentiments for which we unexpectedly find ourselves ready to fight.

===Chinese===

接着为余下两大块墙面该画谁，展开了一场火气十足的争论；叫我们全体吃惊的是，这二十五位十多年来和睦无间的居民，忽然分成了一个个派别，甚至英雄崇拜的营垒。每一派都拿出自家英雄最英雄一刻的画稿；每一张画稿自然都得到街坊热烈的声援——各随英雄的国籍。于是：菲狄亚斯高站在脚手架上，刚画完雅典娜英雄的头像；年轻的大卫在伯利恒放他父亲的羊，梦悠悠地弹着琴；圣方济各给麻风病人洗脚；年轻的奴隶帕特里克领着他的主人走出爱尔兰的泥沼——那泥沼，他后来替它除了害；诗人汉斯·萨克斯在补鞋；贞德在天使显临前惊愕地放落纺锤——这些自然统统在我们那世界主义的街坊里得了那样狂热的追随，选中任何两位都必定开罪于人。还有一派居民，主张这套画该同已画成的两位英雄同时代，力主织机前的威廉·莫里斯、大路上的惠特曼、实验室里的巴斯德，或战场上寻访伤员的南丁格尔。可除了社会主义者，街坊里没有几个人听说过威廉·莫里斯；惠特曼的名声更属传闻；巴斯德只被看作一个没有传奇的聪明科学家——传奇才动众人的情；而提交表决的暂用画稿上，温柔的南丁格尔被说成「瞧着不像救护伤员，倒像在抢死人的东西」。这句话可见群情之高。接着，因为总得赶紧办，我们试着统一定在纯粹本地的英雄身上，比如那位在我们街坊住了多年的著名消防队长——可为什么还要把这番描述拉长呢？它不过再一次表明：艺术即使不总当宗教的婢女，也执意要伺奉那些更深的情操——那些我们意外发现自己竟准备为之相斗的情操。

===Original===

When we were all fatigued and hopeless of compromise, we took refuge in a series of landscapes connected with our two heroes by a quotation from Wordsworth slightly distorted to meet our dire need, but still stating his impassioned belief in the efficacious spirit capable of companionship with man which resides in “particular spots.” Certainly peace emanates from the particular folding of the hills in one of our treasured mural landscapes, yet occasionally when a guest with a bewildered air looks from one side of the theater to the other, we are forced to conclude that the connection is not convincing.

In spite of its stormy career this attempt at mural decoration connects itself quite naturally with the spirit of our earlier efforts to make Hull House as beautiful as we could, which had in it a desire to embody in the outward aspect of the House something of the reminiscence and aspiration of the neighborhood life.

As the House enlarged for new needs and mellowed through slow-growing associations, we endeavored to fashion it from without, as it were, as well as from within. A tiny wall fountain modeled in classic pattern, for us penetrates into the world of the past, but for the Italian immigrant it may defy distance and barriers as he dimly responds to that typical beauty in which Italy has ever written its message, even as classic art knew no region of the gods which was not also sensuous, and as the art of Dante mysteriously blended the material and the spiritual.

Perhaps the early devotion of the Hull House residents to the pre-Raphaelites recognized that they above all English speaking poets and painters reveal “the sense of the expressiveness of outward things” which is at once the glory and the limitation of the arts.

===Chinese===

等到大家全都乏了、和解无望了，我们退守到一组风景画里，靠一句华兹华斯（Wordsworth）——为应急稍稍扭了一扭——把它同那两位英雄连上；那句诗依然说他炽热的信念：有一个能与人作伴的灵效之灵，住在「特定的地方」。确实，我们珍爱的一幅壁画风景里，山峦那一种叠法，发散着安宁；可偶尔有位客人一脸茫然，从剧场这头望到那头，我们便不得不承认：这层连缀并不叫人信服。

尽管命途多舛，这一番壁画的尝试，同我们早年尽力把赫尔会所弄得美的那股精神，接得十分自然；那里面有一份心愿：把街坊生活的追怀与向往，多少凝进会所的外表。

会所为新的需要扩建，又经缓缓滋长的情谊而温润，我们便努力从外面——像从里面一样——给它造型。一座按古典样式做的小墙泉，于我们，是通向往昔世界的一扇门；于意大利移民，它却也许能叫距离和关山失效——他朦朦胧胧地应和着那种典型的美，意大利向来是用它书写自己的讯息的；正如古典艺术不知道哪一个神明的领域不同时也是感官的领域，正如但丁的艺术神秘地把物质与灵性融为一体。

赫尔会所居民早年对拉斐尔前派的钟爱，或许正是认出了：在一切说英语的诗人和画家里，数他们最见「外物之表情」——那既是艺术的荣光，也是艺术的限度。

## XVII / 十七
===Original===

Echoes of the Russian Revolution

The residents of Hull House have always seen many evidences of the Russian Revolution; a forlorn family of little children whose parents have been massacred at Kishinev are received and supported by their relatives in our Chicago neighborhood; or a Russian woman, her face streaming with tears of indignation and pity, asks you to look at the scarred back of her sister, a young girl, who has escaped with her life from the whips of the Cossack soldiers; or a studious young woman suddenly disappears from the Hull House classes because she has returned to Kiev to be near her brother while he is in prison, that she may earn money for the nourishing food which alone will keep him from contracting tuberculosis; or we attend a protest meeting against the newest outrages of the Russian government in which the speeches are interrupted by the groans of those whose sons have been sacrificed and by the hisses of others who cannot repress their indignation. At such moments an American is acutely conscious of our ignorance of this greatest tragedy of modern times, and at our indifference to the waste of perhaps the noblest human material among our contemporaries. Certain it is, as the distinguished Russian revolutionists have come to Chicago, they have impressed me, as no one else ever has done, as belonging to that noble company of martyrs who have ever and again poured forth blood that human progress might be advanced. Sometimes these men and women have addressed audiences gathered quite outside the Russian colony and have filled to overflowing Chicago’s largest halls with American citizens deeply touched by this message of martyrdom. One significant meeting was addressed by a member of the Russian Duma and by one of Russia’s oldest and sanest revolutionists; another by Madame Breshkovsky, who later languished a prisoner in the fortress of St. Peter and St. Paul.

===Chinese===

俄国革命的回声

赫尔馆的居民们见过许多俄国革命的遗迹：一家人数幼小、父母在基什尼奥夫被屠戮的孤儿，由我们芝加哥街区的亲戚收留抚养；或者一个俄国女人，脸上淌着愤慨和怜悯的泪，请你去看她妹妹——一个年轻姑娘——背上鞭痕，她是从哥萨克士兵的皮鞭下捡回一条命的；或者一个好学的青年女子突然从赫尔馆的班里消失，因为她回基辅去了，要在坐牢的哥哥身边待着，好挣钱给他买滋养的吃食——只有那才能使他不得结核病；或者我们出席一场抗议俄国政府最新暴行的集会，演说不时被那些献出了儿子的人的呻吟、和另一些压抑不住义愤的人的嘘声打断。在这样的时刻，一个美国人痛切地意识到我们对这场近代最大悲剧的无知，意识到我们冷漠地看着同代人中也许最宝贵的人材被精蹋。可以确定的是，当那些杰出的俄国革命者来到芝加哥，他们给我的印象是谁都不曾给过的：他们属于那一队高贵的殉道者，这些人一次又一次抛洒热血，好叫人类的进步得以前进。这些男人女人有时在俄国侨民聚居区之外向听众讲演，把芝加哥最大的厅堂挤得满满的，满座的美国公民被这殉道的信息深深打动。一次有分量的大会，主讲人是俄国杜马的一位议员和俄国最年长最稳健的一位革命者；另一次是布列什科夫斯卡娅夫人（Madame Breshkovsky），她后来被囚在圣彼得堡圣保罗要塞里憔悴。

===Original===

In this wonderful procession of revolutionists, Prince Kropotkin, or, as he prefers to be called, Peter Kropotkin, was doubtless the most distinguished. When he came to America to lecture, he was heard throughout the country with great interest and respect; that he was a guest of Hull House during his stay in Chicago attracted little attention at the time, but two years later, when the assassination of President McKinley occurred, the visit of this kindly scholar, who had always called himself an “anarchist” and had certainly written fiery tracts in his younger manhood, was made the basis of an attack upon Hull House by a daily newspaper, which ignored the fact that while Prince Kropotkin had addressed the Chicago Arts and Crafts Society at Hull House, giving a digest of his remarkable book on “Fields, Factories, and Workshops,” he had also spoken at the State Universities of Illinois and Wisconsin and before the leading literary and scientific societies of Chicago. These institutions and societies were not, therefore, called anarchistic. Hull House had doubtless laid itself open to this attack through an incident connected with the imprisonment of the editor on an anarchistic paper, who was arrested in Chicago immediately after the assassination of President McKinley. In the excitement following the national calamity and the avowal by the assassin of the influence of the anarchistic lecture to which he had listened, arrests were made in Chicago of everyone suspected of anarchy, in the belief that a widespread plot would be uncovered. The editor’s house was searched for incriminating literature, his wife and daughter taken to a police station, and his son and himself, with several other suspected anarchists, were placed in the disused cells in the basement of the city hall.

===Chinese===

在这支奇妙的革命者的队伍里，克鲁泡特金亲王（Prince Kropotkin）——或者照他喜欢的称呼，彼得·克鲁泡特金——无疑是最杰出的一位。他来美国巡回讲演时，全国怀着极大的兴趣和敬意听他；他逗留芝加哥期间作赫尔馆的客人，当时并未引起多少注意；可是两年后麦金利总统遇刺，这位一向自称「无政府主义者」、年轻时也确实写过激烈小册子的和善学者的来访，就被一家日报当作攻击赫尔馆的口实；报纸全然不顾这样的事实：克鲁泡特金亲王在赫尔馆对芝加哥艺术手工协会（Chicago Arts and Crafts Society）讲演、概述他那本了不起的《田野、工厂与工场》（Fields, Factories, and Workshops）之外，还在伊利诺伊和威斯康星两所州立大学、在芝加哥领头的文学和科学团体面前讲过。这些机构和团体可没被叫做无政府主义的。赫尔馆无疑因为一桩与一家无政府主义报纸主编被囚有关的事，给这场攻击留了口子；麦金利总统遇刺后，那位主编立即在芝加哥被捕。国难带来的震动、以及刺客自供受了所听无政府主义讲演的影响，使芝加哥逮捕了一切涉嫌无政府主义的人，指望挖出一张大网。主编的家被搜了教人定罪的书刊，他的妻子和女儿被带进警察局，他本人和儿子同另外几个嫌疑的无政府主义者一起，被关进市政厅地下室废弃的牢房。

===Original===

It is impossible to overstate the public excitement of the moment and the unfathomable sense of horror with which the community regarded an attack upon the chief executive of the nation, as a crime against government itself which compels an instinctive recoil from all law-abiding citizens. Doubtless both the horror and recoil have their roots deep down in human experience; the earliest forms of government implied a group which offered competent resistance to outsiders, but assuming no protection was necessary between any two of its own members, promptly punished with death the traitor who had assaulted anyone within. An anarchistic attack against an official thus furnishes an accredited basis both for unreasoning hatred and for prompt punishment. Both the hatred and the determination to punish reached the highest pitch in Chicago after the assassination of President McKinley, and the group of wretched men detained in the old-fashioned, scarcely habitable cells, had not the least idea of their ultimate fate. They were not allowed to see an attorney and were kept “incommunicado” as their excited friends called it. I had seen the editor and his family only during Prince Kropotkin’s stay at Hull House, when they had come to visit him several times. The editor had impressed me as a quiet, scholarly man, challenging the social order by the philosophic touchstone of Bakunin and of Herbert Spencer, somewhat startled by the radicalism of his fiery young son and much comforted by the German domesticity of his wife and daughter. Perhaps it was but my hysterical symptom of the universal excitement, but it certainly seemed to me more than I could bear when a group of his individualistic friends, who had come to ask for help, said: “You see what becomes of your boasted law; the authorities won’t even allow an attorney, nor will they accept bail for these men, against whom nothing can be proved, although the veriest criminals are not denied such a right.” Challenged by an anarchist, one is always sensitive for the honor of legally constituted society, and I replied that of course the men could have an attorney, that the assassin himself would eventually be furnished with one, that the fact that a man was an anarchist had nothing to do with his rights before the law!

===Chinese===

公众当时的激动、全社会对攻击国家元首之事那种深不可测的恐怖，怎么形容都不为过——那是一桩针对政府本身的罪行，迫使一切守法的公民本能地退避。无疑，这恐怖和退避深深扎根于人的经验：最早的政府形式意味着一个对外能作有效抵抗的群体，但假定群体内部任何两人之间无须保护，于是把侵害了内部任何人的叛徒立即处死。这样，针对官员的无政府主义攻击，就为无理性的仇恨和迅速的惩罚提供了公认的基础。麦金利总统遇刺后，这仇恨和惩罚的决心在芝加哥达到顶点；被拘在那些老式、几乎不宜住人的牢房里的一伙倒帞的人，对自己的最终命运毫无所知。他们不许见律师，被「隔绝」着——他们激动的朋友们这样说。我只是在克鲁泡特金亲王住赫尔馆期间见过那位主编和他的家人，他们来拜访过他几次。主编给我的印象是个安静的有学问的人，拿巴枯宁和斯宾塞的哲学试金石挑战社会秩序，为自己烈火般的年轻儿子激惊，又从妻子和女儿德国式的家常里得到许多安慰。也许这只是普遍激动在我身上的歇斯底里症状，但当他那一伙个人主义的朋友来求援，说：「你瞧你那夸耀的法律落得什么下场；当局连律师都不许，也不肯接受这几个人的保释，他们什么罪名也证不了，哪怕十恶不赦的罪犯也不被拒绝这种权利」——我确实觉得超过了我的承受。被一个无政府主义者一激，人总是格外要为合法组成的社会的体面敏感；我回答说，这些人当然可以有律师，连刺客本人最终也会给他指定一名，一个人是无政府主义者，同他在法律面前的权利毫无关系！

===Original===

I was met with the retort that that might do for a theory, but that the fact still remained that these men had been absolutely isolated, seeing no one but policemen, who constantly frightened them with tales of public clamor and threatened lynching.

The conversation took place on Saturday night and, as the final police authority rests in the mayor, with a friend who was equally disturbed over the situation, I repaired to his house on Sunday morning to appeal to him in the interest of a law and order that should not yield to panic. We contended that to the anarchist above all men it must be demonstrated that law is impartial and stands the test of every strain. The mayor heard us through with the ready sympathy of the successful politician. He insisted, however, that the men thus far had merely been properly protected against lynching, but that it might now be safe to allow them to see someone; he would not yet, however, take the responsibility of permitting an attorney, but if I myself chose to see them on the humanitarian errand of an assurance of fair play, he would write me a permit at once. I promptly fell into the trap, if trap it was, and within half an hour was in a corridor in the city hall basement, talking to the distracted editor and surrounded by a cordon of police, who assured me that it was not safe to permit him out of his cell. The editor, who had grown thin and haggard under his suspense, asked immediately as to the whereabouts of his wife and daughter, concerning whom he had heard not a word since he had seen them arrested. Gradually he became composed as he learned, not that his testimony had been believed to the effect that he had never seen the assassin but once and had then considered him a foolish half-witted creature, but that the most thoroughgoing “dragnet” investigations on the part of the united police of the country had failed to discover a plot and that the public was gradually becoming convinced that the dastardly act was that of a solitary man with no political or social affiliations.

===Chinese===

我得到的反驳是：那作为理论也许说得通，可事实仍是这几个人被彻底隔离了，除了警察谁也见不着，警察还不断拿公众叫嚣的传说吓唬他们，威胁说要动私刑。

这场谈话发生在星期六夜里；由于警察系统最终权在市长，星期日早晨我便同一位同样为此事不安的朋友到市长家去，为一种不向慌乱屈服的法与秩序向他呼吁。我们主张，对无政府主义者尤其要证明法律不偏不倍，经得起一切考验。市长带着成功政客现成的同情把我们听完。可他坚持说，这些人至今不过是受到了免遭私刑的正当保护，现在许他们见见人也许安全了；但他还不肯担责许他们见律师；不过，倘若我自己愿意以公道的担保这一人道差使去见他们，他立刻给我写许可证。我立刻落进了圈套——如果那是圈套——不出半小时，就站在市政厅地下室一条走廊里，同那位心神错乱的主编说话，四周是一圈警察，他们向我保证，放他出牢房不安全。主编在悬虑里瘦削憔悴了，第一句就问妻子和女儿在哪里，自亲眼见她们被捕，他没听到她们一个字。他渐渐定下神来——倒不是因为他的证词被相信了（他供称只见过刺客一面，当时就当他是个愚蠢的半傻子），而是因为全国警察联合起来那场最彻底的「拉网」调查未能发现任何阴谋，公众渐渐相信，那件卑镕的勾当是一个孤零零的人干的，没有任何政治或社会的勾连。

===Original===

The entire conversation was simple and did not seem to me unlike, in motive or character, interviews I had had with many another forlorn man who had fallen into prison. I had scarce returned to Hull House, however, before it was filled with reporters, and I at once discovered that whether or not I had helped a brother out of a pit, I had fallen into a deep one myself. A period of sharp public opprobrium followed, traces of which, I suppose, will always remain. And yet in the midst of the letters of protest and accusation which made my mail a horror every morning came a few letters of another sort, one from a federal judge whom I had never seen and another from a distinguished professor in the constitutional law, who congratulated me on what they termed a sane attempt to uphold the law in time of panic.

Although one or two ardent young people rushed into print to defend me from the charge of “abetting anarchy,” it seemed to me at the time that mere words would not avail. I had felt that the protection of the law itself extended to the most unpopular citizen was the only reply to the anarchistic argument, to the effect that this moment of panic revealed the truth of their theory of government; that the custodians of law and order have become the government itself quite as the armed men hired by the medieval guilds to protect them in the peaceful pursuit of their avocations, through sheer possession of arms finally made themselves rulers of the city. At that moment I was firmly convinced that the public could only be convicted of the blindness of its course, when a body of people with a hundredfold of the moral energy possessed by a Settlement group, should make clear that there is no method by which any community can be guarded against sporadic efforts on the part of half-crazed, discouraged men, save by a sense of mutual rights and securities which will include the veriest outcast.

===Chinese===

整个谈话很简单，在我看来，无论动机还是性质，都同我同许多另一个落进监狱的孤苦人的谈话没有两样。然而我前脚刚回赫尔馆，后脚馆里就挤满了记者；我立刻发现，无论我有没有把一个弟兄从坑里拉出来，我自己是掉进了一个深坑。随后是一段尖锐的公众谴责，我想它的痕迹会一直留着。然而在使我的邮件每天早上成为恐怖的抗议信和指责信中间，也来了几封另一种信：一封来自一位我从未见过的联邦法官，另一封来自一位杰出的宪法学教授，他们祝贺我做了一件他们称之为在恐慌时刻维护法律的清醒尝试。

虽然有一两个热烈青年冲进报章为我辩护「救唆无政府主义」的指控，我当时却觉得空话无济于事。我感到，法律自身的保护延伸到最不得人心的公民，是对无政府主义论证唯一的回答——他们说，这恐慌的一刻揭示了他们政府理论的真实：法律与秩序的看守人自己变成了政府，正如中世纪行会雇来保护他们和平营生的武装人员，凭着对武器的掌握终于自己做了城市的主人。那一刻我坚信：只有当一个团体——拥有定居所团体百倍的道德能量——向公众说清楚，除了把最微贱的奔民也包含在内的互相的权利与安全之感，没有任何办法能保一个社会不受半疯半狂、心灰意懒之人的零星攻击，公众才能被判定为看不清自己的路。

===Original===

It seemed to me then that in the millions of words uttered and written at that time, no one adequately urged that public-spirited citizens set themselves the task of patiently discovering how these sporadic acts of violence against government may be understood and averted. We do not know whether they occur among the discouraged and unassimilated immigrants who might be cared for in such a way as enormously to lessen the probability of these acts, or whether they are the result of anarchistic teaching. By hastily concluding that the latter is the sole explanation for them, we make no attempt to heal and cure the situation. Failure to make a proper diagnosis may mean treatment of a disease which does not exist, or it may furthermore mean that the dire malady from which the patient is suffering be permitted to develop unchecked. And yet as the details of the meager life of the President’s assassin were disclosed, they were a challenge to the forces for social betterment in American cities. Was it not an indictment to all those whose business it is to interpret and solace the wretched, that a boy should have grown up in an American city so uncared for, so untouched by higher issues, his wounds of life so unhealed by religion that the first talk he ever heard dealing with life’s wrongs, although anarchistic and violent, should yet appear to point a way of relief?

The conviction that a sense of fellowship is the only implement which will break into the locked purpose of a half-crazed creature bent upon destruction in the name of justice, came to me through an experience recited to me at this time by an old anarchist.

===Chinese===

在我看来，当时讲出的和写出的千百万字里，没有人充分敦促热心公益的公民们担起任务，耐心探究这些针对政府的零星暴力行为怎样才能被理解和防止。我们不知道它们是不是发生在那些失意的、未被同化的移民中间——这批人本可以受到那样的照护，大大减少这种行为的发生；也不知道它们是不是无政府主义宣教的结果。匆匆断定后者是唯一的解释，我们就全不作医治局面的努力。诊断不当，可能意味着医治一种不存在的病，还可能进一步意味着，放任病人所患的可怕疾病不受阻挡地发展。然而，随着总统刺客那点贫乏生平的细节披露，它们是对美国城市里社会改善力量的一个挑战。一个男孩在美国的城市里长大，竟这样无人照管，这样未破更高的关切开垦，生活的创伤这样不曾被宗教医治，以至于他生平听到的第一场谈论人间不平的话——尽管是无政府主义的、暴力的——竟显得指出了一条解脱之路；对于那些以诠释和抚慰苦难者为业的人，这难道不是一纸控诉吗？

这样一种信念——情谊之感是唯一能攻破一个半疯之人锁死的目的的工具，那人以正义之名执意于毁灭——是通过一位老无政府主义者这时向我讲述的一段经历来到我这里的。

===Original===

He was a German cobbler who, through all the changes in the manufacturing of shoes, had steadily clung to his little shop on a Chicago thoroughfare, partly as an expression of his individualism and partly because he preferred bitter poverty in a place of his own to good wages under a disciplinary foreman. The assassin of President McKinley on his way through Chicago only a few days before he committed his dastardly deed had visited all the anarchists whom he could find in the city, asking them for “the password” as he called it. They, of course, possessed no such thing, and had turned him away, some with disgust and all with a certain degree of impatience, as a type of the ill-balanced man who, as they put it, was always “hanging around the movement, without the slightest conception of its meaning.” Among other people, he visited the German cobbler, who treated him much as the others had done, but who, after the event had made clear the identity of his visitor, was filled with the most bitter remorse that he had failed to utilize his chance meeting with the assassin to deter him from his purpose. He knew as well as any psychologist who has read the history of such solitary men that the only possible way to break down such a persistent and secretive purpose, was by the kindliness which might have induced confession, which might have restored the future assassin into fellowship with normal men.

===Chinese===

他是个德国鞋匠，任凭制鞋业怎样变迁，始终守着他在芝加哥一条大街上的小店，一半是对他个人主义的表白，一半是因为他宁要自有之地里的清贫，不要纪律工头底下的好工钱。麦金利的刺客行凶前几天路过芝加哥，拜访了城里他找得到的所有无政府主义者，向他们讨他所谓的「暗号」。他们当然没有这种东西，把他打发走了：有的带着厌恶，全都带着几分不耐烦，把他当作那种心智失衡的人——照他们的说法，这种人总是「在运动边上晃荡，对运动的意义毫无概念」。在这些人中间，他也拜访了那位德国鞋匠；鞋匠待他同别人差不多，可是事后弄清了来访者是谁，心里充满了最痛的悔恨：自己竟没有利用同刺客邂逅的机会拦住他。他同任何一个读过这类孤独者历史的心理学家一样清楚：要攻破这样一个执拗而隐秘的目的，唯一可能的办法，是那种或许能引出坦白的和善——它本可以把未来的刺客重新拉回同正常人的情谊里。

===Original===

In the midst of his remorse, the cobbler told me a tale of his own youth; that years before, when an ardent young fellow in Germany, newly converted to the philosophy of anarchism, as he called it, he had made up his mind that the Church, as much as the State, was responsible for human oppression, and that this fact could best be set forth “in the deed” by the public destruction of a clergyman or priest; that he had carried firearms for a year with this purpose in mind, but that one pleasant summer evening, in a moment of weakness, he had confided his intention to a friend, and that from that moment he not only lost all desire to carry it out, but it seemed to him the most preposterous thing imaginable. In concluding the story he said; “That poor fellow sat just beside me on my bench; if I had only put my hand on his shoulder and said, ‘Now, look here, brother, what is on your mind? What makes you talk such nonsense? Tell me. I have seen much of life, and understand all kinds of men. I have been young and hotheaded and foolish myself,’ if he had told me of his purpose then and there, he would never have carried it out. The whole nation would have been spared this horror.” As he concluded he shook his gray head and sighed as if the whole incident were more than he could bear﻿—one of those terrible sins of omission; one of the things he “ought to have done,” the memory of which is so hard to endure.

The attempt a Settlement makes to interpret American institutions to those who are bewildered concerning them either because of their personal experiences, or because of preconceived theories, would seem to lie in the direct path of its public obligation, and yet it is apparently impossible for the overwrought community to distinguish between the excitement the Settlements are endeavoring to understand and to allay and the attitude of the Settlement itself. At times of public panic, fervid denunciation is held to be the duty of every good citizen, and if a Settlement is convinced that the incident should be used to vindicate the law and does not at the moment give its strength to denunciation, its attitude is at once taken to imply a championship of anarchy itself.

===Chinese===

在悔恨中间，鞋匠给我讲了他自己年轻时的事：多年以前，他还是德国一个热烈青年，刚的依他所谓的无政府主义哲学，他认定教会同国家一样要对人的压迫负责，而这一层最好「以行动」公开处死一名教士或神父来表明；为这个目的他带了一年火器；可是一个愉快的夏日傍晚，他在一个软弱的时刻把意图吐露给了一位朋友，从那一刻起，他不但失去了执行它的一切欲望，而且它在他看来成了想象中最荒唐的事。讲完这故事他说：「那可怜的家伙就坐在我長凳上我旁边；我只要把手搭在他肩上说：『喂，兄弟，你心里压着什么事？怎么净说这种胡话？告诉我。我见过很多世面，什么样的人都僅。我自己也年轻过、冲动过、傻过。』——如果他当时当场把他的目的告诉我，他就永远不会去执行了。整个国家就可以免掉这场恐怖。」说完，他摇着灰白的头叹气，仿佛这件事超过了他的承受——那是罪中之罪，一种疏忽之罪；那些他「本该去做」而未做的事，记忆最难忍受。

定居所向那些因为亲身经历或先入之见而对美国制度惶惑的人诠释这些制度，这努力似乎正走在它公共义务的必经之路上；可是过于激动的公众显然分不清定居所努力去理解和平息的那股激动、同定居所自身的态度。公众恐慌的时刻，火辣辣的谴责被当作每个好公民的义务；倘若定居所认为这事件正应用来证明法律的正当、而不在此刻出力谴责，它的态度立刻被当作无政府主义本身的媚护。

===Original===

The public mind at such a moment falls into the old medieval confusion﻿—he who feeds or shelters a heretic is upon prima facie evidence a heretic himself﻿—he who knows intimately people among whom anarchists arise is therefore an anarchist. I personally am convinced that anarchy as a philosophy is dying down, not only in Chicago, but everywhere; that their leading organs have discontinued publication, and that their most eminent men in America have deserted them. Even those groups which have continued to meet are dividing, and the major half in almost every instance calls itself socialist-anarchists, an apparent contradiction of terms, whose members insist that the socialistic organization of society must be the next stage of social development and must be gone through with, so to speak, before the ideal state of society can be reached, so nearly begging the question that some orthodox socialists are willing to recognize them. It is certainly true that just because anarchy questions the very foundations of society, the most elemental sense of protection demands that the method of meeting the challenge should be intelligently considered.

Whether or not Hull House has accomplished anything by its method of meeting such a situation, or at least attempting to treat it in a way which will not destroy confidence in the American institutions so adored by refugees from foreign governmental oppression, it is of course impossible for me to say.

===Chinese===

公众的头脑在这种时刻落回古老的中世纪混乱——谁给异端饭吃或留宿，凭表面证据他本人就是异端——谁同无政府主义者从中产生的人群关系密切，谁就是无政府主义者。我个人相信，无政府主义作为一种哲学正在消亡，不但在芝加哥，在所有地方都是；他们的主要机关刊物停刊了，他们在美国最杰出的人物离弃了他们。连那些继续聚会的团体也在分化，几乎每一处的多数派自称社会主义无政府主义者——一个明显的矛盾词——其会员坚持说，社会的社会主义组织必须是社会发展的下一阶段，必须先经历过去，然后才能达到理想的社会状态；这几乎是巧词循环，以至于某些正统社会主义者也愿意承认他们。当然，正因为无政府主义质疑的正是社会的根基，最原始的自卫感也要求对付这挑战的方法得到明智的考虑。

赫尔馆用它应对这种局面的方法——至少试图用一种不摧毁信心于美国制度的方法来处理它，那些从外国政府压迫下逃出来的难民是多么崇拜这些制度——究竟有没有成就，我当然无从说起。

===Original===

And yet it was in connection with an effort to pursue an intelligent policy in regard to a so-called “foreign anarchist” that Hull House again became associated with that creed six years later. This again was an echo of the Russian revolution, but in connection with one of its humblest representatives. A young Russian Jew named Averbuch appeared in the early morning at the house of the Chicago chief of police upon an obscure errand. It was a moment of panic everwhere in regard to anarchists because of a recent murder in Denver which had been charged to an Italian anarchist, and the chief of police, assuming that the dark young man standing in his hallway was an anarchist bent upon his assassination, hastily called for help. In a panic born of fear and self-defense, young Averbuch was shot to death. The members of the Russian-Jewish colony on the west side of Chicago were thrown into a state of intense excitement as soon as the nationality of the young man became known. They were filled with dark forebodings from a swift prescience of what it would mean to them were the oduim of anarchy rightly or wrongly attached to one of their members. It seemed to the residents of Hull House most important that every effort should be made to ascertain just what did happen, that every means of securing information should be exhausted before a final opinion should be formed, and this odium fastened upon a colony of law-abiding citizens. The police might be right or wrong in their assertion that the man was an anarchist. It was, to our minds, also most unfortunate that the Chicago police in the determination to uncover an anarchistic plot should have utilized the most drastic methods of search within the Russian-Jewish colony composed of families only too familiar with the methods of Russian police.

===Chinese===

然而，正是在努力对一个所谓「外国无政府主义者」执行一项明智政策的过程中，六年后赫尔馆再次同那个主义连在一起。这又是俄国革命的一声回声，不过同它一个最卑微的代表有关。一个名叫阿弗布赫的年轻俄国犹太人，清晨出现在芝加哥警察局长家门口，为一件说不清的事。那时因为丹佛新近发生一桩归咎于一个意大利无政府主义者的谋杀，各地对无政府主义者正一片惊惇；警察局长认定站在他门厅里的黑发青年是个执意要刺杀他的无政府主义者，慌忙唤人。在恐惧和自卫所生的慌乱中，年轻的阿弗布赫被开枪打死了。年轻人的国籍一经传开，芝加哥西侧的俄国犹太侨民地立即陷入极度激动。他们心里充满了阴暗的预感，迅速预见到无政府主义的骂名——不论真假——落在他们一个人头上，对他们意味着什么。赫尔馆的居民们认为最要紧的是：尽一切努力查明确实发生了什么，用尽一切获取信息的手段，然后才形成最终意见，才把这骂名钉到一区守法公民身上。警察说那人是无政府主义者，可能对可能错。在我们看来同样非常不幸的是：芝加哥警察决意挖出无政府主义阴谋，竟在俄国犹太侨民地用起了最猛烈的搜查手段——这里的家庭，对俄国警察的手段实在熟悉不过。

===Original===

Therefore, when the Chicago police ransacked all the printing offices they could locate in the colony, when they raided a restaurant which they regarded as suspicious because it had been supplying food at cost to the unemployed, when they searched through private houses for papers and photographs of revolutionaries, when they seized the library of the Edelstadt group and carried the books, including Shakespeare and Herbert Spencer, to the city hall, when they arrested two friends of young Averbuch and kept them in the police station forty-eight hours, when they mercilessly “sweated” the sister, Olga, that she might be startled into a confession﻿—all these things so poignantly reminded them of Russian methods that indignation fed both by old memory and bitter disappointment in America, swept over the entire colony. The older men asked whether constitutional rights gave no guarantee against such violent aggression of police power, and the hotheaded younger ones cried out at once that the only way to deal with the police was to defy them, which was true of police the world over. It was said many times that those who are without influence and protection in a strange country fare exactly as hard as do the poor in Europe; that all the talk of guaranteed protection through political institutions is nonsense.

Every Settlement has classes in citizenship in which the principles of American institutions are expounded, and of these the community, as a whole, approves. But the Settlements know better than anyone else that while these classes and lectures are useful, nothing can possibly give lessons in citizenship so effectively and make so clear the constitutional basis of a self-governing community as the current event itself. The treatment at a given moment of that foreign colony which feels itself outraged and misunderstood, either makes its constitutional rights clear to it, or forever confuses it on the subject.

===Chinese===

于是，当芝加哥警察把侨民地里找得到的印刷所全抄了；当他们搜查一家餐馆——他们疑心它，因为它一直按成本价给失业者供饭；当他们闯进私宅搜革命者的文件和照片；当他们没收埃德尔施塔特小组（Edelstadt group）的图书，把书——包括莎士比亚和斯宾塞——搬到市政厅；当他们逮捕阿弗布赫的两个朋友，把他们扣在警察局四十八小时；当他们无情地「疲劳审讯」妹妹奥尔加（Olga），想把她吓出一份口供——这一切如此痛切地让他们想起俄国的手段，以致由旧日记忆和对美国的痛苦失望一起燃起的义慯，扫过整个侨民地。年长的人问，宪法权利难道不能保证免遗警察权力这样的粗暴侵害；性急的年轻人立刻喊出：对付警察唯一的办法就是同他们对着干，全世界的警察都是如此。许多逼真说了许多遍：在异国没有势力没有靠山的人，吃的苦同欧洲的穷人一模一样；所谓政治制度保证保护的那些话，全是胡说。

每个定居所都有公民课，讲授美国制度的原理，全社会对此是赞许的。但定居所比谁都清楚：这些课程和讲演固然有用，却没有什么能像时事本身那样，把公民课救得那么有效，把一个自治社会的宪法基础弄得那么清楚。在一个给定的时刻，怎样对待那个自觉受尽委屈、遭人误解的外国侨民地，要么使它的宪法权利对它变得清楚，要么使它在这个问题上永远糊涂。

===Original===

The only method by which a reasonable and loyal conception of government may be substituted for the one formed upon Russian experiences is that the actual experience of refugees with government in America shall gradually demonstrate what a very different thing government means here. Such an event as the Averbuch affair affords an unprecedented opportunity to make clear this difference and to demonstrate beyond the possibility of misunderstanding that the guarantee of constitutional rights implies that officialism shall be restrained and guarded at every point, that the official represents, not the will of a small administrative body, but the will of the entire people, and that methods therefore have been constituted by which official aggression may be restrained. The Averbuch incident gave an opportunity to demonstrate this to that very body of people who need it most; to those who have lived in Russia where autocratic officers represent autocratic power and where government is officialism. It seemed to the residents in the Settlements nearest the Russian-Jewish colony that it was an obvious piece of public spirit to try out all the legal value involved, to insist that American institutions were stout enough to break down in times of stress and public panic.

===Chinese===

要用来替换那种在俄国经验上形成的政府观念、换上一种理性而忠诚的政府观念，唯一的办法是：难民们在美国同政府打交道的实际经历，渐渐向他们证明这里的政府是多么不同的一回事。像阿弗布赫事件这样的事，提供了一个空前的机会来澄清这个差别，并目毫无误解可能地证明：宪法权利的保障意味着官府在每一点上都受约束和看管；官员代表的不是一个小小行政机构的意志，而是全体人民的意志；因此已经建立了种种办法，可以约束官员的侵害。阿弗uch事件给了机会，向最需要这证明的那一群人证明这一点——向那些在俄国住过的人，在那里专制官员代表专制权力，政府就是官府。离俄国犹太侨民地最近的几家定居所的居民们都觉得：把这件事里全部法律价值试尽、坚持美国制度在艰难和公众恐慌的时刻也足够坚韧、不会垮掉，这显然是一件公共精神之举。

===Original===

The belief of many Russians that the Averbuch incident would be made a prelude to the constant use of the extradition treaty for the sake of terrorizing revolutionists both at home and abroad received a certain corroboration when an attempt was made in 1908 to extradite a Russian revolutionist named Rudovitz who was living in Chicago.
